# D through H

## Dating – Philosophy for Everyone - Flirting With Big Ideas

- ch. 1 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(notes)*
- ch. 4 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** René Descartes, Thomas Hobbes, Albert Camus — Philosophy of Love — In this foreword Joshua Wolf Shenk recounts an intoxicating blind date with 'Rachael' to argue that dating exposes the core existential dilemma of social creatures: we crave ecstasy (ekstasis, 'standing outside oneself') yet can only connect by holding on to a discrete identity. He blames the modern isolation of the self on Descartes' claim that the self inhabits its own subjective realm and Hobbes' picture of natural man as solitary, and cites Camus that once you choose to live, the real question is how to relate to others. His unresolved paradox—needing the mantra 'I was complete before I met you' while knowing it is false—frames love as the tension between self-sufficiency and merger.
- ch. 8 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(notes)*
- ch. 9 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Aristotle — Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Philosophy of Love — Editors Kristie Miller and Marlene Clark introduce the volume by opening with Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics claim that the human drive to couple is even stronger than the drive to live as a political being and that fulfillment rests in loving relationships. They frame dating as a rule-governed 'game' with aims and strategies and preview each contributor's essay—Jenkins on flirting, Langan on conservative men, Samp and Cohen against unconditional love, the elevator metaphor, cyberdating, evolutionary seduction, and Colyvan's game-theoretic mate search—tying age-old ethical questions about treating partners well to new work in evolutionary psychology, decision theory, and the ethics of online romance.
- ch. 10 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 11 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Saul Kripke — Philosophy of Love — Carrie Jenkins does analytic conceptual analysis of flirting, rejecting the Oxford English Dictionary's claim that flirting requires having no serious intentions. She distinguishes 'flirting' from merely 'behaving flirtatiously' (Joe who over-holds eye contact, the tourist touching her earlobe in Italy are flirtatious without flirting) and argues flirting requires an intention to act in ways disposed to raise flirter-flirtee romance to salience, done in a 'knowing yet playful' way. Borrowing Kripke's 'killer yellow' to imagine 'killer-flirts' who die on contact, and citing chatbot studies, she concludes one can flirt with an imagined or non-human target only if one believes it can respond significantly.
- ch. 12 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Philosophy of Love — Emily Langan uses focus-group data from 75 self-described ideologically conservative, mostly Christian young men to argue that flirting is a site of moral decision-making, opening with Hitch's 'right broom' line to show these men reject its libido-driven premise. She reports three shared themes—flirting is not about sex but about honesty of motive, timing within a relationship's stages, and reciprocity ('it has to be a two-way street')—and three tensions (healthy vs. harmful, superficial vs. serious, overt vs. covert), drawing on courtship-stage and flirting-style research to show that, contrary to the stereotype that only women agonize over relationships, conservative men wrestle with being 'risqué yet respectable.'
- ch. 13 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Aristotle — Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Philosophy of Love — Jennifer Samp and Andrew Cohen argue against the Notebook-style romantic ideal of unconditional love, casting dating as a 'calculated exchange' explained by social-exchange and interdependence theory (comparison level and comparison level of alternatives). Adopting Aristotle's distinction between friendships of utility/pleasure and 'complete friendships' in which one values the friend as 'another self' and as an end in itself, they hold the best romances are complete friendships—yet insist even these must be conditioned. Their key thought experiment: a genuinely boundless love would survive discovering one's partner is a child-molesting murderer; since it should not, 'true love knows bounds' and remains part of a good life only because it can be defeated by such reasons.
- ch. 14 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Aristotle, Heraclitus, David Hume, Plato — Everything Flows / Logos; The Golden Mean; Virtue Ethics — John Rowan and Patricia Hallen build an extended elevator metaphor for dating (Ann and Bob riding floors, pushing buttons to raise or lower intimacy, gravity pulling toward breakup) to capture Heraclitus's insight that a relationship is a reality in constant change, while invoking Plato to insist the conceptual reality of a relationship is as real as physical objects. They define dating as gauging the possibility of eventual commitment, cite Hume's canoe image for implicit communication of shared destination, and ground their strategies—be yourself, go slowly, communicate, befriend—in Aristotle's virtue ethics, treating honesty, courage, and loyalty as habits lying in the golden mean between deficiency and excess (e.g., the Look Who's Talking date who overshares is 'excessively honest').
- ch. 15 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 16 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Plato, Aristotle — Platonic Love (the ladder of love); Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Philosophy of Love — Mary Beth Yount asks whether being 'crazy in love' really resembles mental illness, noting lovesickness was a diagnosis for nearly two millennia and that love's mood swings mimic bipolar or schizophrenic symptoms. She organizes the analysis around the four Greek loves—eros (with Plato's ladder from physical beauty to the Forms, i.e., 'Platonic love'), philia (Aristotle's Nicomachean friendship of good character), storge (familial affection), and agape (C.S. Lewis's unconditional charity)—arguing a full romance blends all four. She then surveys Fisher, Aron, and Brown's fMRI work showing romantic love as a dopamine-driven mammalian mate-choice drive stronger than the sex drive, and the 'protest'/'resignation' stages of rejection, to reassure daters their craziness is normal biology, not disorder.
- ch. 17 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Personal Identity; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Kristie Miller asks whether any dating behavior—dating a sibling, a gorilla, or a blow-up doll—is genuinely 'taboo' in the prudential (self-interested, utility-maximizing) rather than ethical sense, using decision theory's notion of maximizing expected utility. She sorts behaviors labeled 'pathological' into merely conventionally pathological, pseudo-pathological (e.g., polyandry explained by resource scarcity and childrearing), and genuinely pathological (arising from malfunctioning evolved mate-selection modules like symmetry or MHC-odor detection). Because inclusive fitness and utility diverge, and using future-selves discounting and weakness of will (the stable partner vs. the bad-boy biker), she concludes that in some circumstances even dating a sibling or a gorilla can be the utility-maximizing, and thus rational, choice.
- ch. 18 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Immanuel Kant, Christine Korsgaard, Aristotle — Humanity as an End in Itself; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Virtue Ethics (arete) — Anne Barnhill uses John Cusack's boom-box serenade in Say Anything, his poolside pleading in The Sure Thing, and Andy Bernard's 'Take a Chance on Me' serenade in The Office to defend 'prospective boundary violation'—violating a boundary that would not exist in a closer relationship in order to create that relationship. She rejects the Kantian reading of Korsgaard and Hill that treating people as ends forbids all non-rational manipulation, arguing instead that such charming pushiness is 'faking it till you make it,' which works via Aristotle's habituation whereby feelings catch up with actions. She then sets four rules (no harm, not too much closer too fast, genuinely wanting the relationship, and that the other would plausibly want it) to separate appropriate from wrong boundary violations.
- ch. 19 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Objectification; Feminism; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Kyla Reid and Tinashe Dune attack the bestselling dating manual The Rules by Fein and Schneider, focusing on Rule 4 (never go Dutch—let the man pay) and Rule 12 (dump him if he gives no romantic gift like jewelry). They line up 'Rules Girls' against sex workers offering a paid 'girlfriend experience' and 'professional girlfriends'/gold diggers, arguing that all three approach relationships in a systematic, 'business-like' way that packages and markets the woman as a product, measures her worth by what the man spends, and treats gifts like performance bonuses. Because monogamy, public recognition, and belief in the relationship cannot make it authentic, they conclude The Rules obstruct genuine intimacy and that everyone should be more critical of dating conventions about who pays and what gifts mean.
- ch. 20 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 21 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Aristotle, Martha Nussbaum — Eudaimonia (flourishing); Virtue Ethics; Aristotle on Friendship (philia) — Joshua Heter mounts a virtue-theoretic case against being set up on blind dates, using Aristotle's notion of telos and the distinction between fleeting 'hot-fudge happiness' and eudaimonia, the flourishing of a well-lived life (illustrated by long-married Bill and Fran). He argues a 'guy's' function is achieved by exercising his whole nature, and dramatizes this with Zoo-bear versus Wild-bear: the zoo-fed bear has its hunger and safety met but cannot flourish because it never hunts or roams, just as matchmaking meets the romantic need while letting a man's 'social muscles' atrophy. Invoking Aristotle's philia (and Nussbaum on Aristotelian virtue), he concedes friends mean well but recommends they merely introduce people and let things progress naturally rather than force an 'unnatural' match.
- ch. 22 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Aristotle — Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Virtue Ethics (arete) — Hamilton reads the pick-up-artist 'Game' (Mystery, Style, negs, peacocking, the Alpha frame) against Aristotle's great-souled magnanimous man, arguing that the jerk and the PUA succeed because they inadvertently mimic the Megalopsuche's self-containment and indifference to others' approval. He contends the ethical route is to genuinely acquire that character through habituation ('we become brave by acting bravely') rather than fake it, so the nice guy's supplication fails while true magnanimity requires the full virtues. He then uses Aristotle's three grades of friendship (utility, pleasure, and the highest disinterested philia) to argue that erotic relationships can rise to real friendship only where partners are equals, citing When Harry Met Sally on whether men and women can be friends.
- ch. 23 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Bertrand Russell, David Hume — Problem of Induction — Terjesen defines a 'real date' as a shared intention to try out romantic possibility, using Abelard's claim that intention (not behavior) determines the nature of an act to show that identical activities are or aren't dates depending on mutual intent. He then invokes Russell's argument-from-analogy account of knowing other minds to explain how daters 'mind-read' each other's intentions, and notes this yields only probable conclusions, generating an infinite regress ('I know that you know...') that only 'the Talk' resolves. He cites Hume's problem of induction as the deeper reason analogical inference can never be certain, and uses the Friends 'Mayor of the Friend Zone' gag and Ross and Rachel to argue the Friend Zone is a step toward, not a trap against, committed relationships.
- ch. 24 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Immanuel Kant, Zhuangzi — The Categorical Imperative; Wu Wei (effortless action) — Brown and Tien build a 'Kantian Gate' from Kant's Formula of Universal Law: you should date only if you wouldn't mind your dates acting on your own dating maxim, which bars the manipulative, deceptive, or coercive dater while permitting many ends. For those who pass the gate, they recommend pursuing Csikszentmihalyi's 'flow' and the Zhuangzi's cultivated spontaneity, describing a two-part self-cultivation (purging debilitating social programming via 'forgetting' and xinzhai, then training to act in accord with the Dao) so one can 'be in the moment' and get truly lucky on a first date. They apply the seven elements of flow directly to dating conversation, eye contact, and feedback.
- ch. 25 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 26 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(no index entry yet)* — Brinkman uses the Second Life case of Ric and Janet (whose avatars Dutch and Tenaj dated and 'married' virtually before the couple left their spouses) to ask ontological questions about whether virtual dating and virtual cheating are real. Drawing on Huizinga's Homo Ludens, he argues dating is itself pervaded by the 'play-spirit' (the magic circle, the dating script, secrecy, rapture) and that virtual worlds and dating both breed 'spoilsports': he maps virtual-world 'griefers' and pick-up-artist 'wizards' and 'script kiddies' (via Strauss's The Game) onto Huizinga's cheater/spoilsport distinction, concluding that PUAs who treat targets as game pieces commit a moral transgression against ludus even when they don't lie, and that Ric really was cheating.
- ch. 27 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Martin Buber, Jean-Paul Sartre, Immanuel Kant, Jacques Derrida — I–Thou Relation; Bad Faith; Existence Precedes Essence; Objectification — Silber uses his own Match.com romance with Karin to argue that virtual 'meeting' differs phenomenologically from face-to-face meeting, which Buber's I-Thou (vs I-It) makes into a whole-being encounter that online traces and aspects cannot supply, invoking 'all real living is meeting.' He treats the dating market as a site of Kantian danger, contrasting price with the dignity of persons in the kingdom of ends, and warns that the market's efficiency threatens objectification and commodification. He then deploys Sartre's bad faith, facticity and transcendence, and 'existence precedes essence' to diagnose online self-misrepresentation (lying about height, age, photos) as inauthenticity, while briefly setting Derrida's 'nothing outside the text' against Plato's Phaedrus preference for speech over writing.
- ch. 28 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 29 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Aristotle, Immanuel Kant — Eudaimonia (flourishing); Consequentialism — Naar and Masala defend the 'Secret Underground Internet Seduction Community' by grounding it in Darwinian sexual selection (the peacock's tail, mate value, women prioritizing status and men prioritizing youth/beauty) and an Aristotelian ideal of happiness as excellence: they read the community's aim of becoming a well-rounded seducer as cultivating virtue and social/emotional 'mating intelligence' that yields eudaimonic fulfillment. Against the Kantian objection that seduction training uses women merely as means, they offer a consequentialist and 'deflationist' reply that deception and manipulation already pervade ordinary courtship (makeup, lying about age), so raising one's mate value is morally neutral, and they extend the point to women's active and passive seduction strategies (Cleopatra, groupies).
- ch. 30 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Jean-Jacques Rousseau, David Hume — The Social Contract — Colyvan applies mathematics and game theory to mating: the 'secretary problem' yields the optimal strategy of dating and rejecting the first n/e (~37%) of candidates then taking the next best, and Rousseau's stag hunt models monogamy as cooperation (hunt stag = stay faithful) tempted by defection (hunt hare = an affair), with the prisoner's dilemma showing why individual rationality can undermine the Pareto-optimal cooperative outcome. He argues that iterated games and tit-for-tat strategies, plus the 'shadow of the future' and public commitments like weddings, make faithfulness explicable purely by narrow self-interest, quoting Hume on reciprocal service, and suggests love and ethics themselves may be useful fictions layered over the game.
- ch. 31 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Plato — Theory of Forms — Clark uses Barry Schwartz's Paradox of Choice and the characters of Sex and the City to argue that too much dating choice turns people into unhappy 'maximizers' who seek the absolute best, rather than 'satisficers' content with the merely excellent. She traces how Charlotte, Miranda, Carrie, Big, and Samantha each attain their maximized 'ideal' mate (Trey, Robert Leeds, Petrovsky/Big, Richard Wright) and find it hollow, only becoming happy when they 'settle' for a reciprocal partner (Harry, Steve, John, Smith) after the post-9/11 shift in the series' mood. She frames maximizing as clinically narcissistic (choosing a mate as a mirror of oneself) and invokes Plato to warn that trying to make the ideal real yields only disappointment.
- ch. 32 "DATING PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(notes)*

## David Bowie and Philosophy - Rebel Rebel

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "i" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "ii" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Copyright" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "A Farewell to David Bowie" **·** Aristotle — Authenticity; Personal Identity — The editor's farewell argues that Bowie's greatness lay in relentless transformation joined to honesty: unlike acts from Kiss to Miley Cyrus whose theatrics are mere spectacle 'in an Aristotelian sense,' Bowie's characters (Ziggy, the Thin White Duke, simulating fellatio with Mick Ronson) opened a discussion of gender and 'the multiple ways of being in the world.' Tracing the career from parents' fear of rock through the Eno-shaped Berlin trilogy's symbolist minimalism and the despised continuity of 'Changes,' it reads The Next Day as a defacement of 'Heroes' and Blackstar as a Mapplethorpe-like series of self-portraits made while unflinchingly facing death.
- ch. 8 "Part I What David Bowie Was Up To" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "1. The Actor Tells the Truth" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre — Existence Precedes Essence; Authenticity; Utopia and Dystopia — Reisch argues Bowie's parade of characters (Ziggy, Aladdin Sane, the Diamond Dogs persona) was never metaphysical chameleonry but a postwar, cold-war project: by crediting himself only as 'the actor' and calling Dylan out as Robert Zimmerman's artifice, Bowie exposed the 'denim and hippies' authenticity cult as itself a construction. Diamond Dogs' '1984' and 'Big Brother' tie this to the era's totalitarian nightmares of manipulable humanity, and Sartre's 'Man is nothing else but that which he makes of himself' becomes 'the first principle of Bowie' - only constant reinvention keeps us from being fooled by ourselves or others, even at the anguished cost of 'Who Can I Be Now?'
- ch. 10 "2. You Don’t Have to Be Stupid to Be Cool" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — Botz-Bornstein argues Bowie's coolness was an ethico-aesthetic achievement: an awkward, thin Bromley boy who, through hard training with mime Lindsay Kemp, kabuki discipline, and Kansai Yamamoto's costumes, stylized his very body movements - Nietzsche's 'wisdom of the body' over the intellect - into an honest attitude rather than a pose. The chapter explains his 'kitsch-cool' ability to manipulate kitsch (the 'Over the Rainbow' octave of 'Starman,' glam space futures) without being absorbed, reading it through Shuzo Kuki's Japanese aesthetic of iki, whose astringent negation 'cools' the sweet, and contrasts Bowie's playful, virtual transgression with the jihadi's 'worst kitsch' of heroic death.
- ch. 11 "3. The Flux of It All" **·** Heraclitus, Parmenides, David Hume, René Descartes, Ludwig Wittgenstein — Personal Identity; Everything Flows / Logos; Being and Changelessness; Bundle Theory of Self — Ammon asks whether anything persists beneath Bowie's serial personas, staging Parmenides's denial of change (with Zeno's paradoxes) and Descartes's melting wax against the Heraclitean flux of 'ch-ch-ch-changes.' Rejecting soul-substance views, he takes the empiricist line - Hume's introspection finds only particular perceptions, never a self - warns via the intentional fallacy (Manson's 'Helter Skelter,' Bloom's hawk) against letting Bowie's own pronouncements fix his songs' meanings, and tests 'meaning is use' on Bowie's calling rock stars fascists. Recurring undercurrents (dancing, apocalypse, distrust of authority) survive, but the conclusion is that Bowie's essence just is flux, 'the river we can't step into twice.'
- ch. 12 "4. Warm Impermanence" *(no index entry yet)* — Auxier reads Bowie's persona changes through Arthur Danto's philosophy of art: Warhol's 1964 Brillo Box ended art history by handing the power to decide what is art to the Art World's theories, and made celebrity itself an art form that Bowie then adopted - hiring Warhol's Pork cast to invent Ziggy and trading his person for a sequence of personas as performance art. By Danto's criterion of embodied meaning Bowie qualifies as an artist, yet the Art World still refuses him the title it grants Warhol, and the chapter closes by conceding that the Brillo Box changed the world while Bowie's works only showed how it had already been changed.
- ch. 13 "Part II Soundings of Bowie" *(front)*
- ch. 14 "5. Squawking Like a Pink Monkey Bird—What" *(no index entry yet)* — Littmann argues Bowie's surreal lyrics are a deliberate nonsense literature descended from Lear, Carroll, the Goons, Dylan, psychedelia, Beat poetry, and absurdist theatre, and that the obscurity does work: cryptic strokes paint larger worlds, voice fractured minds and alienation (Major Tom's 'little green wheels'), and leave interpretation to the listener, so each generation can read itself into songs like 'Moonage Daydream.' The hard question is evaluative - how can art whose effect depends on audience imagination be assessed at all? - and he answers that Bowie's forty-five years of popularity across shifting fashions is strong evidence the nonsense taps something of genuine, lasting artistic value.
- ch. 15 "6. Bowie the Buddhist" **·** The Buddha — Non-Self (anatman); The Four Noble Truths; Impermanence (anicca); Buddhism — Muchall reads 'Quicksand' and 'Where Are We Now?' as suffused with Tibetan Buddhism: 'Don't believe in yourself' voices the anatta teaching Bowie absorbed studying with Chime Rinpoche and from Chogyam Trungpa, whose 'spiritual materialism' warns that ego converts even spirituality and ideologies to its own use. After retelling the Buddha's life, the Four Noble Truths' three kinds of dukkha, and the five aggregates that reveal no permanent self, the chapter maps the song's 'ragged hole' and bardos onto the Tibetan Book of the Dead's after-death journey, and reads Bowie's morphing personas as lived acknowledgment that there is no fixed self - 'there's nothing in here.'
- ch. 16 "7. David Bowie, Political Philosopher" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Karl Marx, John Rawls — Totalitarianism; The Übermensch; Ressentiment — Hill reconstructs Bowie's political odyssey as a working-out of what freedom and oppression are: 'Cygnet Committee' repudiates revolution powered by resentment (echoing Nietzsche on socialism's despotic annihilation of the individual), 'Big Brother' and Diamond Dogs diagnose the crowd's flight from freedom into leadership, and the Nietzschean self-as-artwork - 'give style to one's character,' the 'mortal with potential of a superman' - curdles into the Thin White Duke's 'rock stars are fascists' collapse of celebrity into totalitarian leadership. The Berlin Trilogy then turns the critique inward: 'Heroes'' ironic lovers unmask romantic anti-totalitarianism (either side of the Antifaschistischer Schutzwall), 'Neuköln' discovers immigrants' mere survival, and Lodger's 'Fantastic Voyage' lands on structural, Marxist questions of how 'we' are to become free.
- ch. 17 "8. The Babe with the Power" **·** George Berkeley — Subjective Idealism (esse est percipi); What Is It Like to Be a Bat? — Michaud uses Bowie's Jareth and Labyrinth to make Berkeley's case that only minds and ideas exist: the taste of Sarah's tainted fruit, colors no culture without a word for 'blue' can see, dreams and optical illusions, and hallucinations all show the perceived world is a brain-made prediction, so the Goblin King's magic realm is 'more possible than we realize.' Because each of us builds a private world - Sarah and Jareth can never compare experiences ('I wonder what your basis for comparison is') - Bowie's death is literally the collapse of the world as he created it, 'as the world falls down,' though music may still reach across the void.
- ch. 18 "Part III The Bowie Identity" *(front)*
- ch. 19 "9. When Jumpin’ Jack Flash Met Ziggy Stardust" **·** Simone de Beauvoir, Søren Kierkegaard, Jean-Paul Sartre, Immanuel Kant — The Ethics of Ambiguity; Bad Faith; Authenticity; The Stages on Life's Way — Auxier reads the 1973 Jagger-Bowie intimacy and rivalry ethically: Keith Richards's portrait of Mick's envy frames Kierkegaard's Either/Or warning against the aesthetic life and the Kantian morality-duty wedding Bowie fled, against Beauvoir's Ethics of Ambiguity, which demands that one own one's freely chosen hollowing-out without appeal to any authority. Beauvoir's bad faith and authenticity then explain the outcome - Bowie beat Jagger by killing Ziggy, firing the Spiders, and finally making his very death a persona ('authentically Bowie'), while Jagger, forever only the Stones' singer, can respond only in bad faith, because 'hell is other people': each glimpsed the faker in the other.
- ch. 20 "10. David Bowie and Death" **·** Bertrand Russell — The Absurd — Potter and Cobb sort Bowie's death lyrics into seven interpretations - evil to be feared ('Space Oddity'/'Ashes to Ashes'), source of social unity ('Five Years,' 'Rock'n'Roll Suicide'), absurd defiance ('Heroes,' 'The Next Day'), revelation ('Quicksand,' 'We Are the Dead'), inevitable acceptance ('The Hearts Filthy Lesson'), transition between forms ('Lazarus'), and signifier of human singularity ('Blackstar' as the incommunicable uniqueness every death extinguishes) - all animated by Bertrand Russell's moral psychology of impulses, desires, and the 'principle of growth.' Ambivalence, they argue, is Bowie's honest response: no interpretation is privileged, and 'Blackstar' fuses celebrity's illusory immortality with the impulse to individuality, culminating in the artfully managed death itself.
- ch. 21 "11. Meeting the Monstrous Self" **·** Carl Jung, Friedrich Nietzsche, Sigmund Freud — The Shadow; Archetypes — Piven reads 'The Width of a Circle''s monster sitting by the tree through the doppelgänger tradition (Rank's The Double, Dostoevsky, Kirk's 'Enemy Within,' Luke finding his own face in Vader's mask): the monstrous double is the self, and social identity is the mask that hides it, so 'we have met Sauron and he is us.' Deploying Jungian archetype against mere stereotype, Nietzsche's dictum that truth kills, and the Freudian superego - the devil as its alter ego, perversely enjoying the condemnation it inflicts - the chapter argues Bowie's erotic devil scene shows how moralistic societies brand innocent desire monstrous, and that an agonizing encounter with the inner monster, not clichéd self-mythology, is the dire necessity of self-knowledge.
- ch. 22 "12. David Bowie’s Sincerity" **·** René Descartes — Authenticity; The Cogito; The Übermensch — Cooper distinguishes sincerity - communicating a self to an audience - from authenticity, and uses Critchley's reading of Bowie's visible seams ('maybe we're lying,' 'saying no but meaning yes') to argue that openly displayed artifice can carry real feeling, even as 'Conversation Piece' and the suffocating mask of 'The Mask' dramatize language's failure to transmit a self. Descartes's cogito secures self-certainty but not communication - Bowie stays 'a thinker, not a talker' - and against the Nietzschean 'Supermen' whose self-creation shades into self-alienation, Bowie moves past cold postmodern irony to a new sincerity that admits its own insincerity ('It's not the side-effects of the cocaine... I'm thinking that it must be love!').
- ch. 23 "Part IV Out of Bowie’s Mind" *(front)*
- ch. 24 "13. The Madness of the Musician" **·** G.W.F. Hegel, René Descartes — Simulacra and Simulation (the hyperreal); Geist (World Spirit); Gender Performativity — Lampert explains why Bowie is called the first postmodern pop star - he ironically shows that 'acting like' a rock star just is being one, refusing the genius myth by cobbling influences and confessing 'I lie an awful lot' - and grounds this in Sloterdijk's cynical reason and Diderot's Rameau's Nephew, whose honest liar Lui owns the mask-wearing vices everyone else hides. Hegel's Phenomenology supplies the title: the musician's universal deception is 'the madness of the musician,' the 'I' hollowed into pure ironic distance from all its masks (Descartes's separable self become image-manipulation), yet Hegel's hint that the cynic's vain talk can rise into 'a spiritual self of truly universal worth' points beyond Bowie's hyper-real self-referentiality to punk's DIY injunction to be your own hero - and to reading all sexuality, like Bowie's, as performance.
- ch. 25 "14. David Bowie’s Sadness" **·** Gilles Deleuze, Martin Heidegger, The Buddha, Sigmund Freud — Being-Toward-Death; The Death Drive (Thanatos); Non-Self (anatman); The They-Self (das Man) — Ketcham argues Bowie's sadness is ours: via Deleuze and Guattari's Anti-Oedipus he casts consumer capitalism as a machine producing schizophrenic fad-following (the stuck high-school footballer, the Benetton self), of which Bowie's serial personas are both symptom and escape - 'always crashing in the same car' yet never repeating. Minkowski's lived time and the Buddha's anatman ground the dance as pure becoming without a dancer, while Heidegger's das Man and being-toward-death explain why authentic self-creation - which, unlike Sellers or Williams, Bowie survived - exacts the price Freud's beyond-the-pleasure-principle names as compulsive repetition, turned by Bowie into a life wish.
- ch. 26 "15. Aladdin Sane or Cracked Actor" **·** Michel Foucault, René Descartes, Carl Jung, Aristotle — Methodological Doubt (evil demon); Archetypes; The Collective Unconscious — Riches and Watson weigh two readings of Bowie's 'cracked actor': either his cut-up method and persona-blurring (Ziggy taking over, the family schizophrenia that killed Terry) blurred imagination into hallucination, a Cartesian dream-vs-waking doubt filtered through Bowie's Jungian 'crack in the sky,' archetypes, and collective unconscious; or he deliberately challenged the sanity/madness distinction, as Foucault's Madness and Civilization and antipsychiatrist Thomas Szasz did. On the second reading 'All the Madmen,' the androgyny of 'Queen Bitch' and 'Jean Genie,' and 'Oh You Pretty Things'' Homo Superior rebel against Aristotle's identification of humanity with rationality, winning everyone the freedom to be their unconventional selves.
- ch. 27 "References" *(notes)*
- ch. 28 "Authors Who Fell to Earth" *(notes)*
- ch. 29 "Index" *(notes)*

- ch. 7 "viii" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "It’s About F $&ing Time" *(no index entry yet)* — Editor Nicolas Michaud's introduction argues Deadpool brings an 'obscene number of philosophical questions'—about mortality, morality, the multiverse, killing, and what separates hero, villain, and asshole—and that only waiting for a movie delayed this book. It proposes that philosophers should be more like Deadpool: honest enough to admit no one knows the answers, willing to look into the abyss, laugh, and jump in anyway, and ready to tell unwelcome truths even if it makes them villains. The tone-setting piece closes with bawdy fourth-wall-style gags about the book being 'better than sex' and unlicensed by Deadpool.
- ch. 9 "I. These Timelines Can Get So Confusing Why I Want to Rub Patrick Stewart’s Head" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "1. Breaking Sixteen Walls" **·** Socrates, Ludwig Wittgenstein, George Berkeley — The Unexamined Life; Language Games; Subjective Idealism (esse est percipi); Virtue Ethics — Rhiannon Grant argues Deadpool's fourth-wall breaks make him a great philosopher and sociologist because they enact the examined life: like Socrates's unexamined-life dictum, Deadpool steps outside his world to ask new questions about it ('Stewart or McAvoy?'), and even examines his own habit of examining—the 'sixteen walls' break inside a break. Wittgenstein's fly-bottle becomes the trap of circular philosophical argument that Deadpool escapes by seeing the bottle from outside, while Berkeley's solipsism is the failed attempt to live as if the world existed only in one's mind. A closing dialogue has Professor X coach Deadpool through insider/outsider research ethics and objectivity (the sperm-and-egg microscope example), showing how fourth-wall skill lets a researcher view a community from both inside and outside.
- ch. 11 "2. Deadpool the Dark Angel" **·** Maimonides — Faith and Reason — Christopher Ketcham playfully argues that Deadpool's fourth-wall escapes show he exists partly in dimensions we cannot experience, read through string theory's ten curled dimensions and Maimonides's ten realms of angels—beings of form without matter—in the Mishneh Torah. Since only God and angels traverse those hidden dimensions, Deadpool must be an angel, likely a dark one, whose shuttling between realms explains his vacillation between mercenary violence and saving souls. Ezekiel's chariot vision, the vengeful Hebrew Bible God, and massless dark matter are recruited as supporting evidence for this deadpan-earnest science-and-scripture argument.
- ch. 12 "3. The Only Chapter in This Book You Need to Read!" **·** Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Sigmund Freud, Jacques Lacan, Herbert Marcuse, Socrates — The Mirror Stage; Socratic Ignorance (knowing that you don't know); One-Dimensional Man — Writing as two bickering versions of himself from different universes, Karavitis reads Deadpool Kills Deadpool as showing that the multiverse of Deadpools is the delusion of a single narcissist: the alternate 'Pools' are mirror-image projections of himself (Lacan's mirror stage), his self-love is Rousseau's pathological amour propre rather than healthy amour de soi, and his claim to be the progenitor of all reality is solipsism—hence there is no fourth wall. Freud, Fromm, and Marcuse supply rival diagnoses of that narcissism, from arrested development to proto-fascist willfulness to authentic rebellion against social control through unconscious conformity. A closing twist argues philosophers who claim sole access to truth are the same solipsistic narcissists, which would make Socrates, who never claimed a final answer, the only true philosopher.
- ch. 13 "4. How Much Deadpool Is Too Much Deadpool" **·** Aristotle, Zeno of Citium, Jacques Derrida — The Golden Mean; The Dichotomy of Control; Deconstruction — The chapter argues Deadpool is entertaining because of, not despite, his excesses: his gratuitous violence and rapid-fire wisecracks breach the disciplined hero ideal, measured against Zeno of Citium's stoic dichotomy of control (accept the torture you cannot change, master your response) and Aristotle's golden mean between deficient and excessive force, which Wade flouts when he savors punishing Ajax instead of finishing quickly. Bakhtin's carnival excess explains the grotesque, orifice-obsessed humor that mocks 'normal' decency, and Derrida's 'internal drifting' (via Poe's Purloined Letter) frames Deadpool's first appearance being FedExed back to sender and his threshold position, where gags spill across comic, game, and film frames—making vigilante justice 'a supplement to the supplement' of law and exposing how each medium works.
- ch. 14 "II. I’m No Hero Seriously, Why Do We Like This Guy He Has No Redeeming Qualities Other than a Moderately Sized" *(front)*
- ch. 15 "5. The Anti-Hero’s Journey" *(no index entry yet)* — Andy Vink applies Joseph Campbell's hero's journey, in the Power of Myth version where heroism is a transformation of consciousness from selfishness to self-sacrifice, to the Duggan-Posehn arc 'The Good, the Bad, and the Ugly'. Deadpool starts self-centered—demanding pillowcases of money for killing zombie Presidents—is transformed by revelations (Dr. Butler holding his daughter Eleanor hostage, the North Korean mutate experiments echoing his own Weapon X trauma), and ends risking himself to free Butler's sister and the captive mutates and their families. The verdict: Deadpool genuinely becomes a hero by Campbell's test, which is also why, in Campbell's scheme, the hero never gets the girl.
- ch. 16 "6. The Accidental Hero" **·** The Buddha — Karma and Rebirth; Compassion (karuna) — Darian Shump judges Deadpool by Buddhist lights, applying karma as intentional action and skillful means—the permissibility of compassionate killing, after Rupert Gethin—to his Marvel NOW! career: protecting Agent Preston, saving a child and Nightcrawler Kim mid-battle, and the Niagara Bride assisted suicide whose fee he donates to a home for kids with cancer. A hypothetical Hydra spy with a bomb on a S.H.I.E.L.D. helicarrier shows how killing one murderer-to-be can be the compassionate act that saves everyone. He concludes Deadpool is an 'accidental hero'—a mercenary first whose good intentions make him a karmic hero—while Zenpool's failed pacifism in AXIS shows that renouncing violence betrays what makes Deadpool Deadpool.
- ch. 17 "7. Why Deadpool Isn’t a Hero (Yet )" **·** Søren Kierkegaard — The Stages on Life's Way; The Leap of Faith — Matthew Brake maps Deadpool onto Kierkegaard's three spheres (aesthetic, ethical, religious) and argues Wade is stuck in the boundary zone of humor between the ethical and the religious: his comedy sees the contradictions between everyday life and the heroic ideal but revokes suffering in jest, instead of embracing it as essential the way the religious hero does. Spider-Man is the contrast case—wisecracking as the incognito of inner suffering over Uncle Ben and Norman Osborn—while Deadpool's healing factor and knowledge of his own fictionality let him laugh off decapitation ('RESPAWN. LOL') and regress from every heroic step. The chapter ends with Wade's plan to earn heroism in Spider-Man/Deadpool #1 undercut by his taking a contract on Peter Parker.
- ch. 18 "8. How Shitty Can a Hero Be" **·** Alasdair MacIntyre, Immanuel Kant — Virtues, Practices and Traditions; Deontology — Corey Horn maps the Deadpool movie onto Joseph Campbell's monomyth—ordinary world, call to adventure (the cancer diagnosis), refusal of the recruiter's offer, acceptance, and the ordeal of hunting Ajax—then asks whether a mercenary chasing reward can be a hero at all. MacIntyre's chess example distinguishes instrumental from intrinsic rewards of a practice, and Kant splits motivation into duty versus self-interested motives; since Wade hunts Ajax to recover his face and Vanessa, both thinkers would deny his journey is moral. Still, MacIntyre's allowance for migrating from instrumental to intrinsic motivation leaves Deadpool a hero-in-progress, already promising Colossus he will consider joining the X-Men.
- ch. 19 "III. Wade, Is That You Is It Just Me Who’s Getting Tired of That F$ %ing Avocado Joke" *(front)*
- ch. 20 "9. Is Headless Deadpool Still Deadpool" **·** John Locke, Thomas Reid, Plutarch, Heraclitus — Personal Identity (memory theory); Ship of Theseus; Personal Identity — Ben Fulman runs Deadpool's decapitations, the T-Ray personality split, and Evil Deadpool—his discarded body parts regenerating together in a dumpster—through the theories of personal identity: Locke's memory continuity, Reid's branching-memory 'Deadpool paradox', Chisholm's essential parts, the Ship of Theseus traced from Heraclitus to Plutarch, and later positions by Sider, Wiggins, Noonan, Grice, and Perry on overlapping memory chains and their circularity. Finding each account strained by Deadpool's cases, the chapter proposes that Deadpool stores his memories in the readers via the fourth wall. His identity over time is thus jointly sustained by the audience—a 'non-personal identity' that ends only when every reader has forgotten him.
- ch. 21 "10. Always a Pallbearer, Never the Corpse" **·** Albert Camus — The Absurd; The Myth of Sisyphus; Rebellion and Revolt — Heidi Samuelson argues Deadpool is Camus's absurd hero improved: his origin and his survival of impossible dismemberments embody the absurd as 'disproportion between intention and reality', and facing Camus's one real philosophical problem—suicide—he tries to die via the Hulk to join mistress Death yet must live on, rejecting both actual and 'philosophical' suicide (dogma). His mercenary sense of purpose, jokes in the face of oblivion, and compassion for Blind Al, Weasel, deaf Hawkeye, and Evan exemplify Camus's revolt, freedom, and passion. Sisyphus is the model: like him, Deadpool owns his rock—the healing factor and the killing—and is happy, laughing where Sisyphus is silent.
- ch. 22 "11. Immortality Is Misery" **·** Bernard Williams, Friedrich Nietzsche, Jeremy Bentham — Immortality and the Soul; The Übermensch; Utilitarianism — In dialogue with Deadpool himself, John Altmann tests whether immortality is a blessing: Bentham's utilitarianism would prize a healing factor that lets Wade dive into a gamma meltdown to save the Southern Hemisphere for free, and Nietzsche would hail him as an Übermensch for refusing the Mithras Directive's Messiah and preserving humanity's freedom to create its own values rather than adopt herd morality. Bernard Williams gets the last word: since personalities become fixed—Wade stays a mercenary across every run, Zenpool notwithstanding—an endless life curdles into boredom and misery, so immortality is punishment rather than power unless one could radically change who one is.
- ch. 23 "12. Only Deadpool Is Sane" **·** Michel Foucault, Socrates — Power/Knowledge; Discipline and Docile Bodies; The Unexamined Life — Chad William Timm argues Deadpool is not really insane but was made 'insane' by power: via Foucault's genealogy of madness, experts like Dr. Brighton (a robotic shell for Psycho Man) at Ravenscroft and Dr. Whitby at Crossmore, along with the courts that institutionalize him after the Hulk trial, run a knowledge/power nexus that labels, sorts, and disciplines him until he internalizes the madman identity ('I think I might actually be crazy'). Nineteenth-century psychiatry's invented link between crime and madness secured the experts' authority, and disciplinary power plus technologies of the self made Deadpool discipline himself. Deadpool Kills the Marvel Universe's 'I can see through the bull##$%' and his fourth-wall awareness become resistance to these regimes of truth, and the chapter closes by turning Socrates's examined life on the reader: be careful whom you tell.
- ch. 24 "13. Weapon X Machina" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Gottfried Leibniz, Friedrich Nietzsche — Radical Freedom and Responsibility; Bad Faith; Phenomenology; Free Will vs Determinism — Cole Bowman argues Deadpool is the Marvel Universe's deus ex machina: phenomenologically alienated from a body he treats as a prop (cutting off his own hand to escape Colossus's manacle and flipping the silver behemoth the bird), he can step outside the causal chain the way Leibniz's Primary Truths says any genuinely free agent in a created order must. Sartre supplies the engine—radical freedom against determinism, with She-Hulk's shallow fourth-wall gags (killing the already-dead Porcupine) as bad faith by contrast and Deadpool Kills the Marvel Universe as the extreme exercise of freedom against his creators and fans. Nietzsche's Birth of Tragedy account of the deus ex machina replacing metaphysical consolation frames this new kind of inside-the-story god, who complicates plots rather than conveniently fixing them.
- ch. 25 "IV. Looks Aren’t Everything Why Ryan Reynolds Can’t Act" *(front)*
- ch. 26 "14. I’m an X-Man!" **·** G.W.F. Hegel, Aristotle — Struggle for Recognition; Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Man as Political Animal — Daniel Alloy argues Deadpool keeps insisting 'I'm an X-Man!' because being a hero is not a private choice but a social standing: with Aristotle's beast-or-god line about those who cannot live in society and Hegel's account of mutual recognition in the Phenomenology of Spirit, he contends Wade needs heroes he recognizes to recognize him as one in return. The Thunderbolts ('the selfish Avengers') and X-Force recruited him as a merc and so could not grant that recognition, and Cable offers approval from superiority rather than recognition between equals—a friendship of utility where Wade wants one of virtue. Only Captain America's Avengers card treats him as an equal, and even that recognition must be completed by the whole hero community before Wade can accept himself as the hero he already is.
- ch. 27 "15. Good Citizen Deadpool" **·** Aristotle — Man as Political Animal; Virtue Ethics (arete) — Using Aristotle's Politics, the chapter splits the good man from the good citizen and argues that the Marvel Universe's 'city' needs mercenaries to keep its balance between heroes and villains, as in Deadpool vs Thanos where the In-Betweener steps in when Death is imprisoned and the balance tips toward Chaos. Deadpool's contract killing - hitting Hydra and AIM targets heroes won't touch - fulfills his role in that city, so he can be a good citizen without being a good man, a standard of all-around virtue that Colossus, the 'boy scout' produced by CGI, better fits. The thought experiment of an assassin for 'Deadpooltopia' shows how a dreadful man can still be a good citizen by serving his city's own idea of the good.
- ch. 28 "16. The Beautiful Face Behind the Mask" **·** Emmanuel Levinas — The Face of the Other; Phenomenology — Reading Deadpool through Levinas's Totality and Infinity, the chapter treats his mask and nonstop jokes as the closed totality of an isolated 'I,' and his face-to-face encounters - Siryn touching his scarred head, Vanessa and Mercedes seeing and accepting his face, the dead-shark dialogue, the North Korean clones who share his DNA - as the epiphany of the other that grounds ethics. Levinas's account of murder as denying the other's face explains why Theresa (Siryn) stops Wade from executing Dr. Killebrew, the Weapon X torturer whose eyes showed he regarded Wade as meat. The chapter concludes that Deadpool's face is beautiful as the expression of his inner self, and that living for others rather than for his own (impossible) death is the ethical path he keeps glimpsing.
- ch. 29 "17. Wade Wilson Fights Like a Girl" **·** The Male Gaze; Feminism; Objectification — Staged as a Socratic dialogue in a professor's office, the chapter tests Peter Coogan's definition of the superhero (mission, powers, identity) against the Deadpool movie and finds Wade qualifies while mocking the genre. It then argues the film subverts the superhero genre's sexist conventions: Vanessa refuses the damsel role by grabbing the bar mercenary's crotch and later skewering Francis herself, Angel Dust knocks Colossus out, and Negasonic Teenage Warhead is 'a damsel to the rescue.' Applying Laura Mulvey's male gaze, it shows the camera lingering on Deadpool's body and adopting Vanessa's viewpoint in the Calendar Girl scene, with the Women in Refrigerators trope, Gwen Stacy's death, and the Hawkeye Initiative as evidence of what the movie overturns.
- ch. 30 "V. Really Was That Necessary Why We Would Like to Apologize to Mr. Reynolds Who Really Is Trying the Best He C" *(front)*
- ch. 31 "18. Thus Slaughtered Deadpool" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — The Übermensch; Master and Slave Morality; The Death of God — The chapter argues Deadpool is neither hero nor anti-hero but Nietzsche's Ubermensch beyond good and evil: perceiving the writers as the 'man at the typewriter' who keeps the Marvel cast as unwitting sheep under a slave morality, he kills the Marvel Universe in Deadpool Kills the Marvel Universe to free them from their creators. In Deadpool Killustrated he murders the literary archetypes (Moby-Dick, the Little Mermaid, Tom Sawyer) so the heroes they inspired can never be written, compared to the Overman demolishing inherited values before they corrupt the next generation, and in Deadpool Kills Deadpool he slaughters every version of himself to force the creator-god into the open. Since 'good' and 'bad' are categories of the herd whose very source is corrupt, the author declines to judge the slaughter at all.
- ch. 32 "19. Deadpool Can’t Know Fear" **·** Socrates — Virtue Ethics; Socratic Ignorance (knowing that you don't know) — Borrowing Socrates' definition of courage as remaining steadfast in the face of danger and choosing death above dishonor, the chapter argues that Deadpool's healing factor removes his risk of death, so he cannot experience the fear of helpless annihilation, cannot display courage, and is not an anti-hero but 'post-heroic.' Worse, mapping Luttwak's post-heroic warfare of drones and ballistic missiles onto his invulnerability, the chapter shows how fighting Deadpool drags opponents like the ULTIMATUM contract army and Crossbones into a trap where they can never test his courage and are stripped of their role as defenders, becoming mere targets. It closes that having a little fear and the chance to overcome it might be better than being unable to die.
- ch. 33 "20. Deadpool’s Genocide of the Marvel Universe" **·** Immanuel Kant — Humanity as an End in Itself; The Ethics of Political Violence — Taking Deadpool Kills the Marvel Universe seriously as a genocide story, the chapter reviews Lemkin's coinage and the 1948 Genocide Convention, then tests the mitigating factors - Deadpool's insanity, the X-Men's negligence in committing him to Psycho-Man's fake asylum, and Psycho-Man's manipulation - and finds none of them exculpatory. Killing Uatu the Watcher first and the Avengers next follows the genocide playbook of eliminating a community's intellectuals and pillars, and viewing his fellow characters as sheep completes the dehumanization that makes atrocity possible, while using everyone as means to his liberation violates Kant's demand never to treat persons as mere means. Invoking the Universal Declaration of Human Rights and Thucydides' Melian Dialogue as the classic case of victims left unable to tell their story, the author concludes Deadpool bears full responsibility.
- ch. 34 "VI. I’d Love to Get a Blow Job Why Existence Sucks" *(front)*
- ch. 35 "21. A Naughty Little Puppet, but Still a Puppet" **·** Aristotle, Augustine of Hippo — Free Will vs Determinism; Fate and Foreknowledge — Deadpool's Killogy realization that the 'progenitors' puppeteer him becomes a tour of fatalism's three classic puppet masters: Aristotle's sea-battle argument from On Interpretation that truths about the future are already fixed, Augustine's problem of an all-knowing God whose foreknowledge (like Cullen Bunn's scripts) seems to cancel free choice, and the causal closure of the laws of nature, reinforced by Benjamin Libet's brain-activity experiments. Because cutting the strings of logic, God, or physics is impossible - 'you can hack away forever and never find the beginning . . . or the end' - the chapter concludes that the audience is no freer than Wade. The surviving Deadpool copes by embracing the absurdity of a scripted existence.
- ch. 36 "22. How to Solve the Contradictions in Deadpool Killustrated" **·** David Lewis, David Hume — Possible Worlds — Because Deadpool Killustrated has him kill the real Captain Ahab, Ishmael both dies at Deadpool's hands and survives the Pequod's sinking in Moby-Dick, making the comic a depiction of something logically impossible in Hume's sense that contradictions cannot even be imagined. The chapter canvasses the escape routes: Matravers's claim that reading requires understanding rather than imagining, Gendler's aspect-imagining (Ahab as 'inspiration of Marvel heroes' rather than Melville's creation), Lewis's modal realism - defining the possible as what happens in some possible world, with the Killustrated Ahab a counterpart of Moby-Dick's and 'chunking' for contradictions - and finally Graham Priest's dialetheism and impossible worlds for stories like 'Wade's Impossible Day' with its square circle.
- ch. 37 "23. A Chimichanga for the Mercenary Soul" **·** Antonio Gramsci — Cultural Hegemony; Class Struggle — Through Gramsci's Prison Notebooks the chapter casts Deadpool as the 'Eternal Proletariat': the military superstructure (Weapon X) recruited him precisely because he was broke and class-positioned, and as a mercenary he has no capital, no upward mobility, and no exit from wage-killing. His hunger beyond money - the Bullseye payday that ends in him shooting himself, the dead shark's verdict 'what you are . . . is alone' - drives him toward the X-Men, read as a subaltern coalition of the marginalized like Gramsci's socialist parties. But as a non-mutant 'mutate' who cannot meet the hegemonic standard of heroism (the Hit-Monkey arc with Spider-Man, his bitter plea to Wolverine that 'at least they wanted you'), his only Gramscian options are coalition building or reshaping the X-Men's culture from inside.
- ch. 38 "24. Won’t the Real Wade Wilson Please Stand Up" **·** John Locke, Plutarch, Derek Parfit, Thomas Hobbes — Ship of Theseus; Personal Identity (memory theory); Teleporter Paradox (personal fission) — Evil Deadpool, assembled from Wade's discarded body parts with all his old memories, makes Wade a walking Ship of Theseus, and the chapter runs the puzzle through Plutarch's original, Hobbes's reconstructed-ship variant, and Locke's Prince-and-Cobbler memory criterion, staged as a Thor/Thing body swap. Memory fails because Evil Deadpool shares Wade's memories, so the candidates become Schechtman's narrative and person-life views, Parfit's branching survival - neither is the original, and identity is not what matters anyway - and finally Unger's core-capacity view: Wade remains the real Deadpool by continuing to be a conscious being, while his detached parts ceased to be Deadpool parts the moment they were replaced.
- ch. 39 "VII. Are You There, God It’s Me, Abandoned and All Alone in the Universe, You Stupid Mother- $ #($ %!" *(front)*
- ch. 40 "25. Deadpool Kills God" **·** The Problem of Evil; Fate and Foreknowledge; Euthyphro Dilemma — The chapter declares Deadpool genuinely evil - shooting the defeated for fun, killing for money, then wiping out an entire universe - yet shifts the blame to the writers, the 'progenitors' who author every evil he commits and will not even let him think of escaping, making them the gods of the Marvel universe. By parity of reasoning it argues that if God created a world knowing every evil in it (Hitler, factory farms, tsunamis, SIDS), then all that evil is part of the plan and appealing to free will is blowing smoke. The author's real question is why anyone should obey a being who permits such suffering, dismissing do-as-he-says morality as cowardice before the lightning bolt: 'Go get him, Deadpool. Kick his bearded ass.'
- ch. 41 "26. Why We Love to Watch Deadpool . . . Play with Himself" **·** Sigmund Freud — The Problem of Evil; The Unconscious — Using the Deadpool video game, the chapter argues Wade is a god who is all-powerful (rewriting the game world with his crayon-scrawled script and manufacturing fake credits), all-knowing (reading the player's mind through the fourth wall and literally entering Rogue's), and plainly not all-good (slapping an unconscious Wolverine, ogling dead Vertigo, ignoring Apocalypse three times for a billion dollars), so that dystheism - belief in a god with serious issues - dissolves the problem of evil. The free-will defense collapses because Deadpool wrote the script the villains obey, giving players only the illusion of choice. The voices in Wade's head are explicitly cast as Freud's structural model, with his Ego and SuperEgo talking while WadePrime, the Id, does most of the voice-work.
- ch. 42 "27. The Mystic with the Mouth" **·** Zhuangzi — Taoism (Daoism); The Tao (the Way) — In a lecture Deadpool keeps interrupting, the chapter reads the Merc's violence through Bataille's Theory of Religion: sacrifice as the search for lost intimacy, killing as the moment sacrificer and victim escape the profane world of mere things into sacred immanence, and sovereignty as the freedom glimpsed in destruction, with Rudolf Otto's mysterium tremendum et fascinans naming the awe Deadpool's massacres (the Circle Chase nunchuks, the Ultimate cartoon, the movie's highway slaughter) provoke. His 'dark Dao' of unhappy wandering is then set against the playful ways of the Zhuangzi - Cook Ding's ox-butchering, the Peng bird, the maimed teacher Wang Tai - as a Daoist path of inner-worldly transcendence that is amoral and gore-soaked. The payoff is the human tension of being both animal and angel: Deadpool is 'a savage god of mystical mayhem.'
- ch. 43 "28. Zen and the Art of Deadpool" **·** The Buddha — The Four Noble Truths; Zen Buddhism — The chapter argues Deadpool's unconventional style belies a Zen Buddhist disposition: created as a balance of Spider-Man's wit and Wolverine's lethality (against his disciplined template Deathstroke), he displays mushin, the no-mind calm of the trained martial artist under fire. His story is mapped onto the Four Noble Truths - dukkha in the suicidal depression over his incurable cancer scarring, samudaya in his attachment to belonging that he masks with jokes and loner behavior, nirodha in his ended relationships with Death and Siryn, and magga in shooting himself in the head at the end of Dark Reign as a perverse stilling of the mind. His fourth-wall awareness is read as piercing maya, the veil of illusion, an enlightenment no other Marvel character shares, though he remains 'not a good Buddhist' since he still wallows in ego and attachment.

- ch. 45 "Author bios" *(notes)*

## Dexter and Philosophy - Mind over Spatter

- ch. 1 "section-1" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Title Page" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Dexter and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Popular Culture and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Dedication" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Acknowledgements" *(notes)*
- ch. 7 "Know Thyself" **·** Socrates — The Unexamined Life; Socratic Ignorance (knowing that you don't know); Philosophical Skepticism — George Reisch's introduction frames the volume with Martha Stout's claim that one in twenty-five people is a conscience-free sociopath who hides in plain sight, arguing that we viewers do the hiding work for them just as Deb, LaGuerta and Batista's assumptions shield Dexter. It presents Dexter's incessant inner questioning as the show's philosophical hook and previews the book's competing verdicts on Harry's Code: monster, Kantian-style model actor, utilitarian good, aesthete, or superhero. It ends by inverting Socrates: normally we point 'know thyself' inward, but the real epistemic danger is the sociopath next door, so our skepticism should aim at the guy in forensics - unless we are ourselves among the one-in-twenty-five, in which case Socrates' injunction to self-examination was spot on.
- ch. 8 "BODY PART I - Maiming and Necessity" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "The Only Truly Decent Man Left on the Planet" **·** The Ethics of Political Violence — John Kenneth Muir argues Dexter is best read not as heir to Hannibal Lecter or Patrick Bateman but as a full-blown superhero: his 'impeccable instincts' are a crime-detecting power like Spider-Man's spider-sense or the Shadow's, his gloves, apron and glass visor are a costume, and his 'invisible mask of sympathy' inverts the superhero mask since the Dark Passenger is the true identity. Dexter's orphan origin - Harry lifting the boy from the blood-soaked container - replays the Superman/Batman/Parker template, with the Ice Truck Killer as an amalgam of Riddler and Mr. Freeze who embodies the superhero's shadow potential, and the chapter reads Harry's Code as the American-monomyth vigilante premise that the law can be upheld only by one stepping outside it. The 'killing joke' is that the emotionless sociopath is the only impartial, decent agent of justice in a Miami Metro consumed by passion, and the extra-legal violence question the chapter raises is whether such vigilante force, with its disregard for civil rights, is what justice requires.
- ch. 10 "The Good Life and The Dexter-like Life" **·** Robert Nozick, John Stuart Mill — The Greatest Happiness Principle — Abrol Fairweather uses Dexter as a controlled case study in the philosophy of emotion via Nozick's 'Spock Problem': like Spock, Dexter has beliefs, values and flawless practical reasoning but no emotional feelings, so Mill's Method of Difference lets us isolate what exactly emotions add. Distinguishing 'mere feelings' (beer on the boat, lust for Lila) from emotional feelings that run through our beliefs and values, he adopts Nozick's answer that emotions are value-perceptions - Dexter intellectually recognizes the value of Rita, Deb and his kids but cannot feel it, and that felt value is what his life lacks. Yet Dexter strikes back: he kills because he is too rational rather than too passionate, so whether the Dexter-like life beats the good life depends on whether emotions would help or hinder whatever matters most to us, a question the chapter briefly weighs in Millian terms of the balance of pleasure over pain his meta-killing produces.
- ch. 11 "You Can’t Help What Happened to You. But You Can Make the Best of It" **·** Free Will vs Determinism; Moral Luck; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation — Daniel Haas asks whether we can blame Dexter at all if his Dark Passenger is a compulsion formed by the shipping-container trauma he did not choose, and whether consistency then forces us to excuse the Trinity Killer and Ice Truck Killer too. Distinguishing attributive responsibility from accountability, he argues psychopaths cannot be motivated by moral reasons because they lack the moral emotions, and that it was sheer luck whether a traumatized boy landed in Harry's loving home and Code (Dexter) or a mental institution (Brian). Yet Dexter's critical reflection on and revision of Harry's Code, and his willingness to forgo turning himself in to spare Deb, Rita and the kids, paradoxically make him more accountable than ordinary psychopaths and even praiseworthy for his quest to become 'a little wooden boy' who is more human, even if he still deserves to be locked up.
- ch. 12 "Almost Caught!" **·** Harry Frankfurt — Second-Order Desires (personhood); Free Will vs Determinism; Compatibilism — Nicolas Michaud deploys Harry Frankfurt's attack on the principle that blame requires alternative possibilities: through a 'Dex kills Deb' case with a counterfactual intervener and a locked-steering-wheel driver, he shows one can be blameworthy despite having no choice, provided one acts because one wants to. Applied to Dexter, the question is which level of compulsion he has - and since he never truly restrains himself, never turns himself in, and his Season 2 relapse reads as empowerment rather than addiction, he seems to want to want to kill, so Frankfurt's verdict is guilt despite inevitability. The chapter closes with a fictional Princeton encounter where Dexter faces a dilemma - either his victims are blameless compulsives, making him a killer of innocents who violates Harry's Code, or wanting to kill makes them and him blameworthy - and Dexter's choice to kill Frankfurt himself proves he acts freely and is guilty.
- ch. 13 "More than a Feeling" **·** René Descartes, Plato — Methodological Doubt (evil demon); Reliabilism; Philosophical Skepticism — Rachel Robison-Greene asks whether Dexter's rule to 'be sure' actually gives him knowledge of his victims' guilt. Distinguishing Cartesian certainty - skeptical hypotheses about deceiving senses, dreams and evil demons, echoing The Matrix - from mere felt certainty, she tests the belief-truth-justification analysis of knowledge traced to Plato against the show: LaGuerta's witness theory fails on belief, Deb's conviction that Neil Perry is the Ice Truck Killer fails on truth, and Astor's accusation of Dexter fails on justification. Neither strong internalism (Dexter straps down Jamie Jaworski without knowing how the murder was done), weak internalism, coherence theory (his false but coherent case against Jonathan Farrow), nor reliabilism (his spidey-sense is explicitly not sufficient by his own admission) captures his practice, so his 'certainty' is just a feeling - and the people on his table may include ones he does not know to be guilty.
- ch. 14 "A Story in Search of a Narrator" **·** G.W.F. Hegel, Alasdair MacIntyre, Aristotle, Friedrich Nietzsche — Struggle for Recognition; Personal Identity; Authenticity — Brian Gregor reads Dexter through Charles Taylor's 'self-interpreting animal': Dexter's identity is not a fixed thing but comes into being through self-interpretation within an inescapable framework of value, so even his 'Am I good? Am I evil?' rebellion and the Nietzschean Übermensch reading fail to escape the deeper convictions about innocence, evil and desert that Harry's Code presupposes - as his guilt over the innocent Farrow shows. The chapter interprets Dexter's serial failures at being 'seen' - Harry's 'only one who really saw me', Brian's fraternal 'you can be yourself with me', Lila's probing, and Miguel Prado as Aristotle's friend, 'another self' - as Hegelian struggles for recognition that collapse because his real self can never come into the open. Finally, drawing on MacIntyre's narrative account of identity, it argues Dexter is literally a story in search of a narrator, whose selfhood persists through flashbacks, blood slides and noir-style voice-overs, and whose humanity shows in the need to have his account of himself heard and corrected by another.
- ch. 15 "I’ve Got No Strings . ." **·** Socrates, Immanuel Kant, Plato — The Categorical Imperative; Humanity as an End in Itself; The Ends Justify the Means — Richard Greene asks whether it was practically rational for Dexter to trust Lumen rather than kill her, to help her revenge-kill the Fowler ring, and to train her as a killer. The good outcomes do not help - Masuka's strip-club windfall shows luck is not rationality, and Socrates' Apology argument that corrupters risk harm from those they corrupt, together with Dexter's prior disaster in training Miguel, means the consequences were not foreseeably good. Kantian tests fail too - no one could will a universal law of killing, and killing violates the victim's autonomy and humanity as an end in itself - as do decision theory and instrumentality theories, so no account of practical rationality vindicates Dexter's choices, suggesting instead that the emotionless 'wooden boy' is beginning to become a real one.
- ch. 16 "BODY PART II - The Cut of Dexter’s Jib" *(front)*
- ch. 17 "All for Love" **·** Hannah Arendt, Blaise Pascal, Søren Kierkegaard, Martin Heidegger — The Banality of Evil; The Ethics of Political Violence; Buddhism — Jerry Piven argues Dexter's void is not genetic sociopathy but trauma: witnessing his mother's chainsaw murder and lying in her blood deadened his feelings, and each ritualized kill restages that catastrophe with the roles reversed, Dexter mastering death as the angel of it, reducing the murderer to a drop of blood he imprisons on a slide. Reading Harry's ghost as a Hamlet-father super-ego, he contends Dexter kills not only to master trauma but to please the father who channels his rage - murder 'for love' - and then generalizes: Arendt's banality of evil, Pascal on the void and faith, Kierkegaard and Heidegger on fleeing despair into conformity, Buddhist emptiness, and Becker-style terror-management findings explain how ordinary people, frightened of death and hungry for a leader's approval, can be manipulated into sanctioned slaughter, from Abu Ghraib to jihadism.
- ch. 18 "Rule Number One" *(no index entry yet)* — Aaron Anderson reads Season 4's cold open - the Trinity Killer's slow, intimate bathtub murder - against Dexter's 'clean' detached kills to introduce Kristeva's abjection: the corpse and spattered blood that threaten the boundary of self. He argues Harry's first rule ('Don't get caught') governs the show's aesthetics too - by hiding the abject results of Dexter's kills and softening them with dark humor and voice-over, the series spares viewers abjection so we can safely identify with a vicious serial killer, until Rita's murder finally forces both Dexter (for only the second time, after his mother's death) and the audience to experience it.
- ch. 19 "Dexter’s Aesthetics" *(no index entry yet)* — Patricia Brace opens with Dexter by Design's 'Jennifer's Leg' performance piece - gallery visitors compelled to watch a woman de-flesh her own leg - and asks why horror can please. Her answer runs through Burke's Enquiry: art inducing terror, astonishment and the excessive is sublime rather than beautiful, and Dexter's formula - a heroic serial killer we fear will be caught, walking the line between vigilantism and family man - pulls viewers deeper into exactly that sublime ambivalence.
- ch. 20 "Specimen Watching" **·** Theodor Adorno — Objectification; Speciesism; Feminism — Jason Davis applies Adorno's 'identity thinking' - the way concepts swallow particulars as specimens of the already-known - to argue Dexter is a consumer of serial killers who never asks what makes his victims what they are, only that they fit the type, from therapist Emmett Meridian on down. The same concept-machine works on animals: Buddy's buried bones and the show's predator imagery exploit the criminological animal-abuse link, letting Davis ask whether cruelty-taskforce laws quietly practice speciesism; and Carol J. Adams' sexual politics of meat connects the Ice Truck Killer's blood-drained, butcher-cut women to a meat-eating culture whose objectification makes women and animals interchangeable. Finally, the chapter notes that the women of Dexter - Deb, Rita, LaGuerta, Lila, Christine - are all written without female friendship, a homosocial specimen-class whose sameness feminist philosophers expose, so that when Dexter 'takes back the night', the 'invisible hookers' stay invisible.
- ch. 21 "BODY PART III - What Would Dexter Do" *(front)*
- ch. 22 "Vigilante Hero" **·** Immanuel Kant, John Stuart Mill, Jeremy Bentham — The Categorical Imperative; Utilitarianism; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Epright and Worth argue that rule-following - the Ten Commandments, the Golden Rule, Kant's categorical imperative (whose consistent Kantian never lies even to Nazis), or the Bentham-Mill principle of utility (whose consistent utilitarian must torture if stakes are high) - never made anyone moral, and Dexter's Code is the case in point: it gives the appearance of morality while licensing murder, and its source (Harry, 'God', the texts) cannot vindicate it. Drawing on Jonah Lehrer's neuroscience that moral judgment is emotional first with reason as post-hoc lawyer, they argue the emotionless Dexter is the madman who has lost everything except his reason, and Kohlberg's moral-development stages find him stuck at the pre-conventional level, below a fifth-grader - punishment-avoidance and you-scratch-my-back reasoning, never universal principles or respect for persons. Real Dexter-inspired killers (Mark Twitchell, Andrew Conley) clinch the verdict: he is neither vigilante hero nor moral agent, just a selfish addict whose rule-governed kills justify nothing.
- ch. 23 "You Control Your Urges—They Do Not Control You" **·** Immanuel Kant, Aristotle, John Stuart Mill — Deontology; Virtue Ethics (arete); Utilitarianism; The Categorical Imperative — Waller and McAlear first rebut the amoral reading: Dexter does feel (he sobs over Rita, loves Deb, misses Harry) and does control his urges through the Code, so morality applies to him, and the Dark Passenger is merely everyone's socially unacceptable desires - the very thing that gives rise to moral codes. They then test the three theories: Aristotelian virtue ethics mostly fails because murder admits no mean and Harry's Code teaches killer's skills rather than virtues (though its 'don't kill innocents' dispositions are genuine virtues); utilitarianism fails even in rule-utilitarian form - via the organ-harvesting doctor and Harry's own utilitarian guilt over Laura Moser's death - because Dexter kills because the Code requires it, not because it maximizes happiness. What fits is dark deontology: like Kant's argument that murderers consent to their own execution out of respect for their rationality, Dexter kills only killers, acts from duty rather than enjoyment, and treats 'don't get caught' as a condition for the possibility of his true imperative.
- ch. 24 "Where the Bodies Lie" **·** Bernard Williams, Thomas Nagel, David Lewis — Moral Luck — Daniel P. Malloy uses Dexter to lay out the three kinds of moral luck introduced by Bernard Williams and Thomas Nagel: constitutive luck (witnessing his mother's murder and being adopted by Harry, who taught him the Code instead of getting treatment, made Dexter a controlled killer while his brother Brian became 'an unchecked version' of the same child), situational luck (the choice between Brian and Debra in 'Born Free,' the forced kidnapping of Doakes), and resultant luck (Lila burning the shack with Doakes inside, and Dexter's mistaken kill of the innocent photographer Jonathan Farrow in 'Slack Tide,' which he meets with remorse and agent-regret). Against the thought that luck erases responsibility, Malloy argues that intentions remain fully ours, borrowing David Lewis's claim that attempted and successful murderers deserve identical punishment - so Dexter is answerable for every kill.
- ch. 25 "De-Deontologizing Dexter" **·** Immanuel Kant — Deontology; Reason Is the Slave of the Passions; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Sultan Ahmed argues Dexter is not really a Kantian deontologist: the urge to kill came first (the teenage animal killings, and his brother Rudy/Brian, who developed the same dark desire without Harry and rationalized it differently), and Harry's Code is a post-hoc rationalization built on top of that desire. Drawing on psychological research (Joshua Greene, Jonathan Haidt) that deontological judgment tends to be emotional rationalization rather than moral reasoning, he reads Dexter's killings as serving emotional needs - the need to control the helplessness of the shipping-container trauma and the Ernest Becker-style need to belong, seen in his bonding with Miguel, Rita, and the kids. Even Dexter's refusals to kill Ellen Wolf or the bound Tony Tucci are framed as feelings and standards, not a sense of moral duty.
- ch. 26 "BODY PART IV - Bad Blood and Bad Behavior" *(front)*
- ch. 27 "The Final Thrust to the Abdomen" **·** Michel Foucault, Thomas Hobbes, John Locke — Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation; Discipline and Docile Bodies — Eric Holmes argues that what we enjoy in Dexter is not killing but punishing, and reads the show through Michel Foucault's Discipline and Punish (with Hobbes and Locke on the right to punish): since the eighteenth century punishment has moved from public torture to the abstract, painless deprivation of liberty, and Dexter is a deliberate throwback to the older Sovereign. Every detail of the ritual is analyzed as torture designed to make the victim recognize he deserves it - displaying photos or the corpses of their victims (Mike Donovan), forced nudity and harsh light, and letting the punishment fit the crime (Trinity's own claw hammer, telling Miguel he killed Oscar). Dexter is 'the Sovereign we've been waiting for,' an unemotional agent of deserved pain that modern incarceration no longer provides.
- ch. 28 "A Second Look at Dexter’s Motives" **·** Adam Smith — The Invisible Hand; Utilitarianism; Free Will vs Determinism — David Ramsay Steele first calculates that Dexter is a public benefactor - each killed predator saves several net lives a year - and then explains the deeper appeal through Bernard Mandeville's Fable of the Bees and Adam Smith's invisible hand: like good law, Harry's Code channels an amoral appetite (the thrill of killing) into socially beneficial results without reforming the appetite itself, refuting Christianity's fixation on pure motives. Dexter thus does totally the right thing for totally the wrong reason. Finally Steele doubts Dexter's own story: the spontaneously unlocked door of the Castillos' shed shows real empathy, and it was Harry's deliberate instruction (Nurse Mary), not fate, that made him a killer - 'addiction is always a choice.'
- ch. 29 "Morgan or Moser" **·** Michel Foucault — Power/Knowledge — Everitt Foster reads Dexter's first season through Foucault's History of Sexuality: the relationship with Rita, the comic attempt to 'reciprocate,' and the therapy sessions with Dr. Emmett Meridian all show the 'deployment of alliance' (kinship, family, blood) intersecting with the 'deployment of sexuality,' while the observer-observed 'spiral of power and pleasure' explains Dexter's watching as a power play - letting Rita have carnal knowledge of him would hand her control over his mask. Knowledge of his past (Joe Driscoll, Rudy's true identity) destabilizes the Morgan alliance, and the finale is Dexter's deliberate choice: he kills Brian and saves Debra ('You hurt her, you hurt me'), an act Foster identifies as Foucault's 'right of death' that affirms the identity Dexter Morgan over Dexter Moser.
- ch. 30 "Death, Sex, and Dexter" *(no index entry yet)* — Ewan Kirkland argues Dexter is a very white character - emotionally repressed, fussy about detail, scientific, a serial killer of the Hannibal Lecter type - and uses Richard Dyer's whiteness studies to show how the show makes this invisible ethnicity visible by surrounding Dexter with racially and sexually coded colleagues (LaGuerta, Batista, Doakes, Masuka) and hyper-white imagery: the plastic kill room, the white mannequins, the cut to white on death. Whiteness appears, with Dyer, as self-control and 'mind over body' (set against the hot-blooded Miguel), as ghostly invisibility and privileged 'enterprise,' and as an uneasy link with death and awkward, dysfunctional sexuality (Rita's redemptive blonde femininity versus the corpse-pale Lila). The point is to demystify the myth of whiteness, not to celebrate it.
