- ch. 31 "Can a Serial Killer Be Happy" *(no index entry yet)* — Mike Piero answers whether a serial killer can be happy by mapping Dexter's growth onto Abraham Maslow's hierarchy of needs: the Code secures his physiological and safety needs (don't get caught), Season 2's 'the Code is mine now' and the Narcotics Anonymous meetings open his love needs, the respect of Miami Metro and the Miguel mentorship meet esteem needs, and wanting to live to see Harrison born in 'Do You Take Dexter Morgan?' is self-actualization - 'what a man can be, he must be.' Rita's death at the start of Season 5 threatens a retreat into isolation, but fatherhood keeps supplying moments of Maslow's 'degrees of relative satisfaction.' The upshot is that Dexter, though damaged, has recognizably normal human motivations and a relatively happy, complicated life.
- ch. 32 "BODY PART V - Dexter’s Bloodline" *(front)*
- ch. 33 "Deeply Dissecting Dexter" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — Perspectivism; Will to Power — Deborah Mellamphy uses Nietzsche's masks - 'every profound spirit needs a mask,' the will to power that requires deception for self-preservation - to explain both why Dexter's crafted normalcy works and why only the characters conscious of their own masks (Doakes, Lila, Brian, Trinity) see through his. Nietzsche's perspectivism and 'Truth and Lie in the Extra-Moral Sense' then dissolve any true self beneath the mask: if truths are 'worn out metaphors,' Dexter's question 'If you play a role long enough... does it ever become real?' has no stable answer. Following Sarah Kofman's reading, the chapter asks whether we are all 'but this veil itself' and concludes that Dexter goes a step beyond Nietzsche by locating the truth of identity in the many layered masks themselves.
- ch. 34 "Becoming a Real Boy" **·** Aristotle, Immanuel Kant, Plato — Virtue Ethics (arete); Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); Authenticity — Phillip Deen places Dexter on the scale of character types from Book VII of Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics - temperate, morally strong (Angel Batista), morally weak (Deb), self-indulgent (Masuka), and the beast - rejecting the Socratic view that vice is mere ignorance, and argues Dexter is neither self-indulgent nor simply beastly but a 'morally strong beast.' The decisive test is Kant's tribunal of conscience and the morality-versus-prudence question: Dexter gathers proof before killing, feels remorse for Jonathan Farrow, and tells Trinity 'No, but I want to be' morally superior - yet his delight at Doakes's death exposes mere prudence. Deen closes with Brian's Heideggerian offer of authenticity ('you could be yourself around me') refused: Dexter hopes that by pretending the feelings are there he may one day 'turn into a real boy.'
- ch. 35 "The Ethics of Dexter’s Appeal" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Aristotle — The Death of God — James F. Pontuso reconstructs Nietzsche's Genealogy of Morals - promise-keeping, the unpayable debt to ancestor-founders that becomes God, cruelty turned inward as bad conscience, and Christianity's divine self-ransom that only deepens guilt - to explain why Dexter, who feels no guilt, is not abnormal but a man before morality. Following Gibbon's picture of Roman soldiers whose civil religion justified slaughter without remorse, he imagines Dexter thriving in the legions ('Dexter SPQR') rather than hiding behind Harry's Code, and argues the show appeals because Aristotle's ethics concerned outward conduct and justice rather than inner conscience. In our levelled age we secretly crave what Nietzsche craved: the sight of 'something perfect... still capable of inspiring fear.'
- ch. 36 "Bad or Bad Faith" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre — The Look (hell is other people); Bad Faith; Existence Precedes Essence; Radical Freedom and Responsibility — Rebecca Steiner Goldner reads the show through Sartre's Being and Nothingness: Dexter's watching is the Look that crystallizes his victims into a fixed essence - Matt Chambers can never again reinvent himself in a new town - and the kill room stages the demanded shame and confession ('Look at what you did!'), echoing Sartre's caught peeping-Tom. She then argues Dexter's belief that he was born a killer, that his Dark Passenger leaves him no choice, is itself bad faith: existence precedes essence, and by Sartre's account even the reformed thief remains responsible for his past, as with Jeremy Downs and Dr. Meridian. We viewers root for Dexter because we see the freedom he denies - and love him because he never makes the authentic choice to abandon his murderous project.
- ch. 37 "Dexter, Hero of Our Time" **·** Free Will vs Determinism — David Ramsay Steele compares Dexter with the rational vigilante heroes of earlier popular fiction - Edgar Wallace's Four Just Men, E.W. Hornung's Raffles, Bulldog Drummond, the Saint - who killed bad guys for justice and were read without moral panic, and asks why the Parents Television Council now singles Dexter out. His answer: Dexter is ideologically 'safe' because the modern 'ideology of childhood trauma' works like the folk-tale love potion, presenting the hero as a puppet of irresistible compulsion rather than a responsible agent, so no one can accuse the show of endorsing calculated vigilantism. The chapter is thus a free-will-and-excuse argument: determinism-as-excuse has become the price of admission for unofficial justice on screen.
- ch. 38 "The Perps" *(notes)*
- ch. 39 "Index" *(notes)*
- ch. 40 "Dexter and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 41 "Copyright Page" *(front)*

## Discworld and Philosophy - Reality Is Not What It Seems

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "i" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "ii" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Copyright" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Dedication" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Eureka!" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "I. Rewriting Your Chem" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "1. More Golems around Than You Might Think" **·** Jean-François Lyotard — The End of Grand Narratives; Power/Knowledge — Using Feet of Clay and Witches Abroad, Froehlich argues we are all like golems: our behavior is driven by the 'words in our heads,' the texts that society and the powerful put there. Lyotard's metanarratives explain how Lily Weatherwax forces Genua to live out fairy-tale plots and how Dragon King of Arms rewrites Nobby's family tree to manufacture a king, while dwarfish gender norms keep Cheery Littlebottom trapped in a culturally imposed text. Freedom comes only when, like Dorfl after Carrot buys him his own receipt, a person rejects the imposed words and chooses words of the heart - 'words in the heart cannot be taken.'
- ch. 11 "2. Golem Morality in the Modern World" **·** Peter Singer, Immanuel Kant — The Drowning Child (obligation to aid); Autonomy of the Will; Humanity as an End in Itself — Skene weighs Peter Singer's 'Famine, Affluence, and Morality' - opened with a River Ankh drowning-child variant - against the golem morality of Going Postal and Feet of Clay. Mr. Pump's charge that Moist von Lipwig's frauds 'hastened the deaths' of 2.338 people grounds a golem standard of actuarial murder that Skene applies to the 2008 banking crisis and Mao's Great Leap Forward. With Kant, the chapter holds that free choice is the foundation of moral responsibility and respect for others' wills, but unlike Singer's unlimited demands, golem morality asks us to own the foreseeable consequences of our choices without sacrificing everything.
- ch. 12 "3. We Willna Be Fooled Again! Wee Free Anarchists!" **·** Robert Nozick — Anarchism; The Minimal State — Reading The Wee Free Men, Kugler treats the Nac Mac Feegle, Granny Aching, and Tiffany as embodiments of the anarchist suspicion of political authority, staging Nozick's minimal state and spontaneous protective associations against Huemer's philosophical anarchism from The Problem of Political Authority. The Feegles' creed 'Nae quin! Nae king!', their flight from the exploitative Fairy Queen, the Baron's sheep-killing dog compromise, and the mercy shown the lonely kidnapper Miss Robinson all show law treated as a flexible guideline that must yield to love, community, and care for the weak. Kugler concludes that Pratchett romanticizes anarchism: his anarchists win hearts, watching authority from within the system rather than overthrowing it.
- ch. 13 "4. The Liberating Power of Nanny Ogg’s Bouncing Bosom" **·** Cultural Hegemony — Froehlich reads Witches Abroad through Mikhail Bakhtin's theory of carnival and the grotesque from Rabelais and His World: Lily Weatherwax's palace ball is 'high culture' as tyrannical hierarchy, while the Genua street carnival, Nanny Ogg's bouncing bosom, and Baron Saturday's untamable swamp bring grotesque low-culture energy that crashes the ball and shatters Lily's frozen fairy-tale order. Against Bakhtin's pessimism that carnival merely lets the oppressed blow off steam, the chapter argues that Granny Weatherwax and Nanny break the cycle, giving Genua a real chance at self-determined, more fun lives.
- ch. 14 "5. The Truth Shall Make Ye Fret" **·** Catharine MacKinnon, John Stuart Mill — The Harm Principle; On Liberty; Dominance Approach (pornography and gender) — Watson uses The Truth - William de Worde's accidental invention of Ankh-Morpork's first newspaper and the plot to frame Lord Vetinari - to stage the three classic arguments for censorship: direct harm (character assassination 'works every day'), indirect harm (with MacKinnon's point that the speech of the dominant drowns out the subordinated, applied to speciesist insults at Koom Valley), and the false-beliefs argument. Against them stand the knowledge problem and the equality assumption: Mrs. Arcanum's deference to print, Vetinari's turtle-shaped-world joke, and the Montessori-Broca dispute show we never know in advance which beliefs are false, and any censor like Lord de Worde has his own agenda. Vetinari's verdict that 'free men pull in all kinds of directions' because 'pulling together is the aim of despotism' carries the chapter's Millian case that free speech is a horrible necessity.
- ch. 15 "II. Truth, Logic, and Law Are Nice, but Someone Still Has to Do the Wash" *(front)*
- ch. 16 "6. The Absurdity of the Luggage" **·** Albert Camus — The Absurd; The Myth of Sisyphus — Kempner reads the Luggage - the sapient, homicidal pearwood chest that pursues Rincewind from The Color of Magic through The Light Fantastic and Interesting Times - as the embodiment of Camus's absurd: the 'divorce' between our demand that the world make sense and a world that does not care. Rincewind, who dreams of a rational, scientifically ordered universe while the Disc sails on a turtle and the gods smash atheists' windows, is cast as Sisyphus, and the trolls who 'suffer from philosophy' and freeze solid show where the futile quest for order ends. When Rincewind finally says 'Come on' to the huddled Luggage and readers come to love what once terrified them, the chapter performs Camus's acceptance of the absurd as freedom.
- ch. 17 "7. Pratchett’s Republic" **·** Plato — The Philosopher King; Justice in the Republic; Theory of Forms — Kempner maps Plato's Republic onto Ankh-Morpork: Vetinari is the Philosopher King, the Watch of Guards! Guards! are the Guardians who devote themselves to the good of the whole community, and Platonic justice as keeping everything in its right place is Vetinari's guild-licensing, topiary-tending rule - including the jail cell whose locks are all on the inside. Against Plato's banishment of poets, The Truth shows words serving the just city: William de Worde's pursuit of 'the big truth' exposes the frame-up of Vetinari and extends the circle of Guardians to the free press. The chapter concludes that Pratchett keeps Plato's ideal only by bending it to observable reality - Vetinari rules for the city rather than remaking its people.
- ch. 18 "8. Honesty Trumps Cleverness Sam Vimes and Commonsense Philosophy" **·** Thomas Reid, G.E. Moore, David Hume, Socrates — Common Sense Philosophy; Philosophical Skepticism; Utopia and Dystopia — Skene argues Sam Vimes embodies Thomas Reid's common sense philosophy across Feet of Clay, Thud!, Night Watch, and Jingo: he refuses speculation (his hatred of Holmes-style clue-deduction and the Summoning Dark), is irreverent toward false authorities from Sergeant Colon's 'School of My Dad Always Said' to Lord Rust's glorious wars, and checks every theory against reality. Moore's here-is-one-hand reply to Humean skepticism and Socrates' unmasking of pompous authorities illustrate the method, and Huemer's political realism - taxation as demanded money with menaces, social systems judged by how they work for real people - echoes Vimes's verdicts on Reg Shoe's utopian barricade and Swing's phrenology. The lesson is Vimes's own: 'Common sense and facts! That's what works.'
- ch. 19 "9. Why Would You Put a Con Artist in Charge of the Money" **·** John Searle, Immanuel Kant, Ludwig Wittgenstein — Fallis asks why Vetinari puts the con artist Moist von Lipwig in charge of the Post Office, Royal Bank, and Mint in Going Postal and Making Money. Analyzing what counterfeits are - Goodman's 'false history of production' definition, its failures, and bald-faced cases like Mr. Bent's deliberately transparent 'family heirlooms' lie - explains why it takes a thief to catch a thief, while the John Law affair (with Kant's observation that Law escaped France without ever lying) supplies the cautionary precedent. The deepest answer is Searle's: money is an institutional fact, a collective agreement that X counts as Y, so only a con artist skilled at 'selling dreams' can talk Ankh-Morpork's merchants into accepting paper.
- ch. 20 "10. Lord Vetinari’s Friendly Guide to Tyranny" **·** Thomas Hobbes, Jeremy Bentham — The Social Contract; Utilitarianism; Anarchism — Alloy stages the debate over Vetinari's legitimacy as letters to the Ankh-Morpork Times: a writer channeling Hobbes defends the contracted sovereign - a 'mortal god' who cannot commit crimes and secures freedom through security - while 'the late Jeremy Bentham' judges the Patrician by the greatest good of the greatest number yet would disenfranchise golems and the 'differently-alive' dead. The anarchist Wolff Paul Roberts rebuts both, denying any right to rule, rejecting tacit consent by residence, and holding up the free golems as models of uncoerced virtue, before a final letter defends Vetinari's limited, functional government. The frame - Vetinari quietly orchestrating the whole correspondence and planning Roberts's next letter - shows tyranny curating its own philosophical defense.
- ch. 21 "III. Some Things are Necessary, Not Personal" *(front)*
- ch. 22 "11. Can a Leopard Change Its Shorts" **·** Aristotle — The Golden Mean; Virtue Ethics (arete); Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Alloy asks whether Rincewind - the wizard who runs from Twoflower, the Dungeon Dimensions, and the Agatean Empire alike - is really a coward. Aristotle's doctrine of the mean frames cowardice as the deficiency of fear-regulation between foolhardy Cohen the Barbarian and true courage, with character traits describing, explaining, and predicting Rincewind's flight. But the situationist challenge - Harman's reading of the cookie, hurry, and obedience experiments - denies that character traits exist, and the replies available to the virtue ethicist (Miller's continence/incontinence distinction, Alfano's 'dodge') leave almost nobody with virtues or vices, so the chapter concludes Rincewind cannot be a coward: cowardice, like the leopard's shorts, was never there to begin with.
- ch. 23 "12. Becoming Vetinari Personal Identity on the Discworld" **·** John Locke, Gottfried Leibniz, Thomas Reid, David Hume — Personal Identity (memory theory); Personal Identity; Teleporter Paradox (personal fission); Bundle Theory of Self — Pierce uses Moist von Lipwig's rehabilitation (Albert Spangler becomes Postmaster General), Cosmo Lavish's doomed project of literally becoming Vetinari, the Auditor Myria LeJean, Angua's transformations, the Librarian's orangutan body, Miss Level's two bodies, and duplicated Rincewind teleportation cases to test theories of personal identity. Locke's memory theory, patched by Leibniz into psychological continuity, collapses into circularity under false-memory cases like a wizard implanting Teatime's murders in Rincewind's mind, while bodily and brain-continuity views founder on Igor part-swaps, splitting hemispheres, and identical-twin-style fission. Leibniz's own soul view and Reid's treatment of identity as a primitive, unanalyzable fact get their due, and Hume's skepticism about any continuing self marks the chapter's conclusion that every available view exacts a price.
- ch. 24 "13. How to Be a Dwarf" **·** Simone de Beauvoir — Gender Performativity; One Is Not Born a Woman; Feminism; Cultural Hegemony — Using Carrot (a human raised in a dwarf mine who is accepted as a dwarf), Cheery Littlebottom (an openly female dwarf), and Gladys the golem, the chapter argues that social identity is neither biology nor a one-time choice but an ongoing performance that must also be recognized by insiders and outsiders. It applies de Beauvoir's 'one is not born, but rather becomes, a woman' to Cheery's creation of a female dwarf gender against the deep-downers' equality that treats every dwarf as male, and reads the black ribboners versus the Magpyrs as assimilation versus doubling down on identity. Essentialist outsiders like Fred Colon and the Campaign for Equal Heights fail because they cling to stereotypes when individuals like Cheery or Carrot do not fit the mold, while Vetinari's indifference and Mrs. Cake's accommodations leave identity to those who must choose and perform it.
- ch. 25 "14. Being One’s Me (Witchy) Personal Identity on the Discworld" **·** John Locke — Personal Identity (memory theory); Ship of Theseus; Teleporter Paradox (personal fission); Personal Identity — The chapter reads Granny Weatherwax's analysis of Magrat—'you're your me'—through Locke's memory theory of personal identity, opening with Granny's broomstick, whose stick and bristles the dwarf engineers have replaced dozens of times yet which remains 'the same' broomstick, as a Theseus's-ship puzzle for witches. It stages Locke's view against the classic objections: Butler's circularity charge that memory presupposes identity, and fission worries raised by the duplicated Glods of Al-Ybi and by the mirror magic that splits Granny and Lilith among a billion reflections. Death's challenge to each witch to find 'the one that's real' in the mirror universe becomes the test case, and Granny's simply looking down at herself and saying 'This one' is offered as a vindication of a Butlerian, body-anchored and indefinable conception of personal identity.
- ch. 26 "IV. Ye Canna’ Ken What Ye Canna’ Ken" *(front)*
- ch. 27 "15. The Serious Monk’s Guide to Re-writing History" **·** The Noble Lie; Power/Knowledge; The Metaphysics of Time Travel — Framed as a monkey monk-to-be telling four bored children the 'history' of four Discworld novels, the chapter argues that history is not a record of what happened but a constructed narrative: in Wyrd Sisters the Duchess commissions Hwel's play to rewrite the murder of the king, since 'the past is what people remember, and memories are words.' In Small Gods, Brutha's photographic memory of Ephebe's burning library shows that even perfect recollection is lost unless written down, while in Thief of Time the History Monks openly rebuild time as a patchwork after the Big Crash, and Susan watches historians 'rebuilt the image of the past to suit the eyesight of today.' Drawing on W. H. Walsh's idea of history as intelligible narrative, it concludes that history is performative—what people are told it is—and that we ourselves are the crowbar that opens the box of history from the inside.
- ch. 28 "16. Getting at the Truth" **·** Edmund Gettier, Plato, Alvin Goldman, Robert Nozick — The Gettier Problem; Philosophical Skepticism — The chapter sets out the Platonic justified-true-belief analysis of knowledge with Discworld examples—Archchancellor Ridcully as a fallible authority, the physics of thaum particles as coherentist justification—before constructing a Discworld Gettier case in which a young wizard sees a fake level-seven wizard killed and truly but accidentally reports that 'a level seven wizard was killed.' It then tests the standard repairs, applying Nozick's truth-tracking function and Goldman's causation function to a case where a man blames the notorious Reacher Gilt for a theft actually committed by the dwarf he bumped into, and argues that neither truth-tracking nor specificity makes the problem go away. The upshot is that Discworlders, used to expecting to be wrong, may let go of false 'knowledge' more easily than our confidently wrong Roundworld.
- ch. 29 "17. Sin, My Young Man, Is When You Treat People as Things" **·** Thomas Aquinas — Objectification; Natural Law; Animal Rights (subjects-of-a-life) — Taking Granny Weatherwax's definition that sin 'is when you treat people as things' and Vetinari's that sin is not saying no to evil, the chapter reads Discworld through C. S. Lewis's argument that a universal Law of Human Nature—felt on the Disc by dwarfs, trolls, and even atheists—points to a divine lawgiver. The enchanted wolf in Witches Abroad, whose agony every witch intuits as wrong and who is granted a merciful death and human funeral, shows that shared moral intuition at work, and grounds the chapter's notes on Pratchett's own campaign for assisted dying. Finally, the Assassins' Guild, which only inhumes the rich who can afford bodyguards under its motto nil mortifi sine lucre, illustrates John Paul II's 'structures of sin': killing sanctified by contract and city law, treating people as things.
- ch. 30 "V. Probably Quantum" *(front)*
- ch. 31 "18. Fate on the Discworld and Roundworld" **·** Epicurus, Cicero, Augustine of Hippo, Boethius — Fate and Foreknowledge; Compatibilism; The Metaphysics of Time Travel; Atomism — Written as a dialogue in which Death visits the sweeper Lu-Tze, the chapter distinguishes Discworld fate—narrativium and narrative causality, where million-to-one chances come off and Mort's attempt to save a princess bounced back into an alternative timeline—from the Roundworld debates about determinism. Epicurus's swerving atom, the Stoic compatibilism of Chrysippus (choices flowing from one's own beliefs and desires, which Death admits he prefers), Carneades's 'voluntary motion of the mind' and his truth-without-necessity solution to future contingents, and Augustine and Boethius on desire-based choice and God's atemporal foreknowledge are all worked through. The Disc's changeable past—Dios traveling back to found Djelibeybi, Rincewind's sandwich seeding life, Vimes in his own past—is what motivates Carneades's distinction between truth and necessity, and the Stoic line that fate can work through the witches' own free choices.
- ch. 32 "19. The Alchemy of Flat Worlds" **·** Perspectivism; Feminism; What Is It Like to Be a Bat? — Staged as Professor Moby Dickens Melville's 'Philosophy of Worlds' course uniting Discworld wizards with the geometric Flatlanders of Abbott's novella, the chapter contrasts a storytelling, narrative-causality philosophy of Discworld with Flatland's mathematical, hierarchy-obsessed rationalism, and extends the contrast to physics (wizards favor loop quantum gravity, Flatlanders string theory) and theology (Hex's self-designed universe and words of power versus Flatland's circle-priests who rule women declared 'deficient in Reason'). When a Flatlander woman, Ms. Transversal, protests that calling the new softer approach 'feminine' repeats Abbott's sexism, the class closes with the narrator's suggestion, from a chalk-nub's-eye view, that everything we know may reflect the direction from which we see rather than the truth itself.
- ch. 33 "20. The Intersection of Science and Philosophy, the Seediest Corner in Ankh-Morpork" **·** René Descartes, Aristotle, Ludwig Wittgenstein, Gilbert Ryle, Diogenes the Cynic, Pyrrho — Mind–Body Dualism; The Ghost in the Machine; Paradigm Shifts — A comic sprint through Western thought anchored in the Science of Discworld books, the chapter runs from Aristotle—whose geocentric four-element physics, it notes, fits Discworld better than Roundworld, and whose condemnations of usury it digresses over—through Descartes's cogito and mind-body dualism, Ryle's 'ghost in the machine,' behaviorism, identity theory, eliminative materialism, and functionalism's brain-as-computer picture, to show how philosophy of mind advances by each theory picking on its predecessor. Newton's occult-seeming gravity, later overturned by Einstein, illustrates the chapter's real thesis drawn from Pratchett's 'Lies-to-Wizards': neither science nor philosophy erects a timeless edifice of facts, since 'every so often, you have to unlearn what you thought you already knew,' while Diogenes and the Cynics supply the model of philosophy done in the common tongue that Pratchett shares.
- ch. 34 "21. Welcome to My World" **·** What Is It Like to Be a Bat?; Phenomenology; Embodiment (the lived body) — Built on Jakob von Uexküll's Umwelt—the 'island of the senses' that enfolds each creature, illustrated by a moth that smells only a mate's pheromone and hears only the bat's echolocation—the chapter argues that every Discworld being likewise inhabits a sliced-down life world rather than the world as a whole. The ambiguous puzuma running near light speed, dream-spinning dromes that starve victims inside stolen Umwelten, the two-dimensional Shadowing Lemma, Gaspode's pathogen-warring skin, the hermit elephant's hut, and the re-annual plant that flowers a year before its seeds each show a life world carved out by what an organism can sense and use, whether its world runs on science or magic—prompting the suggestion that the Umwelt is more fundamental than either.
- ch. 35 "22. Cocking a Snook at Death, and Getting Away with It Does the Personification of Death Make It Less Scary" **·** Being-Toward-Death; Immortality and the Soul; Personal Identity — Asking whether Pratchett's personified Death makes mortality less scary, the chapter reads Reaper Man's claim that belief created Death—the moment a living thing grasped non-existence, Death came into being—as a co-arising of dualistic concepts, and surveys psychopomps from Thanatos and Azrael to Yama and even modern assisted-dying organizations. Windle Poons's several kinds of death in Reaper Man ground the distinctions between animalistic, mindistic, and personistic death, showing that whether someone counts as dead can turn on which conception of the person is doing the counting. The closing meditation on Pratchett's own posterior cortical atrophy and the documentary Choosing to Die argues that conceptualizing death in advance—in living wills and bedside conversations with loved ones—is what lets us meet 'THERE'S JUST ME' a little less afraid.
- ch. 36 "23. I’d Rather Stay an Orangutan Because That’s Why Discworld Exists" **·** David Hume — Bundle Theory of Self; Simulacra and Simulation (the hyperreal); Phenomenology — In a self-fictionalizing chapter, editor Nicolas Michaud is summoned by Vetinari to prove Discworld is real, and the answer arrives in the Unseen University library: the Librarian stays an orangutan because language distances us from experience—humans never simply experience a banana, only 'holding a banana,' so for us the words create the reality. Along the way the chapter takes issue with Hume's bundle-of-perceptions view of the self, since word-using humans are never just a collection of present perceptions, and contrasts the Librarian's immediate, unmediated experience of each singular thing with our word-reduced world. It concludes that because we always experience the world as stories, narratives, and terms, 'narratives are all we have,' making the Disc no less real than the Roundworld we talk and think about.
- ch. 37 "From the Ephebian Library" *(notes)*
- ch. 38 "Ephebians" *(notes)*
- ch. 39 "A High Magical Index" *(notes)*

## Disney and Philosophy - Truth, Trust, and a Little Bit of Pixie Dust

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Table of Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "ii" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "iii" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "iv" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Contributors" *(notes)*
- ch. 7 "Acknowledgments" *(notes)*
- ch. 8 "Introduction" **·** Socrates — Editor Richard B. Davis frames the whole volume with Socrates' claim that philosophy begins in wonder, arguing that the Disney universe of films and theme parks is a fitting place to ask who we are, what our purpose is, and how we can live freely and happily. He previews the book's arcs: the feminist evolution of the princesses from Snow White to Moana, whether Hidden Mickeys tell us anything about God, and journeys through Inside Out's emotions and Finding Dory's disability stigmas.
- ch. 9 "Part I “THE SEAWEED IS ALWAYS GREENER IN SOMEBODY ELSE'S LAKE”" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "A Hope for the Future" **·** Simone de Beauvoir, Jean-Paul Sartre, Aristotle — One Is Not Born a Woman; The Male Gaze; The Look (hell is other people) — Reads The Little Mermaid as a step forward from the man-requiring damsels of Snow White and Sleeping Beauty: Ariel reverses the rescue by saving Eric, and Aristotle's 'all men by nature desire to know' is stretched to cover her curiosity about the human world. But Ursula's bargain trading Ariel's voice for legs dramatizes the long history of silenced women (Eve, Pandora, Cassandra, the scold's bridle), and the film's sexy, silent Ariel is exactly what Mulvey's male gaze, built on Sartre's 'the look,' criticizes. Applying Beauvoir's 'one is not born but becomes a woman,' the chapter argues Ariel shows biology is not destiny yet still regresses into needing a man, concluding she is a flawed but real advance toward better heroines.
- ch. 11 "“Your Heart Knows”" **·** Confucius — Xiao (filial piety); Ren (benevolence/humaneness); Authenticity; Moral Luck — Argues that Disney's Mulan transplants a distinctly modern Western ideal of the authentic self ('true to your heart,' 'Reflection') into a Confucian honor culture where the self is constituted by roles and relationships, filial piety (xiao), and ren as the virtue that binds people into larger wholes — which is why the film flopped in China. The matchmaker debacle, Fa Zhou's 'I will die doing what's right,' and the family's shame show a culture where dishonor stains the whole family and even luck (the cricket Cri-Kee) fixes moral standing, conflicting with Western assumptions about individual responsibility. The chapter ends by noting Mulan now resonates with young Chinese viewers, asking whether the Western individualist self is a truth about humanity or a cultural export that, like the Huns, has scaled the Great Wall.
- ch. 12 "Hakuna Matata, Bodhisattva" **·** Dogen, The Buddha — Impermanence (anicca); Non-Self (anatman); Zen Buddhism — Sets up Disney's maxim 'Hey kid, you can be a hero' as the direct opposite of Zen's 'Hey kid, you can be a buddha': escapism versus liberation, told through the life of Dogen, whose monastic choice parallels Disney hero narratives. Mickey in Steamboat Willie — a bundle of still frames with no sound and no real 'he' — illustrates the Buddha's three marks of existence (emptiness, impermanence, frustration), and the chapter argues Disney products are calibrated distractions from these marks while Dogen counsels facing them, with Ariel's Andersen fate as the price of fantasy. It closes on Disney's merchandising machine (Yoda, Star Wars sequels, direct-to-video cash grabs) as everything the countercultural minimalist Yoda or Baloo's bare necessities stand against, concluding that real hakuna matata is accepting impermanence rather than escaping it.
- ch. 13 "Might God Be Hidden for Love's Sake" **·** Faith and Reason — Uses Hidden Mickeys — like the ambiguous log-and-dump-truck at the Once Upon a Toy store — to attack the first premise of Schellenberg's argument from divine hiddenness: just as an arrangement of shapes can be a deliberate Hidden Mickey or a decorative accident, so God's traces in the world can be genuinely ambiguous, letting a non-resistant non-believer rationally fail to believe. Mother Teresa's decades of felt abandonment and C. S. Lewis's 'door slammed in the face' from A Grief Observed frame the experiential problem, and the authors concede the disanalogy that we independently know agents run Disney but not that God exists. As a partial response they offer Lewis's Screwtape suggestion that 'He cannot ravish, He can only woo': God hides precisely so that love can be freely given.
- ch. 14 "Part II “I'LL BE SHOOTING FOR MY OWN HAND”" *(front)*
- ch. 15 "“Cogs that Fit, that Cooperate by Design”" **·** John Stuart Mill, John Locke — The Harm Principle; On Liberty — Reads the government ban on Supers in The Incredibles through Locke's Letter Concerning Toleration and Mill's On Liberty, arguing the ban rests on the 'PREFER' principle — society's likings and dislikings as law — fed by perception management (Winston Deavor's suit-cameras PR campaign) rather than reasons, just as Locke diagnosed intolerance, not diversity of opinion, as the cause of civil strife. Mill's harm principle, illustrated by Elastigirl's 'drinking on duty' remark, should instead govern, so banning Supers' powers and pressuring Dash to suppress his speed ('Everyone's special, Dash' / 'Which is another way of saying no one is') is tyranny. Mr. Huph's 'cogs that fit, that cooperate by design' image of Insuricare shows the conformist cost: a society built on preference rather than reasons will never produce great thinkers.
- ch. 16 "What We Have Learned, or “Always Let Your Conscience Be Your Guide!”" **·** John Locke, John Stuart Mill, Michel Foucault — Philosophy of Disability — Traces Disney disability from the medical model — Dopey as permanent childlike recipient of care, Gus-Gus, and LeFou, echoing Locke's claim that 'lunatics and idiots are never set free from the government of their parents' and Mill's restriction of liberty to those who can care for themselves — to the social model of disability in Finding Dory, where Charlie and Jenny's seashell trail makes the ocean an accommodating rather than disabling environment. Foucault's Birth of the Clinic explains how Gaston uses the asylum to control Maurice, medicine as power over lives. The chapter concludes that Pixar's Big Blue World embodies relational autonomy — Dory thriving 'in your own amazing Dory way' through supported interdependence — while nonverbal sidekicks Becky and Gerald show Dopey's disempowered comic-relief legacy survives.
- ch. 17 "How Far I'll Go" **·** Simone de Beauvoir, Betty Friedan, Judith Butler, Carol Gilligan — One Is Not Born a Woman; The Feminine Mystique (the problem with no name); Gender Performativity; The Ethics of Care — Maps the four waves of feminism onto Disney princess history: Snow White, Cinderella, and Aurora as chattel passed from parents to fairies to princes, betraying even first-wave rights; Beauvoir's 'one is not born but becomes a woman' and Friedan's feminine mystique explaining why the original trio's happiness is measured by marriage. Third-wave princesses are read with Wolf's beauty myth (Ariel and Jasmine as iron maidens), Butler's gender performativity, and Gilligan's care ethics (Ariel's and Belle's contextual, caring moral reasoning versus their fathers' principles). The fourth-wave princesses from Tiana to Moana need no man at all while male leads (Naveen, Flynn, Kristoff, Maui) become vulnerable sidekicks, ending with #GiveElsaAGirlfriend and the thought that equality is balance rather than sameness.
- ch. 18 "Robots and Romance" **·** John Locke, Simone de Beauvoir — Philosophy of Love — Contrasts Disney's 'Irrational Matriarchy and Patriarchy' families (Triton destroying Ariel's grotto, Mother Gothel exploiting Rapunzel, Frollo imprisoning Quasimodo) with its Family Unity Model, using the Filmer–Locke debate: parents are stewards, not owners, and children have Locke's liberty to leave and choose marriage. The positive model is built on John Paul II's theology of the body — Quasimodo's 'Heaven's Light' versus Frollo's 'Hellfire' as gift-love versus using Esmeralda as an object of lust — with the Beast transformed into virtue by Belle's self-sacrificing love. It then answers Beauvoir's charge that housewifery is immanent drudgery with Elastigirl and Edna Mode's 'Done properly, parenting is heroic,' closing with WALL-E and EVE's hand-hold waking screen-addled humans back to family life.
- ch. 19 "Part III “YOUR IDENTITY IS YOUR MOST VALUABLE POSSESSION”" *(front)*
- ch. 20 "Happily Ever After" **·** René Descartes, G.E. Moore, Zeno of Elea, Robert Nozick — Methodological Doubt (evil demon); Philosophical Skepticism; The Experience Machine; Zeno's Paradoxes — Runs Descartes' dreaming argument and evil-genius doubt on Aurora, for whom the scenario is literal: Maleficent is an actually existing deceiver who could have enchanted her whole life in a dungeon. G. E. Moore's common-sense proof — 'here is a hand,' dubbed the Pinocchio test after the real boy — and the Moorean shift answer the skeptic, illustrated by Maleficent deploying Zeno's paradox to stop Prince Phillip's sword, where Phillip rightly clings to the more certain premise that motion is possible. Nozick's experience machine explains why Aurora should care: we want to actually be good and live real lives rather than merely experience them, so authentic 'happily ever after' requires knowing our experiences are backed by reality.
- ch. 21 "“I Am Moana!”" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Aristotle, Friedrich Nietzsche — Existence Precedes Essence; Bad Faith; Authenticity; Radical Freedom and Responsibility — Stages Moana's dilemma between her father's Aristotelian essentialism — everything on Motunui has a fixed function, so Moana's essence as chief precedes her birth — and Sartre's existence precedes essence, with Heihei the unchanging chicken as a being whose essence really does precede existence. Tui embodies bad faith and the crowd (his own aborted voyage left him enforcing 'no one goes beyond the reef'), while Tala and then Moana live authentically through anguish, forlornness, and despair, until 'I am Moana!' marks her self-defined identity. Nietzsche's 'become who you are' and 'what does not destroy me makes me stronger' frame her resilience, which lets her free Maui ('Hook. No hook. I'm Maui.') and unmask Te Ka as Te Fiti whose stolen heart 'does not define you.'
- ch. 22 "Let's Go Fly a Kite!" **·** Socrates, Gottfried Leibniz — The Unexamined Life — Argues Mary Poppins is really the redemption story of George Banks, who leads what Socrates called the unexamined life: his six-minute paternal schedule, shredded nursery advertisement, and blindness to the bird woman show a man who has 'forgotten who he is' and lost the child's wonder. G. K. Chesterton supplies the diagnosis and cure — fairyland as the 'sunny country of common sense,' imagination that 'makes settled things strange,' and astonishment that there is something rather than nothing (with Leibniz's question and a Russell quote) — so that Banks is saved when the bank run strips his ambition. The mended kite, built by the whole family, closes the argument that one can keep the heart of a child while keeping the mind of an adult.
- ch. 23 "Where Do We Go from Here" **·** Thomas Aquinas, Aristotle — The Four Causes; Aristotle on the Soul; Potentiality and Actuality — Argues on Aristotelian–Thomist grounds that WALL-E and EVE cannot truly be intelligent: they are artificial because their formal cause lies outside them in the Buy n Large engineers' minds, whereas natural things like the captain, the cockroach, and the plant have intrinsic formal causes. Intelligence is the act of an intellectual soul — the formal cause of a living thing — so machines at best mimic WALL-E's grasp of spoon-ness, fork-ness, and spork-ness, as Kreeft's comparison of a computer to a library building that does not understand its books makes clear. The conclusion is that AI is qualitatively, not merely technologically, impossible, and Pixar's live-action cinematography is precisely what tricks us into taking the robots as real.
- ch. 24 "Inside the Lived Body" **·** Maurice Merleau-Ponty, René Descartes — Embodiment (the lived body); Mind–Body Dualism; Phenomenology — Pits Merleau-Ponty's embodied self against Descartes' representationalism and mind-over-matter dualism to read Inside Out: Riley's five personified emotions look like homunculi inviting an infinite regress, but the film actually shows a holistic body-subject whose emotions arise in situations and with others. Joy's campaign to keep Riley 'okay' dramatizes the futility of constant happiness and the necessity of Sadness, while Riley's hockey stick becoming an extension of her body illustrates Merleau-Ponty's claim that skillful coping lives in the body rather than in Sadness's 'mind manuals.' The chapter ends on childhood's lived present and the film's power to hold loss before us instead of pushing past it.
- ch. 25 "Part IV “YOU CAN BE ANYONE YOU WANT TO BE”" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 26 "Rafiki Holds the Mirror Up to Nature" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Søren Kierkegaard, Friedrich Nietzsche — Existentialism; The Stages on Life's Way; Radical Freedom and Responsibility — The chapter reads The Lion King as a Disney retelling of Hamlet: Scar's murder of Mufasa parallels Claudius, and Simba's guilt-ridden exile acts out Hamlet's 'to be or not to be.' Timon and Pumbaa's 'hakuna matata' is Kierkegaard's aesthetic stage of pleasure without commitment, which Simba must leave for the ethical stage when Nala, Rafiki ('Whooo are you?'), and Mufasa's ghost demand he 'remember who you are.' Sartre's 'optimistic toughness' and Nietzsche's 'Become who you are' frame Simba's defiant 'I am' before the assembled Pride Lands as existential self-creation.
- ch. 27 "He’ll Be There For You, No Matter What" **·** Authenticity; Utopia and Dystopia — The chapter walks through the three Toy Story films as three conceptions of freedom: freedom as control (Andy's stable room versus Sid's despotism, and Woody's collapse when Buzz displaces him), freedom as faithful identity (Woody torn between the 'Andy' on his boot and the Roundup Gang's museum future), and freedom as self-giving (Woody's sacrifices in Sid's house and the escape from Sunnyside). Lotso embodies control curdled into tyranny after Daisy replaces him, ruling a 'no owners means no heartbreak' utopia that is really a dystopian lie, while Andy giving the toys to Bonnie shows that the freest act is choosing another's good over your own.
- ch. 28 "Changing Fate or Being Changed by Fate" **·** Aristotle, Heraclitus — Virtue Ethics (arete); Fate and Foreknowledge — Colombo and Jones read Brave through Aristotle's virtue ethics and Heraclitus's 'character is fate': the dispute between Merida and Elinor over 'what is a queen' is whether royalty is a mere position or a character requiring cultivated excellence and habituated 'second nature,' which Elinor's poise displays and Merida's hot temper lacks. Merida's witch's bargain to 'change my fate' only scrambles fortune (turning Elinor into a bear) until she follows the riddle 'mend the bond torn by pride,' replacing pride with empathy — and only then, at the Mor'du confrontation where fate, fortune, and character converge, can it be said her fate was changed rather than she by it.
- ch. 29 "Happily Ever After" **·** Plato — Allegory of the Cave; Platonic Love (the ladder of love) — The chapter maps the Beast's enchanted castle onto Plato's cave: the spellbound household, dimming in the decaying West Wing, are prisoners whose human capacities are suppressed, and Belle is the missing cause that breaks the chains Plato never explains. Using Diotima's teaching from the Symposium that beauty compels love, it reads the Beast's curse as externalizing his indifference to beauty, so that the staircase dance, his freeing of Belle, and Belle's 'I love you' trace Diotima's ladder from outward to inward beauty — the light that releases the prisoner.
- ch. 30 "We Can Fix this Hand in Hand" **·** Philosophy of Love — Heit reads Frozen through Marion's claim that 'I love you' usually certifies the speaker's ego: Anna's knocking ('Do you want to build a snowman?') is love as the risky question 'Does anyone out there love me?' asked despite years of rejection, while Hans's whirlwind engagement is Marion's 'vanity' masquerading as love. Against the prince-saves-princess script, the act of true love that thaws Anna's frozen heart turns out to be her own self-giving gesture toward Elsa, showing that true love overcomes fear rather than fulfilling one's own needs.
- ch. 31 "Part V “ALWAYS LET YOUR CONSCIENCE BE YOUR GUIDE”" *(front)*
- ch. 32 "The Rhythm by Which We Live Our Lives" **·** Mencius — Human Nature Is Good; Ren (benevolence/humaneness) — Kowalski interprets Kuzco's arc through Mencius: the emperor's pampered upbringing (eight hands replacing a squeezed toy, Yzma's loveless regency) is the 'hatchets and frost' of the Ox Mountain parable blunting his innate moral sprouts. The gorge scene stages Mencius's child-at-the-well thought experiment — Kuzco's spontaneous snatch of falling Pacha is a 'give-away' action betraying compassion he denies having, which Pacha diagnoses just as Mencius did King Xuan sparing the ox — and Pacha's family, quasi-adopting Kuzco, supplies the loving soil Mencius says the sprouts of ren need to grow.
- ch. 33 "“It's Been Handed Down from Goof to Goof to Goof, and Now, It's Yours, Son”" **·** Immanuel Kant, John Stuart Mill, Socrates, Plato, Peter Singer — The Categorical Imperative; Utilitarianism; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Mentyka uses Goofy to test theories of the good life: Socratic intellectualism stumbles on uneducated-but-good Goofy, Kant's categorical imperative absurdly forbids handing Max the 'goof to goof' fishing pole (Goofy can't will it universal law), Mill's utilitarianism fails when Max's lukewarm reception makes the gift happiness-reducing, and Singer's extension to animals muddies Pluto the dog against Goofy the dog treated as a person. The chapter resolves with Scheler's claim that we first encounter values through moral exemplars — earnest, bumbling Goofy — not through rules or consequences, and a closing plea that philosophers narrate less like the smug 'How to...' narrator.
- ch. 34 "Is Propaganda Permissible" **·** David Hume, John Stuart Mill — Reason Is the Slave of the Passions; On Liberty; The Harm Principle — Manninen surveys Disney's WWII Donald Duck shorts (Donald's Decision, The New Spirit, Der Fuehrer's Face, Commando Duck) to argue propaganda is not only the enemy's tool and not always wrong. Hume's 'slave of the passions' explains why The Spirit of '43 works through the Thrifty-versus-Spendthrift slugfest rather than its implicit argument, and Mill's fallibility-corrigibility case for free expression gives even propaganda a seat in public discourse — though Mill's caveat against expression that instigates mischievous acts marks the real limit, which the contrast with the Axis's 'barefaced lies' (and the Yensid thought experiment) is meant to satisfy.
- ch. 35 "Intergenerational Ships and Sustainable Duties" **·** Aldo Leopold — Anthropocentrism; The Land Ethic; Climate Ethics — Hackett uses WALL-E's lone plant and the Buy n Large Axiom to ask what we owe nature and future generations: WALL-E's tender care of the seedling models treating nature as a rights-bearing 'person' (via Gaia-superorganism thinking), while BnL's obese, screen-hooked passengers on the Axiom satirize capitalist individualism that acknowledges no obligations beyond contracts. He proposes a 'Principle of Sustainable Duties' — minimize net environmental damage and weigh long-term impact — and reads the film's open ending, extending Leopold's expansion of moral community to the biotic whole, as a Platonic aporia about whether an Anthropocene humanity can actually change in time.
- ch. 36 "“Real Life Is Messy”" **·** Thomas Hobbes, Martha Nussbaum — The State of Nature; The Social Contract; Utopia and Dystopia — The chapter tests Zootopia's 'where anyone can be anything' against three ideals: the founding play's predator-prey war is a Hobbesian state of nature ('man is a wolf to man') pacified by social order; Judy's parking duty and Nick's 'you can only be what you are' expose how stereotyping confuses artificial barriers with real biological limits to self-determination; and the evolved end of predation frames a just multispecies society, read through Donaldson and Kymlicka's Zoopolis case for animal citizenship. Nussbaum's hint at 'the gradual supplanting of the natural by the just' is weighed against the dystopia of turning the wild into a world-sized zoo, leaving Zootopia aspirational rather than achieved.
- ch. 37 "Part VI “THE WONDERFUL WORLDVIEW OF DISNEY”" *(front)*
- ch. 38 "Things Worth Believing" **·** Karl Marx, Herbert Marcuse, Theodor Adorno — Alienation; One-Dimensional Man; The Culture Industry — Butterfield stages the Frankfurt School critique of her own Disney love: Marx's three alienations (from work, from others, from species-being) explain the Seven Dwarfs' 'dig dig dig' and the FastPass-scheduled vacation that converts leisure into consumption, while Marcuse's One-Dimensional Man diagnoses the parks' engineered euphoria and 'false needs' as a 'comfortable, smooth, reasonable, democratic unfreedom' that blunts protest — with Adorno adding that consumers see through the advertising and buy anyway. Against the critic, she argues a Disney trip done right can challenge alienation by reviving play, family togetherness, and childlike belief in 'things worth believing.'
- ch. 39 "Hey Goofy! Somebody Just Slipped My Culture a Mickey…" **·** Carl Jung — Archetypes; Simulacra and Simulation (the hyperreal); Cultural Hegemony — Schuchardt argues Disney's real product is 'getting you in there' — colonizing the geography of the imagination: Pirates of the Caribbean is read as an allegory of corporate globalization, White Wilderness fabricated the lemming-suicide myth, and the parks' visual containment keeps visitors inside a world with 'more reality than nature' (Eco's hyperreality, in Baudrillard's territory). He claims Disney built this on Jung's archetypes via Joseph Campbell's Hero's Journey under the mantra 'We Create the Mythology of the Future,' so that media consolidation (the Fox merger shrinking six conglomerates to five) narrows the Overton Window and Disney's manufactured mythology displaces Christianity as the reigning worldview.
- ch. 40 "I think we've all arrived at a very special place, eh Spiritually Ecumenically Grammatically —Captain Jack Spa" **·** Plato, Epicurus, Lucretius, Confucius, Thomas Aquinas — Death Is Nothing to Us; Immortality and the Soul; Xiao (filial piety) — Miller uses Disney deaths to stage the philosophy of death: Plato's immortal soul and Phaedo's affinity argument illuminate Frollo's lust-driven plunge into hellfire, while Epicurus's atomism, his 'death is nothing to us' argument applied to Marlin's terror in Finding Nemo, and Lucretius's symmetry argument are tested against Coco, where Ernesto's forgotten 'final death' versus Hector's restored place on the ofrenda makes posthumous harm plausible. Aquinas's 'my soul is not me' grounds embodiment, Confucius makes Mulan's risking death for father, ancestors, and country a virtuous Confucian death, and Deloria's indigenous web of relations reads Mufasa's Circle of Life, Moana's grandmother, and Brother Bear's ancestral spirits.
- ch. 41 "“Look at This Stuff; Isn't It Neat ” disney and the Commodification of Political Values" **·** John Locke — The Social Contract; Democracy — Dale and Foy tour the parks' political philosophy: Cinderella's Castle looming over Liberty Square embodies the contradiction between princess hierarchy and Lockean self-government, since Locke's Second Treatise grounds rule in consent and majority will, forbids special rights for rulers, and justifies revolution — the very ideals the 'Declaration salad' celebrates under a monarchy's silhouette. The Epcot World Showcase, judged through Kymlicka, Benhabib, and Taylor's recognition-based multiculturalism, reduces eleven cultures to a consumable menu, so that per Barber's 'McWorld' Disney sells pre-packaged 'freedom' and 'multiculturalism' as spectator values that reinforce rather than transform.
- ch. 42 "Index" *(notes)*
- ch. 43 "End User License Agreement" *(front)*

## Divergent and Philosophy - The Factions of Life

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "i" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "ii" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Copyright" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Choose!" **·** Socrates, Plato — Allegory of the Cave; The Unexamined Life — The editor's introduction argues that Roth's series does not just contain philosophy but actually does it, proposing hypotheses about virtue, social order, evil, and forgiveness and testing them for consistency. It casts Tris as a Socratic figure who retraces the Allegory of the Cave: she starts 'chained' inside the cave of Chicago's faction system, climbs out to discover the faction life is a lie, and is tortured and eventually killed for the truth she tries to bring back, just as the cave-dwellers would kill the returning prisoner. The introduction closes by insisting the series is really about choice - that wisdom, unlike knowledge, must be lived, that self-reflection and even self-doubt are the hallmark of the wise, and that when life offers no options, like Tris and the factionless, you have to create your own.
- ch. 8 "Part I Are You Divergent" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "1. Drugging the Kids" **·** Discipline and Docile Bodies; Power/Knowledge — Seymour argues that the serums of Roth's world mirror the real-world medical control of young people: with no objective test, about 6.4 million American children are diagnosed with ADHD and medicated for diverging from adult-defined 'normal' behavior, just as Amity bakes peace serum into its bread and Dauntless uses simulation and truth serums on initiates. Tris's Divergence, and especially her failure to resist the peace serum because some part of her wanted to stop feeling, shows that the desire to conform is what makes chemical control work, while Tobias (re-running his own fear landscape) and Peter (erasing his own memory) model appropriating the serums as tools of self-control. Tris's resistance to the Bureau's memory serum then becomes a vicarious experience of power for young readers who face medication for their own 'divergence.'
- ch. 10 "2. Peculiar!" **·** W.E.B. Du Bois, Antonio Gramsci, Plato — Double Consciousness; Cultural Hegemony; Allegory of the Cave — Ketcham reads the faction system as the Bureau's behavioral-correction experiment after a eugenics disaster, and compares the Bureau to the Tuskegee syphilis study: subjects who never gave informed consent, were denied treatment, and were treated as expendable because 'the experiment must continue at all costs.' He maps W.E.B. Du Bois's 'twoness' - double consciousness - onto Tris, who thrives in Dauntless yet belongs to no faction, and onto Tobias, whose identity collapses when he learns he tests as merely Dauntless. Closing with Gramsci, he argues Chicagoans have bought into the oppressor's story about genes and factions, so they need an organic intellectual to lead them out of the cave, and proposes Freire's praxis as the unfinished sequel.
- ch. 11 "3. Tris Finds Enlightenment" **·** The Buddha — The Four Noble Truths; Non-Self (anatman); Buddhism — Rhine argues that Tris's heroism rests on Buddhist practice: her breath-counting on the Ferris wheel and inside Four's fear landscape, her emotional intelligence, and her awareness of the parental 'voices in her head' are classic meditative disciplines for calming the ego whose clinging, on the Buddhist account, is the cause of suffering (dukkha). She reads Abnegation's gray houses, plain food, and four-times-a-year mirror as ritual ego-diminishment - a middle way between indulgence and the extreme asceticism the Buddha rejected - that explains why most Divergent arise from Abnegation. Contemporary neuroscience is then recruited: the amygdala-versus-prefrontal-cortex struggle Will describes, and monks' heightened gamma waves, explain how Tris can see the simulation as a simulation, like Neo in The Matrix, and act while others are paralyzed by fear.
- ch. 12 "4. Nietzsche’s Faction Test" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — Master and Slave Morality; The Übermensch; Will to Power; Personal Identity — Mallalieu uses Nietzsche's slave morality, will to power, and Ubermensch to read Roth's social experiment: Abnegation's foot-washing, guilt-driven selflessness is slave morality, Amity's drugged peace makes them willing slaves, and Peter - cruel, self-promoting, guilt-ridden - is the most Nietzschean character, whose memory wipe is an attempt to forget his way out of slave morality. Even the heroes turn out Nietzschean when Tris and Tobias dose the Bureau with memory serum, since Nietzsche holds that the old values must be forgotten before new ones can be created. But the chapter concludes the price is too high: losing your values along with your memories means losing your identity ('I would rather be dead than empty'), so Nietzsche's imagined experiment, played out through the Bureau's tools, would be a world good only for the strong and cruel.
- ch. 13 "Part II How to Make Everyone Less Evil" *(front)*
- ch. 14 "5. Overcoming Evil with Love" **·** Aristotle — Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Eudaimonia (flourishing); Philosophy of Love; Virtue Ethics — Bogosian argues the film locates evil not in human nature itself, as Jeanine claims when she calls human nature 'the enemy,' but in cultivable vices - Al's jealousy and fear of failure leading to attempted murder and suicide, Eric's and Jeanine's lust for power - and that only altruistic, self-sacrificial love can counteract them. With Aristotle's point that friendship is essential to developing virtue and to the good life, he shows the faction system fails on its own terms: a single virtue in isolation corrupts (Candor's truth without kindness), and a system that removes real moral and political freedom destroys the flourishing it promises. Tris's parents' sacrifices and the Tris-Four relationship - honest training advice, protection of her Divergence, and the gun-to-her-own-forehead scene where love breaks the simulation's grip - model the love that overcomes the corrupt system.
- ch. 15 "6. Tris’s Compassion and the Problem of Evil" **·** Jean-Jacques Rousseau — The Problem of Evil; Natural Goodness and Corruption by Society; The General Will; The Best of All Possible Worlds — McCrossin reads Tris's whole arc as a struggle with the problem of evil, both the theological version from Job and the modern secular one Susan Neiman describes, in which unreasonable suffering threatens trust in human reason. He casts Tris as cut from Rousseau's mold - her insistence on making the evils of Jeanine, Marcus, and Evelyn intelligible - against Voltaire's Candide refusal to rationalize suffering, and treats the faction system as Rousseau's social-contract strategy for containing the inclination to evil while Tris herself plays a wartime Emile. The key is Tobias's lesson that selflessness and bravery are not that different: selflessness enables the courage that compassion motivates, from pressing her forehead to Jeanine's gun in the fear landscape to taking Caleb's place at the weapons lab, so that the trilogy ends with Tobias's 'we mend each other.'
- ch. 16 "7. Give Peace a Chance" **·** Aristotle, Mahatma Gandhi — Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation; Just War Theory — Lewis argues the Dauntless Manifesto's claim that 'justice is more important than peace' rests on a mistake: following Nicholas Wolterstorff's account of shalom, real peace just is justice - each person receiving what Ulpian called their due and enjoying their rights - so the two cannot come apart. He shows each faction works with a different, deficient conception of peace (Amity's drug-induced calm, Erudite's negotiated order, Abnegation's self-erasure), then uses Aristotle's three types of justice to dismantle Jeanine's best defense, that her slaughter of Abnegation and the Divergents is retributive justice: the accused are innocent and her 'peace' is coercion, propaganda, and fear. The chapter ends with concrete reforms - welcome the factionless as a sixth service faction, let factions teach each other their virtues - so that Roth's Chicago could actually achieve the ethical community of peace-through-justice.
- ch. 17 "Part III Today We Choose" *(front)*
- ch. 18 "8. Plato’s Chicago" **·** Plato — Justice in the Republic; The Philosopher King; The Noble Lie; Democracy — Littmann systematically maps Roth's Chicago onto Plato's Republic: like Marcus, Plato rejects democracy after Athens executed Socrates; both dissolve the family ('faction before blood' versus children raised in common and bred by state schedule); Erudite's IQ-selected leadership mirrors the rigorously trained philosopher-rulers, Dauntless the warrior class whose purely physical training Plato warns turns 'savage and harsh,' Abnegation's poverty the guardians's communism of property, and the factionless the powerless producers - with Plato's noble lie about the metals matching the Erudite's propaganda and serums as instruments of control. The shared flaw, he argues, is concentrated power without external checks, which corrupted Abnegation, corrupts every Dauntless leadership change, and would corrupt Plato's guardians too. The lesson is not to hand power to the least vicious but for democratic voters to confront their own ignorance - or end like Roth's America, in apocalyptic war.
- ch. 19 "9. Class Warfare in Chicago" **·** Karl Marx — Class Struggle; Ideology and False Consciousness — Canode reads the trilogy as a study in class warfare: the factionless are the invisible lower class kept poor as a warning, the factions the middle class distracted by initiation rankings and faction pride, and the Bureau the self-righteous upper class that created and manipulates the whole system. Marx's analysis structures the argument - the ruling class's specter now haunts the powerless, and 'from each according to his ability' shades into coerced harmony - while the American Dream rhetoric of Carter, Reagan, and Obama plays the aptitude test's role: an ideology of merit that converts structural limits into personal failure. The Bureau's genetic sorting of GP scientists above GD support staff mirrors how meritocracy blames the poor for their poverty, so Tris's verdict - the factions 'gave us the illusion of choice without actually giving us a choice' - indicts the US class system too.
- ch. 20 "10. Amity—Directly Democratic" **·** Jean-Jacques Rousseau — Democracy; The General Will — McCrossin identifies Amity's consensus circles as direct democracy - the people exercising government in person, against the republics of Abnegation, Candor, and Erudite and Dauntless's open oligarchy - and hears Tris's skepticism ('How do they get anything done?') and Tobias's admiration ('it makes them kind') echoing the 2011 public debate over Occupy Wall Street. Johanna Reyes acts as a facilitator who helps Amity's general will develop rather than articulating it, but when the planned attack on Erudite threatens innocents she leaves the faction rather than quietly mold its will, confirming Tris's worry about talk in the face of terrible deeds. Through Alexander Meiklejohn's absolutist reading of the First Amendment, the chapter then argues that New Chicago's self-government needs both uncompromisingly protected political speech and a direct-democratic dimension for working out what the common good actually is.
- ch. 21 "11. Should We Execute the Erudite" **·** Michel Foucault, Plato, René Descartes, David Lewis — Power/Knowledge; The Web of Belief; Methodological Doubt (evil demon); The Simulation Hypothesis — Michaud and Watkins ask whether Roth is right that the knowledge-hoarding Erudite are so dangerous we might need to eliminate them, and answer that knowledge is dangerous in three ways. Following Foucault ('it is impossible for knowledge not to engender power'), the powerful define what counts as knowledge - from the church blessing feudalism to the medical community pathologizing gay and transgender people - and following Piaget, new knowledge threatens the interconnected belief schemas (diagrammed as beliefs resting on beliefs) that keep 'faction before blood' and our sense of being special stable, which is why Divergents, evolution, and Copernicus provoke such violent disequilibrium. A Descartes-style simulation scenario - you may be inside Roth's world right now, weeded out for reading the books - and David Lewis's point that investigating knowledge destroys it then show that the real danger is not knowledge but the arrogance of certainty: Jeanine was never a true Erudite.
- ch. 22 "Part IV Wisdom before Faction" *(front)*
- ch. 23 "12. The Courage of Selflessness" **·** Virtue Ethics; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Pless argues that courage and selflessness are not the opposed traits of Dauntless and Abnegation but one and the same virtue: any properly selfless act requires moral courage, any truly courageous act requires sacrifice so that others may prosper, and both depend on an outside party in need and on overriding the instinct of self-preservation. Because a rewarded sacrifice becomes social capital - the lunch-giving and bullet-catching examples - neither virtue can be institutionalized: Abnegation's legalistic rules and Marcus's publicly displayed selflessness destroy selflessness by removing choice, and Eric's fear-eradicating initiation produces glory and cruelty instead of courage, since fear connotes responsibility. Natalie and Andrew Prior's shared, gender-subverting sacrifices, and Tris stepping in to take Al's knives, show the single virtue of selfless courage chosen in the moment, culminating in a death that is an extension of her character rather than a surprise.
- ch. 24 "13. Dauntless Courage" **·** Aristotle, Socrates, Plato — The Golden Mean; Virtue Ethics (arete) — The Bocks test whether the Dauntless are brave or merely reckless using Plato's Laches and Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics. Socrates refutes Eric's definition of courage as never surrendering - the Scythian cavalry fights while fleeing, and endurance without wisdom is foolhardiness - while Aristotle locates courage as the mean between rashness and cowardice, acquired by habituation ('we become brave by doing brave acts'), which redeems much Dauntless training: Tris jumps onto the roof a second time without hesitation because practice has shaped her. Fearlessness is neither necessary nor sufficient - Four ignores his four fears rather than lacking them, and only madmen feel none - so the brave person fears the right things, at the right time, for noble ends: Eric is ruthless rather than courageous, while Tris's mother's sacrifice and Tris's taking Caleb's place at the death-serum lab show courage aimed at the good of others, with Four's grieving perseverance showing it can even serve self-preservation out of love.
- ch. 25 "14. You Have to Be Good before You Can Be Smart" **·** Plato, Martha Nussbaum — The Philosopher King; Allegory of the Cave; Consequentialism; Virtue Ethics — The Bocks argue that the Erudite pairing of ignorance with knowledge is morally loaded: with Linda Zagzebski's and James Montmarquet's virtue epistemology, high-end knowledge requires intellectual virtues like the courage to test your beliefs publicly, so you really do have to be good before you can be smart. The Erudite manifesto's curriculum omits the humanities that open the mind, and its IQ-only leadership test fails Plato's requirement that philosopher kings love wisdom itself and, like the freed cave-prisoner, return to benefit society - which is why Jeanine, driven by lust for power and concealment of the truth beyond the fence, is 'a poor example of a philosopher king.' Both the Erudite and the Bureau reason as consequentialists ('Knowledge is power... power to do good') to justify slaughtering Abnegation and experimenting on unconsenting Chicagoans, when the pursuit of knowledge must be constrained by informed consent and human dignity.
- ch. 26 "15. Tell the Truth at All Costs" **·** Immanuel Kant, Aristotle, Jeremy Bentham — The Categorical Imperative; Utilitarianism; Virtue Ethics; Kingdom of Ends — Bowman argues Candor is the most philosophically important faction because its absolute honesty is Kant's ethics: truthfulness as a perfect duty grounded in free will, making even politeness 'deception in pretty packaging,' and forbidding a lie even when the Erudite come to Merciless Mart hunting Tris - which mirrors Kant's reply to Benjamin Constant that one must not lie even to the murderer at the door. Against this he sets Aristotle's virtue ethics, where truth bows to wisdom, justice, and fortitude when the choice is handing hidden Jews to the SS, and Bentham's utilitarianism, which Amity enacts when it lies at the gates to keep the peace and save lives. The Candor Manifesto's 'web of individual-to-individual relationships' and Kant's kingdom of ends explain why universal honesty alone could make truth safe - and why Christina, the Candor, is the one left standing when the city's deception finally destroys it.
- ch. 27 "Part V Know Thy Faction, Know Thy Self" *(front)*
- ch. 28 "16. What Do We Really Owe Our Parents" **·** Confucius, Aristotle, Mencius — Xiao (filial piety); Aristotle on Friendship (philia) — Karavitis uses Tris and Tobias to stage the filial-piety debate: Tris, who loves her parents and learns to see them with adult eyes after discovering their hidden pasts, remains 'allegiant' to the end, while the abused Tobias owes Marcus and Evelyn nothing and reaches only a cautious adult reconciliation with his mother. After surveying Confucian xiao (with Mencius, and Buddhism's forced accommodation of it) and the Abrahamic commandments on one side, and Norman Daniels's and Jane English's autonomy-based arguments that children never consented to be born and so owe nothing on the other, he concludes no rational argument mandates filial duties from adult children. Aristotle's three types of friendship supply the one defensible model: only when adult children and parents meet as virtuous equals can a chosen, consensual relationship carrying care, gratitude, and love arise - so 'as adults, we owe our parents nothing,' though love may freely flow.
- ch. 29 "17. Waking Up Divergent" **·** René Descartes — Methodological Doubt (evil demon); The Cogito — Seymour reads the Bureau's Chicago experiment as Descartes's Meditations made narrative: the faction system is a dream of beliefs absorbed from upbringing without evidence, the Bureau is the evil demon, and Tris's video is the forced awakening. The characters model the possible responses - most cling to the dream, Tobias trades one dream for another by joining Nita's genetically-damaged cause and acting on withheld information that gets Uriah hurt, Peter rationalizes and finally erases himself with memory serum, while curious Tris tests every belief against falsehood, trusts only her own mind as the cogito grounds Descartes, and acts to wake the other dreamers. The chapter's lesson is Cartesian: values define who you are, so only constant self-examination and the pursuit of knowledge keep your identity from being an imposed lie.
- ch. 30 "18. The Virtues of Choosing Your Faction" **·** Aristotle, Immanuel Kant — Virtue Ethics (arete); Eudaimonia (flourishing); Respect for Persons — Pascoe contrasts Aristotle's and Kant's theories of virtue through the Choosing Ceremony. On Aristotle's account virtues are taught (intellectual) and then habituated into character (moral), tied to one's function, so Susan flourishes because aptitude, upbringing, and choice align while the transfer Al withers - but Tris, Divergent with multiple aptitudes, needs Kant instead: natural virtues are mere 'gifts of nature' that become good only through good will, and her perfect duties to herself and imperfect duties regarding family and faction can justify choosing Dauntless over staying in Abnegation. Kant wins the day: because a corrupted community like Eric's Dauntless redefines bravery into obedience, Tris must think for herself - 'maybe I am wired like the Divergent' - choosing her virtues out of duty and self-respect rather than inheriting them.
- ch. 31 "19. Why Divergent are Actually Convergent" **·** Virtue Ethics; Virtue Ethics (arete) — Schenkewitz argues Chicago's downfall is built into its ideal: each faction drills a single virtue through total lifestyle discipline - Abnegation's mirror rules and gray houses, Candor's black and white dress - until 'you become what you do,' but a solitary virtue inverts into its own vice once it is cut off from the others: Erudite's knowledge becomes a lust for power, Dauntless bravery cruelty, Candor honesty callousness, Amity peace passivity, Abnegation selflessness stifling. The Divergent, who naturally hold all the virtues together in one unified character, are therefore the city's apex rather than its disease: Tris never stops being Abnegation while being Dauntless, and her worth lies in negotiating among virtues and making real choices from real alternatives, unlike the faction archetypes. Justice, the chapter concludes, requires the convergence of all the virtues within each person - the Divergent are actually convergent.
- ch. 32 "Divergent Bibliographic Database" *(notes)*
- ch. 33 "The Bureau of Philosophical Welfare" *(notes)*
- ch. 34 "Index" *(notes)*
- ch. 35 "225" *(front)*
- ch. 36 "227" *(front)*

## Doctor Strange and Philosophy - The Other Book of Forbidden Knowledge

- ch. 1 "Introduction" *(no index entry yet)* — The introduction surveys Doctor Strange's history from Stan Lee and Steve Ditko's 1963 comics to the 2016 Benedict Cumberbatch film, framing him as Marvel's mystic loner defended by Wong and trained by the Ancient One. It previews the book's guiding philosophical questions: how Strange reconciles hard-boiled science with mystical training, why his line about being 'tiny momentary specks within an indifferent universe' was wrong, what his astral self says about mind and body, and why he is always so alone.
- ch. 2 "Part I “YOU’RE JUST ANOTHER TINY, MOMENTARY SPECK WITHIN AN INDIFFERENT UNIVERSE”" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "A Strange Realization" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, René Descartes, Francis Bacon — Amor Fati (love of fate); Eternal Recurrence — Dunn argues that Strange begins as a foot soldier of the Baconian project (Bacon and Descartes' program of conquering nature and treating death as an enemy to be subdued, embodied in his surgical God-complex and neurogenesis research), and that its shipwreck drives him to Kamar-Taj. Using Nietzsche, he reads both Strange and Kaecilius as sick with the 'spirit of revenge against time' — Kaecilius fleeing into Dormammu's timeless Dark Dimension as Nietzsche's Hinterweltler, Strange clinging to his unrecoverable past. The climactic 'Dormammu, I've come to bargain' time loop is interpreted as Nietzsche's eternal return weaponized, and Strange's 'Pain's an old friend' as his achievement of amor fati, echoing the Ancient One's dying claim that death is what gives life meaning.
- ch. 4 "Death Gives Meaning to Life" **·** Martin Heidegger, Aristotle, Albert Camus, Friedrich Nietzsche — Being-Toward-Death; Authenticity; The Myth of Sisyphus — Lee reads Strange's car crash as Heideggerian angst and the Call of conscience that ends his 'ontic' life of treating patients as reputation-building problems, forcing a choice between authenticity and a mask. Kaecilius and Dormammu embody the inauthentic flight from death (Kaecilius going 'beyond good and evil' Nietzsche-style), while the Ancient One's confession — drawing on the Dark Dimension only until Strange could replace her, then stretching one moment into a thousand to watch the snow — models accepting mortality. Dormammu's torment in Strange's time loop is compared to Camus's Sisyphus, supporting the thesis that without an ending life is endless repetition, so 'death gives meaning to life.'
- ch. 5 "A Shared Higher Thirst" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — The Übermensch; Master and Slave Morality; The Death of God — Cleary reads Strange as a would-be Nietzschean Übermensch — brilliant, striving, and flirting with nihilism via the 'tiny momentary speck' line — whose narcissism, not his ideals, blocks both self-overcoming and love. Strange's rage at Christine Palmer's wine and cheese ('don't pity me') is explained through Nietzsche's attack on pity as herd morality, and his fixation on restoring his old surgeon-self as refusing Zarathustra's call to burn oneself to ashes. The Ancient One leaving him to die on Everest, Mordo's hard training, and Wong refusing to laugh at his jokes exemplify Nietzschean friendship as challenge and 'war,' culminating in the 'shared higher thirst for an ideal above them' that lets Strange overcome himself.
- ch. 6 "The Existential Redefinition of Doctor Strange" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Friedrich Nietzsche, Martin Heidegger — Bad Faith; Existence Precedes Essence; Radical Freedom and Responsibility; Eternal Recurrence — DiGeorgio traces Strange's 'existential conversion' from scientific nihilism ('matter and nothing more') through Sartrean bad faith — playing the brilliant surgeon while not really believing life meaningless, refusing the Ancient One's widened keyhole — to authenticity. His hesitation to stay and fight after killing a zealot, and the Ancient One's 'it's not about you,' set up his choice to defend humanity as Sartre's universal decree, choosing for all humankind in anguish and freedom. The endless time loop imposed on Dormammu is read as Nietzsche's eternal recurrence demanding total commitment to one's choices, so that by the end Strange lives existence-precedes-essence: what he does, not his 'brilliant surgeon' essence, defines him.
- ch. 7 "Part II “FORGET EVERYTHING THAT YOU THINK YOU KNOW”" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Out of the Orb" **·** René Descartes, John Locke, David Hume, Gottfried Leibniz, Baruch Spinoza — The Cogito; Methodological Doubt (evil demon); Mind–Body Dualism; Philosophical Skepticism — Boudreaux maps Strange's comic-book journey into the Orb of Agamotto (dragged in by the Silver Dagger, greeted by a hookah-smoking caterpillar, tempted by counterfeit heroes like the Silver Surfer and Hulk) onto Descartes's Meditations. Empiricism (Locke's tabula rasa, Hume's sense-data) collapses when the senses are so thoroughly deceived inside the Orb, and innate ideas plus the Law of Non-Contradiction are not enough, so Strange reasons his way to the cogito — 'Nothing merits belief here — except oneself!' — just as Descartes doubts the evil demon. The caterpillar is revealed as Agamotto, playing the role of Descartes's non-deceiving God whose guidance lets Strange escape, and Strange's astral projections are offered as dramatic proof of mind-body dualism.
