- ch. 9 "A Strange Ending" **·** Aristotle — Virtue Ethics (arete); The Golden Mean; Reliabilism — Manninen applies virtue epistemology — built on Aristotle's virtue ethics and golden mean — to the four students of the Ancient One, asking not what they knew of the mystic arts but how they knew it. Strange's photographic memory exemplifies reliabilist epistemic ability, while Montmarquet's responsibilist virtues (impartiality, intellectual courage) are measured against Pangborn's self-serving magic, Kaecilius's courage without impartiality, and Mordo's epistemic rigidity and blind deference to authority ('no more questions'), each a vice of excess or deficiency. The Book of Cagliostro frames Wong's 'no knowledge in Kamar-Taj is forbidden, only certain practices,' and Strange emerges closest to the balanced intellectual character, though Aristotle demands consistency, not a single virtuous act.
- ch. 10 "The Sorcerer Socratic" **·** Socrates — Socratic Ignorance (knowing that you don't know); The Unexamined Life; Socratic Method (elenchus) — Timm argues Strange follows Socrates' path from arrogance to Socratic heroism: the hand injury forces intellectual humility (the Oracle-at-Delphi moment), the Ancient One's keyhole metaphor and astral-form lesson ('to show you just how much you don't know') enact the Socratic method of questioning the supposedly wise, and the astral projection is literally the examined life — Socrates' unexamined life not worth living. Strange's commitment to protecting humanity from interdimensional evil parallels the gadfly who risks death to improve Athens, his refusal to do injustice even under pressure echoes the Crito, and his repeated willingness to die for others (facing Dormammu, holding the Shambler back) completes the Socratic Hero profile.
- ch. 11 "We Can All Be Socratics Supreme" **·** Socrates, Francis Bacon — Socratic Ignorance (knowing that you don't know); Epistemic Injustice — Pape uses the Ancient One's keyhole metaphor to argue that all knowledge is partial and biased: Bacon's confident scientific method ('human knowledge and human power meet in one') is itself just a useful belief, while Socratic awareness of one's ignorance — knowing that you have blind spots, even if you cannot see them — is the real wisdom. She reads the film's running gags as bias lessons (the 'Mister Doctor' exchange, Strange mistaking the wifi password 'Shamballa' for a mantra, the noble-savage prejudice) and feminist critiques of impartiality as itself partial. The whitewashing controversy around Tilda Swinton's Ancient One — weighed against the Fu Manchu stereotype of the 1963 comics and Iron Man 3's Mandarin-as-Trevor-Slattery subversion — shows how filmmakers can confront the biases they cannot simply remove.
- ch. 12 "I Control It by Surrendering Control" **·** Thomas Aquinas, Cicero — Natural Law — Pérez stages the film's natural-law debate through Ayn Rand's Objectivism (Steve Ditko's avowed influence): Mordo's absolutism — 'there is no other way,' 'the bill comes due' — mirrors the axioms of existence, consciousness, and identity (A is A), with Cicero and Aquinas' divinely eternal natural law as the older backdrop. Rand's system is strained by the film's events: Wong dies and is un-died when Strange rolls back time in Hong Kong, and the Eye of Agamotto's time loop creates three simultaneous realities, so 'A' is no longer self-evidently one thing. The chapter concludes that Strange, exercising exactly the Objectivist freedom to think and choose, violates natural law in order to uphold it — sacrificing himself Prometheus- or Sisyphus-like in the Dormammu bargain — while Mordo's departure is wounded pride rather than principle.
- ch. 13 "Part III “REALITY IS ONE OF MANY”" *(front)*
- ch. 14 "“Strange Magic,” Electric Light Orchestra, 1975" **·** René Descartes — Mind–Body Dualism — Kowalski reads the film's astral-form scenes as a dramatization of Cartesian substance dualism against Strange's opening physicalism ('There is no such thing as spirit! We are made of matter and nothing more'): the Ancient One pushing his astral body out refutes physicalism, his soul reads books while his body sleeps, and he fights Kaecilius's acolyte as a visible astral form in Christine Palmer's ER. The chapter then confronts the classic interaction problem — Descartes' pineal gland and 'animal spirits,' Elisabeth of Bohemia's and Garrett Thomson's objections — asking how an immaterial astral form can turn pages or knock over vending machines, joking that it would take 'a very strange form of interactionism.' The Ancient One's account of magic as energy drawn from other dimensions of the multiverse is offered as the film's own dualist reply, with a closing plea for the epistemic humility Strange had to learn.
- ch. 15 "One Thing We Know Is That Life Is Strange" **·** Aristotle, Thomas Aquinas, René Descartes, Francis Bacon — The Four Causes — Donovan and Richardson read Jason Aaron and Chris Bachalo's 2015 comic run as portraying Strange as a natural philosopher-scientist: he researches the Book of Vishanti to trap Zelma Stanton's mind maggots, combines inductive and deductive reasoning, and must eventually write new laws of magic when the old books fail. Aristotle's four causes and teleology are applied concretely — Strange spares the Een'Gawori slugs because their end or purpose is merely to consume magic (acorns cannot be conquerors), letting him deduce something else destroyed their homeworld — with Aquinas credited for mediating Aristotle to Christendom. Lord Imperator and the Empirikul embody a fanatical, inquisitional 'science' (draining magic from Fandazar Foo and the Sanctum, unleashing the Thing in the Cellar), contrasted with the genuinely entwined history of science, philosophy, and magic from Copernicus, Bacon, Galileo, and Kepler through Descartes and Newton to quantum 'spooky action' — Clarke's law that advanced technology is indistinguishable from magic.
- ch. 16 "Only Time Will Tell" **·** Henri Bergson — Duration (durée); Élan Vital (creative evolution) — Latta reads the film through Bergson's distinction between clock time and duration: Strange is introduced as pure quantitative multiplicity — identifying Chuck Mangione's 'Feels So Good' (1977) mid-surgery, surrounding himself with watches, including Christine Palmer's inscribed 'Time will tell how much I love you' — while the Ancient One lives duration, surrendering to the river's current. Kaecilius embodies 'chronological nihilism' ('time is an insult'), and his and Dormammu's timeless Dark Dimension is existentially impossible for Bergson, since life only progresses and endures in time. The climax shows Strange recreating the present in the endless looped bargain with Dormammu ('pain is an old friend'), growing through qualitative multiplicity and élan vital, until the shattered watch — hands broken, inscription intact — marks his conversion from counting time to living it.
- ch. 17 "Part IV “A MAN LOOKING AT THE WORLD THROUGH A KEYHOLE”" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 18 "Of Science, Sorcery, and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Kuhn, Karl Popper, Paul Feyerabend — Paradigm Shifts; Falsificationism; Anything Goes (epistemological anarchism) — The chapter reads Strange's conversion from neurosurgeon to sorcerer as a Kuhnian paradigm shift: his medical materialism is 'normal science' puzzle-solving that keeps resisting anomalies (Pangborn's impossible recovery, the astral projection, the multiverse tour) until Kamar-Taj offers a rival paradigm with its own exemplars, which he can only adopt by learning 'knowledge how' through tedious practice. It explains incommensurability through Strange's inability to rationally persuade his old medical colleagues, then contrasts Kuhn with Popper's falsificationism, Feyerabend's 'anything goes' anarchism (sorcery once looked as disreputable as early heliocentrism), and Lakatos's progressive versus degenerating research programs to ask whether sorcery could count as a science in Strange's world.
- ch. 19 "We’ve Run Out of Time… or Have We" **·** Ockham's Razor; The Metaphysics of Time Travel — The chapter argues that the film's multiverse, where sorcerers 'harness energy drawn from other dimensions,' is best modeled on the many-worlds interpretation of quantum mechanics, walking through Tegmark's Level I-III multiverses, Schrodinger's cat, the double-slit experiment, and the measurement problem (Copenhagen, von Neumann-Wigner's mind-caused collapse, and Everett's branching) to show that interpreting the formalism is philosophy rather than physics. It defends many-worlds against the objections of counterintuition and Ockham's razor, and suggests that Strange trapping Dormammu in a time loop works like a quantum experiment, with branching worlds dissolving the grandfather paradox.
- ch. 20 "Knock and the Door Will Be Opened… Eventually" **·** Søren Kierkegaard, Socrates — The Leap of Faith; Socratic Ignorance (knowing that you don't know); Faith and Reason — The chapter reads Strange pounding on Kamar-Taj's door as Kierkegaard's passionate collision with 'that which thought cannot think': the materialist surgeon hits the unknown as a frontier, takes offense at the Ancient One's talk of healing through belief, and can only proceed by surrendering intellectual control ('I control it by surrendering control?'). It applies Kierkegaard's two teachers from Philosophical Fragments: Pangborn is healed the Socratic way, recollecting a power already within him, while the Ancient One's astral knock-out and eviction of Strange acts like the god as teacher, forcing him to discover that he is untruth, until faith becomes 'controlling magic by surrendering to it.'
- ch. 21 "Part V “IT’S NOT ABOUT YOU”" *(front)*
- ch. 22 "The Sorcerer So Human" **·** Aristotle — The Golden Mean; Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Virtue Ethics (arete); Eudaimonia (flourishing) — The chapter argues Strange fails Aristotle's golden mean at both stages of his life: as a surgeon he chose cases for money and fame (greed and gluttony rather than generosity and temperance), and as Sorcerer Supreme he swings to the opposite extreme of total self-denial, refusing sleep, love, and friendship with Clea, Wong, and the Ancient One, since relationships between unequals cannot be the reciprocal philia Aristotle demands. It reads his hardest choices—the Dark Dimension left to Dormammu to save Earth, the Illuminati destroying parallel Earths during the incursions, and spending the last drop of the panacea on Wong rather than the world—as tragic dilemmas that show both the virtue ethicist's need for judgment and a defensible-but-excessive partiality toward friends.
- ch. 23 "The Bill Comes Due" **·** Michael Walzer, Immanuel Kant — Deontology; Consequentialism; Utilitarianism — The chapter uses Michael Walzer's Problem of Dirty Hands to weigh the Ancient One's secret lifelong use of forbidden dark magic against Kaecilius's summoning of Dormammu, arguing that only she faces a genuine dirty-hands dilemma because her end (defending Earth) is good while his is not. It asks why dark magic is forbidden at all and answers with rule-utilitarian reasoning that has taken on a deontological life of its own, then invokes Kant's murderer-at-the-door essay to argue, with Mordo's 'the bill comes due,' that whoever does wrong for good ends bears responsibility for the downstream consequences—and that Strange's willingness to break Kamar-Taj's rules while accepting the cost is what makes him a hero.
- ch. 24 "Power Behind the Cloak" **·** G.W.F. Hegel, Immanuel Kant — Master–Slave Dialectic; Respect for Persons; Autonomy of the Will — The chapter asks whether Wong's hereditary service to the Sorcerer Supreme is culpable servility, framing the charge with Kant's line that 'one who makes himself a worm cannot complain' and Thomas E. Hill's Kantian account of servility as failing to claim one's dignity as a person. It answers with Hegel's master-slave dialectic: Strange, free to haunt the astral plane, becomes dependent on Wong for everything worldly, while Wong, by working on the world rather than merely commanding it, develops the self-consciousness Hegel says work produces—he serves the shared mission rather than the master—so the relationship is a partnership in which servitude grounds rather than forfeits Wong's self-respect.
- ch. 25 "By the Crimson Bands of Cyttorak… We Conclude!" **·** Confucius, Laozi — Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Zen Buddhism; Taoism (Daoism) — The chapter weighs Strange's choice in Doctor Strange: The Oath to spend the last drop of the panacea curing Wong's brain tumor instead of having it replicated to cure all disease, arguing it violates 'first, do no harm' and the Hippocratic precepts—not least because Strange is Wong's friend rather than his physician and invokes a no-longer-binding oath. It then argues his sorcerer's ethics fit the martial arts warrior way better: 'hurt rather than harm' from Master Kan in Kung Fu and the Pinan forms, Zen-shaped bushido's self-control, Confucian responsibility and care for society, and the Tao Te Ching's balance and thousand-mile journey as the shared ethos of medical, martial, and mystic training.
- ch. 26 "Part VI “I’VE COME TO BARGAIN”" *(front)*
- ch. 27 "The Dread Conclusion!" **·** Augustine of Hippo — The Problem of Evil; The Two Cities — The chapter asks whether Dormammu is truly evil and tests him against Augustine's three types of evil: metaphysical evil as privation fails because Dormammu is a positive being with a face and a voice, natural evil fails because he is a sentient agent with intentions, so he lands squarely in moral evil—freely intending to deprive intelligent beings of the good of existence. It entertains the Galactus-style alternative that Dormammu, like a lion taking a gazelle, is an amoral force consuming worlds for survival, but his rage in the time loop ('your world is mine, like all worlds') reveals a drive for domination rather than sustenance, while conceding we know him only through the Ancient One's possibly biased testimony.
- ch. 28 "Leo Tolstoy, Sorcerer-Defender Supreme" **·** Mahatma Gandhi — Ahimsa (nonviolence); The Meaning of Life; Anarchism — The chapter pairs Strange's midlife collapse with Tolstoy's crisis in A Confession—both famous egotists who found their glittering lives 'a stupid fraud'—using Tolstoy's fable of the traveler licking honey above the dragon of death to read Strange's desperate medical questing as vain honey-licking. It lays out Tolstoy's doctrine of nonresistance: violence is forcing others against their will, every act of violent resistance multiplies evil, and the state's division of cruelty makes killing mechanical, then asks whether Tolstoy could accept Strange's metaphysical war, concluding from Tolstoy's remark that he would axe a bear to save a peasant (but not kill the highwayman) that he could endorse magical violence against Dormammu while still forbidding violence against human beings.
- ch. 29 "Magic and Deep Magic" **·** Thomas Aquinas, Aristotle — Natural Law; The Five Ways; Faith and Reason — The chapter argues that Strange's universe implicitly contains the Creator God of theism rather than just Marvel's gods: Aristotle's impersonal prime mover will not do because Strange needs a relatable source of an objective moral order, so Aquinas's all-good author of eternal, natural, and divine law supplies the framework. It reads three comics as evidence: Strange defeating Dracula by invoking the Tetragrammaton, the Sise-Neg saga where a sorcerer who attains godhood at the dawn of time learns to recreate the universe exactly as the first cause made it, and the World War Hulk story where summoning Zom shows the hubris of violating the moral order—closing with C.S. Lewis's 'deep magic' as cooperation with the created order versus dark magic's will to control it.
- ch. 30 "Index" *(notes)*
- ch. 31 "End User License Agreement" *(front)*

## Doctor Who and Philosophy - Bigger on the Inside

- ch. 1 "section-1" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Doctor Who and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Popular Culture and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "We’ve Been Abducted by the Doctor, and We Love It!" *(no index entry yet)* — The editors' introduction recounts the show's history from its 1963 premiere (the day after the Kennedy assassination) and the Daleks' first appearance through all eleven Doctors, and describes how companions from Ian and Barbara to Rose, Donna, and Amy were 'abducted' by the Doctor's sense of wonder. It argues that the curiosity and love of adventure the TARDIS inspires in viewer-companions is the very same impulse that drives the love of wisdom, and previews the book's six 'episodes' on personal identity, time travel, logic and science, the Doctor's ethics, existentialism and aesthetics, and the show's cultural meaning.
- ch. 6 "EPISODE 1 - I’m the Doctor. Who" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "A Dead End" **·** John Locke, Derek Parfit, Robert Nozick, Thomas Reid — Personal Identity (memory theory); Teleporter Paradox (personal fission); Personal Identity — Stokes uses regeneration and the multi-Doctor episodes ('The Three Doctors', 'The Five Doctors', 'The Trial of a Time Lord') to test theories of personal identity: Locke's memory criterion, Reid's transitivity objection, Parfit's teleporter and 'branch-line' cases, Nozick's closest-continuer theory, four-dimensional person-stages, animalism, and narrative identity. He argues that no objective-continuity account explains why the Fifth Doctor still cares whether he himself survives in 'The Caves of Androzani', and hints that being the Doctor may be a subjective stance in which each incarnation takes ownership of the others as itself.
- ch. 8 "Doctor Who and the Ultimate Answer" **·** Personal Identity; Personal Identity (memory theory) — Littmann mines Who scenarios as hypotheticals that test identity theories: regeneration, natural doubles like Salamander, impersonating Zygons and Chameleons, bodiless beings like Omega and the Great Intelligence, TARDIS jumps that break spatiotemporal continuity, Cassandra possessing the Doctor and Rose, memory theft by Eldrad and the Daleks, and the Master's year as the unrecognizably good Professor Yana. He argues that sameness of body (including the four-dimensional 'worm' of spatiotemporal continuity), the memory criterion, and psychological similarity each fail, leaving the question of what makes the Doctor the Doctor — and you you — open.
- ch. 9 "What’s Persistence Without Identity" **·** John Locke, Robert Nozick, Derek Parfit, David Lewis, Thomas Reid — Personal Identity (memory theory); Personal Identity; Teleporter Paradox (personal fission) — Hanley first fixes what is true in the Who canon (treating fiction as pretense), then asks how the Doctor persists when regeneration replaces 'every single cell' and when John Smith's amnesia severs memory in 'Human Nature'. Rejecting bodily continuity (the severed hand of 'The Christmas Invasion'), soul 'pellets', and simple memory (Reid's transitivity problem), he defends neo-Lockean mental continuity plus Nozick's closest-continuer schema to explain why Jackson Lake, the regrown Doctor, and DoctorDonna in 'Journey's End' are not the Doctor, and closes with Parfit's claim that psychological continuity rather than identity is what matters in survival, alongside Lewis's space-time-worm alternative.
- ch. 10 "I Think, Therefore I Exist . . . I Think" **·** John Locke, Derek Parfit, Plato, René Descartes, Bernard Williams — Personal Identity; Personal Identity (memory theory); Mind–Body Dualism — Johnson argues that the Platonic-Cartesian soul is refuted by neuroscience (Phineas Gage, Penfield's stimulation studies), and that Locke's memory criterion fails on Reid-style transitivity cases like DoctorDonna and on Bernard-Williams-inspired worry about the amnesiac John Smith, who would still dread the Family of Blood's torture. Parfit's causal-continuity criterion then wrongly makes the meta-crisis Doctor of 'Journey's End' identical with the Tenth, so Johnson defends a four-dimensionalist 'time worm' view on which the Doctors are stages of one person — before a final sorites-style argument (heaps, spin-offs like Torchwood) suggests that 'persons' may not exist at all.
- ch. 11 "EPISODE 2 - Wibbly-Wobbly, Timey-Wimey . . . Stuff" *(front)*
- ch. 12 "We Wouldn’t Be Here Without Time Travel" **·** David Lewis, Gottfried Leibniz, Zeno of Elea — The Metaphysics of Time Travel; Possible Worlds; Zeno's Paradoxes — Worley distinguishes logical from empirical possibility (Leonardo's round-square in 'City of Death') and shows that time-travel paradoxes — Leela's self-caused antibodies, the unauthored 'Blink' Easter egg, the Master's Toclafane — can be handled with Zeno-style analysis of contradiction and David Lewis's distinction between external and personal time, under which travelers instantiate rather than rewrite history ('The Visitation', 'The Time Meddler'). He then reads 'Turn Left' and the Doctor's 'alternative time' in 'Pyramids of Mars' through Leibniz's possible worlds and Lewis's actualism about parallel universes, concluding that in 'City of Death' time travel is essential to life on Earth existing at all.
- ch. 13 "The Daleks Can Stop the Pyramids from Being Built!" **·** The Metaphysics of Time Travel — Goff asks whether the Daleks could genuinely change the past by exterminating the Egyptian pyramid builders, arguing that presentism leaves no past to travel to while eternalism fixes all facts of time so travelers can affect but never change history. He defends the growing block theory — past and present exist, the future does not — and works out that Dalek intervention would generate a new, pyramid-less history replacing the old one no faster than the absolute present advances, so the Doctor could always return to a present still causally continuous with the pyramids.
- ch. 14 "Prognosis" **·** David Lewis — The Metaphysics of Time Travel — Eaton reconstructs the Doctor's 'wibbly-wobbly, timey-wimey' speech in 'Blink' as David Lewis's four-dimensionalism: the universe is one spacetime manifold, time travelers are zig-zag or broken person-stages through it, and in external time effects can precede causes. Lewis's compossibility reading of 'can' dissolves the grandfather paradox and the bilking worry about Sally Sparrow's closed causal chain, and comparison with Horwich and Ismael shows the Doctor agreeing that closed causal loops are possible — indeed, unlike them, he holds they actually happen.
- ch. 15 "This Is the Age of Steel" **·** Thomas Hobbes, John Stuart Mill, David Hume — The Social Contract; The Greatest Happiness Principle; Higher and Lower Pleasures; Reason Is the Slave of the Passions — Littmann asks what a genuinely logical creature would do and grades the Cybermen against three accounts of reason: Hobbes's self-preservation and social contract, which the survival-focused Cybermen of 'The Tenth Planet' roughly satisfy but their later wars of conquest betray; Mill's utilitarianism, whose greatest-happiness principle, higher pleasures, liberty, and individuality condemn emotion-stripping, forced 'upgrades', and uniformity; and Hume's claim that reason alone motivates nothing, under which perfectly logical but emotionless Cybermen would simply do nothing at all. The upshot is that Cyberman 'logic' is incoherent — no theory of reason makes them rational.
- ch. 16 "They May Not Be Human, but Could Daleks Be People Too" **·** Aristotle, John Locke, Peter Singer — Speciesism — The chapter reads the Daleks' hunt for the 'human factor' in 'The Evil of the Daleks' and for human DNA in 'Dalek' and 'Daleks in Manhattan' as species essentialism — an Aristotelian picture of a fixed human nature, resurrected as genetic essentialism — set against philosophical alternatives of species as individuals or sets and Mayr's reproductive-isolation criterion applied to Daleks and Thals. It then asks whether the human-Dalek Dalek Sec could count as a person, using Singer's speciesism to argue that rights should track personhood (capacities for pain, intelligence) rather than bare membership in Homo sapiens.
- ch. 17 "Doomed to Be Perceived" **·** George Berkeley, John Locke, David Hume, William Paley — Subjective Idealism (esse est percipi); Scientific Realism vs Anti-Realism; Philosophical Skepticism; The Watchmaker Argument (design) — Smithka tests Berkeley's 'to be is to be perceived' against two monsters: the Vashta Nerada, whose existence is inferred from indirect evidence (counting shadows, the 'ghosting' of Miss Evangelista) much like Neptune was inferred from Uranus's orbit, and the Weeping Angels, which act only when unperceived and so falsify Berkeley's principle. Working through Locke on substance, Hume's skepticism about unperceivable causes like God, Paley's watchmaker design argument, and scientific realism (Sellars, Churchland) versus van Fraassen's empirically adequate anti-realism, she concludes the Doctor is a realist about unobservables — for whom the Angels are real but 'doomed to be perceived'.
- ch. 18 "Aww, . . . Poor Monster. Should We Feel Sorry for the Carrionites" **·** W.V.O. Quine, Plato — Scientific Realism vs Anti-Realism; Theory of Forms — Hewitt treats the Carrionites — word-wielding beings of 'The Shakespeare Code' who do science without numbers — as a test of Hartry Field's Science Without Numbers and hence of the Quinean indispensability argument for Platonism about mathematics: if numbers are unavoidable in our best physics, they exist. Surveying Field's Newtonian case, Balaguer's mathematics-free quantum mechanics, and the objections to each, he concludes that even Carrionite physics would not refute numbers — the Carrionites would merely lack our knowledge of them, so the poor monsters are missing out.
- ch. 19 "EPISODE 3 - It’s a Different Morality. Get Used to It, or Go Home!" *(front)*
- ch. 20 "“Howzat!” Prometheus Is a Fast Bowler" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Simone de Beauvoir, Ralph Waldo Emerson — The Ethics of Ambiguity; Existentialism; Bad Faith — Decker argues the Doctor's morality resists formula — his motives are private, his timey-wimey perspective complicates consequentialist calculation, and the 'Genesis of the Daleks' child hypothetical breaks rule theories like Kant's and Mill's — so he reads it as an 'ethics of ambiguity' fusing Romanticism (Shelley's imagination, the infinite glimpsed in cups of tea and small beautiful events, Romantic-Gothic divided consciousness) with Sartrean existentialism. Via Sartre's WWII student, bad faith, and Beauvoir's 'adventurer' contrasted with the sub-man and the serious man, he casts the Doctor as a Gallifreyan Prometheus whose gifts of freedom are both dreadful and delightful.
- ch. 21 "A Better Way of Living Your Life" **·** Nel Noddings, Martin Buber, Martha Nussbaum, Immanuel Kant — Caring as the Ground of Ethics; I–Thou Relation; Judgment of Taste and the Sublime — Akers argues the Doctor follows neither rule deontology, utilitarian calculation, nor virtue ethics, but a relational ethics of care in Noddings's and Slote's sense, expressed as Buber's I-Thou stance — insisting an enslaved Ood is 'a "he," not an "it"' and addressing Donna, Jack, and Rose each as individuals in 'Journey's End'. Linking this to Nussbaum's claim that wonder widens moral concern and to Kant's dynamic and mathematical sublime, she argues the Doctor needs human companions like Rose, Martha, and Donna as a human-scale reference point that renews his wonder — and with it his ethics — while his wiping of Donna's memory shows the care tradition's dangers of power imbalance.
- ch. 22 "Toward Caring" **·** Carol Gilligan, Nel Noddings — The Ethics of Care; Caring as the Ground of Ethics; Utilitarianism — Sylvia models the Doctor on feminist care ethics: Gilligan's response to Kohlberg's justice-tinged scales and Noddings's distinction between natural caring (for Rose, Martha, Peri in 'The Caves of Androzani') and ethical caring, which explains his forgiveness of the Master and compassion for Dalek Caan after genocides. Against utilitarian impartiality — which cannot say why he should save his companion over a stranger — the relationship itself grounds the obligation, while his vengeful moment facing the lone Dalek in 'Dalek' shows what caring burnout looks like.
- ch. 23 "Allons-y into the Starry Heavens" **·** Immanuel Kant, Plato, Aristotle — The Categorical Imperative; Deontology; Autonomy of the Will; Virtue Ethics (arete) — Smith reads the Doctor–Rose tragedy through Kantian deontology: shaped by Plato's four virtues and Aristotle's virtue ethics into a good will, both must still choose duty over inclination, so in 'Parting of the Ways' and the Battle of Canary Wharf in 'Doomsday' they each act from the categorical imperative — sacrificing their love to save humanity — because love, as Kant warns, impels partiality. Even in 'Journey's End' the Doctor sends Rose to Pete's World with the meta-crisis Doctor for the greater good, ending alone under Kant's starry heavens and the moral law within.
- ch. 24 "The Dalek Within" **·** Thomas Hobbes, Hannah Arendt, Theodor Adorno, Michel Foucault — The Banality of Evil; The State of Nature; The Culture Industry; Discipline and Docile Bodies — Comparing the Doctor's hesitation at the Dalek hatchery in 'Genesis of the Daleks' with the half-human Doctor's instant genocide in 'Journey's End', the authors argue the Daleks are mirrors of us: their survival-driven wars enact Hobbes's state of nature, while their industrial 'extermination' embodies Arendt's banality of evil and Weber's iron cage of instrumental rationality. Drawing on Horkheimer and Adorno's culture industry and Foucault's disciplinary institutions — modern techniques that mold the inner life as Davros's genetic engineering molds the Kaleds — they conclude the Dalek tendency is a human possibility, so we cannot exterminate the Daleks without indicting ourselves.
- ch. 25 "Why the Doctor Is So Special" **·** Peter Singer, Aristotle, Martha Nussbaum — Speciesism; Animal Liberation; Virtue Ethics (arete) — Applying Singer's animal-liberation concept of speciesism — judging moral worth by capacity to feel pain rather than by intelligence or likeness — the authors argue the Daleks are 'super-speciesist' and that Lumic's emotional inhibitors make the Cybermen the same failure, unable to share a universe with unlike beings. Since emotions are judgments that can be educated into fitting responses (Nussbaum, Aristotle), and since Daleks infused with Rose's or Diagoras's DNA collapse into empathetic beings, the chapter concludes that emotion and empathy are why the Daleks will never win and why the Doctor, marked by his companions, is special among Time Lords.
- ch. 26 "Two New Cyber-Approaches Enlightenment and a Good Ad-Campaign" **·** Immanuel Kant — Deontology; Utilitarianism; Autonomy of the Will; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Lewis defends the Cybermen: using the 'Genesis of the Daleks' dilemma he shows the Doctor reasons like a Kantian duty ethicist — refusing to kill innocent proto-Daleks whatever the consequences — rather than a utilitarian, and that performing bad actions does not make an agent evil. After the Mondas, Lumic, and Morrison origins establish that Cybermen seek to cure pain and death, he argues the Doctor's 'good to strive for but bad to achieve' argument in 'Age of Steel' is invalid, and that upgrading would be morally acceptable — an applied-ethics fix — if the Cybermen obtained informed consent and respected human autonomy instead of forcing conversions.
- ch. 27 "EPISODE 4 - Human Beings, You’re Amazing. Apart from That, You’re Completely Mad" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 28 "Here They Are ... Ready to Outsit Eternity" **·** Peter van Inwagen — Personal Identity; Personal Identity (memory theory); Immortality and the Soul — Argues that regeneration helps show life after death is logically possible: the successive Doctors (Fourth to Fifth in 'Logopolis') prove identity survives radical personality change, so a perfected heavenly Joe would still be Joe. Testing whether identity lies in body or memory, he contrasts John Smith in 'Human Nature' (same body, so same person) with the 'Journey's End' Doctor-clone whose replicated memories Rose rejects ('But he's not you'), favoring bodily continuity. This leaves the unavailability-of-atoms problem, which he solves with van Inwagen's theory that God removes and preserves our bodies at death like a divine cryonicist, echoing the Fourth Doctor's 'ready to outsit eternity' tribute in 'The Ark in Space'.
- ch. 29 "Love and Doctors" **·** John Locke, Saul Kripke, Derek Parfit, Thomas Reid — Personal Identity; Personal Identity (memory theory); Naming and Necessity (rigid designators); Philosophy of Love — Asks whether 'the Doctor' is a Kripkean rigid designator naming one individual or a non-rigid role filled by different people, using the Ninth Doctor's deathbed doubts and the Time Crash memory loop to settle on the rigid reading. He then deploys Locke's body/organism/person distinctions, Parfit's overlapping psychological-connectedness fix to Reid's transitivity objection (Sarah Jane across 1983-1996-2006), and the amnesia-prone regenerations and the Valeyard to show the organism survives regeneration while the person might not. Finally he argues that because DoctorTwo in 'Journey's End' shares all the Doctor's psychologically connected memories of Rose, fission makes them two persons with equal claim, so Rose gets the person she actually loved.
- ch. 30 "I Thought It Was Just the Doctor that We Needed, but It’s Both of You" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — The Übermensch; The Death of God — Reads the Doctor as a Christ-figure and savior (the 'Voyage of the Damned' ascension that drew Christian Voice protests, Martha's year of gospel-telling in 'The Last of the Time Lords'), applying Feuerbach's claim that humanity projects its ideals onto God. But the show under Russell T Davies also distrusts godlike power: Davros's 'you made this' accusation, the Racnoss massacre, and Joan Redfern's question in 'The Family of Blood' show the lonely god's devastation. Against this, companions like Donna embody Nietzsche's Übermensch ('There's no such thing as an ordinary human'), so the chapter concludes the Doctor and ordinary humans need each other, mutually overcoming their weaknesses.
- ch. 31 "Life A Celebration" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Gilles Deleuze — Ressentiment; The Übermensch; Will to Power; Master and Slave Morality — Reads John Simm's Master as Nietzsche's most advanced nihilist: having seen the universe die at Utopia, he judges life valueless and converts ressentiment into a joyful will to nothingness, dancing to the Scissor Sisters over his victims. The Ninth Doctor's arc in 'Dalek' mirrors this danger ('What are you changing into?') until he refuses to destroy the last Dalek, beginning his yes-saying to finite life. Following Deleuze's reading, the Doctor defeats the Master not with Martha's gun but with forgiveness ('I forgive you') and her storytelling network, embodying the Übermensch who carries his affirmation into the abyss of the end of the universe.
- ch. 32 "Escape to Doctor Who" **·** Arthur Schopenhauer, Immanuel Kant — The World as Will; Phenomena and Noumena; Philosophical Pessimism — Reads the Master as the personification of Schopenhauer's blind, pointless Will: the Doctor's line 'In many ways we have the same mind' is taken literally, so the Master is the dark noumenal side in everyone, seen in the hypnotized Farrel whose ambition the Master acts out and Colonel Trenchard whose patriotism becomes the Master's weapon in 'The Sea Devils'. Building from Kant's phenomena/noumena distinction, he explains the Will as the inaccessible force we glimpse through inner bodily awareness, evil because purposeless. Watching the Doctor's serial victories is then offered as a light, Schopenhauerian escape through art from the terror of the Will.
- ch. 33 "The Master The Slave of the Time-Lord Cunning of Reason" **·** G.W.F. Hegel — Master–Slave Dialectic; Struggle for Recognition; History Has a Direction — Uses Hegel's master-slave dialectic to diagnose the Master: his craving to be acknowledged as 'the Master' (Harold Saxon's dominion in 'The Sound of Drums', devouring humans in 'The End of Time') can only win the recognition of inferiors, which is worthless since genuine recognition must come from an equal. As a Hegelian world-historical individual driven by passion, he unknowingly serves the Time Lords' cunning of reason, the four drums being their implanted tool for escaping the time lock. The chapter ends with him finally attaining the slave-consciousness's independence by turning on and killing Rassilon, renouncing the recognition he can never get.
- ch. 34 "I Am the Bad Wolf" **·** Aristotle, Martin Heidegger — Virtue Ethics (arete); Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Authenticity — Answers Davros's charge that the Doctor fashions companions into weapons by examining his character through virtue ethics: his unifying virtue is Heideggerian 'authentic concern', always offering aggressors like the Nestene or the Sycorax a choice so they become genuinely responsible, as in the fly-swatting thought experiment. Companions instead act from compassion, which lacks impartiality and produces the 'Bad Wolf Syndrome' (Rose's Dalek genocide, Martha's Osterhagen Key, Jack's warp star). Since for Aristotle a friend is 'another himself', the companions' actions are in a sense the Doctor's own yet founded on a distorted disposition, which is why he runs from his past.
- ch. 35 "EPISODE 5 - Brilliant! Fantastic! Molto Bene!" *(front)*
- ch. 36 "One More Thing, Don’t Blink!" **·** Harry Frankfurt, Alasdair MacIntyre, Bernard Williams — Personal Identity; Second-Order Desires (personhood) — Uses Carroll's art-horror theory (monster = threat plus disgust) to ask why the Weeping Angels of 'Blink' terrify despite being beautiful, harmless-seeming statues. The answer: by dumping Kathy and Billy in the past they destroy identity itself, understood through Frankfurt's account of caring and MacIntyre's narrative identity, since everything a person cares about is tied to a time and place. Since the Angels provoke no disgust, the chapter revises Carroll: horror is fear plus impotence, and forcing victims to survive by not blinking makes viewers feel their powerlessness over their own bodies.
- ch. 37 "A Beautiful Destiny" **·** Immanuel Kant, G.W.F. Hegel, Plato — Judgment of Taste and the Sublime; Platonic Love (the ladder of love); Theory of Forms — Asks whether the Dalek casing can literally be beautiful, contrasting Kant's aesthetics (free beauty as the mind's fit with reality versus merely 'dependent' beauty of Davros-designed purpose) with Hegel's claim that beauty is thought embodied in matter, peaking in the human form, and Plato's view (via Nehamas's Phaedrus reading) that beauty begins the desire to care for a thing. Alexander of Hales's beauty-within-the-order and the Fourth Doctor's 'Genesis of the Daleks' echo, then Rosenkranz's aesthetic inferno where ugliness exists only as beauty's negation, show beauty and monstrosity need not be opposites. The conclusion is deliberately open: the puzzle forces us into metaphysics about mind and reality, not mere taste.
- ch. 38 "The Odd Thought of Doctor Who" **·** Gottfried Leibniz, René Descartes — Monadology; The Best of All Possible Worlds; Principle of Sufficient Reason; Methodological Doubt (evil demon) — Maps Leibniz's monadology onto the Whoniverse: the Doctor's individual concept survives each regeneration as a thinking monad while its bodily monads are replaced, parallel Liz Shaws in 'Inferno' show incompossible predicates, and the Time Lords play Leibniz's God permitting minimal evil, which the Doctor accepts in refusing to avert the Daleks' creation in 'Genesis of the Daleks'. The dimensionally transcendental TARDIS illustrates the principle of indiscernibles, the Matrix becomes a failed pre-established harmony haunted by confused perceptions, and the Master hallucinates against the Doctor like Descartes's evil demon. The Valeyard is modeled as an incompossible world on Leibniz's Sextus Tarquinius pyramid and Borges's forking paths, closing with Leibniz's own 'odd thought' academy of wonders.
- ch. 39 "EPISODE 6 - Lots of Planets Have a North!" *(front)*
- ch. 40 "Bunker Mentality or Love" **·** Richard Rorty, Socrates — Argues Terry Nation made the Daleks evil by inverting the Socrates-to-Kant tradition equating humanity with rationality: the Daleks of 'The Daleks' (1963) are hyper-rational but feelingless, unable to understand Susan's name or laughter, and Ian's verdict is simply 'They just aren't human'. The chapter situates them in the neutron-bomb panic of the Cuban Missile Crisis era (Skaro's undamaged city, stone trees) and a H.G. Wells lineage of evolution gone wrong (Martians, Morlocks), so the Daleks horrify as the emotionless future we might become after the bomb. Rorty's claim that moral progress comes through enlarged empathy, not reason, explains why Ian teaches the Thals goodness by appealing to love for their captured friend rather than argument.
- ch. 41 "The Doctor, Defender of British Dreams" *(no index entry yet)* — Argues the Doctor became Britain's national hero precisely at the moment of post-war imperial decline and decolonization: an anti-imperial Time Lord who still claims Time Lord identity, he personifies the country Britain hoped to be, in Fiske's phrase a mediator between god and man who wins by 'warm humane science' rather than superior force. The chapter traces this through Verity Lambert's anti-establishment design, Tom Baker's 'romantic wanderer', Romana as the ideal modern woman unfettered by gender roles, the Doctor's Big Brother-critiquing in 'The Sun-Makers' against Britain's own CCTV reality, and the spin-offs, concluding the show supplies a national mirror of hope and how Britain thinks it ought to be.
- ch. 42 "The Doctor Dances" **·** Theodor Adorno — Uses Vico's theory of myth (myths as communal rituals binding passion, decaying into the 'barbarism of reflection') to read the show's myth-versus-science serials: 'The Daemons' dismissively reduces myth to bad science, 'Meglos' shows the Deons' ritual nearly destroying Tigella, while 'Snakedance' shows ancient ritual wisdom, kept by Dojjen, defeating the Mara where decadent scholarship fails. Then Horkheimer and Adorno's Dialectic of Enlightenment grounds the deeper claim that science is itself a limiting myth, so the Doctor's 'laws of time' in 'The Waters of Mars' and 'The Fires of Pompeii' are self-imposed necessity that Donna can puncture. The show's counsel is balance: the Doctor keeps his science humane by respecting myth, dancing rather than shooting.
- ch. 43 "Ah, Doctor. We Know You by Reputation" **·** Socrates, Plato — The Unexamined Life; Socratic Method (elenchus) — Argues Doctor Who is philosophy's ally by way of Plato's Gorgias distinction between teaching, which produces justified true belief, and rhetoric, which wins by persuasion: the Master is the master rhetorician whose hypnotic manipulation manufactures unreliable beliefs, while the Doctor is the Socratic teacher who gives information and lets companions decide. Socrates's descriptions of philosophy (the good life, sense of wonder, love of real knowledge) are matched to the show's career as an educational program that teaches viewers to reflect on how they reason, how they treat 'others' like Daleks and Silurians, and to revise their views, that is, to live the examined life.
- ch. 44 "Would You Like a Jelly Baby" *(notes)*
- ch. 45 "The What, When, and Who of Doctor Who" *(notes)*
- ch. 46 "The High Council of Gallifrey" *(notes)*
- ch. 47 "The Matrix of Time Lord Bibliographic Sources" *(notes)*
- ch. 48 "Logically Organized Index, for the Cyberman and Dalek in All of Us" *(notes)*
- ch. 49 "Doctor Who and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 50 "Copyright Page" *(front)*

## Downton Abbey and Philosophy - The Truth Is Neither Here Nor There

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "i" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "iii" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "iv" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Copyright" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Of the Simple and Complex" **·** Plato, Confucius, Aristotle, David Hume — Li (ritual propriety); Theory of Forms; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — The editors' introduction argues that Downton's quaint domestic plots burst with universal moral and social meaning beneath their surface. Using the season 1 snuffbox incident, in which Thomas frames the innocent Bates for theft, it shows how the episode stages archetypal villainy and innocence akin to the universal figures of Plato's Republic and world mythology, so the 'simple' plot is morally clear rather than superficial. It then reads the hierarchy of Thomas, Bates, and Lord Grantham through Confucian propriety and Aristotelian justice, suggesting that justice partly consists in attention to 'greater and lesser,' something Western viewers raised on equality find foreign yet intriguing. Finally it insists that as concrete persons all three are morally mixed (Thomas takes a beating to spare Jimmy, Bates may have killed outside the law, Grantham is generous to tenants but cold to his daughters), and that this complexity is what rewards philosophical reflection, along with Hume's 'small morals' of etiquette.
- ch. 9 "I. Finding Your Place" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "1. Finding the Meaning of Life in Downton Abbey" **·** Susan Wolf, Immanuel Kant, Robert Nozick, Friedrich Nietzsche — Meaning in Life; The Meaning of Life; The Categorical Imperative; Authenticity — Blessing pits Steve Jobs's 'Do What You Love' creed (and Oprah's 'follow your bliss') against Downton's world of inherited roles, where nobody from Anna to Lady Mary chooses their work. She distinguishes happiness from meaningfulness, since Matthew's war wounds and Mary's grief over his death are painful yet deeply meaningful, and tests subjectivist theories such as Nietzsche's meaning-as-passionate-process against Nozick's demand that a meaningful life leave permanent traces and Susan Wolf's hybrid 'loving objects that are worthy of love.' Kant's duty to develop one's talents rather than idle for pleasure, and Lord Grantham's self-description as 'a custodian, not an owner,' anchor the show's counter-ethos of duty over desire, embodied in Carson, Mrs. Hughes, and Mrs. Patmore's pride in unloved, socially necessary work. The chapter concludes that Downton validates hard honest work as an end in itself, challenging the narcissism of the 'holy You' and expressive individualism.
- ch. 11 "2. I May Be a Socialist, But I’m Not a Lunatic" **·** Karl Marx — Class Struggle; Cultural Hegemony — Walderezak tracks Tom Branson's trajectory from Marxist-quoting socialist chauffeur to estate manager, arguing that the show quietly makes Robert, not Tom, the 'real socialist.' Robert's aristocratic conservatism is read as noblesse oblige (funding the hospital, the Downton Fair, and secretly paying a tenant's arrears), while Matthew's capitalist drive for 'investment, increasing productivity, and reducing waste' is presented as the profit-first ownership system that Marx and Fourier actually criticized. Tom's socialism, closer to the utopian visions of Robert Owen and Charles Fourier than to Marx's class struggle, dissolves as he assimilates into the family ('I haven't abandoned all of my socialism, although sometimes it feels like it'), a capitulation the chapter explains through David Harvey's 'capitalist pacification,' whereby capitalism absorbs all facets of society. The chapter concludes that Robert's treatment of ownership as a duty owed to tenants approaches the cooperative ideals of Fourier and Owen more than Tom's abandoned radicalism ever did.
- ch. 12 "3. Matthew’s Dark Side" **·** Philosophy of Love; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Wills deploys Georg Simmel's The Philosophy of Money, with its thesis that growing wealth corrodes individuality, to reread Matthew Crawley's courtship of Mary as corruption rather than romance: the man who vows Downton won't change him soon accepts Molesley's valeting, savors finding no grounds to challenge the entail, and returns to the fortune he claimed to renounce. He contrasts their 'relationship of convenience,' which conveniently solves the succession problem, with the penniless Anna and Bates courtship as the show's picture of love uncontaminated by money. The darker claim is that viewers are corrupted alongside Matthew, rooting for a marriage that serves the estate's economic structure, so the show indicts our own captivity to wealth.
- ch. 13 "4. Finding One’s Place and Being Useful" **·** Aristotle, Karl Marx, Adam Smith, Bertrand Russell, Jean-Jacques Rousseau — Eudaimonia (flourishing); Ideology and False Consciousness; The Invisible Hand — Karavitis argues that work, not social class, is Downton's real theme, sorting characters in a two-by-two matrix of class membership versus acceptance or rejection of the social order: the Dowager's 'we don't work' stagnation, Isobel and the Crawley sisters' redemptive projects, Molesley's grateful endurance, and the escapes of Gwen, Alfred, and Branson. The Anna scene, in which she answers her attacker Mr. Green with trained servant courtesy moments after the assault, shows how work 'becomes' us, while the alphabetical ordering of the opening credits suggests that only fulfilling work, not class position, matters. Rousseau, Adam Smith, and Marx frame the negative view of work (agriculture breeding inequality, the pin factory's deadening division of labor, wage exploitation and ideology), against which the chapter endorses Aristotle's pleasures of play and leisure, Russell's 'In Praise of Idleness,' and Pieper, predicting that automation will soon force everyone to confront what leisure is for.
- ch. 14 "II. Minding Manners and Making Do" *(front)*
- ch. 15 "5. The Morality of Manners" **·** Aristotle — Virtue Ethics (arete); Respect for Persons — Dougherty argues that in Downton manners are not incidental etiquette but a central means of demonstrating and cultivating moral character, above all respect for persons, though they can also mask its absence. Matthew's growth from calling valeting 'a very silly occupation' to honoring Molesley's dignity, and Branson's journey from tweed defiance ('uniforms of oppression') to worrying that a tuxedo is insufficient for the Dowager's dinner, show manners tracking their growing respect for the Crawleys as particular individuals. The chapter anchors this in Aristotle's person of visible virtue, Nancy Sherman's 'aesthetic of character,' and Sarah Buss's claim that manners constantly remind us that others are worthy of respect, while Sir Richard's boorishness and the Dowager Countess's weaponized courtesy show manners failing or serving self-interest. Mrs. Crawley's etiquette-breaking kindness, praised even by Violet, complicates the picture by showing care can be morally outrun by propriety.
- ch. 16 "6. Keeping It under Control" **·** Epictetus — The Dichotomy of Control; Preferred Indifferents; Stoic Cosmopolitanism (citizens of the world); Stoicism — Mills reads Mr. Bates, the lame, twice-imprisoned, uncomplaining valet, as Downton's Epictetus, using him and the household to teach the Stoic dichotomy of control: only our judgments, desires, and actions are up to us, while jobs, reputations, and inheritances (Thomas's coveted valet post, Mary's scandal, Molesley's flower cup) are 'preferred indifferents' to be pursued without enslavement. Death figures as the great rejected indifferent, handled through Pamuk's demise and Mrs. Hughes's calm 'One day, I will die' upon finding a lump, as well as the grief of Isobel and Mary over Matthew. The closing section turns to Hierocles's concentric circles, with Sybil helping Gwen and Carson comforting Mary as drawing outsiders into the inner circle, a Stoic cosmopolitanism that Thomas, who sees the Crawleys as mere employers, fails entirely. The Dowager's 'I hope we're in control of something, if only ourselves' serves as the chapter's Stoic motto.
- ch. 17 "7. Disabled at Downton" **·** Philosophy of Disability — Richmond argues that Downton's portrayal of disability is morally mixed. Bates is a positive, realistic representation: hired despite O'Brien and Thomas's ableism, rejecting the painful leg brace, loved by and married to Anna in defiance of period stereotypes, and deliberately not a 'super-crip' whose disability is erased by technology or triumph. Matthew's arc, by contrast, is harmful: he pushes away Lavinia and Mary ('Think of me as dead') and is then rescued by the miracle cure of Dr. Clarkson's misdiagnosis, a plot the chapter, drawing on Paul Longmore's disability-studies mandate and the medical versus social models of disability, says teaches disabled viewers their lives need medical erasure to matter, prompting the plea for 'more Bateses and fewer Matthews.'
- ch. 18 "III. What Can We Know" *(front)*
- ch. 19 "8. Lie Is So Unmusical a Word" **·** Immanuel Kant — The Categorical Imperative; Deontology — Neal weighs Dr. Clarkson's coached retraction, that surgery would not have saved Sybil after all, engineered by Violet with 'Lie is so unmusical a word' to end Cora's blame of Robert and reunite the grieving couple, against Kant's absolute prohibition on lying in his moral works and in 'On a Supposed Right to Lie.' She lays out Kant's reasoning that lying destroys the trust, dignity, and rights that make rules binding, with the murderer-at-the-door case as the classic objection, then argues Clarkson's lie approaches an exception because it is rational, carefully deliberated, and aimed at preserving the Crawleys' dignity and the household itself rather than personal gain. The chapter concludes that the Kantian rule still holds generally, but this particular lie, for this particular moral reason, can be excused.
- ch. 20 "9. Why Did Lady Sybil Die" **·** Charles Sanders Peirce — Egg uses the Clarkson versus Tapsell disagreement over Sybil's pre-eclampsia to teach inference to the best explanation, distinguishing deduction (Carson's syllogism about the Australian singer Dame Nellie), induction (Edith's gloomy generalization from her failed suitors), and Peirce's abduction, evaluated via Peter Lipton's twin criteria of likeliness and loveliness. Daisy's numerology linking the Titanic to Pamuk's death and Violet's inference that Edith's Swiss trip hides a pregnancy show loveliness cutting both ways, while Sybil's death tragically vindicates Clarkson's lovelier but less likely diagnosis. The darkest turn is Violet's second-order manipulation: once 'eclampsia plus Robert's misjudgment' threatens her son's marriage, she gets Clarkson to re-weigh the statistics so that 'eclampsia alone' becomes the best explanation of Sybil's death, showing that the best explanation can be engineered.
- ch. 21 "10. Does Bates Know Who Raped Anna" **·** Plato — The Gettier Problem; Reliabilism — The Nisleys ask whether Bates's conviction that Mr. Green raped Anna counts as knowledge, starting from the Theaetetus's definition of knowledge as justified true belief. Bates's belief is true, but his evidential position is compromised: Mrs. Hughes, though genuinely reliable, gives him false testimony pointing to a stranger, Anna denies it was Green, and his own grounds (Green's flirting and Green's admission that he left the concert) are too weak, so the truth of his belief is largely coincidental, exactly the Gettier predicament that true belief is not sufficient for knowledge. The chapter applies reliabilist reasoning about testimony to conclude that Bates 'might have suspected, but he did not know.'
- ch. 22 "11. What Mary Didn’t Know" **·** Daniel Dennett — The Intentional Stance — Demeter argues that Mary's long hesitation over Matthew is radically underdetermined, sketching five rival interpretations of the same first-season events: emotional uncertainty, greed, fear of scandal over the Pamuk affair, bad conscience, and conflicting family pressure, each consistent with her behavior yet mutually exclusive. Via Frayn's play Copenhagen and the quantum measurement analogy he first treats this as a limit on human knowledge, then, using Dennett's 'Real Patterns' and Henry James's The Golden Bowl, defends the stronger metaphysical claim that there is no fact of the matter: psychological interpretation creates the very patterns it claims to find, so even Mary cannot know her own motives. What Mary didn't know may, on this view, be unknowable in principle.
- ch. 23 "IV. Change and Memory" *(front)*
- ch. 24 "12. Should We Really Care about the Crawleys" **·** Class Struggle; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Jones takes on the charge, voiced amid rising real-world inequality, that loving Downton seduces viewers into admiring hierarchy, and answers that the Crawleys deserve our care because they are human beings who share our bereavements (Mary's grief for Matthew, Cora's miscarriage, Edith's jilting) and because they use moments of cross-class contact well: Grantham paying for Mrs. Patmore's eye surgery, Violet throwing the flower contest at Isobel's urging, and the family's genuine embrace of Tom Branson. He reads the show as a manifesto for gradual social change through sympathetic contact rather than the Irish revolutionaries' arson, but ends with a serious objection: this philosophy makes ending oppression partly the duty of the oppressed, from Carson's 'foul' remark about Thomas's sexuality to today's super-rich, so more radical activism may still be required.
- ch. 25 "13. Topophilia; or, How We Got Hooked on Downton" **·** Gilles Deleuze, Carl Jung — Phenomenology — Elvis explains Downton's global appeal through Gaston Bachelard's The Poetics of Space: topophilia, the intense love of place, and topoanalysis of intimate spaces such as the opening shutters, the servant bell's shell-like curl, the letter rack, and the watched pot, all of which invite daydream and fuse our recollections with Bachelard's ideal 'future house.' She borrows Deleuze's haptic, where one sense answers to another, to show how shots like light flooding through the shutters and the flickering lamp draw viewers bodily into the Abbey, and uses the psychoanalytic interplay of conscious and unconscious, introduced through Jung, to explain why images we have never seen feel like home. The chapter's claim is that we are hooked not by the Abbey's grandeur but by small intimate spaces that bring ourselves home.
- ch. 26 "14. Things Can Happen that Nobody Could Imagine Only a Few Years Ago" **·** G.W.F. Hegel — The Dialectic; History Has a Direction; Geist (World Spirit); Deontology — Donna Marie Smith reads the series through Hegel's philosophy of history: the Crawleys' world is a clash of opposing forces - Lord Grantham's traditionalism against Matthew's and Branson's modernization, Violet's fear of electricity against Sybil's and Rose's embrace of the new - and this very conflict drives progress toward greater freedom. She uses Hegel's Elements of the Philosophy of Right both to explain the entail and primogeniture that bar Mary from inheriting (the law preserves the 'house' over the immediate family, which Hegel calls an infringement of the freedom of property) and to frame Robert's refusal to challenge it as duty to his social standing outweighing personal wishes. WWI, which Hegel experienced through Napoleon's occupation of Jena, is presented as a 'march toward freedom' that breaks down class barriers and finally pushes Robert to accept change on the estate: 'Let's give it a go and see what the future brings.'
- ch. 27 "15. Memories. In the End, Is That All There Is" **·** Augustine of Hippo, Cicero — Personal Identity; Personal Identity (memory theory) — David LaRocca takes Mr. Carson's confession to Mrs. Hughes - 'The business of life is the acquisition of memories. In the end, that's all there is' - and asks whether memory really is a possession that can be collected. He traces the spatial metaphors Carson's view rests on: the classical memory palace of Cicero and the Ad Herennium, Augustine's 'huge court of memory' from the Confessions, and Downton itself as a haunted storehouse where photographs of the dead Sybil and Carson's lost Alice Neal keep trauma alive rather than heal it. Against this acquisitional model he sets contemporary neuroscience (Daniella Schiller's reconsolidation research), which shows memories are rewritten rather than collected, undermining both Carson's nostalgia and the idea that personal identity is a fixed archive of the past.
- ch. 28 "16. Female Integrity in Times of Change" **·** Bernard Williams, Immanuel Kant — Integrity Against Utilitarianism; Feminism; Deontology — Melanie Abbott tests Bernard Williams's account of integrity - publicly and consistently standing up for the beliefs that make you you - merged with Kant's claim that only a good will is good without limitation, against five Crawley women across three generations. Sybil elopes with the socialist chauffeur Branson and never wavers in her egalitarian convictions, Edith's secrecy about her baby fails Williams's openness condition, Mary grows from custom-bound heiress into an estate manager who stands up to Robert over selling land, Cora chafes at the voice she surrendered as an American heiress, and even Violet eventually bends. The chapter's guiding question is how much truth and openness integrity requires, and it credits 'the Sybil effect' with spreading proper integrity through the whole family.
- ch. 29 "17. Downton Abbey as Eternal Recurrence" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Immanuel Kant — Eternal Recurrence; Master and Slave Morality; Amor Fati (love of fate); The Death of God — Christopher Ketcham provocatively equates primogeniture and entail - the legal machinery that returns Downton intact to each generation - with Nietzsche's 'greatest weight,' the eternal recurrence. Working through Lawrence Hatab's five interpretations of the doctrine, he asks whether the Abbey's continuity is cosmological (it is not, since estate taxes and squandering heirs keep chipping away), a Zarathustra-style life-affirming yes to the agrarian world against the 'nihilism' of capitalist fashion, or merely symbolic like a listing in Burke's Peerage beside fossilized dinosaur bones. His answer comes via Violet, the family, and the servants, who have 'become one with the property': the Abbey recurs only anthropomorphically, and even that stickiness is dissolving under industrialization, the death tax, and the war that killed off three royal cousins' empires.
- ch. 30 "V. War" *(front)*
- ch. 31 "18. Wondering What It’s All For" **·** The Problem of Evil; Just War Theory — Trip McCrossin follows Lord Grantham's 'We've dreamed a dream' - an allusion to Nebuchadnezzar in Daniel 2 - through the warfare story line of seasons 2 and 3, from Matthew and William missing in action to Matthew's death under a truck. Mr. Lang's shell-shocked confrontations with the eager volunteer William and with Mrs. Patmore over her executed nephew Archie dramatize the gulf between those who know what war is like and those who can only imagine it, while the meaningless deaths of Matthew and the stretcher-bearer who is shot mid-sentence raise the Job-like problem of evil in the Dowager's words, 'we don't always get our just deserts.' The chapter argues against complacency about warfare's inevitability: because total war is dreamed up by the few, the rest of us should 'band together as Daniel,' interpret those dreams in advance, and refuse to let them come to be.
- ch. 32 "19. Of Downton and Drones" **·** Just War Theory; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Jai Galliott contrasts Downton's WWI, where aristocrats like Matthew died in the trenches alongside footmen like William on a roughly level playing field, with today's drone age, in which the fighting burden falls on the lower and middle classes (future Reaper pilots recruited from the 'PlayStation generation') while risk to our own side approaches zero. He traces unmanned weapons from WWI tele-operated motorboats, the Land Torpedo, and the Kettering Bug back through Agincourt's longbows and Omdurman's machine guns to the Gulf War and Kosovo, and argues that when technological asymmetry becomes extreme, Just War Theory breaks down: such war can hardly be a last resort, strains the proportionality calculus, and violates the chivalric justice of treating an equal as an equal (via Paul Kahn's paradox of riskless warfare and Robert Sparrow's threat-based immunity). His proposal is that highly asymmetric force be applied with restraint, more like international policing than war.
- ch. 33 "20. Masters and Servants and Soldiers" **·** Class Struggle — Carl Sobocinski asks whether WWI caused Downton's social upheavals or merely accelerated trends already underway, using the philosopher of history's problem of separating change from continuity. The Boer War bond between Lord Grantham and his former army 'batman' Bates displays the constancy of noblesse oblige beneath the class system, while Thomas Barrow wielding authority over his social superiors at the convalescent home, Sybil's hands-on nursing, and the socialist chauffeur Branson marrying into the aristocracy show genuine change - though he argues most of it (mass recruiting, field commissions for commoners) enlarged pre-1914 trends. Closing with The Clash's 'Something about England,' he notes the same era that transformed the Crawleys' world left the servant class's life largely unchanged, with change ultimately coming out on top.
- ch. 34 "VI. Love and Belief" *(front)*
- ch. 35 "21. God-Talk at the Abbey" **·** Thomas Aquinas, William Paley — The Five Ways; Faith and Reason; The Watchmaker Argument (design) — Samuel Klumpenhouwer uses the Abbey's awkward silences about religion - Anna refusing to discuss it over dinner, Thomas showing off with 'transubstantiation' - to introduce Aquinas on how God can be known and talked about. Mary's wartime bedside prayer ('I'm not even sure that you're there') shows she wants not just proof of existence but a personal God, so the chapter distinguishes Aquinas's argument from design - carefully separated from Paley's divine-watchmaker version - from knowledge of God through revelation and faith. Aquinas's two indirect ways of speaking, analogy (Matthew calling God 'Father' at Lavinia's grave) and negation, explain why the 'God is good' sign over Charles Grigg's workhouse should trouble us, and why Carson's 'it is not our place to reason why' about Sybil's death overstates the mystery of God's otherness.
- ch. 36 "22. Lady Edith and the Trials of the Modern Woman" **·** Judith Jarvis Thomson, Peter Singer — The Violinist Argument; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Gordon Hawkes uses Lady Edith's last-minute walk-out from the abortion clinic (4:7) to run the full philosophical abortion debate. He lays out the classic anti-abortion argument and the biological-continuity case that 'Marigold once was an embryo,' then presents Mary Anne Warren's functional personhood criteria, which - as with Singer and Tooley on infanticide - would deny rights even to newborn George, a consequence he rejects via the comatose Matthew and the sleeping Alfred's retained capacities. Judith Jarvis Thomson's violinist argument is recast as Edith waking back-to-back with the ailing Dame Nellie Melba, and he answers it with the Law of Special Beneficence: if the feckless Major Bryant owes support to the child he never wanted, Edith owes at least as much to her own flesh and blood.
- ch. 37 "23. Justice in Love" **·** Plato — Philosophy of Love; Justice in the Republic; Forgiveness — Adam Barkman and Noah van Brenk apply C.S. Lewis's four loves to Downton, arguing that every love requires Platonic justice - each thing treated according to its role, as in the servants' 'My Lord' hierarchy and Bates's equal right to a fair trial - but that agape, sacrificial love, satisfies justice and then transcends it by loving the unlovable. Anna and Bates's forged thirty-pound note for the destitute Molesley, Bates sparing his hated enemy Thomas from prison, and Lavinia refusing Matthew's 'think of me as dead' dismissal are true agape, while Vera Bates's possessive blackmail and Thomas's scheme against the nanny merely ape it. The upshot is Lord Grantham's line to Alfred: do not abandon your beliefs, 'just introduce a little kindness into the equation' - justice tempered by mercy in both directions.
- ch. 38 "24. Climbing the Ladder of Love" **·** Plato, Socrates — Platonic Love (the ladder of love); Philosophy of Love — Brendan Shea reads Downton's romances through each speech of Plato's Symposium in turn. Phaedrus's love-inspired heroism explains Thomas taking a beating to save Jimmy; Pausanias's contrast of common and heavenly love maps onto Mary learning to work within social rules rather than around them (Pamuk versus Matthew); Eryximachus's orderly love is Carson and Mrs. Hughes's skilled, harmonious running of the house; Aristophanes's severed halves fit Anna and Bates merging their lives through prison and rape; and Agathon's naive praise diagnoses Edith's misdirected pursuit of love. Finally Socrates's ladder of love - desire for immortality ascending from one beautiful beloved to moral beauty itself - is played out by Tom and Sybil, whose romance leads him from chauffeur to estate manager and her from fashionable life to nursing, exactly as the ladder predicts.
- ch. 39 "References" *(notes)*
- ch. 40 "Household Staff" *(notes)*
- ch. 41 "Index" *(notes)*

## Downton Abbey and Philosophy - Thinking in That Manor

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "i" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "iii" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "iv" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Copyright" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Of the Simple and Complex" **·** Confucius, Aristotle, David Hume, Plato, Immanuel Kant — Li (ritual propriety); Theory of Forms — The book's introduction argues that Downton Abbey's genius is letting universal moral and social realities burst forth in particular incidents: the snuffbox affair makes Thomas a classic villain (stealing, defaming Bates) and Bates a Christ-, Socrates-, or Confucius-type innocent, showing that moral clarity is beautiful rather than superficial. It reads the Thomas-Bates-Grantham hierarchy as an invitation to think about 'greater and lesser,' tying propriety (Confucius), dharma, and justice (Aristotle) to paying attention to social difference, which Western viewers raised on equality find foreign yet intriguing. It closes by insisting the characters are more complex than their types - Thomas takes a beating to spare Jimmy, Bates may have killed outside the law - and previews the volume's themes of Humean 'small morals' (etiquette), women's rights, work, place, and war.
- ch. 9 "I. Finding Your Place" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "1. Finding the Meaning of Life in Downton Abbey" **·** Susan Wolf, Robert Nozick, Friedrich Nietzsche, Immanuel Kant — Meaning in Life; The Meaning of Life; The Categorical Imperative — Blessing tests the Steve Jobs/Oprah 'do what you love' creed against Downton, where rigid class structure assigns nearly everyone a role they did not choose. She distinguishes happiness from meaningfulness (Matthew's war wounds, Mary's grief after his death) and purposefulness (Grantham's custodian speech), then weighs subjectivist theories of meaning, including Nietzsche's idea that meaning arises from the passionate process itself, against Nozick's permanence view and Susan Wolf's hybrid 'subjective attraction meets objective attractiveness,' adding Kant's duty to develop one's talents against a world of idle pleasure. She concludes that the Abbey's ethos - Grantham as custodian not owner, Carson's pride in service, Patmore's kitchen, 'I have no right to be unhappy' - shows that socially necessary work one does not love can still make a meaningful life, while DWYL is elitist and narcissistic.
- ch. 11 "2. I May Be a Socialist, But I’m Not a Lunatic" **·** Karl Marx — Class Struggle — Walderczak stages the Branson-Robert-Matthew triangle as socialism, aristocratic conservatism, and capitalism colliding over the estate's duty to its people. He tracks how Tom's Marx-flavored socialism - really closer to Owen's and Fourier's utopian cooperatism - evaporates once he marries Sybil and co-manages the land, the chauffeur 'Branson' becoming family-member 'Tom,' a case of David Harvey's capitalist pacification. Meanwhile Robert's noblesse oblige - secretly paying a tenant's overdue rent from private funds, ranting 'profits, profits, profits,' treating ownership as custodianship of the tenants - leaves the author asking who the real socialist is, and answering that Robert's conservatism has far more in common with socialist ideals than Tom's self-confessed politics ever did.
- ch. 12 "3. Matthew’s Dark Side" **·** Philosophy of Love — Wills uses Georg Simmel's The Philosophy of Money to argue Matthew's two-season arc is a corruption story rather than a romance: the principled middle-class lawyer who vows Downton will not change him, calls valeting 'a very silly occupation,' and wants to dress himself gradually lets wealth enchant him, dodges reviewing the entail, and returns for Mary. He reads the Mary-Matthew match as an economic transaction that neatly solves the succession problem - money as the aristocracy's toy, Mary becoming part of the deal - against Anna and Bates's money-free courtship as the show's only genuinely loving relationship. The chapter ends by turning Simmel's warning on the audience: we root for the couple because we are as powerless before wealth's charm, and as greedy for it, as Matthew is.
- ch. 13 "4. Finding One’s Place and Being Useful" **·** Aristotle, Bertrand Russell, Adam Smith, Karl Marx, Jean-Jacques Rousseau — Eudaimonia (flourishing); Ideology and False Consciousness; Class Struggle — Karavitis argues that work, not social class, is Downton's real theme, sorting characters in a two-by-two matrix of class membership against acceptance or rejection of the existing social order. Those who accept their place stagnate - Violet ('We don't work,' 'What is a weekend?'), Robert the mismanaging custodian, the perpetually jobless Molesley - while those who reject it flourish: Isobel's drive to be 'useful,' Sybil's nursing ('I feel useful for the first time in my life'), Gwen's hidden typewriter, Alfred's Ritz training. He surveys Rousseau on work's role in inequality, Smith on the division of labor, and Marx on labor and ideology on the negative side, and Aristotle, Russell's 'In Praise of Idleness,' and Pieper on leisure and personal projects on the positive side, ending with the prediction that automation will soon give everyone the Crawley daughters' problem of what to do with a life without work.
- ch. 14 "II. Minding Manners and Making Do" *(front)*
- ch. 15 "5. The Morality of Manners" **·** Aristotle — Virtue Ethics (arete); Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Dougherty argues that at Downton manners are not incidental etiquette but a central means of demonstrating moral character and respect. Matthew's shift from calling valeting 'a very silly occupation for a grown man' to valuing Molesley's role shows decorum tracking his growing respect; Branson's tweed suit at white-tie dinners and 'uniforms of oppression' display disrespect even where his politics are sound; Sir Richard's parting boorishness betrays a lack of moral character; and the Dowager Countess's polished barbs prove manners can serve as a mask for self-interest rather than virtue. The framework combines Nancy Sherman's 'aesthetic of character,' Aristotle's person of extraordinary virtue whose goodness must appear in particular ways, and Sarah Buss's idea that good manners constantly remind us that others are worthy of respect.
- ch. 16 "6. Keeping It under Control" **·** Epictetus — The Dichotomy of Control; Preferred Indifferents; Stoic Cosmopolitanism (citizens of the world) — Mills presents Bates - lame leg, repeated prison spells, the public fall when O'Brien kicks his cane - as an Epictetan Stoic who 'manages' misfortune by confining his concern to what he controls, echoing the Dowager's 'I hope we're in control of something, if only ourselves.' The chapter explains the dichotomy of control and preferred indifferents through Thomas's enslaved pursuit of the valet post, O'Brien's soap-on-the-floor scheme, Mary's panicked engagement to Richard Carlisle, and Mrs. Hughes's proportioning of her cancer scare, and treats death as the great rejected indifferent via Pamuk, Sybil, and Matthew. It closes with Hierocles' concentric circles: the household's best moments (Sybil helping Gwen, the staff rallying to free Bates) collapse upstairs and downstairs into one family, while Thomas's closed inner circle marks his failure of Stoic virtue.
- ch. 17 "7. Disabled at Downton" **·** Philosophy of Disability — Richmond weighs Downton's disability portrayals against disability-studies standards, citing Paul Longmore on media images and the 'super-crip' stereotype. Bates is the positive case: he endures Thomas's and O'Brien's ability-based discrimination, throws away the painful leg brace and rejects the idea that disability demands a medical cure, accepts disability as identity ('We all carry scars, Mr. Bates'), and marries Anna, refuting the era's stereotype that disabled people cannot be employees or lovers. Matthew is the harmful case: his paraplegia ends in Dr. Clarkson's 'spinal shock' misdiagnosis - a miracle cure - and talk of a 'normal life,' reinforcing the medical model and teaching real disabled viewers that their lives are over, so the author wants 'more Bateses, and fewer Matthews.'
- ch. 18 "III. What Can We Know" *(front)*
- ch. 19 "8. Lie Is So Unmusical a Word" **·** Immanuel Kant — The Categorical Imperative; Deontology — Neal pits Kant's absolute prohibition on lying against Violet's scheme after Sybil's death: the Dowager pressures Dr. Clarkson to 'review the evidence' until he tells Cora and Robert that surgery could not have saved their daughter, reuniting the marriage his honest second opinion had broken. Working through the Categorical Imperative, the murderer-at-the-door case, and Kant's essay 'On a Supposed Right to Lie Because of Philanthropic Concerns,' the chapter asks whether Clarkson actually lied ('Lie is so unmusical a word'). It concludes that his rational, carefully planned, dignity-respecting lie to save the family meets the criteria for a Kantian exception, so the rule against lying still holds while this particular lie is morally permitted.
- ch. 20 "9. Why Did Lady Sybil Die" **·** Charles Sanders Peirce — Egg uses the fatal Clarkson-Tapsell conflict over Sybil's death to teach Peirce's abduction - inference to the best explanation - organized around Peter Lipton's twin criteria of likeliness and loveliness. He illustrates with Anna eliminating the burglar story of Vera Bates's death, Daisy's too-lovely coincidence theory linking the house's deaths, Violet correctly inferring that Edith's Swiss trip must hide a pregnancy because it alone explains all the facts, and the two doctors' contested readings of pre-eclampsia.
