- ch. 25 "Philosopher of the Dangerous Perhaps" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Hannah Arendt — The Übermensch; Eternal Recurrence; The Last Man; The Banality of Evil — Argues that Herbert is a genuine philosopher extending Nietzsche's Zarathustra into the twentieth century, via three narrative parallels: the Preacher entering Arrakeen mirrors Zarathustra's first down-going into the town with its tightrope walker and mocking buffoon; Paul's empire of water-fat Fremen and Alia's orthodoxy realizes the 'Ultimate Man' whom the crowd begs for instead of the Superman; and prescience reworks eternal recurrence's gateway 'Moment,' where every timeline ends in Krazilec. Reading Paul's sixty-five-billion-death jihad through Arendt's banality of evil and Herbert's 'superheroes are disastrous' thesis, the chapter concludes Paul is the failed tightrope walker and Leto II the goad—'not lightning, but lightning rod'—pushing humanity beyond man toward the Superman, with Spannungsbogen naming the tension of the drawn bow between animal and Overman.
- ch. 26 "In The Land of the Blind, the One-Eyed Man . ." **·** Fate and Foreknowledge; Free Will vs Determinism — Continues the earlier defense of oracular freedom ('Curse of the Golden Path'): because an oracle's gaze from a present moment fixes the very set of future possibilities, Paul and Leto are free precisely where they are blind—above all in their 'vision-battle' in the Tanzerouft, which no oracle can foresee, and which Paul's single whispered 'Don't' concedes to his son's Golden Path. Leto's breeding of Siona, invisible to prescience, is read as his deliberate self-blinding—'the price of freedom is an invigilance of eternity'—and the whole affair vindicates heroism as steering probabilities rather than escaping fate. Paul emerges as a tragic hero in the ancient Greek sense, whose hamartia is his all-too-human refusal to become a monster to save the future.
- ch. 27 "Appendixes" *(front)*
- ch. 28 "References" *(notes)*
- ch. 29 "The Almanak en-Ashraf" *(notes)*
- ch. 30 "Killing and Healing Words" *(notes)*
- ch. 31 "part0030" *(front)*
- ch. 32 "Copyright Page" *(front)*

## Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy - Raiding the Temple of Wisdom

- ch. 1 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein — Language Games — The editors' introduction retells two origin stories of D&D: Gygax and Arneson extending Tolkien fandom and 1970s tabletop wargames, and Gygax's childhood friend Jim Rausch staging live make-believe spy games. It asks whether D&D is a wargame, an art form, or something that resists definition, invoking Wittgenstein's claim that 'game' covers too diverse a range of activities to be defined and Huizinga's Homo Ludens on play standing outside truth and falsity, good and evil. It then frames the volume's guiding questions: whether basement role-playing is mere escapism or a proving ground for ideas, whether players invent new moralities for strange worlds, and what standard of value 'good game' invokes.
- ch. 8 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle — Eudaimonia (flourishing); Virtue Ethics (arete) — Littmann answers the charge that D&D is a waste of time with Aristotle: pleasure is good as an end in itself, humanity's function is thinking, so even munchkin power-gaming and 4E combat roles are worthwhile intellectual activity under the function argument. He then treats D&D as participatory art—improvisational storytelling offering catharsis (smacking goblins to purge aggression) and the Poetics' license to explore what could be, like the dwarf chieftain grieving a disintegrated son or deciding what a friend owes the thief in the scorpion nest. A good DM, on this Aristotelian view, makes plots unfold with internal probability and characters act from consistent personality, yet 'just playing my character' never excuses ruining another player's fun, since artistic value never suspends the virtues governing how we treat people.
- ch. 10 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle — Virtue Ethics (arete); Practical Wisdom (phronesis) — Merli argues that good role-playing refutes Aristotelian virtue ethics: decent players convincingly play genuinely evil thieves and morally mediocre players play truly virtuous paladins, so the skill of judging what the virtuous agent would do cannot require being virtuous or having the habituated emotions McDowell, Hursthouse, and Jacobson say are necessary to 'see what to do'. Using a dice-guessing versus unverifiable aura-reading analogy, he shows that the virtue ethicist's escape—denying non-virtuous players can get it right—makes virtuous judgment look fraudulent and untestable. Because role-playing demands active first-person deliberation rather than the passive reception of literary fiction, it exposes that judging rightly and being virtuous are distinct skills, with implications for how habituation and moral education work.
- ch. 11 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, John Stuart Mill, Hannah Arendt — The Categorical Imperative; The Greatest Happiness Principle; The Banality of Evil — Cogburn protests Fourth Edition's contraction of the nine alignments and rebuilds the system philosophically: Lawful means willing universalizable law in Kant's categorical-imperative sense (the liar and murderer treating themselves as exceptions), Good means willing the greatest happiness in Mill's utilitarian sense, yielding a nine-box grid of moral psychologies. He populates it with fantasy archetypes—Haomane Firstborn as pathological Lawful Neutral, Karsa Orlong as Chaotic Neutral, Abercrombie's torturer Glotka as Neutral Neutral in Hannah Arendt's 'banality of evil' mold, hierarchic vampires and cynical politicians as Lawful Evil, Conan's raiders as Chaotic Evil. On this scheme Chaotic Good is genuinely empty, Drizzt and Robin Hood types are really Lawful or Neutral Good, and a Neutral Good versus Lawful Good dispute over killing one innocent to save thousands shows how gameplay can stage classic ethics thought experiments.
- ch. 12 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, John Stuart Mill, Jeremy Bentham — Humanity as an End in Itself; The Greatest Happiness Principle; The Hedonic Calculus — Bateman replies to Cogburn that Law and Chaos descend not from Kant but from Michael Moorcock's Eternal Champion stories, where they are liberal and conservative political tendencies held in check by the Cosmic Balance, which restores Chaotic Good, Lawful Evil, and the Druid's true Neutral as viable positions. He rejects the 'sausage machine' universalization of maxims for Kant's humanity formula and Realm of Ends, and argues act utilitarianism makes a poor model of Good: an overpopulation scenario could mandate genocide, and Bentham's felicific calculus cannot actually measure the good. Alain Badiou's ethic of truths supplies the positive case for Chaotic Good—pursuing what you take to be good without submitting to a single moral law—while Buddhist and Jain renunciation dignifies Lawful Neutral.
- ch. 13 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Nagel, René Descartes — Personal Identity — Silcox recalls his middle-school Paladin dying to eighty-three orcs while his friends held a memorial service, and asks whether grief for a player character is irrational. He tests Charles Taylor's narrative ethics—the thesis that we grasp our lives as unfolding stories—finding it challenged by Beckett's Godot, the novel-within-a-novel of Atonement, and Samantha Vice's warnings about story-shaped lives, and he notes leveling up and 4E's heroic PCs make characters story-like in ways ordinary lives are not. Ultimately PC death differs from real death because the campaign's story continues without the character, whereas death, as Nagel argues, cuts off our open-ended future; Silcox ends by recasting the self as a 'back-story' or metanarrative that enables storytelling, rejecting Descartes' claim that the power of imagination is inessential to our essence.
- ch. 14 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** David Hume — Dadlez recounts playing an evil Romulan spy who befriended the Enterprise crew, blew up the ship, and got shunned by her real friends, and asks whether role-playing villains makes players complicit in immoral perspectives. Working from Hume on fiction that describes 'vicious manners' without disapprobation, and the imaginative-resistance debate (Walton, Gendler), she argues the role-player is like an author or improvisational actor who makes it fictional that a character holds wicked views without imaginatively endorsing them—no utilitarian or deontological truth of the game world endorsed her sabotage. Her friends' anger was really at her hijacking a collaborative story into one where the villain wins, and her only residual worry about the 'Orc Holocaust' of experience-point slaughter is that game generalizations about inherently hostile monsters might bleed into real-world prejudice about human groups.
- ch. 15 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** David Hume, Plato — Virtue Ethics (arete); Falsificationism — Cooke defends his three-year lawful-evil wizard campaign against religious and secular critics by dismantling each style of argument. The propaganda charge fails because D&D rulebooks present no 'attitudes for export' about the real world; the puzzle of imaginative resistance dissolves once Can'tian and Won'tian readings are rejected for a 'Hardian' view that some things are merely hard to imagine, undercutting Dadlez's explanation of the Romulan spy's angry opponents; and the Werther-effect-style empirical case against D&D collapses on the data like parallel panics over jazz, video games, and pornography. The remaining character-warping objection—the Platonic and Aristotelian habituation worry that enjoying evil play mistrains the sentiments Hume warned about—turns unfalsifiable in the way repressed-desire claims do, leaving a firm moral gap between imagining evil and doing it.
- ch. 16 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** W.E.B. Du Bois, Kwame Anthony Appiah — The Rochas confess to being 'elf racists' about drow and use D&D's elf subspecies to stage the philosophy of race: since human races are not biologically real (no race gene, and greater genomic diversity within Africa than between continents), the Forgotten Realms' elf races work only as stereotypes—wood elves as nature-loving Native Americans, sun elves as model-minority Asians, moon elves as the unmarked white norm, and drow as the inherently evil dark-skinned race whose Underdark mirrors the American ghetto, with redeemed dark elves echoing the racist 'good negro' of lighter skin. Weighing W.E.B. Du Bois's conservation-of-races hope that each group prove its unique message, and Lucius Outlaw's view of race as a support group, against Kwame Anthony Appiah's warning that keeping races inevitably regenerates racism, they conclude racial thinking always slides into hierarchy. The remedy is anti-lazy DMing: build worlds with species differences but individuals who can surprise.
- ch. 17 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Plato — Objectification; The Ethics of Care — Olson opens with her own first session, in which the DM steered her toward a 'sexy Eladrin' miniature in high heels, and asks why women stay away from D&D. She argues the game's mechanics are egalitarian—identical power lists for male and female characters, echoing Plato's Republic claim that women guard the city by the same nature as men—and that party cooperation embodies the relational, care-based feminist ethic Marilyn Friedman and Angela Bolte oppose to male-coded individualism. The trouble is culture, not system: sexualized miniatures that objectify female bodies, tokenism, harassment, and marketing that ignores women, to which female gamers respond by playing male characters like Jenni Dowsett, designing separatist systems such as Portal Worlds, or, like Shelly Mazzanoble, claiming D&D for the 'inner girl'.
- ch. 18 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 19 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Derek Parfit, Thomas Nagel — Teleporter Paradox (personal fission); What Is It Like to Be a Bat? — Silcox and Cox present D&D modules as thought experiments in James Robert Brown's 'laboratory of the mind': the Tomb of Horrors' plane-shifting doorway forces Parfit's teletransporter question of whether the character who emerges is you, a copy, or an annihilation, extended to Tieflings and Warforged; Eberron's sentient Warforged raise Nagel's bat problem of whether a human player can know what it is like to be a machine, answered with the conclusion that players really explore human pretense, like Greek actors in masks. Mary Anne Warren's Space Traveler criteria for alien personhood strain against orcs who meet them yet attack on sight, and against the players who killed the lawful-good Flumph without parley. The deep difference is perspective: philosophy imagines sub specie aeternitatis while D&D demands immersion sub specie temporis, which nonetheless cultivates the open-minded intellectual virtues philosophers prize.
- ch. 20 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, Jean-Paul Sartre — Phenomena and Noumena — Cogburn and Hebert sort fantasy magic into formulaic natural magic (wizards as scientists exploiting a world's universal laws, in Kant's sense of nature—Salvatore's Cadderly, Snape's potions, Eberron's Artificer), sublimate natural magic (shamans, sorcerers, and warlocks surrendering to forces, as in African diaspora possession religions), and unnatural magic that violates a world's laws, whose horror Sartre's Nausea passage about flesh rebelling captures. Using Lovecraft's indescribable monsters and Noël Carroll's theory of art-horror as categorical impurity (with Mary Douglas on Leviticus-style purity codes), they argue Fourth Edition's algorithmic spell rules make casting feel like chemistry homework rather than the impossible, so unnatural magic cannot be modeled ludologically. Dungeon Masters must instead express the inexpressible through narrative techniques—character reaction, reader-relative impossibility, and limit impossibility—since D&D at its best is shared storytelling, not rule fetishism.
- ch. 21 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Martin Heidegger, Edmund Husserl — Phenomenology; Dasein (being-there) — Morton reads D&D as a hands-on workshop in phenomenology: mapping dungeons and caring for fellow party members enact Heideggerian 'worlding', while module texts like the blood-stained, smudged journal in The Lost Caverns of Tsojcanth—alluding to Moria's dwarf-record—show that worlds are opaque, alluring, and never transparent. Developing Graham Harman's 'sincerity' (you cannot jump outside your phenomenological skin) and 'allure', he argues each character class and alignment plane constitutes its own untotalizable world, with the Illusionist a recursive figure for a game already built on illusion, and he situates D&D at the end of realism's history of narrative control, mixing Wagnerian Gesamtkunstwerk with constructivist demystification. What happens in melee rounds is not Coleridge's willing suspension of disbelief but its opposite, a 'willing disbelief' that punctures Romantic immersion to get on with earning experience points.
- ch. 22 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Andy Clark, David Chalmers, Gilles Deleuze, Democritus — The Extended Mind; Atomism — Levi Bryant argues the D&D universe models a 'flat ontology' of active substances whose powers (affects) actualize differently under local conditions, asking Democritus-style individuation questions about whether a band of ogres at a tavern is one entity or many. Characters and their gear form alliances that generate genuinely new beings, so the blind man with his cane, or Moorcock's Elric fed by the soul-drinking sword Stormbringer, is a different entity than without it, and he borrows Clark and Chalmers' extended mind hypothesis to deny that thinking happens only inside the head. The dice then supply chance that generates necessity: the Dungeon Master's throws conjure traps, wraiths and saving throws that form 'regimes of attraction' to which every player and monster must respond.
- ch. 23 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Plutarch — Personal Identity; Ship of Theseus — Monica Evans argues that D&D is identity play in which players explore who they are through characters they create, perform and try to keep alive, opening with Plutarch's Ship of Theseus to ask whether a character re-rolled under a new edition, or replayed by another player, is still 'the same' character. She tests theories of the player-character bond (Gee's virtual, real and projective identities; Fine and Worch's 'identity bubble') on Wil Wheaton's Avenger Aeofel, whose in-character oath made Wheaton split the party and lose him rather than metagame. Bartle's four player types and the bulette-knowledge example show how each player manages the line between what they know and what their character knows, in a shared fiction where a glorious failure can be better than winning.
- ch. 24 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 25 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein, Socrates — Socratic Method (elenchus) — Carl Ehrett and Sarah Worth ask whether D&D is really a game (imagining Bill and Ted challenging Death to a campaign) and model the search for a definition of 'game' on Socrates dismantling Euthyphro's attempted definitions of piety, only for Wittgenstein's family resemblance to dissolve the project. On that view D&D still counts as a game despite lacking winning and losing, because its rule-governedness survives 'rule 0' license to bend rules whenever bending them crafts a better story. To explain why players who know the world is not real are nonetheless engrossed, they present Radford's paradox of fiction and adopt Walton's make-believe theory: the miniature on the table is a prop prescribing the imagining of an owlbear, so genuine quasi-emotions respond to truths in the fiction, making D&D an unusually gripping interactive story.
- ch. 26 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant — Judgment of Taste and the Sublime — Pete Wolfendale and Tim Franklin argue that D&D founded a genuinely new art form because role-playing yields a distinctively Kantian aesthetic experience: not passive button-pushing like a kitten video, but the disinterested 'free play' of the cognitive faculties whose exercise is itself pleasurable. In a D&D session that free play just is the collaborative simulation of a world, where dice and DM rulings let consequences spiral beyond anyone's plan, as when a Polymorph Other turns a spider demon into a small purple platypus or a fumbled Bluff check lands the whole party in jail. They call this autonomous, unforefillable quality the 'depth' of a game world, and the pleasure of role-playing is feeling our own world-building power cope with a friction that mimics reality.
- ch. 27 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Chris Bateman uses Kendall Walton's make-believe (or 'prop') theory of representation to argue that the rules of D&D exist chiefly to prescribe what players imagine, not to regulate conduct. The Dungeon Master's word is law because he authorizes which player-submitted propositions become fictional truths, while dice supply narrative intensity by tying the gambler's 'favor of destiny' to a character's life or death, from Saving Throws against petrification to critical hits and fumbles. Classes, the six abilities (including Gygax's distinction between Intelligence and Wisdom), character sheets, modules, maps and miniatures are all props that let even less imaginative players share the fictional world, which is why Bateman concludes that tabletop RPGs are the purest games of make-believe and therefore art.
- ch. 28 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Alan Turing — The Turing Test — Adam Brackin takes on the ludology-versus-narratology debate over whether games are rule systems or stories and argues that D&D dissolves it, since 'roll-play' and role-play need each other. He surveys the designers quoted in Rules of Play (Crawford, Juul, Laurel, Costikyan), the April Fool's 'Roll-Playing for Roleplaying' parody, and Fourth Edition's simplifications and failed Skill Challenges to show that legislating either side breaks the game. Computer games mark the limit: D&D Online is a closed, on-rails system, and no AI passes the Turing test of replacing a creative human Dungeon Master, while the open 'border region' between game and story is where tabletop play, flow theory, ARGs and McGonigal's 'epic win' all live.
- ch. 29 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Zen Buddhism — Timothy Christopher opens with the Zen archer who never fires but lets the arrow fall, using it as a model of Dungeon Mastering: the planned campaign, once released among the players, goes where it will. He plots DM control on two axes (control of the game world and of the player characters) and profiles four archetypes: the balanced guide, the hands-off host, the rule-enforcing arbiter who is the closest thing to a computer game, and the puppet master who railroads players toward a pre-written story, each with its own criterion of success. Knowing your archetype lets the table agree on the DM-player relationship in advance, though a crafty DM, unlike the archer, can still bend wind and gravity to nudge the arrow in flight.
- ch. 30 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Jason Rose tells the 'gunpowder crisis' of his Second Edition campaign, in which a player's dwarf alchemist legitimately mixed gunpowder from sulfur, charcoal and salt peter gathered in a hobgoblin mine, and the unprepared DM could only ban it outright, breaking the group's immersion. He argues this was a metagaming failure: players must keep out-of-character knowledge (real-world chemistry, monster statistics) out of their characters' heads, while DMs can metagame well, for instance by fudging the alchemy check behind the screen for the players' sake. His central tool is the inversion of choice and chance between perspectives (dice make success chance for the player but skill for the character, while a chosen background is choice for the player but fate for the character), supplemented by narrative mechanics like hero points in Mutants and Masterminds that build storytelling into the rules.
- ch. 31 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Edmund Husserl — Phenomenology — David Aldridge uses Gadamer's hermeneutics to ask how players know their characters and whether the rulebooks' ban on metagaming makes sense. Collingwood's re-enactment thesis ('to know my character as he knows himself') underlies immersionist ideals of play, but total re-enactment is impossible once battle maps and third-person talk intrude, and Collingwood's own 're-enactment plus' model instead demands a dual awareness akin to Husserl's bracketing, knowing what my character knows plus knowing that I am representing him. Gadamer's fusion of horizons goes further: out-of-character knowledge cannot be separated out at all, our prejudices enable understanding and we are 'brought up short' when the character sheet fails, so knowing one's character 'differently' is a transformative event that may even edify us.
- ch. 32 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 33 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 34 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" *(notes)*

## Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy - Read and Gain Advantage on All Wisdom Checks

- ch. 1 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein — Language Games — The editors' introduction tells two origin stories for D&D: Gygax and Arneson adapting Tolkien-flavored tabletop wargames like Tactics (the original 1974 subtitle was 'Rules for Fantastic Medieval Wargames Campaigns Playable with Paper and Pencil and Miniature Figures'), versus Gygax's childhood friend Jim Rausch improvising live spy and murder-mystery games where getting 'killed' meant leaving the house. It argues the trouble with classifying what kind of thing D&D is recalls Wittgenstein's claim in Philosophical Investigations that 'game' covers too diverse a range of activities to be defined, echoing Huizinga's Homo Ludens on play lying outside truth and falsehood. The volume's project is announced: stepping back from immersion to ask whether dungeon-crawling is mere escapism or a proving-ground for ideas about morality, metaphysics, and hedonic value.
- ch. 8 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle — Eudaimonia (flourishing); Virtue Ethics (arete) — Littmann recruits Aristotle to answer the charge that D&D is a waste of time: by the function argument the best human life is one of thinking well, and D&D counts even as hack-and-slash, since munchkin optimization and 4e combat roles are logic puzzles, while role-playing is improvisational theater and therefore art. Aristotle's two art-functions are applied to play: catharsis, so that smacking goblins with a mace (his first cleric Brother Silverwolf) purges aggression and fear, and the Poetics' point that poetry explores 'a kind of thing that might be', so a dwarf chieftain losing a son to disintegration or rescuing a thief from stormclaw scorpions makes players reflect on parenthood and what friends owe each other. A good DM, Aristotle-style, keeps events within internal probability (no medusa petrifying someone facing away), players must act in character (the lawful good paladin cannot ride past the bandit-attacked peasant, and 'just playing my character' does not excuse the assassin murdering the magic-user at the inn), yet D&D's fully participatory, player-authored art can still accommodate unaligned and evil PCs.
- ch. 10 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle — Virtue Ethics; Virtue Ethics (arete); Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Merli argues from alignment role-play against Aristotelian virtue ethics: a morally decent player convincingly runs a vicious treacherous thief who frames others for murder, and a 'moral mediocrity' runs a genuinely good paladin, so the ability to judge what the virtuous agent would do is one thing and being virtuous another. This undermines the McDowell/Hursthouse/Jacobson line that only the virtuous, with habituated emotional responses, can 'see what to do' — the role-playing skill works off-line, without feeling the character's fear or sharing its motives, so right-action judgment does not require trained emotion. He adds that if the virtue ethicist retorts that non-virtuous players never really get it right, the contrast between guessing dice in a fist (verifiable success) and claiming to read auras (unverifiable) shows the retort makes virtue look fraudulent, and that role-playing, unlike reading Austen, demands active deliberation from another's point of view, exposing abilities literary examples miss.
- ch. 11 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, John Stuart Mill, Hannah Arendt — The Categorical Imperative; The Greatest Happiness Principle; The Banality of Evil — Cogburn attacks Fourth Edition's contraction of the nine alignments to five: it deletes archetypes that fantasy literature needs, since Karsa Orlong (who wills the destruction of all civilization without sadism) and Jacqueline Carey's Haomane Firstborn (who loves cold mathematical order while his laws lead to genocide) fit nowhere in the new scheme. He rebuilds alignment by taking it to describe what a character wills in Kant's sense, mapping the law-chaos axis onto the categorical imperative (the liar and murderer must take themselves to be exceptions to universal laws) and the good-evil axis onto Mill's greatest happiness principle, which yields a Lawful Good of Gandhi and King, a Neutral Good modeled on Jesus breaking Sabbath law out of love, and Lawful Evil exemplified by vampire hierarchies and politicians whose policies create hell abroad. Joe Abercrombie's torturer San Dan Glotka becomes the archetypal Neutral Neutral, 'banality of evil' in Arendt's sense, and the chapter concludes Chaotic Good is empty because willing the destruction of universalizability cannot consistently will less pain.
- ch. 12 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, John Stuart Mill, Jeremy Bentham — Humanity as an End in Itself; Kingdom of Ends; The Greatest Happiness Principle — Bateman replies to Cogburn by recovering the alignment system's actual literary source: Moorcock's Elric saga, where Law and Chaos are rival political ideologies (conservative and liberal tendencies) held in check by the Cosmic Balance, so a Chaotic person need only refuse law's authority, not will horror, and Chaotic Good remains possible. He rejects Cogburn's 'sausage machine' reading of Kant's first formula in favor of the humanity formula and the Realm of Ends, and rejects act utilitarianism as a model of the Good alignment, since an overpopulation scenario shows Mill's calculus mandating culling-genocide and Bentham's felicific calculus cannot actually measure goodness. Drawing on Badiou's ethic of truths, Buddhist and Jain renunciation, and the true-Neutral Druid as servant of the Cosmic Balance, he argues neutrality between Good and Evil need not be monstrous and mourns 4e's 'childishly simplistic' new system.
- ch. 13 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** René Descartes, Thomas Nagel — Personal Identity — Silcox's fifth-level paladin died to eighty-three orcs and his middle-school friends held a funeral, and the chapter asks whether grief for a PC death is rational. Charles Taylor's narrative ethics — we grasp our lives as unfolding stories — would vindicate it, but objections from Beckett's Waiting for Godot and MacEwan's Atonement (via critic Samantha Vice) show stories can discourage seeing life as narrative, and the decisive asymmetry is Nagel's point about death: after the paladin falls the campaign story continues and the player can still watch it, whereas a person's own death forecloses any finding out how things turn out, a difference like February cold versus interstellar cold. He proposes instead that the self is a 'back-story' or metanarrative precondition of storytelling, closing against Descartes' Meditations claim that the power of imagination is 'in no way necessary' to his essence — take away someone's capacity for shared fiction entirely and nothing recognizable remains.
- ch. 14 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** David Hume — Dadlez narrates an all-night RPG in which her disguised Romulan spy blew up the Enterprise and got herself shunned by her seminar colleagues, and asks whether role-playing evil makes the player morally complicit. Working through Hume's Standard of Taste claim that we cannot 'enter into' vicious sentiments and the puzzle of imagining morally deviant worlds, she argues the role-player is like an author or improvising actor: generating Waltonian fictional truths about a wicked character requires no first-person endorsement of the character's values, any more than method acting a villain requires sharing his ethics. The other players' real anger is explained not as imagined complicity but as resentment at having their collaborative Star Trek story hijacked into a triumph of the villain, and the 'Orc Holocaust' worry about XP-for-slaughter survives only as a caution that game-licensed 'shoot first' generalizations might bleed into real-world attitudes.
- ch. 15 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Plato, David Hume — Falsificationism; The Noble Lie — Cooke defends his three-year campaign of a lawful evil wizard (Oraximon, who magic-missiled an innkeeper and hid the bodies in a rope trick) against religious and secular critics. The epistemic charge fails because rulebooks are invitations to imagine, not assertions, and the Smurfs-as-Krishna example shows an 'attitude for export' needs authorial evidence D&D lacks; Hume's twin claims about the impropriety of imaginatively endorsing flawed sentiments are dismantled by splitting Can'tians from Won'tians over Gendler's stories and defending a 'Hardian' view on which resistance is just difficulty, making imaginative resistance a chimera. The causal charge fails on the evidence (the Werther effect and its video-game and jazz cousins are post hoc plus confirmation bias), the character-warping charge risks the unfalsifiability of the repressed-desire diagnosis, and Plato's ban on the arts — enforced via the noble lie — would be the price if imagining were as bad as doing.
- ch. 16 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** W.E.B. Du Bois, Kwame Anthony Appiah — The Rochas confess to being 'elf racists' and map Forgotten Realms elf races onto American racial stereotypes: wood elves as Native Americans living in harmony with nature, sun elves as the smart, honor-bound 'model minority' Asians, moon elves as the unmarked white norm against which others are qualified, and drow as Black people — with the lighter-skinned dark elves redeemable through Eilistraee's blood sacrifice, echoing the racist 'good negro' myth and the Underdark as ghetto. After summarizing the biology that human races are not sub-species (no race gene; African genetic diversity so deep two Black people may each be closer to an Asian stranger than to each other), they weigh pro-race philosophers — Du Bois's Conservation of the Races, where each race must deliver its message, and Outlaw's races as support groups — against Appiah's argument in 'Racisms' that salvaging race without biology always smuggles biology back and keeping races regenerates hierarchy. Their verdict: Dungeon Masters should stop lazily stereotyping, so NPCs can surprise players instead of confirming racial essences.
- ch. 17 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Plato — Feminism; Objectification; The Ethics of Care — Olson's first D&D Encounters session handed her a sexualized female miniature in a chainmail bikini and high-heeled boots, and the chapter audits the game as a feminist would: research on the 'token girl' and male-default storytelling, women like Dowsett pushed to play male characters to be taken seriously, and harassment ranging from 'date at least one of the party or bring food' to condescension toward women in the industry. Against this she argues the rules themselves are egalitarian — identical power pools for male and female characters, embodying Plato's Republic claim that women 'are by nature the same with respect to guarding the city', and even the evil deities are stereotyped symmetrically (scheming Lolth and Tiamat versus war-god patrons of power) — while the collaborative party structure (clerics healing strikers, shared goals) has exactly the care-ethical, anti-individualist flavor Friedman and Bolte attribute to women's moral perspectives. Sexism is thus a consequence of unreflective practices, marketing that ignores women, and industry culture rather than the game; all-female systems like Portal Worlds are a real response but, she worries, separatism risks deepening the divide.
- ch. 18 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 19 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Derek Parfit, Thomas Nagel — Teleporter Paradox (personal fission); What Is It Like to Be a Bat?; Personal Identity — Silcox and Cox read D&D modules as thought experiments in James Robert Brown's 'laboratory of the mind'. The Tomb of Horrors' four overlapping plane-shifted buildings restate Parfit's teletransporter puzzle — did the character who stepped through get annihilated, or is an exact copy walking around elsewhere, and does the answer differ for a Warforged or Tiefling? — while the Eberron setting's magically sentient Warforged run into Nagel's 'What Is It Like to Be a Bat?': players cannot access Warforged experience and are really exploring what it is like for a human to suspend disbelief, like Greek actors donning masks. Mary Anne Warren's Space Traveler criteria for alien personhood collapse on contact with orcs who pass them yet eat you, and with the Flumph — lawful good, rational, but killed on sight by the authors' own party — showing personhood cannot be the only criterion for how to treat strangers; the closing twist is that philosophy contemplates scenarios sub specie aeternitatis while D&D forces immersion sub specie temporis, a habit of entertaining the impossible that is itself a philosophical virtue.
- ch. 20 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, Jean-Paul Sartre — Cogburn and Hebert ask how fantasy can stage magic that is by definition indescribable, from Lovecraft's unknowable mirror-monster in 'The Outsider' to Priest's limit-of-thought paradoxes. They sort magic into formulaic natural magic — mage-scientists exploiting a world's universal laws, exactly Kant's conception of nature, as in Snape's potions or Mieville's steampunk — and sublimate natural magic, where the practitioner surrenders to forces as in Candomblé possession or Robin Hobb's possessed soldier, against genuinely unnatural magic that violates law itself, as in Bakker's Prince of Nothing or the reality-liquefying passage from Sartre's Nausea. Carroll's theory of art-horror (via Mary Douglas: impurity as categorical incompatibility and incompleteness, from Leviticus's shellfish to The Dunwich Horror's fusions, fissions, and formlessness) explains how writers express the inexpressible, and exposes why 4e's algorithmic spell rules — the Fireball math every player masters 'like a chemistry student' — naturalize magic and kill the horror, leaving Dungeon Masters only narrative techniques (character reaction, reader-relative impossibility, limit impossibility) rather than rules.
- ch. 21 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Martin Heidegger, Jacques Lacan, Edmund Husserl — Phenomenology; Dasein (being-there) — Morton argues that to play D&D well is to do phenomenology: mapping a dungeon enacts a Heideggerian 'worlding' in which players generate care and concern for the entities around them, and the smudged, blood-stained survivor's journal in The Lost Caverns of Tsojcanth — alluding to the dwarves' book in Moria — shows that worlds are never transparent but allure, drawing players in through mystery (Harman's 'allure'). Against Heidegger's remark that animals are 'poor in world' and Lacan and Žižek's claim that only humans stick out of their life-world, he borrows Harman's 'sincerity' (with Husserl's intentional objects) to say every entity is stuck inside its own incomparable world — Elvish sincerity, Orcish sincerity, the Illusionist whose spells are a recursive play of illusion inside a game of illusion. D&D thereby caps the history of realism: where the novel moved from letters to focalization under a controlling narrator, the RPG replaces the master narrator with 'willing disbelief', letting players handle Romantic story-worlds — Tolkien's Middle-earth template, Greyhawk's continents, the Astral Plane's many planes — as DIY totalities that can be punctured, sorted, and rebuilt at will.
- ch. 22 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Andy Clark, David Chalmers, Democritus — The Extended Mind; Atomism — Levi Bryant uses the D&D universe to expound a 'flat ontology' of active substances: every entity, from enchanted swords to white dragons, has a virtual proper being (powers or 'affects') that actualizes variable 'local manifestations' depending on circumstances like lighting or armor. Adopting Andy Clark and David Chalmers's extended mind hypothesis, he argues a blind man with a cane, a smartphone owner, or Elric wielding Stormbringer is a literally different mind or entity, and that alliances (Raistlin's Staff of Magius, a warrior's plate armor) generate genuinely new powers rather than mere tool-use. Against Democritus's atomism, he holds aggregates can be individuals, and the game's dice embody his claim that chance produces necessity: throws generate 'regimes of attraction' no DM or player controls. D&D thus mirrors our own world of hidden powers, aleatory encounters and collectives of humans and nonhumans.
- ch. 23 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Plutarch — Ship of Theseus; Personal Identity — Monica Evans asks what relationship holds between a player and a character, opening with Plutarch's Ship of Theseus to probe whether a character rebuilt under a new edition, recreated in Neverwinter Nights, or played in succession by two people is still the 'same' character. She applies James Paul Gee's three identities (virtual, real-world, projective) to Wil Wheaton playing Aeofel, whose in-character chase of Leer into an acid pit shows projective identity trumping good gameplay. Worch's 'identity bubble', Bartle's four player types, and the management of player versus character knowledge (the veteran whose wizard meets a bulette, the INT 7 barbarian Dak) frame D&D as a shared fiction in which players explore who they are or might be.
- ch. 24 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 25 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein, Plato — Language Games; Socratic Method (elenchus) — Ehrett and Worth ask whether D&D is really a game and why we invest so much in it. Against the Socratic model (the Euthyphro dialogue's search for a common thread defining piety, here applied to 'game'), they deploy Wittgenstein's family-resemblance account from the Philosophical Investigations, concluding D&D lacks clear winning and losing but is rule-governed, with the twist that rule-breaking is legitimate exactly when it serves the game's primary purpose of group storytelling. They then take up the paradox of fiction: since players know the world is not real, Radford's puzzle arises, and Kendall Walton's make-believe theory of props and fictional truths supplies their answer, making D&D an especially gripping fiction because players are participants rather than observers.
- ch. 26 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant — Judgment of Taste and the Sublime — Wolfendale and Franklin argue D&D is a genuine art form with its own aesthetic. Tracing the medium's birth from Gygax's Chainmail to Arneson's invention of the persistent campaign setting, they use Kant's account of aesthetic experience from the Critique of Judgment, in which disinterested pleasure arises from the 'free play' of the cognitive faculties, and claim that in role-playing that free play just is the collaborative generation of a fictional world. The result is 'depth': consequences no one planned, like a sorcerer Polymorphing a spider demon into a purple platypus or a fumbled Bluff check turning a session into a prison break. This pleasurable because it exercises, under realistic friction and constraint from rules and dice, the mind's world-building power.
- ch. 27 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Chris Bateman’s “The Rules of Imagination” argues RPG rules function primarily as prescriptions for what to imagine rather than as regulations of conduct: Saving Throws, classes, character-sheet attributes, modules, maps, and miniatures are all “props” in Kendall Walton’s make-believe theory of representation, with the Dungeon Master’s authorizing word deciding what becomes fictional at the table. It treats dice as props whose appeal ranges from the Platonic solids’ exoticism to gambling’s “favor of destiny” — D&D’s revolution was tying the die roll to a personally inhabited narrative, complete with critical hits and fumbles. It defends character classes against skill-based systems as accessibility aids for less imaginative players, and closes by claiming tabletop RPGs as art: on prop theory, all artworks are games of make-believe, and RPGs are the purest form, “not just experienced, but lived.” (Slugs empty — Walton and prop theory are taxonomy gaps, logged as holes.)
- ch. 28 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Alan Turing — The Turing Test — Adam Brackin stages the old ludology-versus-narratology fight among game theorists (Juul, Laurel, Costikyan, Rules of Play) and argues D&D refutes both camps: Fourth Edition's rules history, the 'Roll-Playing for Roleplaying' April Fool's joke, and player-versus-character knowledge show that neither rules nor story alone explains the game. Comparing computer RPGs like D&D Online, which are closed systems 'on a rail', with the tabletop's open border region of 'quest' and flow, he invokes the Turing test: no computer game can simulate human interaction believably because AI lacks the human DM's creativity. The real action is the no-man's-land where gameplay and role-play fuse, and gamers were right to shrug at the theorists and roll dice.
- ch. 29 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Zen Buddhism; Wu Wei (effortless action) — Timothy Christopher opens with the Zen archer who 'lets the arrow fall' rather than releasing it: once the arrow is in the air it goes where it will, which he offers as a model of Dungeon Mastering, since a DM plans a campaign but cannot control where players take it once play begins. He maps DM mindsets onto two axes (control over the world, control over the characters) yielding four archetypes: the balanced guide, the minimal host, the rule-enforcing arbiter, and the railroad-imposing puppet master, each with its own measure of success. Like a Zen archer who could subtly bend wind and gravity, a crafty DM invisibly influences the arrow in flight, and groups should agree on the preferred style up front to avoid friction.
- ch. 30 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Jason Rose’s “The Gunpowder Crisis” opens with a campaign story where a player’s dwarf alchemist authentically invents gunpowder, forcing the unprepared DM into a fun-destroying “DM is always right” ruling, and uses it to dissect metagaming — the gap between what the player knows and wants and what the character knows and wants. Working through the ludology-versus-narratology debate, it argues metagaming is a tool rather than a vice: DMs may fudge rolls behind the screen, low ability scores can be played as flaws, hero-point systems (Mutants and Masterminds) build narrative editing into the ludology, and dice give an impartial, “neutral ground” source of narrative tension. Its key move is the choice–chance inversion between in-game and metagame perspectives — characters are accountable for dice they never rolled while unaccountable for backgrounds their players chose — resolving the crisis through in-game consequences instead of fiat. (Slugs empty — metagaming and the ludology–narratology debate are taxonomy gaps, logged as holes.)
- ch. 31 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" **·** Hans-Georg Gadamer, Edmund Husserl, Martin Heidegger, Plato — Philosophical Hermeneutics (fusion of horizons); Phenomenology — David Aldridge reads the D&D manuals' ban on 'metagame thinking' through Gadamer's hermeneutics of the 'event of understanding' from Truth and Method. He tests three models of knowing one's character: Collingwood's re-enactment thesis (total immersion, shown impossible by Fourth Edition's bird's-eye battle maps and the intrusion of rules talk), Collingwood's own 're-enactment plus' amendment joined to Husserlian bracketing (holding out-of-character knowledge apart, knowing the author better than he knew himself), and Gadamer's fusion of horizons, grounded in Heidegger's pre-understanding and illustrated by Plato's paradox of learning, in which no clean having-versus-acting distinction on OOC knowledge survives. Because understanding is always being 'brought up short' by what the character sheet cannot capture, playing a character transformationally teaches us about ourselves.
- ch. 32 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 33 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 34 "Dungeons and Dragons and Philosophy" *(notes)*

## Ender's Game and Philosophy - Genocide Is Child's Play

- ch. 1 "Globalization and Contemporary Art" *(front)*
- ch. 2 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Kevin Decker's introduction traces Card's debt to Asimov's Foundation and its 'psychohistory,' and notes the novel's surprising resonance with military readers such as the army helicopter pilot who drew courage from Ender during flight school. It previews the volume's five parts as an escalating set of questions: whether Battle School's engineered childhood can be justified, how empathy and game-playing make a commander, whether the buggers are enemies or misunderstood others, who bears responsibility for xenocide, and how Peter and Valentine's anonymous net politics remakes Earth.
- ch. 6 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Socrates, Plato, John Dewey — Socratic Method (elenchus); The Noble Lie; The Unexamined Life — Cam Cobb weighs Battle School against competing philosophies of education: Plato's Republic supplies vocational sorting by natural aptitude (complete with the myth of the metals as a 'noble lie'), while Socratic dialogue and Paulo Freire's problem-posing pedagogy demand the shared critical inquiry Ender never gets. He argues Graff's curriculum is almost purely vocational - military history, tactics, the Giant's Drink, battleroom drills - leaving no liberal paideia, and that the teachers' constant rule-changing leaves Ender feeling like 'someone's pawn,' exactly the banking-style teacher domination Freire warned against.
- ch. 8 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Augustine of Hippo — The Problem of Evil; Free Will vs Determinism — Jeremy Proulx reads Ender through F.W.J. Schelling's account of evil as a real, positive force rather than Augustine's mere privation of good: Ender's kicks to Stilson and Bonzo, his killing of the Giant in the mind game, and his 'I destroy them' confession all display the 'contraction into the self' Schelling thinks haunts every moral agent. He argues Graff deliberately isolates Ender to force this dark ground to the surface, and that Valentine's 'welcome to the human race' and the hive-queen's forgiveness teach Ender that evil, though never excusable, can be understood and atoned for - his fate also shows freedom tainted by a necessity we neither choose nor control.
- ch. 9 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle, Plato, Alasdair MacIntyre — The Golden Mean; Virtue Ethics (arete); Tripartite Soul; Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Jeff Ewing argues the moral fabric of Ender's Game is virtue-ethical, first testing Ender's leadership against Plato's cardinal virtues, tripartite soul, and rigid class hierarchy of the Republic, then finding Aristotle a far better fit. Ender's trademark use of the minimum force necessary against Stilson, Bonzo, and the buggers sits at Aristotle's mean between cowardice and excess, while his adaptability and disregard for rules and hierarchy only count as virtues in Aristotle's framework - though his ignorance of crucial facts explains why xenocide, like his beatings, fell outside the mean.
- ch. 10 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Michel Foucault, Friedrich Nietzsche, René Descartes — The Panopticon (surveillance); Power/Knowledge; Discipline and Docile Bodies — Chad Timm applies Foucault's disciplinary power to the International Fleet: Graff sorts monitored children like 'formless clay,' watches them through a Battle School Panopticon of head monitors and battleroom observation, and uses constant testing and the normalizing gaze to make Ender his own overseer and construct the identity of a commander. He closes by mapping the same sort-examine-discipline circuit onto standardized testing and Race to the Top, noting Ender's flicker of resistance when the children finally see through the adults' game.
- ch. 11 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 12 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Plato, David Hume — Philosophy of Love; Reason Is the Slave of the Passions — Andrew Zimmerman Jones argues Ender's genius is empathy cashed out through game theory: from Plato's Laches on courage to Hume's account of sympathy, sizing up the enemy's thinking is the winning strategy, and Ender's love ('in that very moment I also love him') reframes conflicts with Peter and the buggers so they can end rather than merely be won. His one failure is never extending this empathy to Graff and Rackham, so he cannot see that his final battleroom rule-breaking - 'the enemy's gate is down' - is exactly the move the adults' larger game was designed to produce.
- ch. 13 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Michael Walzer, Philippa Foot, Sun Tzu — The Trolley Problem; Just War Theory; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Matthew Brophy argues the I.F. could only get a compassionate child to commit xenocide by presenting war as a game, a framing that neutralizes moral agency just as Milgram's shock experiments and the fake game show The Game of Death did. Using Sunzi's 'know your enemy' to frame the empathy paradox, Walzer's problem of dirty hands for Graff's lesser-evil choices, and Foot's trolley problem alongside drone 'Nintendo warfare' to show how distance makes killing easier, he concludes Ender was likely a willing pawn who let himself be deceived because he endorsed the war's survivalist goal.
- ch. 14 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Sun Tzu, Laozi, Confucius — Strategy Without Conflict; Taoism (Daoism) — Morgan Deane maps Ender's tactics onto Sunzi's Art of War: exploiting Bonzo's hot anger, draining the enemy's martial spirit, Graff's use of 'fatal terrain' to throw students into hopeless situations, and staying 'formless' like water so opponents cannot read his plans. Drawing also on Laozi's Dao as the flow a commander must channel, Confucian role ethics in the general-as-father relation, and Legalist reward-and-punishment systems, he concludes Ender qualifies as a military genius by classical Chinese standards - at the wrenching moral cost both he and Graff pay.
- ch. 15 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein — Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Brendan Shea starts from Wittgenstein's claim that 'game' resists definition and classifies Ender's battleroom fights, buggers-and-astronauts, and the open-ended Giant's Drink, then applies Bernard Suits' definition of games as voluntary attempts to overcome unnecessary obstacles (prelusory goals, constitutive rules, lusory means and attitude). He argues games build Ender's skills and empathy but that Battle School's 'winning is the only thing' ethos endangers moral character - the same rule-breaking that wins the war keeps players from ever questioning whether the buggers needed defeating at all.
- ch. 16 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 17 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Kuhn, Paul Feyerabend — Paradigm Shifts — Cole Bowman explains the human-Formic war through Donald Davidson's critique of the idea of a self-contained conceptual scheme: the buggers' inability to grasp 'father,' 'individual,' or human sentience looks like complete untranslatability, yet Ender donning the bugger mask and the hive-queen's philotic contact show, via Davidson's principle of charity, that translation and a new shared scheme are possible. Kuhn's incommensurable paradigms and Feyerabend's account of how new theories eliminate old meanings frame how both species discard their prior concepts once Ender becomes the single point where the two schemes meet.
- ch. 18 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Emmanuel Levinas, Gottfried Leibniz — The Face of the Other; Monadology — Brett Patterson reads Card's philotes and the ansible as an uncanny parallel to Leibniz's Monadology - simple, internally varied, interconnected building blocks whose 'twining' explains instantaneous communication - and reads Ender's bond with the hive-queen through Levinas, for whom the face-to-face encounter with the Other imposes responsibility prior to self-understanding. That bond, formed through the Giant's Drink landscape the buggers built from Ender's own dreams, is why he cannot go home: he becomes Speaker for the Dead to restore the species he destroyed.
- ch. 19 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Aristotle — The Übermensch; Will to Power; Man as Political Animal; Aristotle on Friendship (philia) — Jeffery Nicholas argues Card tries to write Ender as Nietzsche's self-sufficient superman - Graff's isolation program, the will to power, and Rackham's 'humanity does not ask us to be happy' - but the novel actually vindicates Aristotle's claim in the Politics that man is a political animal who cannot flourish without friends. Ender's difference from the beast-like Peter and the hive-minded buggers, his longing for Valentine, Alai, and Petra, and his despair in isolation show that friendship, not domination, is what makes him human.
- ch. 20 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 21 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Aquinas, Harry Frankfurt, Elizabeth Anscombe — Doctrine of Double Effect; Second-Order Desires (personhood); Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Lance Belluomini tests Card's creed that 'good or evil exist entirely in human motive' against Aquinas' Doctrine of Double Effect and Frankfurt's second-order intentions, arguing Ender's killings of Stilson and Bonzo fail double-effect proportionality because he intends excessive, fear-inducing harm to deter future attackers. He allows that Ender bears only partial responsibility for the xenocide since he could not know the 'simulation' was real, but concludes that, contrary to the Speakers for the Dead doctrine, Ender is not the blameless moral exemplar Card makes of him.
- ch. 22 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Augustine of Hippo, Thomas Aquinas, Immanuel Kant, Cicero — Just War Theory; Humanity as an End in Itself; Doctrine of Double Effect; Ciceronian Duty (de Officiis) — James Cook grades the Third Invasion against the just war principles Ender was never taught: the war is preventive rather than genuinely preemptive (with the 2003 Iraq parallel), proper authority is compromised by deceiving the public, last resort fails since the hive-queen was trying to communicate, and non-combatant immunity condemns destroying the bugger homeworld. He stages Augustine's ban on killing in individual self-defense against Aquinas' permission of it, and uses Kant's humanity formula and Cicero's hostis/perduellis distinction to ask whether the buggers were ever treated as rational beings rather than mere threats.
- ch. 23 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle, James Rachels — Virtue Ethics (arete); Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Danielle Wylie uses Aristotle's conditions for voluntary action - control and knowledge - to argue Ender's planetary strike is one of his 'mixed cases' made under coercion and factual ignorance, so praise and blame attach elsewhere: Graff and Rackham, who foresaw the attack and could easily have stopped it, meet both conditions through culpable omission. Borrowing Rachels' Smith-and-Jones drowning case to show moral responsibility can exist without causal responsibility, she ends with Ender's non-blameworthy 'taking responsibility' as Speaker for the Dead.
- ch. 24 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Augustine of Hippo, Thomas Hobbes, Aristotle, Sun Tzu — Just War Theory; The State of Nature; Natural Law — Kody Cooper runs Ender's life through classical just war theory: Augustine's axiom that peace is the end of all war explains why even his playground battles aim at a just peace, while the I.F.'s deliberate refusal to protect him from Bonzo's gang throws Ender into the Hobbesian state of nature where each person holds personal authority to use deadly force in self-defense. Sunzi's insistence that a commander must know the enemy grounds Ender's empathic preparation, and Cooper concludes the Third Invasion fails right intention, last resort, discrimination, and proportionality - so the guilt Ender carries is really the I.F.'s and humanity's.
- ch. 25 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 26 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Hannah Arendt, John Locke, Plato — Totalitarianism; Personal Identity (memory theory); Ring of Gyges (why be moral?) — Kenneth Sayles reads Peter and Valentine's net personas through three lenses: John Locke's identity theory to ask whether a consistent virtual persona with no physical anchor deserves our trust, Plato's Ring of Gyges to ask what anonymity does to morality, and Hannah Arendt's account of totalitarianism to warn that nameless, unaccountable influence ('action without a name... is meaningless') paves the way for rule by propaganda and a fictitious world. Real-world parallels - identity theft, the hacker collective Anonymous, YouTube politics - make the Enderverse nets look prophetic rather than fantastic.
- ch. 27 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Hobbes, Niccolò Machiavelli — The State of Nature; Virtù and Fortuna — Ted Brown and Christie Maloyed read Ender's Game as a Cold War debate between realism and liberalism: Hobbes' state of nature grounds Graff's survival-at-all-costs logic, and Machiavelli's counsel on seizing opportunities and keeping power by force when persuasion fails shapes Peter's insight that 'the right words can change the world.' The human-Formic war becomes a Prisoner's Dilemma neither species can escape without communication, and Ender's postwar practice of governing his colony 'by persuasion rather than fiat' delivers the book's verdict that the power to persuade can outlast the power to destroy.
- ch. 28 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** John Stuart Mill — Utilitarianism; The Greatest Happiness Principle; The Harm Principle — Greg Littmann reads Graff and Ender as act utilitarians in John Stuart Mill's mold, drawing a detailed parallel between Ender's engineered Battle School childhood and James Mill's grueling raising of his son to be utilitarianism's champion, down to both boys' breakdowns. He argues that although the xenocide was a tragic wrong by Mill's own lights (the hive-queens' happiness counted too), the harm principle permits overriding individual liberty when humanity's survival is at stake - which is why Card's novel, unlike Crime and Punishment or 'The Ones Who Walk Away From Omelas,' shows consequentialism at its most troubling rather than its most absurd.
- ch. 29 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 30 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" *(notes)*

## Ender's Game and Philosophy - The Logic Gate Is Down

- ch. 1 "Globalization and Contemporary Art" *(front)*
- ch. 2 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — The book's introduction explains how Card's novel grew out of his childhood fascination with Asimov's Foundation and its 'psychohistory,' and why the premise of a monitored 'Third' child socially engineered by Colonel Graff into a child commander attracts such diverse readers, including real military trainees who identify with Ender. It previews the volume's five parts: Ender's education and moral making, the philosophy of war games and empathy, the buggers as misunderstood others, the ethics of xenocide and wartime responsibility, and the postwar nets politics of Locke, Demosthenes, and the Hegemon.
- ch. 6 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Socrates, Plato, John Dewey — Socratic Method (elenchus); The Noble Lie; Justice in the Republic; The Unexamined Life — Cam Cobb pits Socratic critical inquiry and Plato's Republic-style vocational streaming (classes sorted by the 'myth of the metals,' sold through a noble lie) against the Battle School curriculum, which is almost purely vocational and lacks the music, poetry, and liberal paideia that harmonize the soul. Testing Socrates' dialogic method, Dewey's aptitude-tracking education, and Freire's problem-posing alternative against the battleroom, the Giant's Drink, and Graff's secretly rewritten game rules, Cobb concludes Ender's schooling mixes conflicting pedagogies and ends in the teacher domination Freire feared, crystallized in Ender's lament 'I've spent my life as someone's pawn.'
- ch. 8 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Augustine of Hippo — The Problem of Evil; Free Will vs Determinism — Jeremy Proulx reads Ender's refrain 'I didn't want to hurt him!' through Schelling's account of evil as a real 'contraction into the self' rather than Augustine's mere privation of good: the beatings of Stilson, the boy on the shuttle, Bonzo, and the Giant reveal a Peter-like monstrosity in Ender that he can neither understand nor control. Graff's engineered isolation forces Ender to confront this dark ground, and his moral development culminates in Valentine's 'Welcome to the human race' — the recognition that freedom is tainted by a necessity we do not choose, so Ender stays responsible for xenocide even though he was tricked.
- ch. 9 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle, Plato — The Golden Mean; Virtue Ethics (arete); Tripartite Soul; Utilitarianism — Jeff Ewing asks which moral theory governs the novel, finding deontology absent and a contest between Graff's utilitarian recruiting pitch and a virtue-ethical focus on character: the I.F. allowed a 'Third' because it wanted Ender's traits rather than his acts. Mapping Plato's cardinal virtues and tripartite-soul politics against Aristotle's doctrine of the mean, he argues Ender's minimum-force self-defense, adaptability, and disregard for hierarchy are Aristotelian virtues, while his killings of Stilson, Bonzo, and the buggers fall outside the mean only because he lacked the intellectual virtue of grasping his full situation.
- ch. 10 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Michel Foucault, Jeremy Bentham — The Panopticon (surveillance); Power/Knowledge; Discipline and Docile Bodies — Chad Timm reads Battle School through Foucault's disciplinary power: monitors in the skull, sorting into armies, minute-by-minute schedules, and constant testing are the power/knowledge that constructs 'the soldier,' while the ever-possible observation of teachers and desks makes the school Bentham's Panopticon that trains students to police themselves. The proof of Ender's constructed identity is his singling out of Bean exactly as Graff singled him out on the shuttle, and Timm extends the lesson to standardized testing and Obama's 'Race to the Top.'
- ch. 11 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 12 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Plato, David Hume, Socrates — Philosophy of Love; Reason Is the Slave of the Passions — Andrew Zimmerman Jones treats Ender's conflicts as games analyzable by von Neumann's minimax theorem and the theory of zero-sum, imperfect-information games, arguing Ender wins by redefining the game itself ('the enemy's gate is down') while his empathy fails only with Graff, whose larger game Ender never suspects because he never treats the adults as the enemy. He unpacks Ender's claim that to understand an enemy is to love him through Socrates' words in Plato's Laches on courage, Hume's account of sympathy, and the Christian agape of 'love your enemies,' mapping a continuum from sympathy through empathy to love.
- ch. 13 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Sun Tzu, Michael Walzer, Philippa Foot — Strategy Without Conflict; The Trolley Problem; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Matthew Brophy argues the I.F. neutralizes Ender's moral agency by masquerading xenocide as child's play: Rackham's paradox that total empathy with the buggers forbids killing them (Sunzi's 'know your enemy') is solved by deception, Walzer's problem of dirty hands is shifted from the child to the politicians, and Milgram-style game framing plus Foot's trolley-problem 'moral distance' show how killing grows easier at a console. Against the innocent-pawn reading, Brophy ends by suggesting Ender was a willing pawn who let himself be deceived in order to save humanity.
- ch. 14 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Sun Tzu, Confucius, Laozi — Strategy Without Conflict; The Tao (the Way); Rectification of Names — Morgan Deane reads Ender's tactics as Sunzi's Art of War in action: winning all future fights by terror, exploiting Bonzo's hot anger, 'fatal terrain' when Graff stacks hopeless odds, formlessness that hides intent, and fluid toon formations that move like water — all grounded in Daoist harmony with the Way and the Confucian role-duty of the general-as-father who throws soldiers into hopeless positions to win their devotion. He also finds Legalist rewards-and-punishments in Ender's command of Dragon Army, concluding Ender qualifies as a Sunzi-style military genius whose empathy is the steep moral price of victory.
- ch. 15 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein — Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); Language Games — Brendan Shea starts from Wittgenstein's point that 'game' resists any definition, then applies Bernard Suits' four-part analysis (prelusory goal, constitutive rules, lusory means, lusory attitude) to the battleroom contests and to the Giant's Drink as an 'open game' whose real goal is simply to keep playing. He argues games build Ender's skills and even his empathy — buggers and astronauts makes him take a bugger's perspective — but Battle School's 'winning is the only thing' attitude, which celebrates breaking the rules, trains brilliant commanders while corroding moral character.
- ch. 16 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 17 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Kuhn, Paul Feyerabend — Paradigm Shifts; Anything Goes (epistemological anarchism) — Cole Bowman explains the three Formic invasions as failures between 'conceptual schemes,' deploying Davidson's 'On the Very Idea of a Conceptual Scheme' — translatability, the principle of charity, and the closet analogy — alongside Kuhn's incommensurable paradigms and Feyerabend's account of how new theories eliminate old meanings. Ender donning the bugger mask in Peter's game and his final philotic bond with the hive-queen show the two schemes touching at a single shared point, making Ender 'the metal in between' who begins translating between human and Formic worlds.
- ch. 18 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Gottfried Leibniz, Emmanuel Levinas — The Face of the Other; Monadology — Brett Patterson compares Card's philotes and the ansible to Leibniz's monadology — simple, internally varied building blocks whose twining connections mirror monadic 'intercommunication' at any distance — and reads the Giant's Drink landscape and the hive-queen pupa as the buggers' engineered face-to-face encounter with Ender. Through Levinas he argues the Other's ethical claim precedes self-knowledge, so the philotic bond installs a responsibility Ender cannot escape: he cannot go home because he must become Speaker for the Dead and restore the species he destroyed.
- ch. 19 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Aristotle — Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Man as Political Animal; The Übermensch; Will to Power — Jeffery Nicholas contrasts the superman Card seems to write — Graff's isolated Ender conditioned to impose his will like Nietzsche's overman, for whom happiness is only the feeling of overcoming resistance — with Aristotle's claim that man is a political animal who cannot flourish without friends. Peter is Aristotle's 'tribeless, lawless, hearthless' beast, the speechless buggers lack a common good until the queen communicates with Ender, and Ender's despair lifts only through friendship with Valentine, Alai, and Petra, proving the novel a meditation on 'without friends no one would choose to live.'
- ch. 20 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 21 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Aquinas, Harry Frankfurt, Elizabeth Anscombe — Doctrine of Double Effect; Second-Order Desires (personhood); Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Lance Belluomini tests Card's creed that 'good or evil exist entirely in human motive, and not at all in the act' against Aquinas's Doctrine of Double Effect, finding that Ender's self-defense killings of Stilson and Bonzo fail the proportionality condition — he intended to 'win all the next fights' — so he bears moral responsibility despite his tears. After weighing Frankfurt's second-order intentions as a way to keep Ender a good person who did bad things, he concludes with Anscombe's 'double-think about double effect' that Ender is deceived about his own intentions and is no moral exemplar.
- ch. 22 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Augustine of Hippo, Thomas Aquinas, Immanuel Kant, Cicero — Just War Theory; Humanity as an End in Itself; Doctrine of Double Effect; Ciceronian Duty (de Officiis) — Air Force colonel James Cook walks the just war tradition through Ender's world: the Third Invasion is preventive rather than preemptive (with the 2003 Iraq war as the parallel), proper authority is undermined by the I.F.'s lies to its citizens, and last resort fails because the hive-queen was already trying to communicate with Ender. He contrasts Augustine's ban on killing in self-defense with Aquinas's permission, invokes Kant's formula of humanity for the buggers' rational nature and Cicero's hostis against dehumanizing enemies, and argues the I.F.'s failure to teach military ethics is itself part of the novel's tragedy.
- ch. 23 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle, James Rachels — Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Danielle Wylie applies Aristotle's conditions of voluntary action — control and knowledge, including his 'mixed' coerced cases like the wallet at gunpoint — to argue Ender's final attack was involuntary, since he believed he was throwing an unfair simulation to get himself thrown out, so his guilt is real but full blame is misplaced. Using Rachels's Smith and Jones drowning case she extends moral responsibility to omissions, showing Graff and Rackham foresaw the xenocide and could have stopped Ender, and closes with Ender's Speaker-for-the-Dead work as taking responsibility without being to blame.
- ch. 24 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Augustine of Hippo, Thomas Hobbes, Sun Tzu, Thomas Aquinas — Just War Theory; The State of Nature; Natural Law; Strategy Without Conflict — Kody Cooper argues Ender's tortured conscience demonstrates the classical just war framework: from Augustine's axiom that peace is the end of war and the five jus ad bellum requirements, to the verdict that the Third Invasion fails right intention, last resort, discrimination, and proportionality because the rational hive-queens were never given a real chance at coexistence. He reads the I.F.'s withholding of protection as throwing Ender into a Hobbesian state of nature where his fights with bullies count as private just wars, and concludes Ender carries the sins of the I.F. and humanity rather than his own guilt.
- ch. 25 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 26 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Hannah Arendt, Plato, John Locke — Totalitarianism; Ring of Gyges (why be moral?); Personal Identity (memory theory) — Kenneth Sayles reads Peter's and Valentine's anonymous rise on the nets through three lenses: John Locke's identity theory asks how a virtual identity can lack a physical anchor, Plato's Ring of Gyges asks what anonymity does to justice, and Hannah Arendt warns that 'action without a name... is meaningless' and that faceless power is a precondition of totalitarianism. Comparing the personas to the hacker collective Anonymous and to identity theft, he concludes our own Internet could breed its own Locke and Demosthenes and asks how much trust we owe virtual people whose controllers we can never know.
- ch. 27 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Hobbes, Niccolò Machiavelli — The State of Nature; Virtù and Fortuna; The Social Contract — Ted Henry Brown and Christie Maloyed stage the Enderverse as the international-relations debate between Hobbesian realism — Graff's 'if one of us has to be destroyed...' and the human-bugger Prisoner's Dilemma where the absence of communication forces mutual defection and war — and liberal institutionalism, which Peter's Locke and Valentine's Demosthenes argue out on Earth's nets. Machiavelli supplies Peter's theory of power: fortune opens the moment, virtù seizes it with 'the right words at the right time,' and force must back persuasion when belief fades, so that 'influence is power' and governing by persuasion beats fiat.
- ch. 28 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" **·** John Stuart Mill — Utilitarianism; The Greatest Happiness Principle; The Harm Principle; Consequentialism — Greg Littmann defends Graff and Ender as act utilitarians in Mill's own image, noting that Mill's father raised him in calculated isolation and brutal study as a tool to reform humanity just as Graff engineers Ender, so both acted rightly given the stakes even though Mill's harm principle and his regard for animal suffering also condemn the xenocide itself. Against Crime and Punishment and 'The Ones Who Walk Away from Omelas,' he argues Ender's Game shows consequences can genuinely justify breaking any moral rule when human extinction is on the table.
- ch. 29 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 30 "Ender’s Game and Philosophy" *(notes)*

## Facebook and Philosophy - What's on Your Mind_

- ch. 1 "Start" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "index" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Arthur Schopenhauer, G.W.F. Hegel, Friedrich Nietzsche, Plato — Existence Precedes Essence; The Meaning of Life; The Last Man — This file holds the book's front matter, Wittkower's introduction 'A Reply to facebook Critics', and the 'facebook Itself' essays. Wittkower answers critics of trivial status updates with Sartre's claim that lives have no given meaning (so their value is ours to create, from the inside), Hegel's argument that real freedom means choosing alongside others (against both Sunstein's 'Daily Me' echo chamber and the forced community of family and hometown), and Schopenhauer's microscope image from 'On the Vanity of Existence', concluding that we care about a friend's dinner update for the same reason we care about our own. James Grimmelmann's 'The Privacy Virus' rebuts the claim that Facebook users don't care about privacy by arguing they practice a contextual, Nissenbaum-style privacy (untagging keg-stand photos, the News Feed revolt, the Miss New Jersey blackmail), while Facebook's architecture breaks the 'know your audience' rule and recruits users into spreading violations like a virus.
