Graham Meikle reads wall posts and status updates as a convergence of personal messaging and broadcast media (citing the MI6 chief's wife's holiday photos and the Sunday Express's Dunblane survivors story), and Ian Bogost applies McLuhan's media ecology to Facebook as an 'extension of man': it enhances the directory and the yearbook, obsolesces the reunion and the secret, retrieves the small town and the soda shop, and reverses into campus life and high school.
- ch. 3 "index" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Aristotle, John Stuart Mill, Simone de Beauvoir, Plato, Bernard Williams, Walter Benjamin, Cicero, Jacques Derrida, Jeremy Bentham, Peter Singer, Frank Jackson — Ring of Gyges (why be moral?); Aristotle on Friendship (philia); The Look (hell is other people); Utilitarianism — This file spans book chapters 4-11. Elizabeth Losh reads Facebook games (Zombies, Mob Wars, Scrabulous, Parking Wars, PackRat, (Lil) Green Patch) through Goffman's 'face', Brown and Levinson's politeness, and Derrida's gift-obligation, showing that aggressive games can be played politely while altruistic ones coerce; Jeremy Sarachan analyzes profile pictures through Barthes's studium, punctum and 'air' and Benjamin's mechanical reproduction (the webcam image that has no original). Mimi Marinucci pairs Glaucon's Ring of Gyges with Suler's online disinhibition effect to argue that Facebook's forced integration of audiences (students, colleagues, Mom all reading one profile) suppresses 'fronting' and so can foster authentic self-expression; Mariam Thalos answers with Sartre's analysis of the gaze and Beauvoir's woman-as-Other that the elective, endlessly plastic profile cannot anchor the bonding only face-to-face encounter provides - hence 'Why I Am Not a Friend' - while Wandel and Beavers use Groundhog Day's Phil Connors and Goffman's presentation of self to defend identity play as real self-discovery, and Vejby and Wittkower ask whether Facebook is Debord's Spectacle 2.0 or a platform for Situationist detournement. Finally, Craig Condella measures 'friending' against Aristotle's three friendships and Cicero's 'handmaid of virtue' (via the Seinfeld episode where Keith Hernandez asks Jerry to help him move), and Matthew Tedesco sets 322 Facebook friends against Godwin's burning archbishop to press the utilitarian impartiality challenge, deploying Williams's 'one thought too many' shipwreck case and criticizing Frank Jackson's sector-plan defense of partiality.
- ch. 4 "index" **·** Martin Heidegger, Nel Noddings, Aristotle, Theodor Adorno, Herbert Marcuse, John Stuart Mill, Friedrich Nietzsche — The Question Concerning Technology; Caring as the Ground of Ethics; Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Utilitarianism — Six essays on friendship in the Facebook age. Tedesco asks whether the low-demand 'Facebook friendship' can reconcile the utilitarian demand for impartiality (Mill, Railton, Kagan) with the partiality of real friends, and concludes Facebook ties are only supplementary to relationships built elsewhere. Hamington surveys a hundred users and argues in Noddings's care-ethics terms that Facebook's information transfer makes caring possible (knowledge of the concrete other is necessary for care), though it also makes dropping a student over one racist comment effortless. Bloor reads Facebook through Heidegger's technological nihilism and the anonymous 'one', warning that speed of exchange erodes Aristotle's few deep, morally formative friendships, while Briggle's farewell letter to Facebook judges the site by Ivan Illich's convivial-tool criteria and Cuonzo recasts pokes and virtual gifts as efficient successors to primate grooming; Lattimore applies Bakhtin's 'excess of seeing' to how viewers 'consummate' other users' self-presentations.
- ch. 5 "index" **·** Michel Foucault, Jürgen Habermas, David Hume, Jean Baudrillard, Søren Kierkegaard, Jeremy Bentham, Aristotle — The Panopticon (surveillance); The Public Sphere; Reason Is the Slave of the Passions; Simulacra and Simulation (the hyperreal) — Fairweather and Halpern defend status updates against the vanity charge using Hume's 'natural sympathy': ambient awareness of hundreds of News Feed lives exercises the fellow-feeling from which Humean moral standards grow, and Facebook's non-overlapping friend lists remove the dominance hierarchies of face-to-face groups. Butera uses Baudrillard's promiscuous 'ecstasy of communication' to read friending as gatekeeper, the status update as moderator of an avatar-self, and the News Feed as a synthesizer that lets users censor encounters, asking with Kierkegaard whether this control forecloses the openness to interruption that makes life meaningful. Doyle and Fraser stage Foucault against Habermas over the MI6 chief's wife's exposed profile, Facebook's Beacon advertising surveillance, and Iran's Twitter revolution: the same diffuse, productive power that lets citizens watch institutions back makes narcissistic self-disclosure ever more available for categorization and control. Bar-Tura's account of organizing Jewish-Muslim teen bowling nights tests Habermas's ideal speech situation and concludes that wall-to-wall interaction must become face-to-face meetings to build real solidarity.
- ch. 6 "index" **·** Karl Marx — Class Struggle; Ideology and False Consciousness — Scholz completes his 'playground and factory' argument: user translations, photos and profiles are unpaid digital labor locked inside a walled garden whose data trails are sold to marketers and governments, even as the same platform hosts a genuine power to act together. Morgan and Clulow frame activism with the Communist Manifesto's class struggle, staging fictional discussion-board posts from Engels, Luxemburg and Chomsky that update the proletariat into the internet-era 'iProles', then show Facebook's ease breeding slacktivism, from the '1,000,000 For Obama To Grow An Afro!' group dwarfing real causes to the platform itself banning a father's 'Justice for Francesca Bimpson' page. Muhr and Pedersen use Zizek's interpassivity to argue the Facebook profile is a surrogate self that enjoys, believes and mourns on our behalf, like canned laughter or a Tibetan prayer wheel, deferring the social duty to be authentic while the site quietly installs its own ideology of relentlessly interesting happiness.
- ch. 7 "index" *(front)*

## Fallout and Philosophy - Thinking Outside the Shelter

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Table of Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Series Page" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Copyright Page" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "The Vault Dwellers" *(notes)*
- ch. 7 "Flesh Is Weak, but Questions Endure" *(no index entry yet)* — The editors' introduction recounts how Fallout overcame early adaptation jitters (Bethesda's Peter Hines warned in 2015 that more could go wrong than right in a screen version) because the games' blank overworld maps and faction-defining choices, from the original water-chip quest to New Vegas's player-decided ending, invite wondering about everyone else's story in the wastes. It argues that this world-building raises genuinely philosophical questions - why we are drawn to fictional atrocities, whether wasteland life is too bad to bring children into, whether ghouls and synths are persons, and what makes anything right or wrong - and presents the book's essays as attempts to raise, clarify, and answer them.
- ch. 8 "Part 1 LIVING AND DYING IN THE WASTELAND" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "Notes" **·** Epicurus — Death Is Nothing to Us — Mackenzie Graham uses Fallout 3's Project Purity - James's sacrificial death and the Lone Wanderer's choice among feeding the purifier with their own life, poisoning the water for the Enclave, or destroying it - to ask whether death and posthumous events can be bad for the one who dies. Against Epicurus's claim that death is nothing to us, the chapter defends deprivationism with the Megaton quest (the Lone Wanderer's desire to save the town is thwarted even if the rearmed bomb destroys it unbeknownst to them) and weighs Feinberg and Pitcher's ante-mortem person against the problem of the subject, using the Great War's redefinition of October 22, 2077 to block backward causation. The 'Blood Ties' quest with the Family's cannibals then raises posthumous wrongs: since no existing subject is harmed or wronged, the chapter ends by asking whether cannibalism is an impersonal wrong against personhood itself, on the model of respecting nature.
- ch. 10 "Notes" **·** Thomas Hobbes, Immanuel Kant, Aristotle — The State of Nature; Virtue Ethics (arete); The Categorical Imperative — Eric Silverman asks whether the Amazon series' Lucy and Maximus should have children, framing the wasteland through Hobbes's state of nature where life is 'nasty, brutish, and short' and even vaults are limiting experiments. He lays out Benatar's Harm Argument (pain outweighs pleasure, so procreation is procreational Russian roulette) and Shiffrin's Consent Argument (no one consents to being born, as Sartre's 'I did not ask to be born' concedes), then rebuts them: Socrates, the Stoics, and Aristotle make virtue and meaning rather than pleasure the goal of life, so a harsh wasteland full of people needing help suits a virtuous, sacrificial life like the hero's exile at the end of the original Fallout. Parental proxy consent legitimizes procreation as it does children's medical decisions, and Kant's universalization test defeats the demand for pre-natal consent, since universalizing it would eliminate the very rational beings whose consent it protects - so Lucy's 'Okey dokey!' optimism wins.
- ch. 11 "Notes" **·** Personal Identity; Embodiment (the lived body) — David Lawrence opens with Fallout 4's 'Blind Betrayal', where Elder Maxson orders the player to execute Paladin Danse once his synth origin is revealed, and argues that moral status is a matter of capacities - sense of self, notion of past and future, values, life-plans, moral agency - drawing on Charles Taylor's concept of a person and narrative identity, not on biological substrate. Nick Valentine's hunt for Eddie Winter in 'Long Time Coming' and Curie's chosen embodiment in 'Emergent Behavior' show machines weaving reconstructed pasts into new life-plans, while ghouls like Hancock and Raul ('Old School Ghoul') and super mutants like Fawkes (who refuses to enter the lethal radiation chamber: 'I would not rob you of that') and the community-builder Marcus prove branded monsters can be more human than human. The factions embody warring philosophies of moral status: the Institute's property-slavery of its creations, the Brotherhood's 'wisdom of repugnance' (Leon Kass), and the Railroad's capacity-based abolitionism, with DiMA's Far Harbor question - how do you know you are not a synth? - turning the issue on the player.
- ch. 12 "Notes" **·** Aristotle, Immanuel Kant — Virtue Ethics (arete); Humanity as an End in Itself; The Golden Mean — Trevor Hedberg asks whether 'Kill Everything' playthroughs of New Vegas are wrong, from the Goodsprings opening (where the player may massacre both Powder Gangers and settlers) to installing a mod that makes Freeside's unkillable children killable. He rejects the empirical aggression argument (the evidence is mixed and effects tiny, and alcohol or contact sports set a precedent) and the Kantian disrespect argument, since Fallout's NPCs - stuck on terrain, repeating dialogue - lack the rational agency the Humanity Formulation protects. The best case against virtual slaughter is Aristotle's virtue ethics: courage as the mean between recklessness and cowardice, eudaimonia as activity in accord with virtue, so the morality of playing turns on motives - exploring narratives or seeking combat challenge can be innocent, but killing for the pleasure of settlers' horrified flight corrodes character.
- ch. 13 "Part 2 SYNTHS, CYBORGS, AND THE SELF" *(front)*
- ch. 14 "Notes" **·** Andy Clark, David Chalmers — The Extended Mind; Philosophy of Disability — The Funkes apply Clark and Chalmers's extended mind hypothesis to the Pip-Boy, arguing it meets all three criteria for cognitive extension - availability (worn at all times), reliability (trusted rad and health monitoring), and active cognitive engagement (V.A.T.S. offloads targeting calculations) - so it is part of the Sole Survivor's and Courier's minds, as in 'The Glowing Sea' and the search for Virgil's cure. Donna Haraway's cyborg frames this human-machine hybridity alongside cyberdogs and Kellogg, but Fallout inverts her liberationist vision: the Pip-Boy is a survival lifeline forced by the wasteland, not an emancipatory choice, making the cyborg a dystopian necessity. Comparing the Pip-Boy with cochlear implants, prosthetics, and brain-computer interfaces, and the Old World Blues Think Tank with a dehumanizing bio-tech extreme, the chapter uses Alison Kafer's feminist crip critique to insist real augmentation carries pain, stigma, and unequal access the game's frictionless fiction erases, closing on a cautious transhumanist note.
- ch. 15 "Notes" **·** Nick Bostrom — Existential Risk — Walter Barta presents transhumanism as a real philosophy (via Bostrom's history of the movement and 'Why I Want to Be a Posthuman When I Grow Up') that Fallout both practices and indicts: ghouls, FEV super mutants and the Children of the Cathedral, stimpak-Mentat-Jet self-medication, Brotherhood power armor, Mr. House, the Master, Robobrains, John Henry Eden, and Liberty Prime all embody enhancement or post-humanity, and the level-up loop makes every player a transhumanist. Bostrom's transhumanist value of 'the opportunity to explore' maps onto leaving Vault 13 for the open world, but the Red Queen race becomes the game's adaptive-leveling treadmill, and C.S. Lewis's Abolition of Man plus Fukuyama's 'Factor-X' warn that optimization can delete what made enhancement worth wanting - as when the Overseer exiles the too-powerful Vault 13 hero and the Enclave President rationalizes genocidal bioweapons ('No price is too high for the survival of the human race'). Existential risk (Toby Ord's Precipice, Bostrom's vulnerable world hypothesis) supplies the final warning: the G.E.C.K.'s energy is also a destruction kit, and the cycle of war repeats until post-humanity or extinction.
- ch. 16 "Notes" **·** W.E.B. Du Bois, Jane Addams, John Dewey, William James, Charles Sanders Peirce, Kimberlé Crenshaw — Double Consciousness; Pragmatism; Intersectionality; Social Ethics of the Settlement House — Albert Spencer reads Fallout 4's synth crisis as a philosophy-of-mind allegory - Brotherhood dualism, Institute reductive physicalism, Railroad functionalism - which he grounds in the classical pragmatists: Peirce's sign-interpreting mind, James's stream of consciousness, and Dewey's interactive consciousness make personhood a matter of social participation rather than essence. Quests dramatize the stakes: 'Human Error' has Covenant's interrogation of Amelia Stockton enact Du Bois's double consciousness (self-understanding against a devaluing gaze, as with the tragic Phyllis Daily), the Sole Survivor of 'The Battle of Bunker Hill' and Far Harbor becomes Anzaldua's Nepantlera straddling the human/synth binary, and the Railroad's memory-wipe 'resets' reproduce the domination they fight by ignoring Crenshaw's intersectionality (the Feral Historian's case that the Railroad kills the selves it saves). Acadia, where DiMA murdered and replaced Captain Avery to preserve peace, becomes Jane Addams's test case of democracy as associated living - an imperfect, lying community that nonetheless offers the only future of shared synth-human personhood.
- ch. 17 "Notes" **·** John Locke, Hilary Putnam, Derek Parfit, Martha Nussbaum, Peter Singer — Personal Identity (memory theory); The Capabilities Approach; Speciesism — Matthew Watts sorts Fallout's artificial beings into cyborgs, synths, and pure robots and tests each against a theory of the self: Locke's memory theory explains the Robobrain socialites of Vault 118 and Nick Valentine's implanted prewar past, Parfit's psychological continuity (identity as a matter of degree) makes the Old World Blues Think Tank a caricature of a self stretched too thin, and Putnam's machine-table functionalism with multiple realizability (the Red Menace holotape running on any hardware) undermines Covenant's SAFE test by making function, not substrate, the mark of a mind. DiMA's excision of his own guilty memory in Far Harbor shows identity as Evan Thompson's enactive process rather than stored recall, since pruning memories can maintain or improve the self-in-progress. For moral standing below full personhood, Nussbaum's capabilities approach fits Curie's flourishing (research, companionship, watching a sunset in a synth body) and Singer's sentience criterion fits Victor the Securitron's avoidance of harm, exposing 'substrate prejudice' as a robot analog of speciesism - down to ED-E's triumphant arrival on the West Coast.
- ch. 18 "Part 3 OVERSEER, OVERSEEN FALLOUT AND POLITICAL PHILOSOPHY" *(front)*
- ch. 19 "Notes" **·** John Locke — Natural Rights; The Social Contract; Utopia and Dystopia — John Irish reads the Amazon series' Vault 33 as Locke's Two Treatises staged and then betrayed: Overseer elections (Betty Pearson against Reg McPhee and Woody Thomas), town-council deliberation over captured Vault 32 raiders, and Lucy's 1950s-styled domesticity all mimic natural equality, consent of the governed, majority rule, and the preservation of property. Norm's discovery that every election is rigged in favor of cryo-preserved technocrats from Vault 31, that Lucy's marriage 'application' is engineered population control, and that Vault-Tec executives - including Lucy's mother Barb - voted to launch the Great War itself shows Locke's forms of consent, law, and rights preserved as empty ceremony. The chapter's argument is that Enlightenment liberalism stripped of transparency becomes authoritarian experiment management: consent becomes choreography in a commonwealth that is really a lab, right down to composting the raiders' bodies.
- ch. 20 "Notes" **·** Thomas Hobbes, Xunzi, John Locke, Sun Tzu — Just War Theory; The State of Nature; Human Nature Is Bad (and needs ritual) — Curry and Lang test 'War never changes' through Hobbes and Xunzi: the wasteland - where the Ghoul asks who ever elected the 'President' in 'The Trap' and Cooper Howard decays outside all political order - is Hobbes's state of nature of restless power-seeking, while Xunzi's claim that bad human nature needs ritual (li) and righteousness (yi) explains a world with no moral education, where Elder Cleric Quintus pairs noble talk with 'power is taken, not given'. Fallout's Great War then fails every just-war test: China's invasion of Alaska stretches just cause, the corporatist US lacks right authority, propaganda undid last resort, mutually assured destruction explodes proportionality, and Vault-Tec shelters plus battlefield mini-nukes breach discrimination and proportionality in bello - with Operation Anchorage's propaganda simulation illustrating the revisionist point that fighting cleanly cannot redeem an unjust war. China's 'righteous army' (yi bing) tradition - Sunzi's strategic restraint, Mohist defensive war, Mengzi's true king, Xunzi's punitive righteous war - leaves no trace beyond Captain Zao, so the war's constancy marks the failure of every philosophical attempt to bind war to justice.
- ch. 21 "Notes" **·** Miranda Fricker — Epistemic Injustice — Sergio Uruena argues the Brotherhood of Steel converts preservation into monopoly: Scribe Yearling buys prewar books as sacred relics, squads seize a settlement's water-purification rigs, and Knight Titus dies on a mission to recover 'a fucking toaster' - read through Langdon Winner's 'Do Artifacts Have Politics?' and Feenberg's contrast between authoritarian and democratic rationalizations of technology. Fricker's epistemic injustice does the central work: 'Blind Betrayal' makes Danse's testimony inadmissible once his synth identity is known (testimonial injustice), while hoarding diagnostic terminals, schematics, and archives strips wastelanders of the interpretive devices through which they understand their world (hermeneutical injustice, extended via Ihde's hermeneutic mediation of technology). Jasanoff's co-production explains how this gatekeeping builds social order itself - Maximus is trained into obedience alongside repair, with access tiered even inside the Brotherhood - against the Followers of the Apocalypse's open archives, closing the parallel between locked bunkers and Silicon Valley's gated source code.
- ch. 22 "Notes" **·** Plato — Allegory of the Cave; The Philosopher King — Dal Barco maps Lucy's arc in the Amazon series onto Plato's cave stage by stage: Vault 33's fake sky, curated history lessons, and Vault-Tec-managed information are the shadow-puppet show, her first step into the sun after Hank's abduction by Moldaver is the freed prisoner's blinding ascent, the hidden hospital vault tempts her (and Maximus, who wants to stay) like the return to the cave, and the truth - her father helped orchestrate the nuclear war, her mother is now a ghoul - plays the role the sun and the Good play in the Republic. Plato's claim that only the educated philosopher who has seen beyond the cave should govern (Republic 496a, 519a-d) explains why Lucy, unlike the frightened Norm or the tempted Maximus, goes back out into the wastes: she is becoming the philosopher-ruler whose hard-won knowledge enables Moldaver to restore power to the surface and begin a civilization no longer built on shadows. The chapter answers its own question - yes, it was worth leaving the vault for the truth.
- ch. 23 "Notes" **·** Democracy — Andrea Conque argues the adult-free ('Mungo-free') children of Little Lamplight are genuinely political: they socialize each other into distrust of grownups (mandatory exile at 16 to Big Town), elect mayors by secret popular vote - MacCready's punch against Princess was a coup made legitimate by subsequent election - and fill every office from doctor Lucy to teacher Joseph to Knock Knock, whose Vault Boy jokes 'keep the peace' against social isolation. Sherif's Robber's Cave experiment supplies the comparison: unlike the Eagles and Rattlers, Lamplighters avoid in-group conflict because the radiation-absorbing cave fungus and shared survival give them permanent superordinate goals, while Biwwy's high-damage 'wazer wifle' shows they deter oppression by being, not just posturing as, tough. Against MacCready's claim of 'pure anarchy', the chapter concludes Little Lamplight is the Capital Wasteland's most successful government - freer and better ordered than Megaton, Andale, Paradise Falls, or even the Republic of Dave.
- ch. 24 "Part 4 MORALITY AT THE END OF THE WORLD" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 25 "Notes" **·** John Stuart Mill — The Harm Principle; On Liberty — The chapter asks whether Fallout's chems should be banned and answers no on Millian grounds: chem addiction harms only the user (and is trivially curable at any wasteland doctor, unlike Cait's Psycho addiction in Fallout 4, which shows what real addiction actually costs), so the New California Republic's firing squads for chem dealers and Caesar's Legion's crucifixion of the smuggler Anders violate the Harm Principle. Combat chem use is self-protection, drugging a Charisma check harms no one because NPCs act on their own programmed preferences, and paternalism fares no better in a wasteland where a failed speech check can get you killed. The analysis then extends to real-world performance-enhancing drugs: permissible for soldiers fighting a just war, but coercive in sports, where PED users force clean athletes to choose between their own health risks.
- ch. 26 "Notes" **·** John Rawls, Immanuel Kant, Friedrich Nietzsche — The Golden Rule; The Veil of Ignorance — When Lucy shouts the Golden Rule at the Ghoul after he dunks her in a poison river to bait a gulper, the chapter takes her appeal seriously and grades the rival 'rules' of the wasteland (the Iron, Arsenic, Neon, and Bronze Rules) before arguing the Golden Rule needs St. Francis's 'in a similar situation' clause. Kant's criminal-versus-judge objection disqualifies the unqualified Platinum Rule of treating others as they want, Nietzsche's charge that mutual restraint denies life is parried, and Rawlsian imagining of ourselves in divergent positions explains why family partiality like Lucy's search for Hank is consistent with the rule. The chapter closes with Lucy's saving of the Ghoul retold as the Good Samaritan parable, extending the rule even to enemies.
- ch. 27 "Notes" **·** Aristotle, Immanuel Kant, Jeremy Bentham, Thomas Aquinas — Virtue Ethics (arete); The Categorical Imperative; Utilitarianism; Natural Law — The chapter maps four schools of ethics onto Fallout 4's Commonwealth: Aristotle's virtue ethics reads Preston Garvey's settlement defense as the mean between recklessness and cowardice and temperance as moderate chem use; Kant's Categorical Imperative is tested on stealing ammo in Concord and on the Battle of Bunker Hill choice to warn the Railroad, where loyal silence to Father fails universalization and treats synths merely as means. Aquinas's natural law ethics, keyed to the telos of power armor, workbenches, and Pip-Boys, condemns the Institute's creation of synths by analogy to the IVF debate while still demanding dignity for synths who already exist. Finally, Bentham's utilitarianism is the one framework under which Father's Institute — adaptive labor force, scientific progress, stability and order — can sincerely believe itself morally justified.
- ch. 28 "Notes" **·** Plato, Aristotle — Allegory of the Cave; Theory of Forms; Practical Wisdom (phronesis) — Tracing Fallout's own censorship history — the GURPS licensing cancellation threat, the invisibility of child NPCs in the European release, the moral panic after Columbine — the chapter asks whether it is wrong to simulate evil, framing the issue with Morgan Luck's gamer's dilemma. Plato's cave and the Forms ground the 'media impacts morals' worry: games are shadows of shadows whose simulated vice accumulates 'a large evil in our souls', which is also why Lucy's Vault upbringing leaves her underprepared for the wasteland's emotions. Against this, Aristotle's catharsis and Schulzke's claim that Fallout trains phronesis support depicting wrongdoing as practice for moral judgment, so the chapter concludes the right wrongs to depict are whichever maximize the simulation of choice, with Braun's player-controlled Tranquility Lane simulation as the dystopian counter-model.
- ch. 29 "Notes" **·** Immanuel Kant, Jeremy Bentham — The Categorical Imperative; Humanity as an End in Itself; Utilitarianism; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Weighing Vault-Tec's secret experiments — Vault 11's yearly human sacrifice, Vault 13's 200-year lockdown, the cryogenic freezing of the Sole Survivor — the chapter asks whether the ends could ever justify the vaults. On Kant's Universal Law and Humanity Formulations the answer is flatly no: dwellers were deceived into becoming mere means to data, whatever the hypothetical space-colonization motive that designer Tim Cain favors. Act and rule utilitarianism in Bentham's tradition give a more equivocal verdict — exposed deception would doom any future cooperation, yet safeguarding humanity might in extraordinary circumstances tip the calculus — and the chapter situates the games in real research-ethics history from the eugenics era to the Tuskegee study and the Belmont Report.
- ch. 30 "Part 5 A BEAUTIFUL DISASTER THE AESTHETICS OF FALLOUT" *(front)*
- ch. 31 "Notes" *(no index entry yet)* — The chapter asks why Fallout's genre fusion — RPG plus first-person shooter plus post-apocalyptic Western plus black comedy — coheres rather than collapses, and answers with Kendall Walton's 'Categories of Art': works carry standard, variable, and contra-standard properties relative to each category they belong to, so every added category raises the number of expectations a designer must meet. C. Thi Nguyen's account of games as prescribing striving play explains why obstacle-free software would not even count as a game, while a hypothetical forced Moby-Dick book-report quest illustrates a contra-standard property that 'does violence' to the category. Fallout's S.P.E.C.I.A.L. system implicitly promises 'play how you want', so weak melee builds or underused speech skills are aesthetic risk — the designer's gamble that players will recognize every fused genre at once.
- ch. 32 "Notes" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Baruch Spinoza, Simone de Beauvoir — Radical Freedom and Responsibility; Spinozist Necessity — The chapter argues modding is the deepest freedom Bethesda-era Fallout offers: Sartre and Beauvoir's absolute freedom and facticity explain why vanilla players merely pick among given priorities while modders inject new meaning into the game, constrained but not determined by the Creation Engine. Spinoza's causal determinism becomes a model of 'immanent freedom' — just as Spinoza's agent is free by understanding their place in Nature's web of causes, the modder is free by grasping how they fit into the load orders, plugin files, and tools of the Creation Kit. Simone Weil's ideal of self-directed labor culminates the argument: Fallout: London's five-year, unpaid total conversion (the Atta-Boy, Churchill the bulldog, the punk-anarchist Pistols) embodies the convergence of thought and action that Moira Brown's line about putting broken glass back together captures.
- ch. 33 "Notes" **·** Alan Turing, Thomas Nagel — The Turing Test; What Is It Like to Be a Bat? — The chapter argues Fallout 4's grip comes not from graphical fidelity but from first-person moral agency: deciding the fate of Gen 3 synths who do not know they are synths — supporting their escape, hunting them, or installing them as unwitting spies — forces the player through emotionally real moral deliberation. It connects this to Turing's worry about proving to another that you think ('it could've been me in their shoes') and to Nagel's bat, proposing that imagining what it is like to be a synth is a stepping stone toward ever stranger perspectives. Playing thus becomes the joy of actually doing philosophy — reawakening imagination, reshuffling our space of possibilities, and returning us to our own problems refreshed rather than merely escaped.
- ch. 34 "Notes" **·** Aristotle — Against the complaint that Fallout 3 is just Morrowind or Oblivion with a post-apocalyptic coat of paint, the chapter tests three aesthetic theories. Aristotle's didactic mimesis explains the tragic 'war never changes' framing and its moral lessons about resource wars and the AI president John Henry Eden, while Francis Hutcheson's 'uniformity amidst variety' locates Fallout's fun in the orderly S.P.E.C.I.A.L. rules and the 'crunch' of complex gear and crafting. Clive Bell's formalism finally secures the difference: Vault Boy's atomic-age cartoon style, the Pip-Boy's green monochrome, and the western motifs are formal properties no Elder Scrolls game shares, making Fallout 3 a formally distinct artwork to be appreciated for its own sake.
- ch. 35 "Notes" **·** Personal Identity — The chapter rereads Fallout 3's original Project Purity ending — the forced walk into the lethal radiation chamber while radiation-immune followers Fawkes, Sergeant RL-3, and Charon each refuse — as the game's worst case of narrative paradox: the pre-authored story compels an action the player-authored character narrative cannot accommodate. On the narrative-identity view (mounted against Galen Strawson's denial of narrativity), the Karma system and companions like Fawkes matter through the story the player tells, so an evil, greed-driven Lone Wanderer dying nobly is a 'bad death' that unravels the motivation for the entire playthrough. Dying well requires death to fit the story, and Sarah Lyons's substitutable sacrifice — or a last-page redemption epiphany — is what restores narrative coherence.
- ch. 36 "Part 6 APOCALYPTIC PSYCHOLOGY" *(front)*
- ch. 37 "Notes" **·** Carol Gilligan, Nel Noddings — The Ethics of Care; Caring as the Ground of Ethics — Opening from the author's own grief — she nearly stopped playing Fallout 4 because its murdered spouse and kidnapped son echoed the loss of her real infant son — the chapter applies the ethics of care of Gilligan, Noddings, and Tronto to Fallout's wasteland. Care relationships structure the games: the Fallout 3 father whose flight from Vault 101 propels the plot, Dogmeat's loyal responsiveness as relational reciprocity, and the Fallout 4 son Shaun, where caring for your child collides with caring for a Commonwealth his Institute endangers. Settlement-building, faction choice (Lyons's Brotherhood handing out clean water versus the Railroad's liberation of synths), and even the Black Widow perk path that saves Megaton all become care-laden moral acts — care as protest against the wasteland's ethos of bare survival.
- ch. 38 "Notes" **·** Thomas Hobbes — The Meaning of Life; The State of Nature — The chapter asks why audiences relish stories of civilization's end and answers with psychology: the wasteland is Hobbes's 'nasty, brutish, and short' (raiders abducting Hank, Vault-Tec's experiments on dwellers), yet Ernest Becker's death-denial and Maslow's hierarchy of needs explain the appeal — fiction lets us confront mortality safely while watching characters meet needs for security, control, and meaning. The show's scenes carry the argument: the Vault 33 Council suppressing Lucy's search party trades freedom for safety, Maximus letting Titus bleed out reclaims control of his destiny, and Lucy's gift of life-sustaining vials to the unconscious Ghoul models hope and retained humanity. Apocalypse originally means 'an uncovering', so these narratives reveal what viewers value and let them rehearse their own endings.
- ch. 39 "Notes" **·** Aristotle, David Hume — Why is living in Fallout's hell — ghouls pouring through the ceiling, radroach dinners, Kellogg, Pickman, Vault-Tec — fun? The chapter tests the two classic solutions to the puzzle of dark art: Aristotle's katharsis works for Oedipus but stumbles because playing is actively enjoyable rather than unpleasant purgation, and RPGs blur feeling pity for a character with fear for oneself; Hume's 'Of Tragedy' account, on which artistic skill transforms negative emotions into pleasure by mixing them with beauty, fails because the wasteland is deliberately ugly, not beautiful, and Hume never explains how the transformation works. Neither the ancient nor the modern answer quite succeeds, so the 2,500-year-old puzzle is left open, like the best unfinished quests.
- ch. 40 "Index" *(notes)*
- ch. 41 "End User License Agreement" *(front)*

## Fashion – Philosophy for Everyone - Thinking with Style

- ch. 1 "FASHION - PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Globalization and Contemporary Art" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "FASHION - PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "FASHION - PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "FASHION - PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "FASHION - PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "FASHION - PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "FASHION - PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "FASHION - PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(notes)*
- ch. 10 "FASHION - PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Peter Singer, Michel Foucault — Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Objectification; The Drowning Child (obligation to aid) — The editors' introduction argues that fashion, despite philosophers' contempt for it, raises serious questions in aesthetics, ethics, identity, and political philosophy. It surveys the volume's contributors: fashionability as an expert-plus-masses property (Prinz/Farennikova), Zangwill's alienation thesis, Russell on 'cool' as aesthetic virtue, the objectification of women through Barbie play and sweatshops, and Singer's demand (via the drowning-child argument) that spare money go to poverty relief rather than trendy clothes. It uses concrete cases — Ugg boots, the National Airlines 'Fly me' campaign, Tommie Smith and John Carlos's 1968 Olympic salute, foot binding — to show fashion carries the zeitgeist and can both liberate and enslave.
- ch. 11 "FASHION - PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 12 "FASHION - PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Moral Relativism — Farennikova and Prinz ask what makes something fashionable, arguing through thought experiments (Calvin Klein jeans, khakis, Robinson Crusoe's leather jacket, the accidental thrift-store punk, Gang Green, the mod couple) that four conditions must hold: many people wear the look, recognized experts endorse it, wearers intend to conform, and they regard it as having aesthetic value. They distinguish being fashionable from being merely popular or 'cool,' show fashionability is relative to group and to time, and note fashion must change to serve its signaling purpose — the Amish are 'intentionally unfashionable.'
- ch. 13 "FASHION - PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Thomas Nagel, Bernard Williams — Alienation; What Is It Like to Be a Bat? — Zangwill argues that 'fashionable' is a relational, sociological property (like 'famous'), yet things also directly *appear* fashionable or 'cool' to us in first-person perceptual experience as if coolness were a simple, non-relational quality (illustrated by seeing 1965 Mick Jagger as 'groovy'). He claims these two aspects — the third-person sociological concept and the first-person phenomenological one — cannot be united, so the concept of fashion is 'schizoid.' Invoking Nagel on consciousness and Bernard Williams's charge of alienation in Kantian and utilitarian ethics, he concludes fashion involves a projective illusion and is a genuine source of alienation.
- ch. 14 "FASHION - PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Aristotle — Virtue Ethics (arete); Virtue Ethics — Russell argues that being 'cool' is an aesthetic virtue distinct from being fashionable: the fashionable but anxious tryhard 'Allegra' fails to be cool precisely because her studied effort to win social approval is visible, whereas figures like James Dean or Marlene Dietrich seem effortlessly cool. Drawing an analogy to happiness and to moral virtues like compassion, he treats cool as a *self-effacing* goal — achieved only by caring about aesthetic matters for their own sake rather than for status, the way a genuinely virtuous person is moved directly by others' welfare rather than by a desire to be seen as good. He uses a Simpsons exchange (Homer, Marge, Bart, Lisa on what makes someone cool) to show that not-caring alone is insufficient.
- ch. 15 "FASHION - PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 16 "FASHION - PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Theodor Adorno — The Aura of the Artwork; Commodity Fetishism — Hamilton defends a dual account of design — solving functional problems *and* improving look or feel through style — against both the reduction of design to fashion/styling and the modernist reduction of it to pure engineering, using the London Design Museum dispute (Dyson vs. Rawsthorn), the Citroën 2CV, Dyson cyclonic cleaners, and Pye's notion of 'useless work' (directly functionless work like a jewelled sword handle). Turning to fashion in clothing, he draws on Simmel's analysis of fashion as simultaneously imitation and differentiation, and on Adorno's account of autonomous art, to argue fashion can never be autonomous art because it is inescapably functional and commodified, yet still counts as an art with classic and ephemeral, high and popular forms.
- ch. 17 "FASHION - PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Karl Marx — Commodity Fetishism; The Male Gaze; Objectification — Freeland analyzes perfume advertising — chiefly Chanel's mini-film campaigns for No. 5 (Kidman/Luhrmann, Tautou/Jeunet) versus the younger, jauntier Coco Mademoiselle ads with Keira Knightley — to trace how fragrance is sold 'aspirationally' by tying scent to lifestyle, romance, and couture. She examines male-fragrance ads (Bronson's Mandom, Chanel Egoiste, Old Spice's Isaiah Mustafa) and celebrity scents fronted by women of color, exposing gender anxiety, colonialism, and near-explicit racism in perfume marketing. Invoking commodity fetishism and feminist critique, she argues the ads manipulate female desire but do not make consumers passive dupes: blog parodies and critical commentary show active resistance to the fantasy.
- ch. 18 "FASHION - PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Andy Clark, David Chalmers — The Extended Mind; Embodiment (the lived body) — Brunstein surveys emerging wearable technology — solar-charging jackets, the M-Dress cell-phone dress, invisibility cloaks, the Hug Shirt, Diana Eng's Heartbeat Hoodie — framed by superhero costumes (Wonder Woman's lasso, Iron Man's exoskeleton) and Haraway's 'Cyborg Manifesto.' She argues that as garments start to *do* things and integrate aesthetically, the grotesque cyborg becomes the glamorous 'supermodel' cyborg, and that this reshapes how we construct the self. Her central philosophical lens is Clark and Chalmers's 'extended mind' / active externalism (with the Tetris example): if clothing becomes a functional extension of cognition, our garments' capabilities become part of a reconstructed self.
- ch. 19 "FASHION - PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 20 "FASHION - PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** John Stuart Mill — On Liberty; Authenticity; Civil Disobedience — Yim argues that clothing is a primary vehicle for autonomy, authenticity, and moral solidarity. Drawing on Mill's *On Liberty* and 'experiments in living,' he treats a teenager testing modest-vs-expressive dress against her mother, and 'Fashion Freaks' patterns for wheelchair users, as cases where wardrobe choice actualizes self-determination. He then reads dress as an emblem of communal solidarity and critique — the 1968 Smith/Carlos Olympic salute, the Israeli Kibbutz and Amish plain dress (with Rumspringa as a test of authenticity), gang colors, hippie androgyny, vegan 'cruelty-free' clothing, gay Levi's 501s, and Lubna Hussein's trouser trial in Sudan — concluding 'we are what we wear.'
- ch. 21 "FASHION - PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein, Judith Butler — Gender Performativity; Feminism — Brennan argues that fashion sits at the boundary of public and private and is central to the 'sexual citizen' and the politics of recognition for gay, lesbian, and bisexual people. Using Hanson on philosophy's gendered fear of fashion and Phelan/Weeks/Walker on visibility as a political strategy, she shows that queer identities (femme, boi, hanky codes) are communicative and require a community that reads them — offering a 'private hanky' argument echoing Wittgenstein's private-language point. She draws on her own experience (labrys earring, later bisexual invisibility) and on Butler-style gender performativity to argue visibility empowers recognition but marginalizes those, like femmes, who cannot be read.
- ch. 22 "FASHION - PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Immanuel Kant, Simone de Beauvoir, Mary Wollstonecraft — Objectification; Humanity as an End in Itself; Woman as the Other — Ashwell and Langton ask whether fashion is a 'badge of slavery' (Wollstonecraft), opening with Socrates in the *Phaedo* disdaining adornment and Rousseau's girl who becomes 'her own doll,' and using Kant's humanity formula and Nussbaum's seven-feature account of objectification. Focusing on denial of autonomy in ordinary followers, they distinguish the 'Servile Fashionista' (obeys experts) from the 'Sensible Fashionista' (follows what looks good), then argue that via Johnston's 'authority of affect' even the sensible one is under an *epistemic* bondage: fashion's extrinsic social pull is misperceived as the garment's intrinsic goodness, a projective illusion (like the Müller-Lyer) that on a Kantian view undermines agency and masks misogynist or racist norms such as straightened hair or the 'lotus feet' of stilettos.
- ch. 23 "FASHION - PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Michel Foucault, Jeremy Bentham — Discipline and Docile Bodies; The Panopticon (surveillance); Objectification — Collins analyzes paradigmatic Barbie play — dressing the doll and identifying with her as an idealized future self, from the 1959 TV ad to BarbieGirls.com — arguing it trains girls to define identity through fashionable bodily self-presentation for a male gaze (Brumberg's 'body project'). Using Sandra Bartky's feminist reworking of Foucault's *Discipline and Punish*, she treats beauty norms as a discipline producing 'docile bodies' whose self-surveillance internalizes a 'panoptical male connoisseur' (Bentham's Panopticon), offering cycles of pleasure and pain that bind women without any single sovereign to blame. Rather than banning Barbie (the failed 'Happy to be Me' doll), she proposes subversive play — Plump and Proud Barbie, Mursi lip-plate Barbie — as Heyes-style 'practices of freedom.'
- ch. 24 "FASHION - PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 25 "FASHION - PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Aristotle, Adam Smith — Cynicism (living according to nature); Objectification; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Pierlott examines sweatshops in the garment industry through the lens of ancient Cynicism (Antisthenes, the Stoics' Zeno of Citium and his thieving slave), using the Cynic critique of fashion-driven consumption to strip legitimacy from the demand that feeds sweatshops. He rebuts the 'inevitability argument' (comparing it to excusing the Holocaust as inevitable) and the free-choice defense using Aristotle's category of 'mixed' action — sweated workers' consent is coerced by desperate alternatives — and argues, via Adam Smith on the undervaluing of labor, that giant retailers like Wal-Mart distort natural labor prices. He stresses that sweatshops overwhelmingly exploit young women, tying objectification of women as fashion consumers to their oppression as producers.
- ch. 26 "FASHION - PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Peter Singer, Nel Noddings — The Drowning Child (obligation to aid); Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Caring as the Ground of Ethics — Cassidy, through an interior monologue of a shopper eyeing Indonesian-made sandals, weighs individual responsibility for consumerism. She contrasts Singer's drowning-child duty to relieve suffering with Pogge's 'you break it, you buy it' claim that the Global North caused Southern poverty, then uses Scheffler's 'restricted' common-sense responsibility and Cannon's butterfly-effect virtues (replacing the American Dream's acquisitiveness with interconnectedness). Adapting Claudia Card, she distinguishes three senses of responsibility — credit (backward-looking blame), management (of one's lifestyle), and care-taking — and, invoking Noddings's ethics of care, argues that since globalization's impact is uncertain we should shift from assigning blame to caring *about* distant workers, buying less out of boredom-driven habit.
- ch. 27 "FASHION - PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Immanuel Kant, Simone de Beauvoir — Judgment of Taste and the Sublime; Virtue Ethics — La Caze rehabilitates fashion against Kant's own dismissal of it (in the *Anthropology*) as vanity and imitation, arguing that Kant's aesthetics actually supply the tools to value it. She applies his account of the disinterested judgment of taste, free vs. adherent beauty, and 'genius' (originality that provides an exemplary model — Chanel's freer dressing, Dior's New Look, punk DIY, the Sartorialist's street style) to both the making and the wearing of clothes. Drawing on Kant's claim that the beautiful is a symbol of the morally good and the 'virtues of social intercourse,' and on Iris Marion Young's pleasures of touch, bonding, and fantasy, she argues fashion can be a proto-moral, cosmopolitan force, countering Plato's, Beauvoir's, and Svendsen's denigration.
- ch. 28 "FASHION - PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(notes)*

## Fatherhood – Philosophy for Everyone - The Dao of Daddy

- ch. 1 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 11 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 12 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Augustine of Hippo — Augustine on Time; Living in the Present — Davison uses St. Augustine's claim from the Confessions that only the present moment is real – past and future exist only as memory and anticipation – to argue that a father should stop missing his children's lives by fixating on past regrets or future worries. Drawing on Steindl-Rast and Thomas Sterner, he contrasts mechanical 'clock time' with quality-driven 'experienced time' and recommends process-focus over end-product focus, illustrated with lying beside his sleeping daughter Grace and dreading bedtime routines with his sons.
- ch. 13 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Martin Heidegger, Plato — Dasein (being-there); Being-Toward-Death; Immortality and the Soul — Allred applies Heidegger's Being and Time – the world as a meaningful context of projects, the hammer-as-tool example, and death as what makes projects meaningful – to contrast the father's world with the 'man-child' world of Seth Rogen's Judd Apatow characters (especially Knocked Up). He argues fatherhood reveals the fragility of all projects, using Plato's account of seeking immortality through children and Paul Celan's poem 'Epitaph for François' about his stillborn son to show that after a child is born the parent's world is forever haunted by the possibility of its emptiness.
- ch. 14 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Personal Identity — Fink-Jensen, an anthropologist, uses narrative analysis and rites-of-passage theory (Van Gennep's separation/liminal/reincorporation stages) to examine how men construct the internal identity of 'father.' Drawing on interviews with first-time dads (Marc, Jim, Craig, Steve), she argues that because men lack the biological markers of pregnancy they feel alienated from the transition, and that Western 'couvade' activities – attending prenatal classes and appointments, hearing the heartbeat – plus telling birth stories function as rituals that cement fatherhood as an identity.
- ch. 15 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Albert Camus, Jean-Paul Sartre — The Meaning of Life; The Myth of Sisyphus; Existence Precedes Essence — Barnwell argues that fatherhood can constitute the ultimate meaning of a man's life, answering the paradox that a meaningful life 'all about the kids' seems not to be about the father by noting fatherly acts are still the father's own freely chosen activities. He confronts Camus's Myth of Sisyphus – the worry that childrearing is endless pointless rock-pushing across generations – by insisting children, unlike rocks, have intrinsic value, and invokes the Sartrean existentialist idea that choosing an activity confers value on it (illustrated by his buying oatmeal for 'Sally').
- ch. 16 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 17 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Aristotle, Immanuel Kant, Friedrich Nietzsche — Virtue Ethics (arete); The Golden Mean; Practical Wisdom (phronesis); The Ethics of Care — Nease lays out a game plan for raising ethical children, using Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics – eudaimonia as a flourishing life, virtue as the mean between extremes, and the master-skills of practical wisdom and self-control – as the core, mocking 'character counts' posters as no substitute for early habit-formation. He folds in Kant's motive of duty, Nietzsche's will-to-power (glossed via Janet Jackson's Control) as the drive to master oneself, and argues empathy (Theodor Lipps' inner imitation), self-awareness, concentration (Husserl's foreground/background), and a respect for justice must be cultivated to 'stack the deck' toward a good adult.
- ch. 18 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Nel Noddings, Carol Gilligan — The Ethics of Care; Caring as the Ground of Ethics — Terjesen defends a distinctively paternal form of caring against care ethics' maternal bias. Reviewing Kohlberg's moral-development scale, Gilligan's justice-vs-care orientations, and Noddings' 'engrossment' model of caring, he argues these frameworks make it seem fathers like his own undemonstrative Norwegian dad don't care. He reconceives 'paternalism' (via Gerald Dworkin's soft paternalism and examples like Darth Vader and Bambi's father) as tough-love caring focused on the child's future self, complementary to maternal caring and distinct from the impersonal justice orientation.
- ch. 19 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** John Locke, Jean-Jacques Rousseau — Tabula Rasa; Natural Goodness and Corruption by Society; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Florell and Wilson, psychologists, survey parenting through the nature–nurture debate, casting Locke's tabula rasa against Rousseau's noble-savage view of children, alongside Piaget and Kohlberg on moral development and Walter Mischel's marshmallow experiment on impulse control. They map the four parenting styles (authoritarian, permissive, uninvolved, authoritative) on responsiveness and control, argue authoritative parenting is optimal, and advise fathers to adjust discipline to each child's innate temperament rather than enforce rigid gender roles.
- ch. 20 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Immanuel Kant, Aristotle — Autonomy of the Will; The Social Contract — Swindler argues the ideal of fatherhood is to engender autonomy – a blend of independence and interdependence – in one's children. Treating fatherhood as an abstract social role (compared to a baseball pitcher) constituted by collective intentions, he uses Kant's 'ought implies can' to link accountability to autonomy and his constitutive-vs-regulative rules (the promise to take his son fishing) to show fathers must teach children to reflect on the concept of a rule, not merely follow it, modeling self-governance rather than authoritarian control.
- ch. 21 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 22 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre — Authenticity; Bad Faith; Existence Precedes Essence — Collins-Cavanaugh asks what makes a 'real' father, framing it as authenticity. He pairs Sartre's existentialist authenticity – free choice, anxiety, and the bad faith of adopting others' ready-made plans, with 'real cookie' and Derek Jeter examples – against Charles Taylor's dialogical authenticity, in which choices are made within a 'horizon of meaning' set by others. He argues authentic fatherhood combines both: a father must freely own his choices (provider dad vs nurturer dad) while grounding them in his child's needs, concluding he'll go bake cookies with his son Connor.
- ch. 23 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Confucius, Plato — Li (ritual propriety); Ren (benevolence/humaneness); Xiao (filial piety); Virtue Ethics — Komasinski explains Confucius's advice to fathers through three ideas: ritual (li, glossed as 'cultural grammar' – engagement rings, saying 'I love you'), excellence/virtue as excelling at relating to others (ren, 'co-humanity'), and wisdom. Using the Analects' 'Upright Gong' passage (fathers and sons cover for each other) beside Plato's Euthyphro, he shows Confucius prizes the father-son bond over law, and reads the parable of the prodigal son and Tu Wei-ming's 'personality ideal' to argue fathers should lead by virtuous example rather than punishment.
- ch. 24 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Authenticity; Xiao (filial piety) — Balogun presents a traditional Yoruba-African conception of 'authentic' fatherhood as a holistic role – spiritual, moral, metaphysical, physical, and material – within an extended, collective family where paternal uncles and grandparents share fathering. He stresses moral education through alo apamo riddles, moonlight folktales (the tortoise, his wife Yonibo, and the priest), and proverbs, and argues contemporary fathers everywhere should learn from this model: total rather than piecemeal responsibility, storytelling over mere cartoon-watching, and treating children as 'ours' rather than 'mine.'
- ch. 25 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Thomas Aquinas, Martin Buber — Cardinal and Theological Virtues; I–Thou Relation; The Ethics of Care — Mattern argues the heart of good fatherhood is mercy – 'faithful, compassionate love' – as an attitude of the heart, not a technique. Drawing on Aquinas's account of habitus and his ranking of mercy among the greatest virtues, plus Gabriel Marcel's 'creative fidelity,' Solovyov, Jean Vanier, and Karol Wojtyla on love as affirming another's value, he contrasts remote, justice-oriented fathers with empathic ones. He illustrates with overlapping Venn-diagram circles of father and child and with responding to his 7-year-old son's shoplifting through listening and repair rather than spanking.
- ch. 26 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(front)*
- ch. 27 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Plato, Aristotle — Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); The Myth of Sisyphus; Allegory of the Cave — Baron, a new father, weighs whether to let his son Maxwell consume violent popular media (from Halloween to SpongeBob, Grand Theft Auto, and Schindler's List) by testing three hypotheses. He uses Plato's Republic warning against exposing the young to stories they can't interpret for the 'causal hypothesis'; Aristotle's Poetics on learning pleasure from painful images for the 'edification hypothesis'; and Aristotle's account of tragic catharsis (homeopathic vs allopathic purging) for the 'catharsis hypothesis,' concluding that context matters more than ratings and that fathers watching alongside their children are the decisive influence.
- ch. 28 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Socrates — Bad Faith; Five Faces of Oppression; The Unexamined Life — Owen argues that parenting is a political act and that fathers, especially privileged white heterosexual ones, should raise anti-oppressive children. Prompted by the question 'Daddy, why is her face brown?', he uses the social construction of racialized difference and the mutually constitutive culture–individual relation (the Zulu maxim 'a person is a person through other persons') to reject color-blindness. He prescribes knowledge, practice, and character steps, invokes Socrates' 'unexamined life' for relentless self-reflection, and answers the worry about raising 'alienated' children via Sartre's bad faith – authentic existence means accepting responsibility for reproducing or resisting injustice.
- ch. 29 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Aristotle — Autonomy of the Will; Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Personal Identity — Carreras, writing as a son, examines the ethical tension in the father-son relationship between using a father's inevitable influence and fostering the son's autonomy. Opening with Aristotle on parents loving children as 'something of himself,' he uses the case of Jerry (a lawyer) pressuring his son Jose (who loves 18th-century English literature) to argue for a 'foundational view' of autonomy – that the best life is one embraced 'from the inside,' likened to seeing without a value-blindfold – and advises fathers to ask 'Is this what he truly wants, or what I want?'
- ch. 30 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** John Rawls — Autonomy of the Will; The Meaning of Life; Faith and Reason — Morgan asks when it is wrong for fathers to impose their ideals on children, arguing that forcing activities (like violin practice) risks alienation because meaningful pursuits must be embraced 'from the inside.' Using Robert Noggle's Rawls-derived hierarchy of goods (basic, pre-primary, primary, secondary, specialized), he grants fathers may guide toward universal goods but rejects Noggle's claim that parents may impose a full religious worldview, favoring David Archard's weaker right to 'guide family life' while respecting the child's growing capacity to choose – permitting compelled practice of talents only in light of the child's emerging identity.
- ch. 31 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" **·** Aristotle, Socrates — Virtue Ethics (arete); The Unexamined Life; Meaning in Life — Austin offers practical wisdom for fathers of daughters, grounding fatherly obligations in daughters' fundamental interests and focusing on self-knowledge, moral development, and the common good. Invoking the Delphic 'know thyself,' Charles Taylor's 'strong evaluations,' and Aristotle's view that virtue grows through practice, he explains how to cultivate humility (including 'existential humility' before nature), courage (including self-sacrifice), and wisdom (curiosity, versatility, critical thinking) – e.g., analyzing advertising assumptions and limiting screen time – so a daughter can flourish and contribute to the common good.
- ch. 32 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(notes)*
- ch. 33 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(notes)*
- ch. 34 "FATHERHOOD PHILOSOPHY FOR EVERYONE" *(notes)*

## Final Fantasy and Philosophy - The Ultimate Walkthrough

- ch. 1 "section-1" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Final Fantasy and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Final Fantasy and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Final Fantasy and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Final Fantasy and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Final Fantasy and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Final Fantasy and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Final Fantasy and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Benjamin Chandler argues that Final Fantasy VII realizes Roland Barthes's ideal of the writerly text: its nine playable characters carry bundles of preset signifiers (Cloud's spiky hair, Aeris's Cetra fate) that players multiply through customization, so each player produces the game's narrative for themselves. He shows how identification with Cloud makes the Sector 7 plate drop feel like the player's own moral burden, how Cloud's identity crisis and Aeris's death force players to reinterpret the whole text, and how Crisis Core (playing the doomed Zack), the passive film Advent Children (the least writerly text), and Dirge of Cerberus (Vincent) add further entry points into the FFVII universe.
- ch. 9 "Final Fantasy and Philosophy" **·** Michel Foucault, Friedrich Nietzsche, Immanuel Kant — Master and Slave Morality; The Death of God; The Übermensch; Power/Knowledge — Kylie Prymus asks whether Kefka is truly mad, using Foucault's Madness and Civilization to argue that madness is a label society applies to dismiss what it cannot control, so players and heroes refute Kefka with the ad hominem 'you're crazy' rather than answering him. She then reads Kefka's 'monument to non-existence' through Nietzsche: his godlike discovery that existence has no justification echoes the death of God, our horror at him reflects slave morality's relabeling of noble strength as evil, and the heroes' appeal to purposes he lacks fails because existence is only a hypothetical imperative. Kefka ends up a failed Ubermensch who overthrows the gods but cannot overcome nihilism, leaving the World of Ruin heroes the genuinely Nietzschean task of creating meaning where none is given.
- ch. 10 "Final Fantasy and Philosophy" **·** David Hume — Alex Nuttall applies Hume's essay on the standard of taste to video games as art: beauty is not in objects (Sin's FFX rendering is just digital information) but in trained sentiment, yet quality differences are real, as the gap between Final Fantasy VII and the broken Superman 64 shows. Using the Malboro's Bad Breath as a metaphor for the confused, blinded, charmed state of novice judges, he lays out Hume's remedies of practice, repeated viewing, comparison, and reason against bias, and defends Hume against the circularity charge that good judges and masterpieces like FFVII define each other.
- ch. 11 "Final Fantasy and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 12 "Final Fantasy and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Jay Foster runs a conceptual analysis of Marlene's opening description of the Lifestream in Advent Children, showing it can be read literally (a holist-organicist living planet, AVALANCHE's view) or metaphorically (a reductionist-mechanist aggregate of energies, Shinra's and Hojo's view). Comparing Dr. Cid's Gaia in The Spirits Within with James Lovelock and Lynn Margulis's Gaia hypothesis, understood as a homeostatic cybernetic system rather than a living spirit, he argues that General Hein's skepticism toward Cid's vitalist holism is warranted, that mechanism and reductionism have the stronger empirical record, and that Dr. Cid's Gaia belongs entirely in the world of Final Fantasy.
- ch. 13 "Final Fantasy and Philosophy" **·** Anthropocentrism; Ecofeminism — Jason Blahuta reads The Spirits Within as staging the debate between General Hein's anthropocentric, domination-of-nature outlook (traced to the Genesis tradition and thinkers like Bacon and Descartes) and Aki's Gaia-hypothesis and ecofeminist view of nature as a self-regulating whole. Hein's repeated firings of the Zeus Cannon, which injure rather than kill the Phantoms and doom New York, dramatize what happens when we meddle with a self-regulating Gaia, while the chapter's survey of intrinsic-value versus enlightened-self-interest accounts of nature's worth ends with Lovelock's bleak verdict that humanity has become a tumor inside Gaia and must choose to be a benign or a malignant one.
- ch. 14 "Final Fantasy and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, John Stuart Mill, Aristotle, Friedrich Nietzsche — Objectification; Humanity as an End in Itself; Utilitarianism; Virtue Ethics (arete) — Robert Arp and Sarah Fisk test three moral theories against objectification in Final Fantasy: Kant's ban on using rational beings as mere means (Kuja's manipulation of Queen Brahne, the Geostigma children of Advent Children), Mill's greatest-happiness principle (Yuna's and Aeris's self-sacrifice), and Aristotle's character-based virtue ethics (Hojo and Sephiroth as vicious, Aeris as virtuous). They argue Kantianism generates a stalemate between 'moral sanctity' and 'moral autonomy' camps over cases like Guardian Force summoning in FFVIII, and utilitarianism a stalemate over mandatory self-sacrifice, leaving virtue ethics, Aristotelian or Nietzschean, as the view that escapes both, ending with the question 'Aeris or a Cloud?'
- ch. 15 "Final Fantasy and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 16 "Final Fantasy and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Hobbes, John Stuart Mill, Aristotle — Ethical Egoism; Higher and Lower Pleasures; The Golden Mean; The Social Contract — Greg Littmann asks what sort of life is worth living by holding Final Fantasy protagonists and FFXI Online players up to three standards. Hobbes's egoism and defense of absolute monarchy condemn the altruistic, authority-defying heroes (AVALANCHE, the rebels of FFII and FFVI) while partly approving the gil-grubbing, rule-breaking FFXI player; Mill's utilitarianism praises the world-saving heroes but condemns grinding down beastmen and rates most gaming a lower pleasure; Aristotle's function argument and golden mean find heroes like Yuna immoderately self-sacrificial yet credit ordinary FFXI adventurers with genuinely virtuous moderation, helping others while keeping their own interests in view.
- ch. 17 "Final Fantasy and Philosophy" **·** Harry Frankfurt, Thomas Aquinas — Free Will vs Determinism; Fate and Foreknowledge — Nicolas Michaud asks whether the prophesied Four Warriors of Light of Final Fantasy I deserve thanks if the Sage Lukahn's foreknowledge meant they could not do otherwise, framing their plight with the problem of divine foreknowledge and Aquinas's answer that God stands outside time. He then deploys Frankfurt's preemption cases, Garland ready to mind-control you either way, to show moral responsibility can survive without alternative possibilities, but concludes the Warriors may still be unworthy of praise because they know they are predestined, a worry that spreads to us through Phineas Gage-style biological determinism: if our characters are hardwired, neither we nor Garland deserve praise or blame.
- ch. 18 "Final Fantasy and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 19 "Final Fantasy and Philosophy" **·** Martin Heidegger — The Question Concerning Technology — Jonah Mitropoulos reads Final Fantasy VII through Shinto: the Lifestream is a kami-filled world of spiritual energy pervading nature, and Jenova's corrupting influence works like tsumi, pollution contracted without intent that requires water purification, as when Aerith's Lifestream cures Geostigma in Advent Children and Lucrecia retreats to a waterfall. He reads Jenova as an allegory of Western, Christian, capitalist influence on Japan, citing Aizawa Seishisai's fear of ideological invasion, Wutai's postwar-style disarmament, and the disease metaphors both sides use. The chapter closes with Heidegger's Question Concerning Technology: Shinra treats the planet as a standing resource, and only a syncretic way of the kami reconciles technology and nature.
- ch. 20 "Final Fantasy and Philosophy" **·** Karl Marx — Historical Materialism; Class Struggle — Michel Beaulieu reads the series through Marx's materialist conception of history: the 'Proletariat Patrons' Movement' sign in FFIX's Alexandria, the job system as a class system, and Shinra dropping the Sector 7 plate on its own laboring class all instantiate class struggle, while Vaan's life in Rabanastre shows social existence determining consciousness. But he argues the heroes are not Marxist revolutionaries, since Cecil ends up King of Baron and the Light Warriors leave feudal structures intact, and the Moogles, the series' true proletarian serfs, never revolt, because no Final Fantasy world has yet matured to the stage where the Marxian transformation becomes possible.
- ch. 21 "Final Fantasy and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Niccolò Machiavelli — Master and Slave Morality; The Ends Justify the Means; Virtù and Fortuna; The Death of God — David Hahn argues that Spira's Yevon religion is exactly what Machiavelli and Nietzsche warned against: hope for the Calm and a Sin-free future makes Spirans otherworldly and passive, forbidding machina, revering self-sacrificing Summoner martyrs, and blaming themselves for Sin, just as just-war Christianity left Machiavelli's princes passive and dependent on mercenaries, paralleled by Seymour and Kinoc standing idle at Operation Mi'ihen. Nietzsche's slave revolt in morality explains Yevon's sanctification of meekness and death, while Machiavelli's account of religion as a political instrument, from Numa to Scipio's oath, explains why the lie of Yevon held Spira together, and why its collapse may bring either Nietzschean rebirth or Machiavellian chaos.
- ch. 22 "Final Fantasy and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 23 "Final Fantasy and Philosophy" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Friedrich Nietzsche, Martin Heidegger — Bad Faith; Authenticity; The Death of God; Existence Precedes Essence — Christopher Wood reads Cloud's arc as an existentialist quest: Heidegger's thrownness in Cloud's tumble off the Midgar train and his initial 'It's not my problem' denial of being-in-the-world; Nietzsche's death of God in Mako-powered Midgar's nihilism, with AVALANCHE as a call for revaluation of values and Sephiroth as a false aspirant god; and Sartre's bad faith in Cloud 'playing at being a SOLDIER,' a persona borrowed wholesale from Zack along with his sword and mercenary dream. Through the Lifestream reunion with Tifa, Cloud faces his fabricated past, admits 'I never was in SOLDIER,' and turns to authenticity by resolving to live without pretending, which Wood presents as the player's own existential task.
- ch. 24 "Final Fantasy and Philosophy" **·** Epicurus — Death Is Nothing to Us; Epicurean Pleasure (ataraxia); The Meaning of Life — Kevin Fitzpatrick uses Vivi Orunitia and the self-aware Black Mages of Final Fantasy IX, who call death 'stopping,' to ask whether the fear of death is justified, answering that death harms only insofar as it frustrates a person's values, such as Vivi's morality, his thirst for knowledge, and his care for others' happiness. He brings in Epicurus's argument that death is nothing to us because the dead feel no pain, casts Necron, who wants to return all life to a painless 'zero world' because fear of death has corrupted it, as a darker Epicurean, and weighs the deprivation objection, death cheats us of future goods like Chocobo Hot and Cold, against the puzzle of when death's harm could even occur.
- ch. 25 "Final Fantasy and Philosophy" **·** Saul Kripke, Bertrand Russell, John Searle — Naming and Necessity (rigid designators) — Andrew Russo and Jason Southworth use the recurring Cid in every Final Fantasy game and the apparently identical Cloud Strife of FFVII and FF Tactics to test theories of reference: direct reference theory trivializes informative identity claims like 'Squall is Leon,' and Russell's descriptivism, names as abbreviations for definite descriptions, handles Cid but stumbles on Cloud, whose FF Tactics birthday makes him an Aquarius rather than a Leo, and on a Spira where Schmidt, not Braska, actually defeated Sin. Kripke's causal-historical theory, with its dubbing ceremonies and name-transference chains illustrated by Yuna tracking the Tidus lookalike in X-2, answers the modal challenge but faces the qua problem of 'Balamb Garden has moved,' leading the authors to call for a reflective equilibrium between the two theories.
- ch. 26 "Final Fantasy and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 27 "Final Fantasy and Philosophy" *(notes)*

## Food & Philosophy - Eat, Think, and Be Merry

- ch. 1 "Cover Page" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Title Page" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Inhaltsverzeichnis" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 4 "In memory of" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 5 "A tavola non s’invecchia" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 6 "Preface" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Chapter 1" **·** Plato, Aristotle, Epicurus — Food Ethics; Epicurean Pleasure (ataraxia); Aristotle on Friendship (philia) — Verene argues that cooking marks the transition from nature to culture, reading Plato's Protagoras myth of Prometheus (fire and the practical arts) and the Republic's claim that a city's first need is food as evidence that humans alone 'eat' rather than 'feed.' Tracing Greco-Roman banquets, Vico's account of Roman dining, and Brillat-Savarin's Physiology of Taste, he connects the ordered courses of a proper meal to Aristotle's view of imagination as rooted in memory and to friendship at table. He ends by mapping Brillat-Savarin's gourmandism onto Epicurus's distinction between kinetic pleasures (eating fine food) and catastematic pleasure (ataraxia, the settled well-being of good health and stored memories of shared meals).
- ch. 8 "Chapter 2" **·** Aristotle, Plato, Epicurus — Eudaimonia (flourishing); Food Ethics; Aristotle on Friendship (philia) — Verene interprets Plutarch's Dinner of the Seven Wise Men, in which Thales, Solon, Bias and the others gather at Corinth under Periander and turn a plain dinner into a symposium on good government, household economics, and the soul. He reads their exchanges through Aristotle: Thales' claim that a ruler's happiness is to die naturally in old age is glossed by Aristotle's eudaimonia (no one is called happy until life is complete), the assigned-seating companions exemplify Aristotle's friendship of utility, and the pivot from politics to home management mirrors Aristotle linking Politics to Economics. Solon's Stoic argument that dependence on food enslaves the soul, and Thales' correction of the Ethiopian king's riddle-answers (virtue not God as most helpful), show the Seven speaking as philosophers rather than gourmets, with Epicurus invoked on death being nothing to the living.
- ch. 9 "Chapter 3" **·** Epicurus, Plato — Food Ethics; Epicurean Pleasure (ataraxia) — Verene condenses the fifteen rambling books of Athenaeus's Learned Banqueters, where the character Athenaeus reports to Timocrates a multi-day banquet hosted by Larensius, threaded with catalogues of fish, wine, cups, courtesans and gluttons. He argues the sprawling work embodies dining as a two-part act of food plus discourse, and that its implicit ethic is Epicurean: Book 12's Cyrenaic doctrine that pleasure exists only in the passing moment (Aristippus) is shown to be historically superseded by Epicurus's modified hedonism, which ties bodily pleasures to ataraxia and even welcomes hetaerae like Leontion into the philosophical circle. Book 13's closing attack on the Cynics as homeless 'foul-mouthed gluttons' confirms the banqueters' preference for a regulated, prudent pursuit of pleasure at table.
- ch. 10 "Chapter 4" **·** René Descartes, Aristotle — Food Ethics; Methodological Doubt (evil demon) — Verene frames the two great cookbooks, Apicius's De Re Coquinaria and Artusi's La scienza in cucina, through a contrast between a 'Cartesian' and a 'Vichian' epistemology of cookery: Descartes' Discourse on Method, which discards custom for clear-and-distinct reasoning, is blamed for the restaurant chef's invented 'stacked food,' while Vico's New Science, which grounds knowledge in historical tradition, underwrites the trattoria and the home kitchen. Reading Apicius's garum and dormice and Artusi's health-and-economy motto (glossed via Aristotle's Economics as household management), he defends recipes as repeatable topoi like songs rather than one-off novelties. He closes by tying table manners to Rousseau's moeurs and to Socrates' claim in the Phaedo that speaking (and by extension cooking and eating) badly does harm to the soul.

## Football and Philosophy - Going Deep

- ch. 1 "Preface" *(front)*
- ch. 2 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Copyright Page" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Foreword" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Acknowledgements" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Introduction The Pregame Warm-up" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "First Quarter Football’S Lessons For the Game Of Life" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "Vince Lombardi and the Philosophy of Winning" **·** Karl Marx, Friedrich Nietzsche — Alienation; Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Belliotti reconstructs Vince Lombardi's 'winning isn't everything, it's the only thing' as a deeper philosophy of pursuing excellence and perfection as process values, not victory as such. He rebuts Marxist charges (via Sage) that competitive sport is mere capitalist superstructure by arguing, with Marx and Nietzsche, that hard creative labor and self-overcoming are human goods in themselves rather than mere capitalist demands. Lombardi is cast as a 'grand transcender' whose danger of fascism arises only when winning becomes an end in itself.
- ch. 10 "On Fumbling the Ball" **·** Aristotle, Thomas Nagel, Harry Frankfurt — Moral Luck; Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Fry uses fumbles—Tony Romo's botched hold, Jerome Bettis's near-disaster saved by Roethlisberger, Leon Lett's showboating—as psychodramas illustrating the fragility of the good life.
