He invokes Aristotle's worry that one cannot call a life happy until it is complete, and Thomas Nagel's 'moral luck' (the negligent truck driver) to argue our culpability for fumbling, on the field and in life, is shaped by contingency, so responses should be discriminating. He draws on Frankfurt on caring and closes on redemption through effort, luck, and grace.
- ch. 11 "Football and Aristotle'S Philosophy of Friendship" **·** Aristotle, Bertrand Russell — Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Man as Political Animal — Gallagher maps Aristotle's three kinds of philia onto football: friendship of utility (the quarterback needing his offensive linemen), of pleasure (Friday-night high school and college ball), and of shared goodness (Bo Schembechler's bond with Anthony Carter, who deflected reporters to teammates). He reads the Steelers dynasty and Paterno's locker-room 'love' through Aristotle's 'concord' and 'a single soul in two bodies,' and answers Bertrand Russell's worry about violent competition by insisting football is grounded in philia, not enmity.
- ch. 12 "Inside the Helmet What Do Football Players Know" **·** René Descartes, Hilary Putnam — Methodological Doubt (evil demon); Brain in a Vat; Philosophical Skepticism — Geivett uses football to illustrate epistemology, distinguishing factual (propositional) from competence (skill) knowledge through what running backs and quarterbacks must know and enact under split-second pressure, including 'football physics' vectors on a rollout pass. He treats the athlete's need to be mindful of knowledge amid stadium noise as a lesson in reliable belief, then critiques classroom skeptics who deploy Descartes's evil-demon and the brain-in-a-vat thought experiment, arguing mere logical possibility is no evidence for skepticism.
- ch. 13 "Second Quarter Playing Well Between the Lines" *(front)*
- ch. 14 "The Beauty of Football" **·** Immanuel Kant — Judgment of Taste and the Sublime — Parker asks what pleases football fans and argues the answer is beauty, framed around a Michael Vick scramble against the Ravens. He applies Kant's Critique of Judgment—beauty as disinterested, an end in itself, and its judgments nonconceptual yet universal ('you should too')—to explain why fans watch games rather than read box scores, and why skilled offensive play by Barry Sanders or Joe Montana strikes us as beautiful. Using Ted Cohen's 'circles of taste' and an informal survey, he concludes beauty admits of degree and depends on the viewer's grasp of context.
- ch. 15 "Virtue and Violence Can a Good Football Player Be a Good Person" **·** Immanuel Kant, Socrates — Humanity as an End in Itself; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Consequentialism — Davison defends a 'Violence Principle'—intentionally causing violent harm known to be unnecessary for a greater good is wrong—and applies it to in-the-rules football hits like Jack Tatum on Darryl Stingley and Warren Sapp on Chad Clifton. Against a consequentialist defense of intimidation, he invokes Kant's requirement to treat persons as ends, not mere means, arguing players consent to accidental injury but not intentional harm. Citing the Socratic examination of the soul, he warns of moral danger to players who use excessive force and to fans who relish violence for its own sake.
- ch. 16 "What's so Bad about Performance-Enhancing Drugs" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); The Harm Principle; Justice as Fairness — Ryan evaluates three arguments that using performance-enhancing drugs is wrong, drawing on cases like Barry Bonds, Lyle Alzado, and Ben Johnson. The Against-the-Rules argument shows only that PED use breaks current rules, not that it should be banned; the Unfairness argument overgeneralizes, since sport is full of tolerated unfairness (better coaching, nutrition, natural gifts); and the Harm-and-Pressure argument turns on empirical facts and a paternalism-versus-autonomy debate about whether adults may choose the risk. She concludes no argument yet establishes that PEDs ought to be banned as inherently wrong.
- ch. 17 "The True Nature of Cheating" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Justice as Fairness — Swain and Brand build a definition of cheating by successive counterexamples set in a fictional State-versus-Central championship. They move from breaking a formal rule for unfair advantage, to including informal conventions (the hypothetical pre-rule Bonds steroid case), to 'enhancing one's chances of winning' (a coach fielding ineligible players to level the field), and finally add a defeasibility clause so that convention-sanctioned tactics like running out the play clock are not cheating. Trash-talking within the rules is classed as gamesmanship, not cheating.
- ch. 18 "“They Don't Pay Nobody to Be Humble!” Football's Ego Problem" **·** Epictetus, Cicero, Seneca, John Dewey, Immanuel Kant — Stoicism; Stoic Cosmopolitanism (citizens of the world); The Dichotomy of Control — Holowchak diagnoses football's ego problem—Keyshawn Johnson's deactivation, Terrell Owens's self-promotion, Deion Sanders's showboating—as fed by sensationalism (via Dewey) and statistical obsession that pull players out of the team framework. He proposes 'Aretism,' a modern progressivist ethics built on Stoic oikeiosis (Chrysippus, Cicero, Seneca, Epictetus and Hierokles's concentric circles) balancing self- and other-concern, and on Kant's injunction to treat others as ends. The virtuous athlete competes fully while respecting opponents, sport, and the 'moral zone' of Stoic sagacity.
- ch. 19 "Third Quarter Philosophical Armchair Quarterbacking" *(front)*
- ch. 20 "Crowning a True Champion The Case for a College Football Playoff" **·** Justice as Fairness — Austin argues the BCS is unjust and that a sixteen-team college football playoff should decide the champion on the field, using the undefeated 2006 Boise State Broncos as his key case. He applies a merit-based principle of distributive justice, taking 'victory on the field' rather than economic contribution as the appropriate basis of desert, and invokes Robert Simon's ideal of athletic competition as 'a mutual quest for excellence through challenge.' He answers BCS defenders and academic-integrity worries (Gordon Gee) by proposing reforms that let sport enhance the university's mission.
- ch. 21 "Heroes of the Coliseum" **·** Seneca, Epictetus, Marcus Aurelius, Cicero — Stoicism; The Dichotomy of Control; The View from Above — Reid draws an extended analogy between big-time college football players and Roman gladiators—both socially marginalized entertainers, controlled by coaches/editors and crowds, risking their bodies for others' entertainment. Through Roman Stoicism (Seneca, Epictetus, Cicero) she argues virtus does not depend on external wealth, status, or freedom but on accepting fate and excelling within one's role, so exploited athletes can still be genuine heroes of virtue. Following Marcus Aurelius's distaste for the games, she concludes those with power over players' unjust conditions are morally obliged to improve them.
- ch. 22 "A True MVP" **·** Consequentialism — Kershnar analyzes what makes a football player valuable, arguing a player's value is his net benefit to his own team measured against a baseline. He compares three models—no-substitute, average/reasonable-substitute, and actual-substitute—using cases like Joe Montana backed by Steve Young and Emmitt Smith behind an all-pro line, and defends the actual-substitute model as the correct measure of value even though it yields counterintuitive results. He connects the account to a broader question of measuring a person's contribution to any organization or to the world.
- ch. 23 "Upon Further Review Instant Replay Is an All-or-Nothing Affair" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Justice as Fairness — Smith argues that consistency in applying rules and their motivations forces instant replay to be all-or-nothing: either every play is reviewable or none is. He shows the two rationales—'get the calls right' and 'preserve the flow of the game'—each apply to all plays, then dismantles the reviewable/nonreviewable line by arguing neither the 'judgment call' distinction (goal-line and fumble calls are judgment-laden, field goals are not) nor the 'correctable call' distinction (every play is correctable, e.g. via replaying the down) can hold, using the Roethlisberger Super Bowl XL touchdown as a case.
- ch. 24 "Does the Salary Cap Make the NFL a Fairer League" **·** John Stuart Mill, Jeremy Bentham — Utilitarianism; The Greatest Happiness Principle; Justice as Fairness — Collins-Cavanaugh presents the NFL salary cap as a model of utilitarian distributive justice via Mill's greatest happiness principle: owners and fans gain (guaranteed revenue and a fairer, more competitive league) while players lose relatively little. He then tests empirically—competitive-balance scores, playoff berths, championship distribution, and comparison to MLB and the NBA—and finds little evidence the cap actually increases fairness. Because the claimed fan happiness is not genuinely caused by the cap, the utilitarian justification collapses, leaving only the players' net loss against the owners' gain.
- ch. 25 "Fourth Quarter Metaphysical Mojo" *(front)*
- ch. 26 "Is the Gridiron Holy Ground" **·** Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Augustine of Hippo — Faith and Reason; The Meaning of Life — Hamilton analyzes the fusion of football, patriotism, and religion (Friday Night Lights' Odessa, Super Bowl rituals) as Rousseau's 'civil religion' from the Social Contract—socially unifying but content-empty and anti-intellectual, keeping faith bifurcated from real life. Drawing on Augustine's account of virtue as 'rightly ordered love' and vice as disordered love, he argues football has been wrongly elevated above higher goods. He rejects Higgs's incompatibility thesis for a Christian worldview approach—exemplified by Tony Dungy, Lovie Smith, and Joe Ehrman's 'men for others'—that makes the gridiron holy ground.
- ch. 27 "Touchdowns, Time, and Truth" **·** Peter van Inwagen — Scientific Realism vs Anti-Realism; Free Will vs Determinism — Campbell applies the truthmaker theory and correspondence theory of truth to 'touchdown truths,' using plays like Devin Hester's opening kickoff return in Super Bowl XLI. He distinguishes truthbearers (propositions) from truthmakers (events), separates an agent's 'making it the case' from a truthmaker's 'necessitating,' and uses a tenseless view of semantics plus Perry's being-true/being-made-true distinction to defuse fatalism. He then argues that since only the final score fixes 'real' touchdowns, their truthmakers are events involving referees' beliefs, endorsing a Modest Touchdown Antirealism while retaining bivalence and semantic realism.
- ch. 28 "Feel the Big MO'" **·** Scientific Realism vs Anti-Realism; Problem of Induction — Letson asks whether momentum in football is a real explanatory phenomenon, opening with the Bills' 1993 comeback over the Oilers. He notes momentum is invoked only for success and only for one team at a time, and struggles to separate it from the mere evidence of playing well, comparing it to 'talent.' Surveying psychological models (Taylor and Demick's momentum chain) and David Romer's fourth-down analysis denying momentum, he argues that even if a small momentum effect exists, its size relative to chance means appeals to it in any particular game are usually unjustified—like blaming one heart attack on smoking.
- ch. 29 "Contributors" *(notes)*
- ch. 30 "Index" *(notes)*

## Frankenstein and Philosophy - The Shocking Truth

- ch. 1 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — The editor's introduction surveys the sprawling Frankenstein pop-culture corpus the book will dissect - Whale's 1931 'It's Alive!' scene, the lovable Herman Munster, Mel Brooks's Young Frankenstein, Dean Koontz's Dr. Helios, Boris Karloff's grunting brute, and I, Frankenstein's Adam - insisting the creature now lives in hundreds of films, shows, and novels rather than Shelley's text alone. It frames the book's guiding shocks: whether a re-animated corpse-man has a soul, whether making monsters differs from making babies, and whether Frankenstein's maligned creation would be right to seek to end all of us. It closes by inviting readers to peer into the monster's dull yellow eyes with the lights left on and the door barred.
- ch. 9 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" **·** Epicurus, Aristotle — Death Is Nothing to Us; The Golden Mean; Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Hauskeller argues Shelley's Victor was sane and benevolently motivated, and reads the 'playing God' warning (Whale's 'now I know what it feels to be God!', the Genesis tree of life, the sorcerer's apprentice) as a claim that crossing nature's lines is dangerous rather than sinful. He then flips the story: like the creature, we are children abandoned by a creator who withholds eternal life, so the real wrong is a creator's failure of duty and love - while Epicurus's death-is-nothing-to-us argument and the picture of a childless, static immortal world undermine the transhumanist case that death is the greatest evil. The chapter ends with Aristotle's mean: obsessive pursuit of knowledge and immortality wrecks happiness, which lies in discovering that our native town is the world and living in the present.
- ch. 11 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" **·** Jürgen Habermas, Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Hannah Arendt, Mary Wollstonecraft — Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Natural Goodness and Corruption by Society; Utopia and Dystopia — Chaib reads Craig Venter's 2010 synthetic bacterium 'Synthia' as a real-world Victor Frankenstein and tests it against Habermas's The Future of Human Nature, which condemns liberal eugenics because turning reproduction into manufacture undermines moral equality, rights, and autonomy - the same irresponsible isolation that made Victor abandon his creature and doom Justine. Rousseau and Thomas More supply the counter-program: hunger persists through unequal property ('This is mine'), not scarcity, so improving the human heart and social structures beats Venter's engineered fixes. Frankenweenie - where pets reanimated without love turn evil while Victor's beloved Sparky saves the town - lands the chapter's Arendtian moral that 'to live' is always plural (inter homines esse), so creators must care about their creations' relations with others.
- ch. 12 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" **·** Peter Singer — Utilitarianism; Speciesism; Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation — Littmann argues that stripped of religious scruple, Shelley's own supreme virtue - kindness, embodied in Victor's family, Elizabeth, Clerval, and the cottagers - actually supports creating artificial life: the monster is benevolent until mistreated, and superior corpse-people would be a race of geniuses rather than a threat we may preemptively suppress. The monster himself invents utilitarianism ('relative as they were, as I applied them, to pleasure and pain alone'), De Lacey nearly lives it by promising the stranger rehabilitation over punishment, but Victor's case against building a mate is exposed as speciesism - he tallies unborn humans' futures while ignoring the joys of unborn cadaver folk, though utilitarians were the first to speak for animal rights. Since intelligent otherness is humanity's only chance at non-human friendship and the risks of Prometheus's fire paid off, the chapter rewrites the modern Prometheus as a blessing and ends with a re-imagined creation scene where the monster says 'I love you' to a Victor who finally names him Nigel.
- ch. 13 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein — Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); The Meaning of Life — Jacquette treats Frankenstein as the modern myth of scientific overreach whose two-century grip on pop culture (over a thousand films, Frankenberry cereal) rests on three stitched-together ingredients: Enlightenment faith in applied science, the mortality-awareness of the logician's 'All men are mortal,' and Prometheus-style retribution for challenging nature - while insisting Shelley's tale is science fiction, not supernatural. Victor's real crime is not daring science but abandoning his nameless creation to raise itself, so that 'if he had taken appropriate care of the monster he made, he might not have made such a monster.' The chapter closes with Wittgenstein's remark (Philosophical Investigations §261) that philosophy ends in the wish to 'emit an inarticulate sound,' matched to the Creature's ultimate question 'What was I?' answered only with groans, as Igor's hunting horn fades over the credits of Young Frankenstein.
- ch. 14 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 15 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" **·** Saul Kripke, Aristotle — Naming and Necessity (rigid designators) — Casetta and Tambolo dissect the 'Frankenfood' metaphor: metaphors map a familiar source domain (the Monster) onto a poorly known target (genetically modified organisms, like the 'Frankenfish' salmon), but the mapping fails because the Monster is a genetically heterogeneous chimera assembled from dead bodies while the engineered salmon is a genetically homogeneous hybrid grown from a single fertilized egg. Framing the question with Aristotle's view of metaphysics as the study of what things ultimately are, and applying Kripke-style origin essentialism and natural-kind essentialism, they show that being a patchwork is no essential property at all - our own symbiotic bacteria outnumber our 'human' cells - so Monster and GMO are not entities of the same kind. The metaphor should be discarded as intellectual junk, yet its allure persists because the living corpse-patchwork disturbs our fantasy of ourselves as pure, unmixed organisms.
- ch. 16 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" **·** John Stuart Mill — Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Kokotz casts Victor as a pioneer of human 'enhancement' whose eight-foot daemon - stronger, faster, smarter, kinder, and vegetarian - actually achieved his goal of perfecting the human body, yet whose hideous appearance locked him out of society and love, twisting success into catastrophe: the 'Midas problem' of getting exactly what you wished for. Victor's deeper error was trusting Mill's On Liberty test that what people actually desire is thereby desirable; desires come from the human mind rather than nature, so a desired enhancement can make things worse, as the daemon's revenge on William, Henry, and Elizabeth proves. The lesson is institutional as well as conceptual: science must not proceed behind closed doors but under shared ethical oversight, since a Frankensteinian breakthrough carries too much responsibility for any single scientist to bear.
- ch. 17 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — The Übermensch; Will to Power; Eternal Recurrence; The Death of God — Ketcham reads the monster as Zarathustra's dark double: both are exiled 'godless men' who begin their journeys at a cottager's door, but where Nietzsche's prophet preaches the Übermensch, the will to power, and living so one could will eternal recurrence, the monster traverses the three metamorphoses in reverse - born the innocent child, becoming the lion who tells Victor 'you are my creator, but I am your master,' and ending the camel carrying his maker's corpse to a self-immolating pyre in the polar dark. Shelley's abyss is science usurping God and drawing Promethean punishment; Nietzsche's abyss is the shadow of the dead God (The Gay Science) that keeps humanity from the great noontide and traps Frankenstein as a 'last man' whose will is subjugated to pleasant happiness. The monster, bastard of a godless man and bridge to neither animal nor Übermensch, shows a will that is sheer power without any foundation for overcoming man - 'whither humanity?'
- ch. 18 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" **·** Karl Marx — Alienation; Class Struggle; Commodity Fetishism; Historical Materialism — Fitzpatrick runs Marx's account of alienated labor from the 1844 Manuscripts through the novel: Victor's creature is the product of estranged labor, 'an alien object exercising power over him,' which he cannot name, comes to loathe, and which finally destroys him - the devaluation of the creator tracking the increasing value of the thing made, while workers become commodities producing commodities. The De Laceys' eviction in Branagh's Mary Shelley's Frankenstein and the Ingolstadt cholera riots stage class struggle at feudalism's end, and Victor's failure to self-actualize (to 'hunt in the morning, fish in the afternoon... criticize after dinner') shows how capitalism turns science into 'a tool of our own destruction.' The chapter closes with 'Frankenstorm' climate change as the alienated externality par excellence - capitalists selling us the rope with which we hang ourselves.
- ch. 19 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 20 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant — Free Will vs Determinism; Compatibilism; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Zuk argues Shelley needs the monster to be a free, responsible villain for her Romantic allegory against Enlightenment science to work, then puts him on trial: libertarian free will collides with Honderich's determinism, compatibilism demands only rational responsiveness to reasons, and Strawson's regress - built on Kant's opening demand that we make ourselves morally good or evil by free choice - suggests no one is ultimately responsible for character. The monster's biography tells against guilt: benevolent toward the De Laceys, beaten by Felix, shot after saving a drowning girl, then vowing 'eternal hatred and vengeance to all mankind' - a good nature corrupted beyond his control, overpowered by passion rather than acting with a clear head. Even Victor's dying encouragement to Walton's crew ('be men, or be more than men') concedes the creation was good in itself, so the novel indicts not science but its unguided release among the prejudiced.
- ch. 21 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" **·** Jean-Jacques Rousseau — Natural Goodness and Corruption by Society; The State of Nature — Pötzsch explains the monster through Rousseau's Émile: innately good ('Everything is good as it comes from the hands of the Author of Nature'), the creature is corrupted because he experienced civilization 'too much and too soon' - the exact inverse of Rousseau's negative education, which shields the heart from vice instead of commanding, and lets the child learn 'through the mere force of things.' Victor knew his parents' model of paternal duty ('a deep consciousness of what they owed towards the being to which they had given life') yet abandoned his offspring and refused the female companion Rousseau deems essential to social development, so that wickedness born of weakness ('if I cannot inspire love, I will cause fear') murders William, Clerval, and Elizabeth. Shelley, a Rousseau admirer, makes Victor's science - an attempt to conquer rather than follow nature, fed by competitive professors at Ingolstadt - the very thing that dooms his parenting.
- ch. 22 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Jean-Paul Sartre — Moral Relativism; Master and Slave Morality; Existentialism — King weighs whether Victor or his creation bears the blame, opening with a thought experiment in which a neighbor's self-aware android burns your town and asking 'does he deserve to share that fate?' Maslow's hierarchy of needs explains the monster's fixation on a mate as a starved 'love need' created by Victor's abandonment, and Piaget's cognitive stages test whether the monster had matured enough to 'could have done otherwise' - while Nietzsche's Beyond Good and Evil attack on morality as tyrannical herd-style slave morality threatens to dissolve blame entirely, and Sartre's existentialism leaves each agent choosing without objective moral foundations. Refusing both the absolutist's unconditional verdict and dogmatic relativism, the chapter applies the criminal law's mens rea ('guilty mind') test to monster, android, and creator alike, and leaves the final verdict of guilt deliberately open.
- ch. 23 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Just War Theory; Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation — Galliott treats Frankenstein as the template for blaming lethal autonomous robots: Victor alone created his creature, but today's 'frankennovation' - Ronald Arkin's 'ethical governor' algorithms, DARPA funding, degree programs in robot warfare, and the South African robotic cannon that killed nine spectators - distributes responsibility across engineers, educators (Victor's father and Professors Krempe and Waldman, who never properly warned him about Agrippa's 'sad trash'), universities, and the military-industrial complex. Yet Shelley's articulate, Paradise Lost-quoting monster is a rational, intentional killer who can be held morally responsible and even punished, as Rob Sparrow demands of machines. Because responsibility does not dilute with numbers - two murderers do not get half a life sentence each - the chapter concludes that everyone in the chain, and all of us who do nothing to stop it, shares the blame for monstrous creations.
- ch. 24 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 25 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" **·** Frank Jackson, Alvin Plantinga — The Knowledge Argument (Mary's Room); The Ghost in the Machine; Immortality and the Soul — Hess asks whether the monster has a soul and makes him the test case between physicalism (Kim, Churchland, van Inwagen) and dualism: recasting Frank Jackson's Mary as a color-blind monster who learns every physical fact about color vision, receives new eyes on Walton's ship, and then learns the 'what it is like' of seeing red for the first time, he argues there are nonphysical mental facts, so physicalism is false. He then runs Plantinga's argument that we are not our bodies or brains through a machine that replaces the monster's parts and hemispheres one by one while he stays conscious, and adds near-death out-of-body evidence like Maria's window-ledge shoe to suggest we can exist disembodied. The upshot is symmetric: whatever we conclude about the monster's soul we must conclude about our own - so be careful about assuming who does and does not have a soul, even monsters.
- ch. 26 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" **·** John Locke — Personal Identity; Personal Identity (memory theory); Ship of Theseus; Tabula Rasa — Lopez uses the monster - 'one man made out of many' - to stress-test personal identity: brain-swap and amnesia-torture thought experiments first drive us to identify with our memories (the memory-based psychological criterion) and then, when Victor offers to wipe memory before the pain experiments, with our bodies, yielding inconsistent answers. The monster's own testimony - waking confused, prodding fire like an infant, acquiring language by eavesdropping on Safie's lessons, and building his values from scratch out of Paradise Lost and the cottagers - shows he is a genuinely new person rather than whoever once owned his corpse-parts, since we would not say a memory-wiped torture victim ceases to exist. He concludes the monster satisfies the five listed personhood criteria (consciousness, reasoning, self-motivated activity, communication, self-awareness), with identity riding a continuing stream of consciousness running on a body that is, in the end, secondary 'hardware.'
- ch. 27 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" **·** Martha Nussbaum, Immanuel Kant, Tom Regan — The Capabilities Approach; Speciesism; Respect for Persons; Animal Rights (subjects-of-a-life) — Nicolas Michaud argues the creature is a person on every serious criterion: it satisfies Kant's rationality test and Mary Anne Warren's five criteria (consciousness, reasoning, self-motivated activity, communication, self-concept) in both Shelley's novel and I, Frankenstein, and it possesses all ten of Nussbaum's capabilities for flourishing. The twist is that judging the monster a blameworthy murderer already concedes the personhood we deny him, while our exclusion of him is bare Othering and speciesism of the kind Tom Regan attacks in animal ethics. Michaud ends by reversing the question: after humanity's record of crushing minorities, which the creature learns from Volney's Ruins of Empires and weeps over with Safie, what argument could Victor give Adam for sparing us?
- ch. 28 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" **·** Plato — Philosophy of Love; Justice in the Republic; The Meaning of Life — Mirko Garasic reads Victor as the Modern Prometheus of the myth Plato tells in the Protagoras: like the Titan punished for stealing fire, Victor's purely technical gift of life fails because, as Zeus tells Hermes, justice and reverence must be shared by all or cities cannot exist. The chapter's real subject is love - Prometheus suffers for loving the humans he made, and the creature asks for a female companion while Victor's hate-filled destruction of her unleashes the second wave of revenge. Tim Burton's Frankenweenie splits this love/hate dualism: young Victor's tear over Sparky makes the experiment work, the classmates' prize-greed and the xenophobic mayor produce the real monsters, and Mr. Rzykruski teaches that science severed from emotion is empty. Via Zygmunt Bauman's liquid modernity, Garasic concludes that our own detached, networked isolation is the danger: scientific progress alone never makes a better society, love and community do.
- ch. 29 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" **·** René Descartes, Gilbert Ryle, John Locke — Mind–Body Dualism; The Ghost in the Machine; Methodological Doubt (evil demon) — Spyros Petrounakos reads Frankenstein as a Cartesian narrative whose external events run parallel to the inner worlds of Victor and the creature, from Victor's solitary conception of 'bestowing animation upon lifeless matter' to his imprisonment in a truth nobody would believe when Justine's life is at stake. The monster's acquisition of language at the De Lacey cottage - grasping 'fire, milk, and bread' but not 'good,' 'dearest,' and 'unhappy' - becomes the test case between Descartes's private inner theater (with its evil demon) and Gilbert Ryle's reply, glossing Locke on ideas of reflection, that we learn emotion words by responding directly to behavior rather than by cracking a hidden code. On Ryle's reading the monster's deformity is horror precisely because it is put to Cartesian use, a body that seems to shield its inner life, so the creature only truly comes alive when it behaves like a human being.
- ch. 30 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" **·** Alan Turing, Jean-Jacques Rousseau — The Turing Test; Natural Goodness and Corruption by Society; Binary Oppositions; Feminism — Caroline Mosser treats the creature's tragedy as humanist prejudice rather than necessity: the blind De Lacey accepts the eloquent creature sight unseen, so he passes Alan Turing's test, and only the sighted family's terror at his body rejects him - bias against the artificial, not reason. She reads the creature's development through fire, language, and revolution as Rousseau's progression from natural innocence to corruption by society, and Donna Haraway's cyborg as the figure he prefigures: an unfaithful illegitimate offspring who rebels against his father and undermines patriarchal Western self/other dualisms. The chapter concludes that society cannot accept enhanced or posthuman beings until it finds a middle ground beyond its binary grids of good/bad and Self/Other, since otherwise any new life we create will be Othered, hated, and oppressed like Frankenstein's.
- ch. 31 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 32 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" **·** Personal Identity; Binary Oppositions — Michael Mendelson rereads James Whale's 1931 Frankenstein as a metaphysics of appearance versus reality: the seemingly monstrous creature killed Fritz only after Fritz tormented him with fire and the whip, killed Dr. Waldman while Waldman was trying to dissect him alive, and drowned Maria in innocent play, expecting her to float like the flowers they had been throwing. The 'criminal brain' was Fritz's accident and explains less than we assume, since the creature spares Henry's fiancée when she screams and faints. The real point is alterity: otherness is a relation, not a property, and individual and social identity are built by excluding an outside where monsters reside - so the horror was inside the well-ordered village all along, the monster is us, and we are its creators.
- ch. 33 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" **·** Mary Wollstonecraft, Jean-Jacques Rousseau — Natural Goodness and Corruption by Society; Communitarianism; Philosophy of Love; Feminism — Mike Kugler argues Shelley's novel tests the radical idealism of her father William Godwin, her mother Mary Wollstonecraft, and her husband Percy: Victor's Promethean project of perfecting humanity in isolation, away from the 'intimate circle' of family and friends, produces a creature whose natural benevolence is destroyed only by repeated exclusion, first by the De Laceys. Drawing on Rousseau's and Wollstonecraft's view that children raised by benevolent parents develop their innate sympathy, the chapter reads the creature as an abandoned child - a new Adam who becomes a Satanic rebel when every community rejects him - and Victor's attempt at reproduction without women and mothers as the novel's deepest violation. The redemptive counterfactual is adoption: what if Victor had raised the creature in his home, as his father did Elizabeth?
- ch. 34 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" **·** Karl Marx, Val Plumwood — Ecofeminism; Historical Materialism; Anthropocentrism — Jeff Ewing reads Victor's science through ecofeminist and Marxist critiques: M. Waldman's promise to 'penetrate into the recesses of nature' and 'show how she works in her hiding-places' exemplifies exactly what Val Plumwood condemns - male knowers wringing secrets from a nature pictured as a passive female slave - and Victor studies natural elements 'in their death, not in their life' as raw material for power. Marx and Engels supply the parallel diagnosis: capitalism biases science and technology toward profit, turning tools and knowledge into means of domination rather than human flourishing, just as Victor's knowledge serves only his glory and kills everyone he loves. The chapter concludes that we breed Frankenstein's monsters whenever science treats nature, workers, women, and othered peoples as mere instruments, and only replacing those social relations frees science from power and profit.
- ch. 35 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" **·** Sigmund Freud — The Unconscious; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); Free Will vs Determinism — Cynthia Coe argues the novel's horror lies in the creature's liminality: he is neither human nor inhuman, and Victor's instant labeling of him as 'monster,' 'demon,' and 'fiend' shields Victor from his own responsibility, since the word 'monster' descends from a sign or omen we mistake for the evil itself. Freud's uncanny - the heimlich turning unheimlich, the repressed coming to light - explains why a being 'more horrid from its very resemblance' to us terrifies more than an obvious alien, a mechanism the chapter parallels with European anti-Semitism as hatred of an internal enemy. Shelley's moral point is that failures of empathy are moral failures: Victor holds the creature responsible as a free-willed demon while denying him personhood, and the tragedy grows from that contradictory projection until the neat categories of victim, monster, and evil collapse.
- ch. 36 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" **·** Jacques Derrida — Deconstruction; Binary Oppositions — Jessie Dern uses Derrida's Rogues and The Animal that Therefore I Am to show that naming is power: Victor cries 'Devil!' before the creature has done anything, and labels like 'monster,' 'rogue,' and 'animal' are performative verdicts that reveal the labeler, who secures his own goodness by negating the Other, more than the labeled. The novel multiplies the pattern, from Justine's confessor making her 'think that I was the monster that he said I was' to Elizabeth's 'men appear to me as monsters thirsting for each other's blood,' while Victor, guarding his self-image, becomes the monster himself. Against the Nietzsche epigraph about fighting monsters, the chapter closes with Derrida's playful self-application ('the animal that therefore I am') as the move the creature never gets to make: contesting the label and claiming a name and a self.
- ch. 37 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 38 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" **·** Michael Sandel — Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — John Karavitis uses Dean Koontz's series, in which Victor Helios/Frankenstein engineers a 'New Race' lacking free will and empathy, to test the bioethics of human enhancement: Michael Sandel's bioconservative Case against Perfection condemns enhancements that bypass effort and agency - performance-enhancing drugs, off-label Ritalin and growth hormone, designer children - as a 'Promethean aspiration to remake nature' that destroys human nature. Allen Buchanan's bioprogressive reply that evolution's blind tinkering can be safely improved upon is challenged by the bonobo/chimpanzee contrast, dogs versus wolves, and Belyaev's tame silver foxes, all showing that small genetic changes cascade into large behavioral and physical ones. Since behavior defines morality, the chapter concludes that genetically remaking ourselves risks remaking morality itself, ending in Koontz's posthuman world where 'efficiency equaled morality' and inefficiency was the only sin.
- ch. 39 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" **·** John Stuart Mill, Derek Parfit, Peter Singer, G.E. Moore — The Harm Principle — Joe Slater builds a two-premise anti-natalist argument from Shelley's Paradise Lost epigraph ('Did I request thee, Maker, from my clay / To mould me man?'): since it is wrong to inflict harm on an autonomous being without consent, and every autonomous life inevitably contains harms, creating autonomous beings - Adam in the lab or a baby in the womb - is wrong. He answers the objection from Parfit's non-identity problem via the damaged-brain Adam case, and defends the Harm Premise with Mill's On Liberty: consent matters because autonomous agents must be free to harm themselves, as with the smoker, the drinker, and Frankenstein's stolen kidney. G.E. Moore's 'Moorean shift' and Benatar's genetic, social, and institutional pro-natal biases explain why we resist the conclusion, with a 'Super Adam' incapable of harm as the only escape.
- ch. 40 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" **·** Jean-Jacques Rousseau — The Problem of Evil; Natural Goodness and Corruption by Society; The Best of All Possible Worlds; Faith and Reason — Trip McCrossin casts the creature as a modern Job: repeatedly and unreasonably mistreated by the De Laceys, the rustic whose child he saved, and Victor despite his benevolence, he cries 'This was then the reward of my benevolence!' - but unlike Job he loses faith, because his problem of evil is secular, justifying not 'the Ways of God to Man' but 'the Ways of Man to monsters.' The chapter traces the problem from Job and Paradise Lost through Bayle's defense of faith over reason and the Optimists' 'best of all possible worlds,' whose credibility drowned with the 1755 Lisbon earthquake, setting Rousseau against Voltaire: Rousseau's Emile ('All is good leaving the hands of the author of things') demands that we make evil intelligible through self-knowledge, while Candide counsels tending our garden. The creature embodies Rousseau's line - 'I was benevolent and good; misery made me a fiend' - yet ends in self-abhorring remorse over Victor's death, leaving open whether self-knowledge can ever answer unreasonable suffering.
- ch. 41 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" **·** Plato, Sigmund Freud — Theory of Forms; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — William Rodriguez argues Branagh's 1994 Mary Shelley's Frankenstein deliberately overturns what Plato's account of art as mimesis that forms character, and Colin McGinn's aesthetic theory of virtue (G-beings versus E-beings), lead us to expect - that beauty of soul shows in beauty of body - since the handsome Victor is the moral degenerate while De Niro's hideous creature is a moral virtuoso whose first words are 'friend' and 'father.' The beautiful Frankenstein family harbors ugliness: Oedipal scenes between Victor and his mother Caroline, the wedding that makes Elizabeth 'brother, sister no more; husband and wife,' and Victor's relish in abandoning his creation. The creature's questions - 'Do I have a soul?', 'Who am I?' - and his secret harvest for the peasant family credit moral worth to the ugly, so the monster fascinates us because the monster is us: evil may be attractive and lurking inside rather than deformed and outside.
- ch. 42 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 43 "Frankenstein and Philosophy" *(notes)*

## Futurama and Philosophy - Bite My Shiny Metal Axiom

- ch. 1 "Futurama and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Futurama and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Futurama and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Futurama and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Futurama and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Futurama and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Futurama and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — The editors' introduction argues that Futurama is more than a cartoon: Matt Groening and David X. Cohen built a gold-standard sci-fi show whose storylines engage ethics, time travel, science, normalcy, and the environment. It frames Fry as a twenty-first-century flawed hero in the lineage of Moses, Gilgamesh, Odysseus, Don Quixote, and Frodo, called beyond his inadequacies, and reads his opening 'Space Pilot 3000' musings about the gorilla-throwing-barrels game as a glimpse of life's ups and downs. The chapter presents the show's strategy of making the mundane strange as the backdrop the volume's philosophers will use to examine life and meaning, and jokes that fans' resurrection of the canceled show (wave after wave, like Zapp Brannigan's troops) guarantees Futurama's permanence.
- ch. 8 "Futurama and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "Futurama and Philosophy" **·** John Stuart Mill — The Harm Principle; On Liberty — In 'A Taste of Freedom,' Zoidberg eats Earth's flag Old Freebie on Freedom Day, is tried by Earth's Supreme Court, and his death sentence triggers a Decapodian invasion that enslaves Earth under the Mobile Oppression Palace. The chapter pits Mill's On Liberty defense of unpopular expression (mankind cannot silence a lone dissenter; unchallenged beliefs are mere 'true opinions' akin to prejudice) against Feinberg's offense principle, mapping the case onto Texas v. Johnson and the symbolic-speech line from Stromberg to Tinker. It ends with Zoidberg burning the flag 'to preserve the freedom it represents': liberty and majority will often conflict (doomsday devices, Proposition Infinity's robosexual marriage ban), so preserving freedom requires tolerating offensive expression.
- ch. 10 "Futurama and Philosophy" **·** Jean-Jacques Rousseau, John Stuart Mill — The General Will; The Harm Principle — Young tests Isaiah Berlin's distinction between negative liberty (absence of external interference) and positive liberty (rational self-government) on three episodes: Fry's super-Slurm compulsion in 'Fry and the Slurm Factory,' human Bender's lethal gluttony in 'Anthology of Interest II,' and the worms whose upgrades win Leela's love in 'Parasites Lost' - each agent is externally unobstructed yet not self-governing. Bender's jacking-on addiction and the Temple of Robotology's threat of Robot Hell in 'Hell Is Other Robots' illustrate Rousseau's 'forced to be free,' the coercion that Berlin warned has licensed totalitarian regimes. The chapter concludes Futurama takes no side, suggesting genuine freedom needs both positive and negative liberty.
- ch. 11 "Futurama and Philosophy" **·** Commodity Fetishism — Buchanan reads 'A Big Piece of Garbage' as political ecology: the trash ball Old New York blasted into space in 2000 returns to threaten New New York, showing that even a society that has 'mastered recycling' cannot escape a capitalist economy whose commodities are designed to end as waste. On the trash ball, Fry's nostalgia ('junk isn't garbage... a glorious monument to the twentieth century') debates Leela's 'this stuff was garbage when it was new,' and Fry's rescue plan - building a second trash ball to knock the first into the Sun - merely recreates the disaster, celebrated as 'the twentieth-century spirit' of willful ignorance. Drawing on Bryant and Bailey's political ecology and Rodgers's waste stream, the chapter argues trash unmasks the exploitation of nature crystallized in commodities, so green technology and recycling alone cannot avert ecological crisis.
- ch. 12 "Futurama and Philosophy" **·** Michael Walzer — Just War Theory — Malloy defines war as organized, political, violent conflict and tests Futurama's many invasions against just war theory: Zapp's invasion of Spheron 1 in 'War Is the H-Word' fails just cause ('they stand for everything we don't stand for'), the first Omicronian invasion over the interrupted Single Female Lawyer broadcast fails proportionality and last resort, while the second, over eaten Popplers, passes. Zapp's Hyperdeath laser ribbon-cutting and the Bender suicide-bombing plot against the Brain Balls illustrate discrimination, proportionality, and means evil in themselves, and the postwar demand that Lrrr eat one human - ending with blameless Free Waterfall Jr. - raises war-crimes punishment and restitution. The chapter closes by endorsing Walzer: a state's moral authority hinges on at least acknowledging the rules of war.
- ch. 13 "Futurama and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 14 "Futurama and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, Friedrich Nietzsche — Humanity as an End in Itself; Ressentiment — Piven argues Zapp Brannigan's 'sexlexia' - his deluded velour-and-spanking seductions of Leela and his relief at finding Lee Lemon is a woman - typifies a whole cast of clueless lovers (Fry coaching Zoidberg's courtship of Edna, Morgan Proctor's mess fetish, Bender coldly dumping the Planet Express ship in 'Love and Rocket'). Kant's condemnation of masturbation as self-abuse that throws away one's personality and violates humanity in one's own person is exposed as unsupported prejudice, and Nietzsche's claim that morality projects fear, envy, and disgust onto the despised other explains why 'perversion' is an arbitrary cultural judgment. Freud, Stoller, and McDougall then recast perversion as unconscious flight from anxiety and death, so the show's kink - up to the Monsterpus of The Beast with a Billion Backs and Amy's 'general sluttiness' - mirrors our own sexlexia and existential dread.
- ch. 15 "Futurama and Philosophy" **·** René Descartes, John Locke — Personal Identity; Personal Identity (memory theory); Ship of Theseus — Jenkins asks what makes Bender Bender when Leela still recognizes him as the walnut-panelled steam robot of 'Obsoletely Fabulous.' Scruffy's progressively repaired conference table stages the Ship of Theseus problem and the same body theory, while the Roboticon 'Soul Detector' (Fry sets it off, robots pass through) rules out Descartes' same soul theory for robots. Episodes from 'Anthology of Interest II' and 'The Honking' to 'The Prisoner of Benda' mind swap and Roberto's instant 1-X re-education support Locke's psychological continuity: Bender persists through an overlapping chain of memories and traits stored on his hard drive - 'Everything that is Bender is right here' - which is why an empathic, ascetic Bender ('The old Bender is gone') threatens his very identity.
- ch. 16 "Futurama and Philosophy" **·** Albert Camus — The Myth of Sisyphus; The Meaning of Life; Philosophy of Love — Lewis argues that Futurama's answer to the fear of being forgotten - Zoidberg thriving on negative attention, Bender's billion-cubit statue and the Shrimpkins' forced worship both failing - is to become existentially intertwined with others through friendship, love, and family. Bender's arc in 'A Pharaoh to Remember' is read through Camus's Sisyphus: life itself is meaningless, but our freedom lets us fill it with meaning, which Bender finds not in monuments or worship but in seeing others remember him. Fry's devotion in 'Godfellas' and 'Jurassic Bark' (Seymour waiting outside Panucci's forever), Bender's Big Score, the holophonor opera of 'The Devil's Hands Are Idle Playthings,' and Yancey naming his son after Fry in 'The Luck of the Fryish' show remembrance surviving inside the people we have shaped, including the viewers.
- ch. 17 "Futurama and Philosophy" **·** Epicurus, Thomas Nagel, Viktor Frankl — Death Is Nothing to Us; Man's Search for Meaning (logotherapy) — Salazar takes Professor Farnsworth's cry that the crew's age regression in 'Teenage Mutant Leela's Hurdles' will end in 'a fate worse than death: pre-life! Then death' as a test of whether death is bad. Epicurus's 'death is nothing to us' says death cannot be experienced, so the deprivation account of Nagel's 'Death' is needed to explain how death is bad for the one deprived of a valuable future - a dispute staged by Bender's suicide-booth despair in 'Space Pilot 3000' after learning his bending built the booths. The chapter then argues losing one's identity and values can be worse than death (Bender's and Fry's flight from career-chip assignments, the Professor's love of being 161, Leela's frustrated teen redo), closing with Frankl's Man's Search for Meaning and Leela's sacrifice of her regained youth: inner integrity, not erasing the past, makes life worth living.
- ch. 18 "Futurama and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle, David Hume — Problem of Induction — Elsby argues Fry's 'brain thing' illuminates how humans form beliefs and adapt to misconceptions: Fry accepts swans as swan boats and deduces 'boat eggs,' romances the radiator woman from the radiator planet, and insists he is in the ruined New York of 4000 rather than L.A. - each time reasoning flawlessly from a false premise before readjusting when corrected. Using Aristotle's incidental perception (seeing the pale thing as Cleon's son), Hume's warning that grouping similar-but-non-identical objects breeds ambiguity, and Mom's grandiose misreading of Fry's anchovy purchase in 'A Fishful of Dollars,' the chapter traces how error enters at each step of identification and inference. The payoff: Fry differs from us only in degree, and our capacity to adapt to overturned assumptions might be inferior to his.
- ch. 19 "Futurama and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 20 "Futurama and Philosophy" **·** Peter Singer, Thomas Nagel — Speciesism; What Is It Like to Be a Bat?; Free Will vs Determinism — Von Gunten asks whether Bender, Calculon, and the robot mafia have moral worth, arguing first that they are sentient: behavioral similarity plus functional equivalence (pain as electric signals in neurons or circuits) defeat both behavior-only objections (the Sphex wasp's preprogrammed loop, knife-avoiding toy robots) and composition-based prejudice - Fry's 'I'm all squishy and flabby' defense at the Hal Institute - which would be 'biologism' analogous to racism. He then distinguishes moral patiency (Nibbler can be harmed but only behaviorally reinforced) from moral agency (deliberation and accountability), concluding that Bender's flexible life - Iron Cook, wrestling, folk music with Beck's head, jacking-on addiction - shows robots transcend their programming as humans transcend evolutionary presets. Limiting moral status to biology or species would be speciesism, so the thirty-first century's robots deserve the full protections and responsibilities humans have.
- ch. 21 "Futurama and Philosophy" **·** Jeremy Bentham, Peter Singer — Speciesism; Animal Liberation; Food Ethics — The chapter uses 'The Problem with Popplers' to map the real-world ethics of eating meat: Free Waterfall Jr.'s 'You shouldn't eat things that feel pain' restates Bentham's 'Can they suffer?' and Singer's equal consideration of suffering; Fishy Joe's profits-first stance echoes agro-ecological defenses of meat eating; and Leela's 'eating an intelligent animal is different' voices the common intuition Singer condemns as speciesism. The food-chain justification collapses when the Omicronians claim the apex and demand 198 billion humans in return, and Bender's 'fight to the death and I'll cook the loser' flips the diner's perspective, while Waterfall's orangutan-for-Leela trade sets up Singer's future-directed preferences distinction from Practical Ethics as a possible rescue. The chapter ends on the episode's real target: gluttonous consumption ('stuff the ship!') and the arbitrary sentimentality governing what we will and won't eat.
- ch. 22 "Futurama and Philosophy" **·** Free Will vs Determinism; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Ethical Egoism; Utilitarianism — Pless asks whether Bender is responsible for his misdeeds given that MomCorp designed him to be an alcoholic kleptomaniac (revealed through Bender Vision in 'Mother's Day') and Planet Express regularly orders him into morally dirty work. Against Asimov's utilitarian Three Laws - embodied by the servile, selfless Robot 1-X of 'Obsoletely Fabulous' - Futurama's 'robot subjectivism' makes every robot a self-interested agent, and Bender's autonomy is proven by his refusal of Mom's 'kill all humans' order and by his fear of punishment in Robot Hell ('Hell Is Other Robots'), which also shows the show's universe is not morally relativist. Because Bender can rewrite his own code - his unprogrammed friendship with Fry, the adoption scam that turns heartfelt in 'The Cyber House Rules' - he sits on the same sliding scale of moral agency as humans, so if Bender can break his programming, we have no excuse for not exercising our own free will.
- ch. 23 "Futurama and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 24 "Futurama and Philosophy" **·** David Lewis — The Metaphysics of Time Travel; Possible Worlds — Burkett uses Fry's accidental incest in 'Roswell that Ends Well' - protecting Enos only to cause his death and then becoming his own grandfather via Mildred - to examine backwards time travel through David Lewis's Grandfather Paradox. Killing one's grandfather is shown to be logically impossible (it makes Enos both dead and alive in 1947, so every would-be assassin fails for ordinary reasons like a jammed rifle), while becoming your own grandfather is a consistent causal loop, and branching timelines merely kill a man who is no one's grandfather. The 'paradox-correcting' time spheres of Bender's Big Score fail on Hermes's stolen body, and Bender's year-long loop would spawn infinitely many duplicates were it not for the Globetrotters' Doom Field - so backwards time travel is bizarre but logically possible.
- ch. 25 "Futurama and Philosophy" **·** Bertrand Russell, William of Ockham — The Watchmaker Argument (design); Principle of Sufficient Reason — DePoe weighs the multiverse against the fine-tuning argument for God's existence: just as Farnsworth's box in 'The Farnsworth Parabox' spills out universe-1's golden Bender plus togas, pirate, and hippie universes, an infinite multiverse would realize every possibility and make our life-permitting constants certain rather than miraculous, while 'Time Keeps on Slippin''s chronoton-disturbed cosmos illustrates what fine-tuning means. He then asks what is metaphysically primitive, staging the 1948 Copleston-Russell exchange ('I should say that the universe is just there, and that's all') and applying Ockham's razor to weigh one infinitely complex God against infinitely many unobservable universes. The chapter concludes in evidential stalemate, closing with the God Entity's counsel in 'Godfellas': 'When you do things right, people won't be sure you've done anything at all.'
- ch. 26 "Futurama and Philosophy" **·** The Problem of Evil; The Meaning of Life — Oschman reads 'Godfellas' as an answer to the problem of evil: Bender, made god of the Shrimpkins, destroys them by meddling too much, and the God Entity explains 'if you do too much, people get dependent. And if you do nothing, they lose hope.' After testing the contrast response (pain makes Slurm sweet) and the Free Will Defense (which covers moral evils like the returning garbage ball of 'A Big Piece of Garbage' but not natural evils or the taste of anchovies), the chapter adopts John Hick's soul-making theodicy: God's epistemic distance - compared to Fry's obsession after tasting super Slurm - keeps creatures uncompelled so they can grow into free moral agents. Fry's slow evolution from delivery boy to 'slightly less lazy slob' is soul-making, and 'The Late Philip J. Fry' wryly confirms life's purpose is 'probably some hogwash about the human spirit.'
- ch. 27 "Futurama and Philosophy" **·** René Descartes — Ship of Theseus; Mind–Body Dualism; Personal Identity — Williams uses Hermes's slide from chest-harpoon to extendo-arm to Cylon eye to full robot body in 'The Six Million Dollar Mon' - until Zoidberg's 'Monster Mash' surgery restores his brain to Li'l Hermes - to ask what makes a person themselves under voluntary enhancement. The 'Ship of Planet Express' and the seven-year cell-replacement fact recast the Ship of Theseus, exposing our terror of upgrades as inconsistent with our constant physical change, while the therapeutic-versus-enhancement line (medical steroids vs doping, Aimee Mullins's blades) explains why Hermes's chosen upgrades disturb us. Cartesian interactionist dualism predicts Mecha-Hermes stays the same person, yet his personality shifts with his parts, so the chapter turns to physicalism and lands on functionalism: Hermes persists through each swap until the Roberto brain-chip replaces the brain that houses his memories and motivations.
- ch. 28 "Futurama and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Aristotle — Eternal Recurrence; Amor Fati (love of fate); Stoicism — Laderoute reads 'The Late Philip J. Fry' - the forward-only time machine overshooting past the year one billion and the last protons to a second Big Bang and an identical universe (ten feet lower, conveniently crushing its duplicates) - as a tour of cosmology from Aristotle's static, eternal, Earth-centered heavens through the Stoics' providential determinism and fiery ekpyrosis to Big Bang cosmology and heat death, which is why the Professor is so shocked. Nietzsche's Gay Science 341 supplies the existential payoff: faced with living identically forever, one can react with horror or delight, and eternal recurrence becomes a challenge to become the person you want to be. Fry, a billion years late for his Cavern on the Green dinner, reads Leela's message carved in stone, and in the repeated universe finally arrives on time - 'That was the old Fry... he's dead now' - amor fati enacted.
- ch. 29 "Futurama and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 30 "Futurama and Philosophy" **·** Socrates, Plato — Socratic Ignorance (knowing that you don't know); Socratic Method (elenchus) — Mills-Knutsen reads the Brain Spawn - floating brains whose pain at others' thoughts drives them to dumb life down and finally to hoard all knowledge in the Infosphere ('The Day the Earth Stood Stupid,' 'The Why of Fry') - as Futurama's funhouse mirror of the West's distrust of intellectuals, from Pandora and Eve to Frankenstein and comic-book evil geniuses. Fry, the delta-brainwave-lacking hero made stupid by doing 'the nasty in the pasty,' stabs the Big Brain with Bonfire of the Vanities and later banishes the Brains to another dimension, just as Athens executed Socrates. A close reading of Plato's Apology - Socrates's feigned ignorance exposing everyone else's, his demand of free meals in lieu of exile, and his prophecy of even more annoying successors - maps onto the Big Brain's snobbery and onto universities (Wernstrom's tenure scam in 'A Big Piece of Garbage,' Amy's doctorate in 'That Darn Katz!') as society's way of paying intellectuals to stay out of the way.
- ch. 31 "Futurama and Philosophy" **·** Michel Foucault, Aristotle, David Hume — Heteronormativity; Discipline and Docile Bodies — Onishi argues humor exposes cultural normalcy: Futurama transplants twenty-first-century prejudices into the future, where Leela's one eye still gets her mocked by the blind-and-deaf orphan Kirk ('The Cyber House Rules') and same-sex marriage is unremarkable while robosexual marriage is taboo ('Proposition Infinity,' a parody of California's Proposition 8). Leela's phaser-eye surgery and her self-loathing until 'Leela's Homeworld' reveals she is a mutant show standards of normalcy are cultural rather than natural, and are wielded oppressively. Drawing on Aristotle's habituation, Hume's habit, and Foucault's disciplinary production of subjects, the chapter uses Bender's change of heart after dating the Lucy Liu-bot head in 'I Dated a Robot' to argue habits change through meeting actual people rather than abstract 'ideas' of communities - which is how satire quietly extends what counts as normal.
- ch. 32 "Futurama and Philosophy" **·** Plato, Simone de Beauvoir — Allegory of the Cave; Woman as the Other — Reitter reads the sewer mutants - banished underground, surviving on what the surface flushes, as in 'The Mutants Are Revolting' where Leela is deported without trial the moment Fry reveals she is not an alien - as philosophy's Other, illuminated by Plato's cave (surface dwellers fear and demonize what they have never seen) and Beauvoir's The Second Sex (Leela passes as an acceptable alien until exposed as a mutant). Mrs. Astor's United Mutant Scholarship Fund film, with its 'noble sewer mutants... uh, people, I guess,' is read alongside Said's Orientalism and racist charity imagery as a third face of Othering. The chapter then adds folklore theory: the mutants are a folk group with their own urban legends (alligators in the sewer, and the 'sub-urban legend' of sub-mutants), ballads like 'The Legend of El Chupanibre,' and freegan-style recycling ethics - a counterculture whose difference the dominant universe-view casts as monstrosity.
- ch. 33 "Futurama and Philosophy" **·** Philosophy of Love — Taft pairs Fry with Don Quixote: both are media-saturated characters who live as if inside fiction, break the fourth wall, and are dismissed as madmen - Fry scripting a finale for Single Female Lawyer to placate the Omicronians in 'When Aliens Attack,' Quixote blaming enchanters whenever his fantasies misfire. The chapter asks where reality ends and fiction begins, contrasting the Canon's charge that novels merely delight with the Priest's reply that stories display the beauty of a mind - dramatized when Fry's holophoner music, not worm-given strength or the Robot Devil's hands, is what moves Leela in 'Parasites Lost' and 'The Devil's Hands Are Idle Playthings.' Ending with the opera that spills into its own audience until Hermes sings 'Is this really happening or just being staged?,' the chapter argues a life of enchantment can bend reality toward fantasy - Fry authors his own story just as Quixote's neighbors finally beg him to ride again.
- ch. 34 "Futurama and Philosophy" **·** G.W.F. Hegel, René Descartes — The Cogito; Personal Identity — Micic uses Fry's hundred-cups-of-coffee rescue in 'Three Hundred Big Boys' - a mysterious orange blur to onlookers while Fry calmly watches each cork pop - and the chronoton time skips of 'Time Keeps on Slippin'' as breaks in experience that Hegel's Phenomenology of Spirit explains better than Descartes. Against the cogito's inference of a separate thinking 'I,' Hegel treats consciousness as the act of relating subject to object, whose ceaseless repetitions (here1, here2, here3...) we knit into the illusion of a unified self. The identical universes of 'The Late Philip J. Fry' (U1 = U2 = U3...) then model the same collapse of enumeration: the gaps in experience feel abnormal only because we never notice the constant gaps our own consciousness smooths over - including Nibbler's shadow in the pilot's cryogenics lab.
- ch. 35 "Futurama and Philosophy" **·** Søren Kierkegaard, William James — Subjectivity Is Truth; The Will to Believe — Hamer tests sanity against truth using Kierkegaard's asylum anecdote - the escapee intoning 'Boom, the earth is round' proves his insanity precisely by resting it on objective fact - and applies it to 'Insane in the Mainframe,' where Fry, wrongly committed to a robot asylum, is only released once he subjectively believes he is a robot, and to 'The Sting,' where comatose Leela's plan to keep Fry's corpse under her mattress to 'prove I'm not insane' fails the same traitorous way. Pure objectivity cannot certify sanity and pure subjectivity licenses lunacy, so the chapter brings in James's 'The Will to Believe': some beliefs must be held first before their evidence can even count. Fry, who abandons his robot belief the moment his knife-nick bleeds human blood, models the justified believer who pursues the objective truth subjectively.
- ch. 36 "Futurama and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 37 "Futurama and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 38 "Futurama and Philosophy" *(notes)*

## Game of Thrones and Philosophy - Logic Cuts Deeper Than Swords

- ch. 1 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Foreword by Elio M. Garcia and Linda Antonsson (founders of Westeros.org). They argue that Martin's epic succeeds because its real subject is 'the human heart in conflict with itself': unlike Tolkien-style epics with a Dark Lord and morally obvious heroes, A Game of Thrones gives characters in shades of gray - Ned wrestling with honor and honesty, Jon with vows versus love, Tyrion craving his father's approval - and presents this volume's essays as illumination of that moral complexity, from Littmann's Hobbes-as-maester to Jacoby on warg consciousness.
- ch. 7 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 9 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" **·** Socrates — The Meaning of Life; Virtue Ethics — Henry Jacoby's introduction frames the volume around mortality: winter is coming, all men must die (valar morghulis), yet through honor, duty, and philosophy 'first we'll live' meaningful lives. Playing on Arya's mantra 'fear cuts deeper than swords,' he claims logic cuts deeper still - swords defend bodies, but logic defends our ideas, beliefs, and values - leaning on Socrates' claim that no evil can befall a good person, since the virtuous soul stands in harmony against desire and influence. He previews the essays: Hobbes as a maester, the dark side of chivalry, Robb's questionable war, Arya and Zen, and Ned's need of Machiavelli.
- ch. 10 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 11 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Hobbes — The State of Nature; The Social Contract; Ethical Egoism — Greg Littmann asks who should rule Westeros and answers with Hobbes's Leviathan: people act only from self-interest ('we are all Lord Littlefinger under the skin') and fight for gain, safety, and glory - the mountain clans, Robert's preemptive strike on Daenerys, Drogo's wars for pride - so without a sovereign life is a war of all against all, and only an absolute ruler makes oath-keeping self-interested. Mapping Hobbes's own civil war (the Stuarts onto the Targaryens), he argues that even Varys is right to serve 'the realm' and peace by betraying Ned, and that loyalty rightly transfers from Aerys to Robert to Joffrey because protection, not lineage or justice, is the point of the sovereign. He then criticizes Hobbes: Ned and Jon show people really do die for principle, and Mad King Aerys's overcentralized power left rebellion as the only remedy - the War of the Five Kings shows civil war's horror must be weighed against appeals to honor.
- ch. 12 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, Augustine of Hippo, Bernard Williams, John Locke, John Stuart Mill — Deontology; Consequentialism; On Liberty — Don Fallis starts from Joffrey's lie about Arya and the butcher boy Mycah - which costs Mycah's life and Sansa's direwolf Lady's - to dissect lying versus other deception in Westeros: Catelyn accusing Tyrion is not lying because she believes it, Ned's coerced confession at the Great Sept is a real lie (Austin's duress excuse fails, as Thomas More's counterexample shows), and Ned's silence about Joffrey's parentage before the dying Robert is deception by withholding that he himself mistakes for lying. He asks whether lying is morally worse than misleading - Kant and Chisholm say yes, Williams and Scanlon no - testing breach of invited trust (Mirri Maz Duur's 'only death may pay for life'), responsibility-shifting to the audience ('That was a lie you told yourself'), and preserved autonomy (Kant's Groundwork, Mill's On Liberty). Finally, is it worse to lie to a king? Consequentialism explains the danger of misleading the powerful, oaths of fealty and divine right suggest a special duty, but Locke's demolition of divine right (seconded by Monty Python) leaves only the punishment, not the wrongdoing, greater.
- ch. 13 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" **·** Niccolò Machiavelli — Virtù and Fortuna; The Ends Justify the Means — Marcus Schulzke reads the War of the Five Kings through The Prince: who wins the Iron Throne is decided by virtu - skill adapted to shifting circumstances - against fortuna, and morality is no guide, since actions are good or bad only as they gain or hold power. The failures illustrate the lessons: Viserys's arrogance binds him to the wrong allies, Robert's emotions make him capricious, Joffrey makes himself hated instead of feared, Ned's uncompromising honesty and trust in Littlefinger destroy him, and Robb's dependency on Walder Frey's auxiliary forces proves fatal. The true Machiavellians are the mask-wearers - Littlefinger, Arya, Jon as lion-and-fox - and above all Tyrion, who wins the Blackwater by planning, and Daenerys, who builds her own army from freed slaves and so makes her own luck.
- ch. 14 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" **·** Just War Theory — Richard H. Corrigan tests Robb Stark's war against Joffrey with just war theory, criteria for resorting to war (legitimate authority, just cause, right intention, last resort, probability of success, proportionality) and for conduct (discrimination, treatment of prisoners, no reprisals). Robb is a legitimate authority acclaimed King in the North, offers rejected peace terms, and fights cleanly - Jaime is humanely held at Riverrun while Sansa is beaten by Joffrey, and Gregor Clegane's murders, rapes, and the Dothraki sack of the Lhazareen mark the other side's atrocities. Yet Corrigan concludes Robb fails right intention: his dominant motive is vengeance for his father, not the free North he professes, so although we root for him, his war cannot strictly be called just.
- ch. 15 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 16 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" **·** Plato, Aristotle, Blaise Pascal, Socrates — Justice in the Republic; Eudaimonia (flourishing); Tripartite Soul; Pascal's Wager — Eric Silverman argues Game of Thrones explodes the storybook link between virtue and worldly happiness: Ned, 'one man in ten thousand,' is betrayed and executed, so Plato's claim that the just person is happy must mean psychic order rather than material flourishing - reason ruling spirit and appetite - as with Bran, who flourishes as a greenseer though he will never walk again. Cersei is Plato's tyrant, her soul ruled by insatiable appetite and paranoia, so no external goods can ever satisfy her, while Aristotle's correction - virtue necessary but not sufficient for eudaimonia, which also needs health, friends, and good fortune - explains why both Ned (virtue without external goods) and Cersei (goods without virtue) end up unhappy. Finally he inverts Pascal's Wager: since playing the game of thrones risks total loss (torture, humiliation, death) for limited gain, and even winning hollows you out - Robert confesses he was 'never so alive as when I was winning this throne or so dead as now that I've won it' - only a fool would play.
- ch. 17 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Hobbes, Niccolò Machiavelli — The State of Nature; The Ends Justify the Means; The Social Contract — David Hahn contends Ned died because of his honor, not for it. Hobbes explains why: words bind no one without fear of enforcement - Cersei ripping up Robert's will ('Is this meant to be your shield, Lord Stark?') exposes Ned's belief that allegiance alone secures the King's Justice - and the Iron Throne itself is a Leviathan, forged by Aegon's dragons ending the state of nature, whose force rather than justice holds the peace. Machiavelli supplies the indictment of Ned's idealism: 'we who presume to rule must do vile things for the good of the realm,' so Ned's refusal to assassinate Daenerys, his merciful warning to Cersei, and backing the rightful but war-prone Stannis over Renly cost him his head and plunged the realm into civil war; the security of the state demanded exiling or killing the Lannisters and crowning Renly.
- ch. 18 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle — Virtue Ethics (arete); Practical Wisdom (phronesis); The Golden Mean; Consequentialism — Albert Anglberger and Alexander Hieke contrast Ned, the virtue ethicist's genuinely good man, with Cersei, a 'minimally extended' egoistic consequentialist whose only morally relevant subjects are herself and her children. Aristotle's apparatus does the work: phronesis lets Ned rank his daughters' lives above honesty when he confesses falsely ('the lies we tell for love'), his 'you're too fat for your armor' quip shows the golden mean among competing virtues, and Cersei's unbalanced maternal love is excess, hence not a virtue. They then unsettle moral judgment itself: utilitarianism would license the Slaver's Bay slaveholders, virtue ethics lacks action rules and cross-cultural agreement (charity is no Dothraki virtue), and Martin's POV narration gives direct access to Ned's inner life but only Varys's and Littlefinger's unreliable reports about Cersei - so our verdicts on her, like real-life judgments of others, rest on shaky evidence.
- ch. 19 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" **·** James Rachels, Peter Singer — Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Matthew Tedesco reads Bran's coma and Drogo's living death as biomedical ethics: Jaime's 'good clean death' for Bran raises active versus passive euthanasia, and with James Rachels he argues the AMA's killing/letting-die distinction merely licenses more suffering, while Brock recasts withdrawal of treatment as intentional killing anyway. The Bran case frames end-of-life decisions for children - the Johns Hopkins and Baby John Pearson cases - and whether parents may weigh a handicap's burden on the family, a question made vivid by Bran riding Hodor. Dany's smothering of Drogo, by contrast, does not strike readers as murder because personhood rather than biological humanity carries moral standing: on Tooley's self-consciousness, Warren's five criteria, or Marquis's future-like-ours, Drogo is human but no longer a person - which leads straight to Peter Singer's claim that killing a disabled infant who is not a person may not be wrong at all, leaving us to ask whether our recoil is reason or taboo.
- ch. 20 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 21 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Nagel, René Descartes, Bertrand Russell, David Chalmers — What Is It Like to Be a Bat?; Mind–Body Dualism; Philosophical Zombies — Henry Jacoby wields Westeros's strange beings against the mind-body problem: Nagel's 'what it is like' question becomes 'What is it like to be a direwolf?', and wargs like Bran-in-Summer and Orell's eagle ask whether one point of view can literally inhabit another. Against Descartes's denial of animal consciousness (only language users have souls; Mormont's raven crying 'Corn!' is a machine), he argues Ghost's pain behavior and direwolf physiology ground animal consciousness exactly as our brothers' behavior grounds theirs. Wargs do not refute materialism - in Westeros magic violates physical law without invoking nonphysical stuff - and 'Zombie Sansa', the philosophers' phenomenal zombie, together with the reanimated wights, is impossible in our world (with Russell, experiences are brain events known directly) and conceivable beyond the Wall only because the supernatural breaks the laws of nature.
- ch. 22 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Edward Cox asks whether physicalism - everything is physical, and everything depends on the arrangement of physical stuff - is true in Westeros. The causal argument shows Jaime's love could move Bran's body only if it is itself a brain state, and the maesters' science (boiling wine against infection, Baratheon black hair as heredity, the Eyrie's winch) reveals a world running on our physics. But magic strains the picture: reanimated Othor and Jafer Flowers must physically change to walk, and Daenerys's walk into Drogo's pyre requires her resolve to disrupt combustion itself - so Cox concludes magic is either violation of physical law or a nonphysical force, and that in our world immaterial souls would be exactly as improbable as magic.
- ch. 23 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" **·** René Descartes — Socratic Ignorance (knowing that you don't know); Reliabilism; The Gettier Problem; Philosophical Skepticism — Abraham Schwab turns Ygritte's 'You know nothing, Jon Snow' into a theory of epistemic humility: Jon misreads wildling courtship norms, Sam's dragonglass shatters on wight Small Paul, Lord Mormont assumes Mance fights like a southern king and pays for it at the Fist of First Men, while Arya's accurate self-knowledge keeps her alive. Formalizing with justified true belief - Sam's lucky dagger thrust is not knowledge, and the Watch's warranted belief that the dead Othor was harmless justifies falsity - he uses Lysa's letter, which sent Ned to King's Landing on a lie, to ask what justification is: Descartes's absolute certainty is too strict, reliabilism vindicates testimony ('dark wings, dark words'), so that only Sansa need witness Ned's beheading for the Starks to know it, and between Melisandre's circular dogma about Azor Ahai and total skepticism, moderate skepticism best governs belief - a humility the south lacked about the Horn of Winter.
