- ch. 24 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" **·** Augustine of Hippo, David Hume, Epicurus, Gottfried Leibniz — The Problem of Evil; The Best of All Possible Worlds — Jaime Lannister's question to Catelyn Stark - 'If the gods are real and they are just, then why is the world so full of injustice?' - launches a tour of the logical and evidential problems of evil across the religions of Westeros. Augustine's privation theory and free will defense are tested against the Faith of the Seven (with Catelyn answering 'Because of men like you'), Hume's dilemma of impotence or malevolence is applied to the old gods that Arya scolds at Harrenhal for failing to save Ned, and Leibniz's best-possible-world reply is weighed against natural evils like the storm that sank the Windproud. The chapter concludes that only the evidential problem sticks: the sheer quantity of suffering in Westeros is evidence against just gods, though nothing counts against the uncaring gods Brienne describes.
- ch. 25 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 26 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" **·** Plato, Thomas Hobbes — Ring of Gyges (why be moral?); The Social Contract; Justice in the Republic — Joffrey on the Iron Throne, immune from punishment for beheading Ned, is read as Glaucon's ring of Gyges made flesh: the chapter asks what reason he has to be moral when he can get away with everything, noting he lacks even Gyges's advantage of secrecy and is hated like the Mad King. Hobbes's social contract is considered and rejected as a motive for Joffrey - King's Landing is no Hobbesian community like the Night's Watch, and Joffrey does better free-riding on everyone else's morality. The Platonic answer carries the chapter: like Ned Stark, who refused the throne, lived honorably in Winterfell, and regretted trading his honor at Baelor, the just person with an ordered soul has the only genuine happiness, which Joffrey's desire-satisfying cruelty forfeits.
- ch. 27 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Nagel, Immanuel Kant — Moral Luck; Humanity as an End in Itself; The Categorical Imperative — Tyrion - born a reviled dwarf who killed his mother, yet heir to Lannister wealth, wits, and Jaime's knightly example - frames Nagel's paradox of moral luck: his compassion toward Jon Snow and Sansa is constitutive luck, and his carefully run wildfire defense of King's Landing shows resultant luck, since the same prudent act deserves praise in one outcome and blame had it burned the city. Kant's good will, applied to Tyrion's protection of Shae as treating her as an end and never merely as a means against Cersei, is offered as a rescue but only neutralizes resultant luck, leaving constitutive and circumstantial luck untouched. The chapter ends with Nagel's shrinking 'extensionless point' of genuine agency, concluding we cannot keep both moral responsibility and the principle that we are blamed only for what we control.
- ch. 28 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" **·** James Rachels — Moral Relativism — Through Daenerys's horror at her Dothraki wedding - public sex, lethal duels, and later the gang-rape of captives that Drogo calls 'the way of war' - the chapter distinguishes descriptive cultural relativism from normative moral relativism. The diversity of ethical codes in Westeros (crannogmen, wildlings, Craster) plus Cersei and Jaime's incest and the Targaryen brother-sister marriages show moral codes genuinely vary, and the audience's harsher judgment of Tyrion's whoring than of his siblings' incest illustrates our own culturally conditioned gut reactions. But the chapter concludes relativism must be rejected: no culture's say-so makes rape acceptable, while eating horse meat is fine and sibling incest depends on circumstances.
- ch. 29 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" **·** Simone de Beauvoir, Immanuel Kant — Feminism; One Is Not Born a Woman; Heteronormativity — Sansa's journey from tourney-romance believer to 'they are no true knights' anchors a feminist argument that chivalry is unjust as a code, not merely corrupted by bad knights like Ser Gregor and Ser Meryn Trant, who beats Sansa at Joffrey's command. The pedestal, as Gloria Steinem says, is a prison: chivalry makes knights eternal guardians and ladies permanent childlike wards, a move the chapter explains through Spivak's account of saviors who turn the people they help into dependents and through Beauvoir's point that the custom of ladyhood manufactures the very 'fact' of female fragility it claims to protect. Arya's needlework, Brienne's unbeaten sword, Loras crowning Sansa while loving Renly, and Varys's riddle on power show the knight/lady roles are neither natural nor exhaustive, so 'there are no true knights' because chivalry itself blocks human flourishing.
- ch. 30 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 31 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Martin Heidegger, Aristotle, Cicero, Heraclitus — Authenticity; Fate and Foreknowledge; Bad Faith; Existence Precedes Essence — The chapter asks whether Daenerys's fatalism - she feels truly free only once she believes fate is in control - can coexist with freedom in a Westeros where class and gender fix everyone's essence. Aristotle's sea-battle argument from bivalence and Cicero's Idle Argument (Dany will rule whether or not she leaves Meereen) are dismantled as having no practical consequences for deliberation, while Sartre's anguish and bad faith explain Cersei's 'necessity' excuse for arresting Ned and Sansa's refusal to own her lie about Joffrey and Mycah. Heidegger's authenticity then resolves the paradox: like Ned choosing between family and honor and Jon choosing his Night's Watch vows over kinship, freedom is not escape from one's past but owning it - Heraclitus's 'character is destiny,' becoming who you are.
- ch. 32 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle, Laozi — Virtue Ethics (arete); Wu Wei (effortless action); Zen Buddhism; Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Arya's arc from Needle to answering 'No one' is read as Aristotelian virtue ethics fused with East Asian philosophy: virtues are habits acquired only through practice, so the Water Dance's endless drills and martial-arts blocks that must become automatic mirror the honest person who tells the truth without deliberating. Syrio's lessons - 'looking is not seeing,' the story of the ordinary tomcat the other bravos mistook for a fabulous beast, being fully present with your trouble - teach Zen seeing and mindfulness, while 'be like water' and the steel that must be part of your arm embody Laozi's effortless wu wei. The Faceless Men's initiation, from discarding her possessions to the literal removal of Arya's face, becomes the Zen slaying of the ego, making her a 'Zen-Ninja-Aristotelian' on the path to eudaimonia.
- ch. 33 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" **·** Plato, Friedrich Nietzsche — The Golden Rule — The chapter models court intrigue as game theory: games of incomplete information over Joffrey's legitimacy, the predictability of honorable opponents like Barristan Selmy, and Catelyn's reverse induction from the seemingly 'irrational' attack on Bran to deduce that Jaime threw him. Tyrion is the star player, converting one-shot death sentences into repeated games - recruiting Bronn as his champion at the Eyrie, buying off the mountain clans with Lannister arms - and showing how 'A Lannister always pays his debts' works as strategic reputation. Against Plato's view of love as the madness that ruins rational play (Maester Aemon's 'love is the bane of honor'), the chapter uses Nietzsche's 'drop of reason in madness' to argue that lovers' merged interests expand rationality itself, culminating in a Golden Rule upgraded to 'understand others as they understand themselves.'
- ch. 34 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" **·** Michel Foucault — Power/Knowledge; Discipline and Docile Bodies — Applying Foucault's History of Madness, the chapter argues that sanity in Westeros is an act of naming by the powerful: Joffrey literally remakes the drunken Ser Dontos into a court fool, and 'Mad King' Aerys's brutality gets labeled madness while comparable violence by Jaime (throwing Bran from the window) and Robert (ordering Daenerys's assassination) counts as sane necessity. The power/knowledge nexus and the psychiatric fusion of crime with insanity explain how Robert's personal hatred of Rhaegar hides behind a medicalized diagnosis of Targaryen madness. The chapter then tracks Foucault's shift from sovereign to disciplinary power and technologies of the self: Dontos internalizes and performs his fool identity, Dany wonders 'perhaps I am mad' at Drogo's pyre, and Catelyn's freeing of Jaime is written off by Robb's bannermen as 'a mother's madness.'
- ch. 35 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 36 "Game of Thrones and Philosophy" *(notes)*

## Gardening – Philosophy for Everyone - Cultivating Wisdom

- ch. 1 "The Encyclopedia of Eastern Orthodox Christianity" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "CYCLING" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "CYCLING" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "CYCLING" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "CYCLING" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "CYCLING" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "CYCLING" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "CYCLING" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "CYCLING" **·** Aristotle, Epicurus, David Hume, Plato — Virtue Ethics; Epicurean Pleasure (ataraxia); Eudaimonia (flourishing) — O'Brien's editorial introduction frames gardening as engaging core philosophical questions and previews the volume. It argues that gardening promotes a good life not through hedonistic pleasure but through virtuous traits like patience and a Stoic-tinged tranquility that ancient philosophers from Epicurus to Hume prized, and it invokes Kant to cast the gardener as a visual artist. It also sketches gardens as cosmic symbols (Pythagorean ratios at Versailles) and closes on Plato's Phaedrus image of educating the soul as tending plants.
- ch. 10 "CYCLING" *(front)*
- ch. 11 "GARDENING" **·** Aristotle — Virtue Ethics; Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Isis Brook argues that gardening objectively improves both the land (through composting, nurturing plants, and Warwick Fox's 'responsive cohesion' with wider contexts) and the gardener's moral character. Taking a virtue-ethics approach and drawing on David Cooper, she holds that gardening practices incidentally cultivate patience, humility, recognition of reality, care for others, and open-heartedness. She uses concrete cases—Sackville-West's white garden at Sissinghurst, her own failed Cerinthe and thriving ferns—to show how collaborating with nature nurtures these flourishing-related virtues.
- ch. 12 "GARDENING" **·** Virtue Ethics — Meghan Ray reads classical agrarian texts to show that Greeks and Romans invested cultivating the soil with civic, moral, and religious meaning. Through Hesiod's Works and Days and Xenophon's Oeconomicus (with its Gardener/King Cyrus), and the Roman Scriptores Rei Rusticae (Cato, Varro, Columella) plus Virgil's Georgics, she argues farming was portrayed as the only honorable, pious profession that builds character—diligence, endurance, justice—and sustains the state. The chapter connects horticulture to a virtue-centered vision of the good and righteous life.
- ch. 13 "GARDENING" **·** Tom Regan — Anthropocentrism; Animal Rights (subjects-of-a-life) — Matthew Hall challenges the exclusion of plants from gardening ethics, tracing it to the Garden of Eden where plants are denied life and given only instrumental value for humans. Against this he sets kincentric alternatives—the Achuar mothering manioc, the Raramuri treating plants as kin, and the living apple trees of the Hesperides—and Albert Schweitzer's reverence-for-life ethic. He proposes gardeners offer plants three 'gifts': acknowledging their autonomy, giving them care, and giving weeds space, extending moral consideration to plant life itself.
- ch. 14 "GARDENING" **·** Food Ethics; Wilderness and Nature Aesthetics — Helene Gammack surveys the seventeenth-century self-sufficient English estate as a fusion of pleasure and productivity, using Schellinks's account of Bridge Place, Celia Fiennes's journals, and figures like John Evelyn and Sir William Temple (whose Upon the Gardens of Epicurus links fruit to health and tranquility). She traces dovecotes, fishponds, deer parks, orchards, and glasshouse exotics, and connects the modern 'grow your own' revival—driven by sustainability, food miles, and provenance—to that older harmony of beauty and use in cultivating food.
- ch. 15 "CYCLING" *(front)*
- ch. 16 "GARDENING" **·** Wilderness and Nature Aesthetics — Jo Day uses archaeological and textual evidence to argue that the earliest elite gardens of Mesopotamia, Egypt, and Minoan Crete carried symbolic roles beyond pleasure. Assyrian kings like Sennacherib and Ashurnasirpal II displayed captured exotic plants as 'botanical imperialism' and tokens of power over nature and conquered lands, while Egyptian temple and tomb gardens (Hatshepsut's incense trees, plants tied to deities) served ritual and mythic functions. The chapter shows the ancient garden as a stage for status, military prowess, and religious performance.
- ch. 17 "GARDENING" **·** Martin Heidegger, Maurice Merleau-Ponty — Virtue Ethics; Embodiment (the lived body) — Michael Moss examines how the British grew Brussels sprouts and other 'English' vegetables across the Empire to recreate home and 'put down roots,' reading gardens as tropes of Britishness and imperial identity. He draws on David Cooper's claim—rooted in Heidegger's and Merleau-Ponty's notions of dynamic, embodied engagement—that 'certain garden practices necessarily induce virtues,' which colonial writers invoked to justify gardening's moral and civilizing purpose. He exposes the irony that most 'British' vegetables were themselves appropriated introductions, mirroring Kew's plant-hunting empire.
- ch. 18 "GARDENING" **·** John Locke — Natural Rights — Laura Auricchio shows how General Lafayette made his estate at La Grange an embodiment of liberal, transatlantic political ideals. Inspired by Washington as a Cincinnatus figure and by Arthur Young's agrarian reform, Lafayette imported American plants and English husbandry, keeping meticulous farm records and staging tours that framed the land around liberty (Fox's planted ivy, self-sufficiency, beneficent stewardship). The chapter argues his cultivation of crops and identity presented the garden as 'American ground' expressing natural rights and republican virtue.
- ch. 19 "GARDENING" **·** Karl Marx, Aristotle — Class Struggle; Man as Political Animal — Elizabeth Scott argues that the wartime allotment gardens of East London's Poplar were engines of working-class political development. Through allotment associations, laboring men learned letter-writing, accounting, public speaking, and democratic self-governance, negotiating with borough councils and the Port of London Authority and building solidarity that fed movements like the Poplar Rates Rebellion. She reads the 10-rod plot as restoring self-sufficiency and citizenship to a disenfranchised class, making gardening a grassroots site of cooperation, socialist activism, and political enfranchisement.
- ch. 20 "CYCLING" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 21 "GARDENING" **·** René Descartes — The Question Concerning Technology — MacDonald reads the garden at Dumbarton Oaks as a 'technology of enchantment,' arguing against the nature/culture dualism that frames gardening as either a 'battle' with or a 'fusion' of nature. Drawing on Jane Bennett's account of enchantment (wonder mixed with the uncanny) and actor-network theory, he treats the garden as a shifting assemblage of humans and non-humans—Charles Simonds's hybrid 'Growth' sculpture, leaking water pipes, the inscribed Arbor Terrace wall speaking with Dante's and Mildred Bliss's voices—whose surprising 'crossings, complexity, and circuits' produce magic. Descartes appears as the philosopher who prized wonder as the most fundamental passion, and the disenchantment-of-the-world narrative (Weber) is set against gardening as re-enchantment.
- ch. 22 "GARDENING" **·** Immanuel Kant — Judgment of Taste and the Sublime — Barwell and Powell challenge Kant's classification of landscape gardening as a subcategory of painting ('for the eye only') by arguing that gardens, unlike static paintings or sculpture, essentially present the passing of time visually. Because gardens are made of living organisms that grow, flower, senesce and decay at different rates—an oak, a drift of crocuses, a managed lawn—they generate visual rhythms analogous to musical rhythm, with diurnal and seasonal cycles supplying a 'rhythmus firmus' (pulse and meter) that nature, not the designer, provides. Using Susanne Langer's claim that 'music makes the passage of time audible,' they conclude gardens make time visible and so deserve status as a distinctive art form.
- ch. 23 "GARDENING" **·** Michel Foucault, Jeremy Bentham, Jacques Derrida, Plato, Gilles Deleuze — The Panopticon (surveillance); Deconstruction; Pragmatism — Shapiro argues that Frederick Law Olmsted's Central Park is a major artwork realizing a 'pragmatic picturesque,' and reads the English picturesque garden as a 'diagram' (in Foucault's and Deleuze's sense) of the glance—the roving, liberated look—explicitly contrasted with Bentham's Panopticon, the diagram of the objectifying gaze. Using Derrida's paradox of the frame, he shows the picturesque hides its boundary (the ha-ha) while Olmsted inverts the diagram, making the frame explicit and admitting the city so citizens meet in democratic 'gregarious' recognition—a Platonic argument (aesthetic education in the Republic) turned pragmatic in the climate of Peirce, James and Dewey. Christo and Jeanne-Claude's 'The Gates' distills the park's diagram as a gift of time.
- ch. 24 "CYCLING" *(front)*
- ch. 25 "GARDENING" **·** Pythagoras, René Descartes, Plato — Pythagorean Harmony of the Spheres; The Apollonian and the Dionysian — Neuman decodes the Bosquet de la Colonnade at Versailles as a symbolic Temple of Apollo that glorifies Louis XIV as overseer of cosmic harmony. He argues Hardouin-Mansart fused the circular peripteral temple with Hadrian's Maritime Theatre, and used simple ratios (1:2 bays) drawn from the Pythagorean-Platonic system of universal proportion—the harmony of the spheres tied to Apollo's seven-stringed lyre and seven planets—to align architecture, the seven planets, and monarchical order. The spandrel pairing of lyre and panpipe evokes the Apollo–Marsyas/Pan contests, read as the reason-versus-passion, Apollonian-versus-Dionysian dichotomy; the whole garden is also linked to Descartes's geometrization of boundless axial space.
- ch. 26 "GARDENING" **·** Augustine of Hippo, René Descartes, The Buddha, Friedrich Nietzsche — Augustine on Time; Impermanence (anicca); Eternal Recurrence — Mara Miller argues gardens do not merely sit inside time but actively structure six kinds of time (scientific, objective/shared, kairos, subjective, temporality, historical), opening with Augustine's puzzle that he knows what time is until asked to explain it. She contrasts Greek chronos with kairos (the right moment to plant after the last frost), shows how Versailles propagated a shared time (eternal, historic, recurring) and eighteenth-century English landscape gardens taught the long-range vision capitalism needed, and describes cyclical 'eternal recurrence' (Eliade). Watching a dry Zen garden or fireflies, the visitor becomes the constant who notices—reworking Descartes's cogito as 'I notice, therefore I am'—while Japanese stepping-stones enforce a Buddhist mindfulness of impermanence in the present moment.
- ch. 27 "GARDENING" **·** David Hume, Epicurus, Albert Camus, Ludwig Wittgenstein — On Miracles; Philosophical Skepticism; Epicurean Pleasure (ataraxia) — O'Brien reads Voltaire's closing injunction in Candide, 'we must cultivate our garden,' through David Hume to argue that gardening is therapy—a retreat from metaphysics and religion into the tranquility of common life. Hume rejects belief in miracles and religious 'enthusiasm' not by logic but by recalling everyday reasoning (a melon seed, not a miracle, explains melons in the greenhouse), and the gardener is the sophisticated artisan-reasoner attuned to nature's regularities. Gardening inculcates Humean virtues (industry, patience, balancing action, pleasure and indolence) and Haybron's attunement/engagement/endorsement, yielding Epicurean ataraxia; the Sisyphean gardener (Camus) fighting slugs, and Wittgenstein taking up gardening as a cure, illustrate the point.
- ch. 28 "CYCLING" *(front)*
- ch. 29 "GARDENING" **·** The Philosopher King — Evans presents Texcotzingo, the hilltop garden-retreat of the fifteenth-century Texcocan king Nezahualcoyotl, as the achievement of an Aztec philosopher-king who was landscape designer, hydraulic engineer and statesman. She argues the botanical garden as a genre—a 'green encyclopedia' cataloguing a realm's flora—was pioneered by Aztec kings, and that within an animist cosmos where mountains and water pulse with sacred spirit, Nezahualcoyotl's aqueduct, pools and terraced gardens made the hill a living embodiment of the altepetl (the 'water-hill' city-state). The garden thus symbolically enacts his political domain, dynastic history (three pools for three ancestral capitals) and cosmic order, even as she cautions his reputation as poet and monotheist was inflated by later Spanish and Christian chroniclers.
- ch. 30 "GARDENING" **·** Epicurus, Lucretius, Seneca — Epicurean Pleasure (ataraxia); Death Is Nothing to Us; On the Nature of Things — Campbell explains why Epicurus set his school in a walled garden on the edge of Athens ('Live unnoticed'), contrasting it with the Stoics' public Stoa: the Garden was a self-sufficient community of friends pursuing ataraxia through natural, necessary pleasures and philosophy as therapy for the soul. Drawing on Lucretius's account of prehistory in On the Nature of Things, he shows the Epicureans saw cities as a decadent fall—driven by the fear of death and the gods—from a near-paradise of proto-Epicurean friendship pacts, and read the garden and the locus amoenus of pastoral picnics as a recovered golden age. Diogenes of Oinoanda's inscription imagines a future in which all become gardening philosophers and city walls become redundant.
- ch. 31 "GARDENING" **·** Plato, Socrates — Allegory of the Cave; Socratic Method (elenchus) — Cotton reads the grower image in Plato's Phaedrus—the wise gardener who sows seed in proper soil for a slow harvest rather than the frivolous 'gardens of Adonis'—as Plato's model of philosophical education as natural growth. The educator plants logoi (discourse) in a 'proper soul,' where it must be cultivated by the learner's own reflection and eventually reproduces itself in others, against Thrasymachus's vessel model of pouring knowledge in. She connects this to the Republic's cave, the Apology's gadfly, and the Symposium/Theaetetus images of pregnancy and midwifery, then argues Plato's own aporetic, contradiction-laden dialogues and the corpus as a whole act as the wise grower, teaching readers to become 'gardeners of their own souls.'
- ch. 32 "CYCLING" *(notes)*

## Girls and Philosophy - This Book Isn't a Metaphor for Anything

- ch. 1 "Girls and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Girls and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Girls and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Girls and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Girls and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Girls and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Girls and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Girls and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "Girls and Philosophy" **·** Socrates — The Unexamined Life; The Meaning of Life — The editors' introduction argues that Girls is philosophical because it is stark, raw, and accurate about what is common to human existence: identity, friendship, expectations, and ultimately the meaning of life. It claims Hannah, for all her horribleness (the funeral pitch to her dying editor's widow, doing coke to write truthfully about drugs), follows Socrates's call to know thyself in a millennial register. It then surveys the volume's approaches - existentialism, business ethics, the seven deadly sins, Daoism and Confucianism, Freud, Kristeva - as ways of knowing Hannah, Marnie, Jessa, Shosh, Ray, and Adam, and thereby ourselves.
- ch. 10 "Girls and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 11 "Girls and Philosophy" **·** Theodor Adorno, Immanuel Kant — The Culture Industry; Autonomy of the Will; Feminism — Frost argues that critics who call Girls unwatchable because Hannah steals the hotel housekeeper's tip and the characters are unlikeable are watching television the wrong way. Using Adorno's 'How to Look at Television,' he shows how mainstream standardized shows (Friends and its copies) pre-digest viewing, how Sex and the City exemplifies Adorno's ideological 'fake-realism,' and how hidden messages of adjustment work through audience identification - none of which Girls permits with its raw sex scenes, Hannah's unglamorous body, and Jessa's non-judged promiscuity. Because identification with Hannah, Marnie, and Jessa (the diary-reading, the request to Laird for heroin) is impossible, Girls escapes the culture industry and exercises the viewer's critical faculties toward the Kantian autonomy and emancipation Adorno demands - which is also why role-model feminist criticism (Catherine Scott's) misses the point.
- ch. 12 "Girls and Philosophy" **·** Catharine MacKinnon — Feminism; Dominance Approach (pornography and gender) — Robison-Greene defends Girls against the twin criticisms that it is bad feminism (Hannah 'sluttin' around' jokes, Adam's degrading sex with Hannah) and irresponsibly all-white. She argues there is no single correct conception of feminism from which to judge Dunham, that 'demeaning sex is anti-feminist, so portraying it is anti-feminist' is an invalid argument (by analogy with portraying racism in 12 Years a Slave), and notes MacKinnon's dominance view that sex itself may be inherently demeaning only to conclude the debate is legitimate, not settled against the show. On race, she contends Dunham writing authentically from her own half-Jew, half-WASP experience produces better art than tokenism would, and that the obligation to fix representation falls on networks and creators with the relevant experience, not on punishing one writer.
- ch. 13 "Girls and Philosophy" **·** Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); Philosophy of Love — McCrossin asks whether Adam's Season Two finale run across Brooklyn to break down the door of a dangerously unraveling Hannah ('I was always here') is heroic, set against Hannah's 'You don't need to be like a hero' chair-and-mattress scene and Adam's 'I don't want to help... I was doing it because I love you' quarrel. He argues an act is heroic - being an 'Extraordinary Samaritan' like Wesley Autrey, the Subway Samaritan, unlike the trained Sullenberger - only when done on purpose to help, not from obligation, training, or love's compulsion, testing the point with Batman's motives. Since Adam insists no one ever does anything from a want to help, his rescue's heroic status is left genuinely open.
- ch. 14 "Girls and Philosophy" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Greene constructs the conditions of a 'legitimate spoiler' - the thing cannot be too old (with never-expiring exceptions like Darth Vader being Luke's father, Snape killing Dumbledore, and the Planet of the Apes reveal), cannot be common knowledge (Anne Boleyn's beheading, the Titanic hitting the iceberg), and must be significant rather than mundane. Applying them, he concludes Girls cannot be conventionally spoiled: nobody 'ships' Hannah and Adam the way audiences shipped Ross and Rachel, because the show's plot turns are mere backdrop for its conversations and meltdowns. The only true way to spoil Girls is to reveal its hilariously written lines in advance.
- ch. 15 "Girls and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 16 "Girls and Philosophy" **·** Susan Wolf, Socrates, Friedrich Nietzsche, Jean-Paul Sartre — The Meaning of Life; Meaning in Life; The Unexamined Life; The Myth of Sisyphus — Blessing and Wezowiczi test theories of life's meaning on the two narcissists of Girls: Hannah, whose purpose-driven pursuit of being a writer (manufacturing experiences, ignoring her father's medical procedure) shows that goal achievement alone collapses into Taylor's and Camus's Sisyphus problem, and Jessa, whose 'life addict' desire-satisfaction illustrates the Nietzschean live-in-the-moment view the authors find reckless and irresponsible. Using Wielenberg's excrement-eater and the subjectivist/objectivist debate, they land on Susan Wolf's 'loving objects that are worthy of love,' then argue via Socrates's examined life and Sartre's bad faith that neither Hannah's phony narrative nor Jessa's protective persona counts as authentic, examined living.
- ch. 17 "Girls and Philosophy" **·** Søren Kierkegaard, Albert Camus, Jean-Paul Sartre, Simone de Beauvoir — The Absurd; Authenticity; Existence Precedes Essence; Bad Faith — Archibald reads Jessa as Girls' quintessential existentialist: her unbothered abortion ('I'm offended by all the supposed to's'), her 'I am a junkie' honesty, and her refusal to blame her rough past (Sartrean facticity) enact existence precedes essence and Camus's embrace of the absurd. Kierkegaard's 'the crowd is untruth' and 'leveling' explain her contempt for rehab group conformity, while her panic marriage to Thomas-John after Katherine's motherly intervention is diagnosed as bad faith and angst. Her assisting the dying Beadie's suicide attempt - and calling 911 when Beadie recants - becomes the chapter's closing confrontation with Camus's one serious philosophical question, answered by living 'without appeal.'
- ch. 18 "Girls and Philosophy" **·** Martin Heidegger — The Question Concerning Technology; Thrownness (Geworfenheit); Authenticity — Addis uses Heidegger to explain why Hannah cannot 'rewrite her world': her enframing habit treats everything and everyone (Laird, Marnie, Adam) only in terms of feeding her 'writer' identity, so her world stays tiny even in vast New York. Heideggerian world, thrownness (Jessa's surprise wedding Shoshanna attends in white, Charlie's departure), and the power of art to de-enframe structure contrasts with Marnie, whose singing - prompted by Ray's question - expands her horizons. Hannah's crying-at-her-desk realization at the ad agency is read as her one genuine acceptance of thrownness, though her enframed self-absorption pulls her back.
- ch. 19 "Girls and Philosophy" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre — Bad Faith; Authenticity; Radical Freedom and Responsibility — Crossley reads the millennials of Girls through Sartre's anguish: Hannah, cut off financially in the pilot, is 'condemned to be free' in a recession that owes her nothing, and Marnie's firing from the gallery ('I don't know what I want... I wish someone would tell me') confronts her with facticity and the terror of self-determination. Marnie's flight into wishing for a moral dictator and her later choice to sing map the bad faith/authenticity distinction, as does Hannah swapping her hoarder-story reading for a pretentious piece on death to please Ray. Even Jessa's total rejection of the 'supposed to's' is outed as its own bad faith, and the ad-agency arc shows twenty-first-century authenticity is inevitably compromised by the need to eat.
- ch. 20 "Girls and Philosophy" **·** Søren Kierkegaard, Jean-Paul Sartre — Anxiety (the dizziness of freedom); Bad Faith; The Leap of Faith; Authenticity — Van Loon explains the q-tip scene ('On All Fours') as Kierkegaardian anxiety made flesh: freed by her parents and facing the e-book deadline, Hannah confronts the infinity of possibilities and, like Sartre's vertigo over the cliff, shoves the q-tip into her ear - embracing all possibilities at once and relapsing into bad faith by becoming a victim of fate. Each girl handles freedom differently: Shoshanna remains in an innocence prior to freedom, Jessa jokes her way out of seriousness, Marnie denies her anxiety. Against these, the Season Two FaceTime call to Adam models Kierkegaard's leap of faith and the 'knight of faith' - surrendering, making oneself vulnerable, and accepting that anxiety will return.
- ch. 21 "Girls and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 22 "Girls and Philosophy" **·** Søren Kierkegaard, Friedrich Nietzsche, Aristotle, Judith Butler, Immanuel Kant, Jeremy Bentham, John Stuart Mill, Jacques Lacan — Virtue Ethics; Deontology; Utilitarianism; Ressentiment — Sirvent and Avery take Shoshanna's Listen, Ladies! advice book to ask whether rules can govern romance, contrasting Kantian rule-duty ethics (Ray's dating rules that are 'not commandments') with Bentham-Mill utilitarian calculation (Jessa's 'depends on the virgin'). Kierkegaard and Nietzsche - extended through Žižek's Lacanian 'Big Other' of God, Duty, or Utility - show such impersonal systems let us dodge responsibility: Jessa plays Kierkegaard's aesthete escaping boredom, while Hannah's 'just to be an asshole' boss seduction and the Marnie-Elijah hookup express ressentiment and self-hatred. Their positive proposal is Aristotelian virtue ethics - becoming a certain kind of person - completed by Butler's Undoing Gender: to be ethical you must risk being 'undone by each other,' as Adam demands of Hannah ('You don't wanna know me').
- ch. 23 "Girls and Philosophy" **·** Augustine of Hippo, Thomas Aquinas, Immanuel Kant, Seneca — Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); Virtue Ethics; Objectification — Mahon and Walker rebrand the seven deadly sins for Girls: Self-Absorption as Augustine's 'love of one's own excellence' (Hannah pitching her e-book at her editor's funeral, calling her grandmother 'the old Flo Job... sluttin' around'), Entitlement for Hannah's 'voice of my generation' demands and Marnie's rage at Charlie's success, and Sleeping Around as the John Paul II point about reducing a person to an object of gratification - Adam's fantasy sex with Hannah and his degradation of Natalia. Envy (Hannah on Tally's tragedy), gluttony in drug-fueled relapses (Aquinas's five ways), flakiness as Kant's indolent free-rider who needs others but never helps (Jessa missing her own abortion), and Senecan irritability (Jessa spitting at the Bushwick party, Ray's hatreds, Shoshanna's 'whiny nothings' outburst) complete the survey - redeemed by the girls' stubborn friendship.
- ch. 24 "Girls and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, Karl Marx — Humanity as an End in Itself; Alienation; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Wible asks whether the workplace abuse in Girls - Hannah's two unpaid intern years, the harassment at the law firm, the 30-day e-book deadline that triggers her OCD, Marnie hostessing at a gentlemen's club - is defensible by young workers' inexperience and the 'experience paradox.' Against the boot-camp defense he argues from the meaning of work (Gini's 'I work, therefore I am,' Schumacher's good work) and from Marx on alienation from the products of labor, but his core standard is Kant's: persons must be treated as ends, never merely as means, which the editor who wants the book and not the person violates. He concludes that young workers' vulnerability (no savings, no leverage, reluctance to blow the whistle) warrants paid internships, transparency, and two-way respect.
- ch. 25 "Girls and Philosophy" **·** John Rawls, Thomas Hobbes — Moral Relativism; Justice as Fairness — Ketcham sets Hannah B (Brooklyn) beside an imagined Hannah D (a Darfur village terrorized by the Janjaweed) to ask whether personal fulfillment depends on culture or on the individual. Rejecting relativism (which, per Alford, cannot even condemn suppression of belief), he turns to Rawls's Law of Peoples - five minimal human rights any well-ordered society must grant - and builds a thought experiment, 'Blandland,' a minimally compliant country where both Hannahs relocate. His conclusion: even Rawlsian minimum rights, under which life can remain Hobbes's 'solitary, poor, nasty, brutish, and short,' plus personal drive and the attitudes of others, jointly and indeterminately decide what either Hannah can become.
- ch. 26 "Girls and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 27 "Girls and Philosophy" **·** Sigmund Freud, Jacques Lacan — The Unconscious; Philosophy of Love — Fisher explains the show's cycle - the girls want someone only while that person rejects them - through psychoanalytic theories of desire. Freud supplies the id/ego/superego machinery (cocaine-era Hannah dancing to 'I Don't Care (I Love It)' as pure id; Dunham's body-critical audience as the superego), while Lacan's desire - the 'untold' that must be named (Hannah's bedroom kink questions to Eric), drive, and jouissance beyond the pleasure principle - shows desire circling its object and never being satisfied, as when Hannah's e-book deal collapses into her worst OCD episode. Žižek's objet a as the object-cause of desire reads Marnie loving Charlie only once he is over her: desire reproduces itself rather than reaching its goal.
- ch. 28 "Girls and Philosophy" **·** Søren Kierkegaard, Herbert Spencer — The Stages on Life's Way — Keszeg first maps Sex and the City's four women onto classic fashion theories - Barthes's fashion system for Carrie, Carlyle's clothing morality for Samantha, Spencer's evolutionary equity for Charlotte, Veblen and Simmel's status signaling for Miranda - then argues Girls' four break these inherited codes. Marnie's perfectly status-calibrated outfits fall into 'complete freefall' with her life; Jessa wears bohemian otherness (Yves Saint-Laurent, 'Down with the Ritz - long live the street'); Shoshanna embodies the Kierkegaardian aesthetic stage of shifting, consequence-free equivocation ('I think it's time to unchoose some of those choices'); and Hannah's one-piece pyjamas, worn outside, invent a 'sophomoric dress' that negates the fashion system entirely - the generational attitude (via Mannheim) the show both celebrates and satirizes.
- ch. 29 "Girls and Philosophy" **·** Judith Butler, Simone de Beauvoir — One Is Not Born a Woman; Gender Performativity; Objectification — Adleberg asks whether Jessa is right that she is not 'the ladies.' Against Shoshanna's common-sense biological determinism and pop-neuroscience like Brizendine's 'male brain' (refuted via brain plasticity and Fausto-Sterling), she deploys Butler's point that identity categories are always normative and exclusionary (Marnie's 'that's what men do' to Charlie) and Beauvoir's and Spelman's social constructionism - mothers already treating infants differently - to show gender is made, not born. With Haslanger's call to resist unjust gender meanings, she concludes that neither conforming (Marnie craving dominant Booth Jonathan) nor defying stereotypes (Jessa's bathroom sex, 'what if I want to feel like I have udders?') should be compulsory; only objectifying harm, as with Adam and Natalia, is wrong.
- ch. 30 "Girls and Philosophy" **·** Sigmund Freud — Philosophy of Love — Zurba reads all four girls through Julia Kristeva's adolescent malady: the faith that 'there certainly can not not be an absolutely satisfying other,' which curdles into a 'kamikaze syndrome' of nihilism and self-destruction when the ideal love object disappoints. Hannah's idealized Adam ('too absent, too present or too weird') fails her, producing self-deprecating sex, compulsive counting, and the q-tip collapse; Marnie's disappointment becomes boredom and cruelty toward Hannah; Jessa's absent father leaves her 'swimming without a life vest' on heroin; Shoshanna invests hers in Sex and the City pastiche yet alone learns to love Ray as he is. Following Kristeva (and Freud) the cure is not the perfect partner but love as psychic openness, imagination, and treating the self as 'a work in progress.'
- ch. 31 "Girls and Philosophy" **·** Plato, Aristotle — Virtue Ethics — LaRocca asks what the liberal arts (Dunham's Oberlin, the girls' rural-college degrees) are for, reading Girls through the Greek notion of paideia: Plato's discussions of whether virtue can be taught (Protagoras), rhetoric as a mere 'knack' versus care of the soul (Gorgias), and education as becoming human (Republic), joined to Isocrates's 'development of judgment' as the father of liberal education. Against contemporary self-expression-and-selfie culture, he holds that the self is constituted outer-directed - Hannah's mentorship of her traumatized cousin Rebecca being the show's clinic in empathy - and he uses the agon (Honig, Mouffe) to recast the Beach House blowup as contest over whether the others are 'worthy' opponents. Dunham-as-doppelganger (like Plato's Socrates, like Kaufman in Adaptation) makes the show itself a work of paideia and a criticism of modern academic humanism, with Aristotle's Poetics invoked for drama's basis in conflict.
- ch. 32 "Girls and Philosophy" **·** Confucius, Zhuangzi, Laozi, Mencius, Xunzi — Rectification of Names; Wu Wei (effortless action); Zhuangzi's Perspectivism (butterfly dream) — Carleo and D'Ambrosio read Hannah's 'busy trying to become who I am' through the classical Chinese debate over names: her self-claimed title 'writer' preceding any conduct is the Confucian nightmare of names divorced from actuality, diagnosed by the Analects's rectification of names ('rulers should rule, fathers should father') and the sheep-stealing passage warning against over-identifying with a single role. Adam, by contrast, lives the Daoist ideal - the empty vessel of the Daodejing acting by wu wei, and Zhuangzi's butterfly dream fluidity in his role-play sex and absorbed woodworking, unattached to titles. Mencius and Xunzi frame the resolution: by Season Three Hannah finally rectifies names at GQ while Adam's non-identity turns delusional, vindicating the ideal of being an 'internal sage and external king' - Daoist in private, responsible to roles in public.
- ch. 33 "Girls and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 34 "Girls and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 35 "Girls and Philosophy" *(notes)*

## Golf and Philosophy - Lessons from the Links

- ch. 1 "The Philosophy of Popular Culture" *(front)*
- ch. 2 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Copyright Page" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Acknowledgments" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Introduction Warm-Up" **·** Plato, Aristotle — The Unexamined Life; The Meaning of Life — Andy Wible's introduction argues that if Plato and Aristotle were alive they would be avid golfers, because both golf and philosophy seduce great minds with problems that look easy yet prove endlessly difficult. It frames golf as 'a Sudoku that cannot be solved' and pairs it with the eternal questions (right and wrong, who am I, beauty, God, meaning) to preview the volume's chapters. Golf becomes a vehicle for the examined life, enlivening philosophy through the lived experience of the game.
- ch. 7 "The Front Nine" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "I. The Beauty of the Game" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "First Hole Golf and the Importance of Play" **·** Plato, William James — The Meaning of Life; Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Al Gini defends play as intrinsically valuable and essential to being human, drawing on Huizinga's three criteria of 'true play' and William James's ideal of the amateur who plays 'for the love of it.' Using his golf-obsessed father as an extended case, he shows how golf functions as a self-contained, absorbing 'refuge from ordinary life' that delivers completion, coordination, and cooperation, echoing Plato's claim in the Republic and Laws that sport forms both person and community. The chapter treats golf as an end in itself whose joy sustains a flourishing life rather than as a means to external reward.
- ch. 10 "Second Hole On the Beauty and Sublimity of Golf" **·** Immanuel Kant, Arthur Schopenhauer, Thomas Hobbes — Judgment of Taste and the Sublime; The Categorical Imperative; Respect for Persons — Fudge and Ulatowski argue that golf's aesthetic appeal has two sources: the course as a designed landscape best appreciated (via Walton's 'Categories of Art') under the category of gardens, and the sublimity of players' character. They apply Kant's 'Observations on the Feeling of the Beautiful and Sublime' to distinguish beautiful courses (Augusta) from sublime ones (Pebble Beach, St. Andrews) and to explain Bobby Jones's self-imposed penalty at the 1925 U.S. Open as the noble sublime, since 'subduing one's passions through principles is sublime.' They link the consistent rule-following golf demands to Kant's categorical imperative and read human dignity as 'beyond price' against Hobbes's price-based account, while Schopenhauer's critique of the French garden frames the environmental objection to courses.
- ch. 11 "II. Golf and Moral Character" *(front)*
- ch. 12 "Third Hole Finding the (Fair)Way with Confucius and Ben Hogan" **·** Confucius, Aristotle — Ren (benevolence/humaneness); Li (ritual propriety); Xiao (filial piety); Practical Wisdom (phronesis) — Laumakis builds an analogy between pursuing excellence in golf and living a flourishing human life, then contrasts two models. Reading Confucius's Great Learning and Analects onto golf, he casts a repeating swing as a 'well-ordered kingdom' rooted in self-cultivation, and reinterprets ren, li, yi, and xiao so the 'authoritative player' masters the game through ritual practice, a teacher-parent (xiao), and doing what is appropriate in each lie. Against this social conception he sets Ben Hogan's Five Lessons as a 'rugged individualist' account stressing solitary practice and personal responsibility, and proposes a contextual pluralism as a mean between communitarian absolutism and individualist relativism.
- ch. 13 "Fourth Hole “Quiet…Please!” Reflections on Golf and Civility" **·** Aristotle, David Hume — Virtue Ethics; The Golden Mean; Respect for Persons — McNaron argues that golf promotes civility both empirically and normatively through its rules (self-scoring, self-called penalties) and its etiquette (silence during a swing, care for the course). Using an Aristotelian virtue-ethics frame plus Kekes's 'conduct according to custom' and Calhoun's expressivist account of civility as communicating respect, he treats civility as a mean acquired by habituation and opposes it to skloka, the petty hostility of win-at-all-costs sport. He defends golf's quiet against charges of classism, reads Tiger Woods's outburst at a shutter-clicking photographer as justified anger at an etiquette breach, and names the Ryder Cup's jingoism, boorish spectators, and intrusive media as threats to golf's civil, contemplative character.
- ch. 14 "Fifth Hole How Golf Builds and Shapes Moral Character" **·** Aristotle, Carol Gilligan — Virtue Ethics; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); The Ethics of Care — Beller and Stoll ask why empirical moral-reasoning research finds that sport generally does not build character, yet golfers score highest of all athletes. Using Aristotle's account of right conduct and Lickona's triad of moral knowing, feeling, and acting, they argue character forms through role models, social environment, and cognitive dissonance, supported by neuroplasticity research on the brain's capacity for moral growth. They contrast golf's self-policing tradition (Bobby Jones, Meg Mallon, Greg Norman, the film Bagger Vance) with the gamesmanship coached in team sports, and address Gilligan's 'different voice' by reporting that female golfers reason at least as highly as males, concluding that golf's honor-bound moral community sets it apart.
- ch. 15 "Sixth Hole Virtue Ethics From Caddyshack to Better Golf" **·** Immanuel Kant, Aristotle — Virtue Ethics; Consequentialism; The Categorical Imperative — McElreath tests three ethical theories against golf intuitions and argues virtue ethics wins. Consequentialism rightly judges a postal carrier's choice to work over golf but wrongly permits Judge Smails in Caddyshack to kick his ball when no bad consequences follow; Kantian ethics correctly condemns Smails's deceptive maxim as non-universalizable but mishandles the play-versus-work case. Virtue ethics, judging acts by what an ideally virtuous person would characteristically do, gets both right via the relevant virtues of justice and honesty, and he then applies benevolence, justice, and tolerance to argue golf should better treat women, minorities, disabled players (Casey Martin's cart), and homosexuals.
- ch. 16 "III. Ethical Issues within Golf" *(front)*
- ch. 17 "Seventh Hole Cheating and Gamesmanship among Amateur and Professional Golfers" **·** Ring of Gyges (why be moral?); Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); Moral Relativism — Lumpkin argues that amateur golfers routinely cheat while professionals do not, and dissects four rationalizations amateurs use: appeal to ignorance, appeal to popularity, appeal to tradition (Callahan's 'cheating culture'), and the belief that 'no one will ever know' — a golf-course version of Plato's Ring of Gyges where invisibility dissolves restraint. Drawing on Kohlberg's stages of moral development, she claims professionals reason at higher levels because officials, peers, and disqualification (e.g., J. P. Hayes) enforce the sport's social contract, whereas amateurs face no punishment. She concludes casual golfers would do better not to keep score than to keep a 'mythical' one, earning the right to score only once they follow the rules.
- ch. 18 "Eighth Hole Playing Through Racism and Sexism in Golf" **·** Feminism; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Objectification — Gray examines whether golf is a model of equality or a preserve of 'wealthy white men,' using George Carlin's polemic and the Tiger Woods 'Chosen One' narrative as foils. He documents 'social closure' and the 'Grass Ceiling' through Augusta National's history, the Shoal Creek and Martha Burk/NCWO controversies, and the PGA's former Caucasian-only clause, then turns to sexism via McGinnis, McQuillan, and Chapple's interviews and Connell's 'hegemonic masculinity,' showing how gendered 'red/women's tees' entrench a false dualism of male and female play. Citing McDonagh and Pappano's Playing with the Boys, he argues coercive sex-segregation rests on false assumptions and calls for transparency about club membership.
- ch. 19 "IV. Golf and Rationality" *(front)*
- ch. 20 "Ninth Hole Is Golf Inherently Irrational" **·** Aristotle — The Golden Mean — Shier asks why it is not insane to deliberately place obstacles (rules, hazards) between oneself and one's goal, quoting Woodrow Wilson and Mike Seabrook on golf's 'essential lunacy.' He distinguishes internal goals (holing the ball in fewest strokes) from external goals (challenge, competition, fresh air) and, echoing Aristotle, argues excellent conditions lie at the mean between too-hard and too-easy: the regulatory and environmental restrictions make internal goals difficult precisely so external goals become achievable, so the obstacles are rational, not irrational. Using Bernard Suits's view of games as 'the voluntary attempt to overcome unnecessary obstacles,' he applies the framework to handicapping, ad hoc anti-Tiger course changes, and distance technology that threatens golf's characteristic skill set.
- ch. 21 "The Back Nine" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 22 "V. Personal Reflections" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 23 "Tenth Hole Life Lessons" **·** Wu Wei (effortless action); Zen Buddhism — Tom Regan narrates his return to golf, playing hallowed Pebble Beach and then a humble nine-hole course at Pismo Beach, dogged throughout by a perfectionist inner 'doppelganger' who demands he hit his best shot every time and berates him when he fails. The lesson comes from Jason, an ex-convict at Pismo, who tells him it doesn't matter how good you are 'as long as you're there when you play' and to 'just let it go' rather than think about golfing. Regan then plays nine holes without a word from his doppelganger, dramatizing an effortless, present, ego-quieting acceptance close to wu wei and Zen letting-go rather than any doctrine he names.
- ch. 24 "VI. Golf, Mysticism, and Self-Understanding (Amen Corner)" *(front)*
- ch. 25 "Eleventh Hole Philosophy in the Kingdom Golf, Mysticism, and Philosophy" **·** William James, Bertrand Russell, Jean-Paul Sartre, Ralph Waldo Emerson — Zen Buddhism; Radical Freedom and Responsibility — Mark Huston diagnoses why golf, more than baseball, gets mystical treatment in pop culture, using Michael Murphy's novel 'Golf in the Kingdom' (Shivas Irons, 'true gravity') and the film 'The Legend of Bagger Vance' (Junuh's 'authentic swing'). He plays William James's four marks of mysticism against Bertrand Russell's skeptical criteria, ties golf's appeal to individuality, contemplative time, nature (citing Emerson's 'transparent eye-ball') and Sartrean existential freedom, arguing that a golfer's freedom over whether to follow the rules mirrors Sartre's claim that in play man sets his own rules and must take responsibility. He reads Shivas Irons and Bagger Vance as Zen roshi figures using paradoxical, koan-like teaching to bring the student to a self-transforming 'authentic swing.'
- ch. 26 "Twelfth Hole Midround and Midlife Defining Moments" **·** John Locke, René Descartes, The Buddha, David Hume, Heraclitus — Personal Identity (memory theory); Personal Identity; Non-Self (anatman) — Andy Wible uses defining mid-round shots (the water ball on Augusta's twelfth, the shank that ruins a round) as a way into theories of personal identity, asking why some events make us who we are. He runs through body theory, Descartes's soul theory ('I think, therefore I am'), and Locke's memory theory (the prince-and-cobbler case), refining it with Shoemaker's quasi-memories, to argue that memory is central to identity and explains why a golfer cannot shake a bad shot. He then weighs the Buddhist/Humean/Heraclitean denial of a permanent self as a coping tool ('someone else hit the shank') and debates whether the beta-blocker Propranolol, which dulls adrenaline-fixed memories, could ethically erase the yips much as it treats PTSD.
- ch. 27 "Thirteenth Hole Free and Easy Wandering on the Golf Course Swing Like a Taoist" **·** Zhuangzi, Laozi — Wu Wei (effortless action); The Tao (the Way); Taoism (Daoism) — Scott F. Parker reads golf's paradox—that you play your best shot when you stop trying so hard—through the Taoism of the 'Tao Te Ching' and the 'Chuang Tzu.' Using the parable of the archer (skill is constant but attachment to the prize ruins the shot), Cook Ting carving the ox by spirit not force, and Master Yu accepting his deformity, he draws out nonattachment to results, 'doing without doing' (wei wu wei), and self-knowledge/self-acceptance, illustrated by Zach Johnson never going for a par 5 in two at the 2007 Masters and Tiger Woods rarely swinging all-out. The essay embodies the Taoist point that the Tao that can be told is not the eternal Tao: to try to not-try is self-defeating, so one must simply not try.
- ch. 28 "VII. Golf and Idealism" *(front)*
- ch. 29 "Fourteenth Hole Plato and Confucius on the Form of Golf From the Real to the Ideal" **·** Plato, Confucius, Socrates — Theory of Forms; Li (ritual propriety); Ren (benevolence/humaneness) — Stephen Laumakis stages a thought experiment pitting Plato the Idealist against Confucius the Realist/Pragmatist on how to think about and play golf. On Plato's theory of Forms, every club, ball and swing is an imperfect copy of a timeless, unchanging Form—there is a 'perfect' swing (Tiger Woods, Ernie Els) that par and the handicap system let us measure ourselves against, so golf is about striving toward the Ideal. Confucius, read through the 'Great Learning' and 'Analects' (li, ren, self-cultivation, unrelenting practice), instead prizes the situation-sensitive sage who negotiates each bad lie (Phil Mickelson's flop shots). Laumakis concludes the good golfer must integrate both: pursue perfection while wielding practical wisdom about when to go for it and when to lay up.
- ch. 30 "Fifteenth Hole The “Ideal” Swing, the “Ideal” Body Myths of Optimization" **·** Judgment of Taste and the Sublime — Jason and Laurence Holt attack two 'optimization' myths in golf culture via a philosophy of kinesiology: the averaging model (that the ideal swing is the statistical mean of elite swings) and the athlete model (that more general fitness makes a better golfer). Against the first they invoke multiple realizability from philosophy of mind—there is no single perfect swing, since Bobby Jones's over-parallel backswing, Hagen's short fast swing, and the quirky swings of Trevino and Furyk all succeed on different bodies. Against the second they note golf's modest physical demands and argue Tiger Woods's heavy conditioning caused injury without improving results. They diagnose the false ideal as an aesthetic bias favoring 'beautiful' swings and athletic-looking bodies, using Suits's definition of games to defend golf as a genuine sport of gross physical skill.
- ch. 31 "VIII. Golf and Meaning" *(front)*
- ch. 32 "Sixteenth Hole Golf and the Meaning of Life" **·** Robert Nozick, Epicurus, Arthur Schopenhauer, Albert Camus, John Stuart Mill — The Meaning of Life; The Experience Machine; Philosophical Pessimism; The Myth of Sisyphus — Randy Lunsford uses Annika Sorenstam's early retirement to ask what makes golf—and life—meaningful, surveying religious versus scientific accounts of purpose and E. J. Bond's claim that meaning comes from pursuing objectively valuable ends, not just satisfying desire. He tests Epicurean hedonism and Nozick's experience machine (we want to really shoot the course record, not merely feel we did), Taylor's reworking of the Sisyphus myth (meaning via injected desire), and Schopenhauer's pessimism that satisfied desire only yields boredom. He argues golf's very finitude—eighteen holes, like a mortal life—gives it meaning, so excellence, relationships, playing by the rules, and taking responsibility for each shot are what make both golf and life worthwhile.
- ch. 33 "Seventeenth Hole More Than a Playing Partner Golf and Friendship" **·** Aristotle — Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Philosophy of Love — Andy Wible argues that although golf looks like the ultimate solitary sport, it is unusually good at fostering friendship, and analyzes friendship through Aristotle's three kinds (utility, pleasure, and the highest, based on the friend's good character), supplemented by Laurence Thomas (choice, equality, mutual trust) and Blum on care as a moral phenomenon. Golf's long unhurried rounds, self-called penalties, handicap system, shared memorable shots, and retirement communities build the intimacy, trust and equality Aristotle prizes, and Robert Simon's 'mutual quest for excellence' reconciles competition with goodwill. Wible then confronts friendship's partiality—exclusionary country clubs and the sex-segregated LPGA—using Marilyn Friedman's voluntary friendship communities to argue clubs should drop discrimination on race, sex, religion and orientation while keeping friendship's care and loyalty.
- ch. 34 "IX. Parting Shots" *(front)*
- ch. 35 "Eighteenth Hole Swing Thoughts Tough Questions for a Tough Game" **·** Plato, George Berkeley, Augustine of Hippo — Ring of Gyges (why be moral?); Subjective Idealism (esse est percipi) — Andy Wible closes the volume with fourteen open-ended 'swing thoughts'—short provocations applying philosophy to golf rather than arguing a thesis. He asks whether golf's subdued, rational fans make it morally superior (Plato and Aristotle on reason ruling emotion), whether Tiger Woods effectively wielded Plato's Ring of Gyges by sinning while invisible, whether Berkeley's idealism means your clubs and playing partners are mere perceptions (and whether that would matter), and invokes Augustine's 'an unjust law is no law at all' to license civil disobedience against unfair rules like spike marks. Further questions probe environmental harm, prayer as cheating, Casey Martin's cart, and whether perfect virtual golf would count as 'real' golf.
- ch. 36 "The Field" *(notes)*
- ch. 37 "Index" *(notes)*

## Green Lantern and Philosophy - No Evil Shall Escape This Book

- ch. 1 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 7 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — The editors' introduction explains why Green Lantern merits a philosophy volume: an intergalactic corps of different species that must cooperate, rings limited only by willpower and imagination, and a cast (Hal, Kyle, John, Guy, Sinestro, Mogo, Soranik Natu) dense with philosophical questions. It frames the book's guiding problems: what limits justice places on the ring, whether a Green Lantern can do literally anything, and how the color-coded emotional spectrum should affect judgment and decision-making.
- ch. 8 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle, John Stuart Mill, Plato — The Golden Mean; The Greatest Happiness Principle; Virtue Ethics (arete); Tripartite Soul — Southworth uses the War of Light to ask what role emotion should play in moral reasoning: Sinestro rules Korugar through fear, the Star Sapphires kidnap Sinestro Corps prisoners to convert them to love, Atrocitus's rage was forged by the Manhunters' massacre, Larfleeze hoards, and the Blue Lanterns merely hope that hope matters. On Aristotle's account every corps errs by excess: Atrocitus's rage makes him miss chances to kill Sinestro, Fatality's love benches John Stewart from saving his friends, and even the Green Lanterns' will-only ideal fails, since total fearlessness is itself vicious and Plato's picture has reason directing emotion like a general directing soldiers. He ends by defending Mill's utilitarianism: the Guardians' real failure was not detachment but pretending to be detached, and reading them as flawed 'benevolent, disinterested spectators' vindicates the Corps while explaining their moral growth after Scar and the Blackest Night.
- ch. 10 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, John Searle, Gilbert Ryle, David Hume — The Ghost in the Machine; Autonomy of the Will; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); Free Will vs Determinism — White asks whether Sinestro's quip that Hal's mind is 'like a muscle unused for years' is a mere metaphor and argues willpower is a real, exercisable strength. Against the Humean desire-belief model, which cannot explain Tom Kalamaku fixing planes instead of browsing Facebook, he uses Searle's 'gaps' to locate an independent will that can choose against one's better judgment, and Kant's account of virtue as moral strength in fulfilling duty despite inclination. Holton and Baumeister's findings that self-control depletes and strengthens like a muscle fit the comics exactly: Ollie is exhausted by a single ring use in Rebirth, Hal sweats through moving a planet, and John Stewart must 'suppress my own terrors' to hold the Mosaic walls.
- ch. 11 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" **·** Carol Gilligan, G.W.F. Hegel, Pythagoras, René Descartes — The Ethics of Care; Feminism; Binary Oppositions — Donovan and Richardson read the Maltusian split, with the male Guardians banning emotion through the Book of Oa on Oa and the female Zamarons leaving to found the violet light of love on Zamaron, as the Western association of men with reason and women with emotion. They deploy Gilligan's different-voice reply to Kohlberg, the Pythagorean table of opposites via Genevieve Lloyd, Hegel's exclusion of women from the public sphere, and Jaggar's liberal-feminist critique of 'ahistorical' reason, whose comic-book analogue is the third law banning relationships between Lanterns. The chapter ends by arguing the standoff is a false dichotomy the comics themselves begin to dissolve: Kyle's 'Lanterns with heart' speech to the Guardians, the repeal of the third law, and the Indigo Tribe's plan to recombine the seven corps into the white light of creation.
- ch. 12 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 13 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" **·** Bernard Williams — Moral Relativism — Terjesen asks how Sinestro and Universo ever passed recruitment and answers that the Guardians' two-prong test of honesty and fearlessness reflects moral pluralism: universal rules kept deliberately thin so that different moral codes can coexist, which is why the vicious 'good bad man' Jack T. Chance keeps his ring on Hellhole. But honor, honesty, and justice are thick ethical concepts in Bernard Williams's sense, requiring cultural context to apply, so Laira's execution of a surrendered Amon Sur and the Guardians' own cover-ups (the Corpse, Kentor Omoto) cannot be settled by appealing to thin 'justice,' and the Alpha Lanterns' internal-affairs role cannot fix it. Moral particularism leaves the Corps with unresolvable disagreements about who the bad apples are.
- ch. 14 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" **·** G.W.F. Hegel, Johann Fichte — Dryden reads Hal and Sinestro through Hegel's account of the tragic hero: each acts from a one-sided individual moral compass (Hal rebuilding Coast City in Emerald Twilight and trying to reboot the universe in Zero Hour; Sinestro dictating order to Korugar), collides with communal ethical life, and is destroyed, yet stays aesthetically admirable for the magnificence of the commitment. Fichte's planned police state of ID cards, registered addresses, and total surveillance is exactly the order Sinestro would have liked, the scheme Hegel mocked as tyranny. Kyle escapes the tragic pattern because fear and self-doubt keep him responsive to his groups' shared values, making him 'corps conscience' rather than 'the greatest.'
- ch. 15 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" **·** Harry Frankfurt — Compatibilism; Free Will vs Determinism — Michaud argues Hal is a murderer who should not be forgiven for Emerald Twilight even though the Parallax retcon blames a fear entity. Using Frankfurt's cases (a parasite that would force you to rob a bank you were already choosing to rob), he shows that lacking alternatives does not excuse, then applies Fischer and Ravizza: Hal's rampage issued from his own reasons-responsive mechanism ('it was still my hand that did it'), so he had guidance control. Since Hal's willpower later let him resist Parallax to reignite the sun in Final Night, Michaud concludes he likely had regulative control too, and that absolving him insults the very will that made him the greatest Lantern.
- ch. 16 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" **·** Plato — Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); Forgiveness — Darowski traces Hal's shifting motivations across fifty years: the Showcase #22 origin where honesty and fearlessness make heroism a simple vow, the selfish Parallax era after Coast City's destruction, the Spectre's atonement, and the guilt-driven Lantern of Rebirth. Plato's claim that knowing the good suffices for doing good would mean Hal never really chose heroism, but the chapter rejects it, since morality alone failed to stop Hal's turn to Parallax. Set against the darker post-Watchmen industry, it asks whether guilt-driven atonement is a nobler motive than simple duty and what standards we should hold heroes to.
- ch. 17 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 18 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" **·** David Hume, Adam Smith — Reason Is the Slave of the Passions; The Impartial Spectator — Terjesen reads the O'Neil-Adams pairing as moral rationalism (Hal) versus moral sentimentalism (Ollie): Hal jails the boy assaulting slumlord Jubal Slade because 'He was breaking the law' and defends a racist senator's speech on free-speech principle, while Ollie works from the gut. Hume's 'Reason is and ought only to be the slave of the passions' explains both Ollie's effectiveness and Hal's collapse into Parallax, since no argument from Kilowog could restrain a grief only a stronger passion could. But sentiment needs an impartial filter: Smith's impartial spectator condemns Ollie's near-murder of Black Canary's attacker and his abandonment of the heroin-addicted Speedy, while Hal's cooler principle keeps the Native American land dispute from Ollie's violent plan. The conclusion is that morality is sentiment-based but reason must police it, so each hero needs the other.
- ch. 19 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" **·** Plato, Aristotle — Theory of Forms — Patterson maps the Comics Code censorship debate onto Plato's Republic Book X, where imitative art is thrice removed from the Forms (the god's table, the carpenter's table, the painter's picture) and feeds the passionate soul, versus Aristotle's Poetics, where imitation is natural and tragedy's pity and fear yield catharsis. The O'Neil-Adams run embodies Aristotle: the slum story where a black tenant asks why Green Lantern helps 'orange skins' and 'purple skins' but never 'black skins,' the Ulysses Star land dispute, and Speedy's heroin addiction broke Code taboos so readers would learn from engagement rather than distance. Within the comic, Hal plays the establishment Platonist who would change an unjust law rather than disobey it and Green Arrow the Aristotelian activist, so their friendship dramatizes the ancient quarrel over whether art corrupts or teaches.
- ch. 20 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, Epictetus — Autonomy of the Will; The Dichotomy of Control — Dryden asks whether the Lanterns' relationships are weaknesses or strengths. Epictetus's dichotomy of control would have recruits greet the Sinestro Corps' display of their families' eyeballs with Stoic indifference, and Kant brands fear for loved ones heteronomous; yet Donna Parker refuses the ring for her children, Amnee and Matoo Pree surrender their rings rather than stop loving, and Kilowog dines with energy constructs of his dead family, showing attachments partly constitute who people are. Feminist relational autonomy resolves the tension: Kyle escapes Parallax only by grasping Hal's hand ('we don't have to do it alone'), and Soranik Natu cannot excise Sinestro's crest or parentage any more than Donchin's Huntington's-disease daughter can sever family ties, so autonomy competency means negotiating unchosen relationships rather than escaping them.
- ch. 21 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 22 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" **·** Bernard Williams — Deontology; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Tallman and Southworth set the Hippocratic Oath against the Green Lantern oath in Soranik Natu, a Korugarian surgeon who must either stop healing mid-battle or disobey the Book of Oa. They survey the options, with Wonder Woman discarding vows for consequences, Isamot Kol letting the Corps trump his vow to Qalyra, Dr. Mid-Nite always putting patients first, and Dick Grayson going by context, then argue through Hursthouse that Natu, who treats both oaths as absolute, is in an irresolvable dilemma of her own making, more like the double-betrothed man than Williams's tragic Jim and the twenty Indians. Their verdict is that she should turn in the ring, a cautionary tale for real military medics whose oaths also collide.
- ch. 23 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, G.W.F. Hegel — Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation; Humanity as an End in Itself — White distinguishes retributivism from deterrence and rehabilitation through the Corps' execution vote on Sinestro and Fatality's lex talionis pursuit of John Stewart over Xanshi ('You have four billion eyes to account for'). Kant grounds retribution in dignity, so punishment must treat the criminal as an end, never merely as a means to deterrence, and is owed to him for his crime ('You did this to yourself,' Hal tells Sinestro), while Hegel adds that punishment annuls the crime and embodies the criminal's own right. Feinberg's expressive theory explains the Korugarian crowd that felt 'cheated of Korugar's redemption' when the execution stalled, and the chapter closes by separating justice from vengeance: Ollie's arrow through Prometheus and Laira's 'It was justice!' killing of a surrendered Amon Sur were personal revenge, which only impartial state agents may convert into justice.
- ch. 24 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" **·** Iris Marion Young, John Stuart Mill, Aristotle — The Ethics of Political Violence; The Greatest Happiness Principle; Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation — Novy uses Terry Berg's gay-bashing coma in 'Brother's Keeper' to argue hate crimes are a kind of terrorism: justice, in Iris Marion Young's enabling sense, requires social conditions in which citizens can develop and exercise their capacities, and an attack that makes every gay teenager police holding hands in public harms the whole group, so Mill's greatest-happiness principle and Feinberg's expressivism justify enhanced penalties. The chapter then turns on Kyle, who tortures Daniel Hirsh on Rikers Island and nearly beats his two accomplices to death, asking whether extraordinary crimes justify extraordinary procedures in a post-9/11 world. Kyle's horror afterward, begging Flash to undo the last twenty-four hours and losing his will to protect, and Batman's pointed 'But that is me, isn't it?' mark the line between punishing brutality and becoming it.
- ch. 25 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" **·** Plato — Ring of Gyges (why be moral?); Tripartite Soul; The Philosopher King — Barkman retells Glaucon's Ring of Gyges, a shepherd's invisibility ring used to seduce the queen and murder the king, as Lord Acton's claim that absolute power corrupts, and answers with Plato: injustice disorders the tripartite soul, so the unjust sacrifice the happiness that matters most. The Guardians of the Universe are Plato's guardians writ cosmic, wise enough to rule themselves; Yalan Gur fell only when the yellow weakness was removed from his ring, Sinestro never had the wisdom ('You have never understood us'), and Hal's lapses, resigning for Carol Ferris and the Emerald Twilight rampage, are ignorance of the proper value of things rather than evil, since 'Hal's not evil... He believes he's acting for the right reasons.' The power ring is a modern Ring of Gyges, and Plato's test explains both who passes it and who fails.
- ch. 26 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 27 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle, Plato, Heraclitus, Parmenides — Aristotle on the Soul — Finkelman uses the Corps' strangest members, Mogo the sentient planet, Leezle Pon the smallpox virus, the billion-puffball Collective, and Dkrtzy RRR the mathematical equation, to run the philosophy-of-biology debate over what counts as an individual. Plato's 'carving nature at its joints' frames the question; Heraclitean flux and Parmenidean changelessness picture monism's extremes; Aristotle's substance and soul deny Mogo is a living individual since its ecosystems (the Resal) do not serve it as organs serve a body; Darwin's natural selection makes organisms the real units, with Sinestro's attacks a kind of artificial selection that adapted the Corps. Gene reductionism (George C. Williams and Dawkins) versus Gould's contextual pluralism then decides how to count one billion puffballs as one Lantern.
- ch. 28 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" **·** Charles Sanders Peirce — Jaissle reads the Book of Oa as Peirce's communal mind made physical: Kyle's ring translates the sounds of sector 3599 only because language 1,456 is already cataloged by the community, and even the Guardians cannot translate Hal's cursing until some Lantern has heard it. Because the individual apart from his fellows 'is only a negation,' the Guardians' deletion of the Blackest Night prophecy from the Book shows the fallibility of even immortal individual minds, while the Corps' shared experience across the War of Light vindicated the Cosmic Revelations. The green light beats the emotion-driven corps because willpower uniquely balances individual agency with communal knowledge.
- ch. 29 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" **·** The Metaphysics of Time Travel; Fate and Foreknowledge; The A-Series and B-Series of Time — Kind uses John Stewart's Justice League arc, thrown into the future by Chronos, meeting his son Warhawk, and hiding it from Hawkgirl while dating Vixen, to stage fatalism: Pike and Taylor's arguments that a foreknown future, whether God's or a time traveler's, cannot be otherwise. Time travel itself forces a choice between presentism and eternalism, since Warhawk must 'exist now, already, in the future' for GL and Batman to visit him, and Smart's tenseless four-dimensionalism, with GL a series of timeslices and 'now' as subjective as 'here,' dissolves the fatalist worry by banishing tensed facts. The chapter lands on GL's battlefield advice to his son: 'Plant your feet,' owning a fate you cannot escape.
- ch. 30 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 31 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" **·** David Hume, William James — Malloy explains Hal's endless giant hands and boxing gloves as an inherent limit of imagination rather than lazy writing: only the self-contradictory (square circles, married bachelors) cannot be imagined at all, and on Hume's empiricism imagination merely recombines copies of impressions, ideas, by the associations of contiguity, resemblance, and cause and effect, so Hal reproduces his most deeply impressed, most ordinary experiences. James's 'living options' bound what is worth imagining, and Kyle's JLA speed-to-sound machine shows invention's two stages: the experience-limited Lantern supplies the idea while the semisentient ring handles execution. The upshot is advice to go out and make new impressions.
- ch. 32 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" **·** Gottfried Leibniz, David Hume — The Best of All Possible Worlds; The Problem of Evil — Nielsen asks whether a Green Lantern with unlimited willpower would be a god and answers no through Leibniz: God is the absolutely perfect being, where perfection excludes contradiction so that knowledge and power admit of perfection but 'the greatest of all numbers' does not, and such a being creates the best of all possible worlds. Hume's trilemma (impotent, malevolent, or whence evil?) is met with the theodicy that finite minds, like Lanterns baffled by Guardian decrees, cannot compare our world to the discarded possibilities, each perhaps as bad as Qward. Since omnipotence requires omniscience and omnibenevolence, not even Parallax, the Anti-Monitor, or Kyle as Ion counts as omnipotent: admirable and powerful, but not gods.
- ch. 33 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Jones tests Clarke's law, that any sufficiently advanced technology is indistinguishable from magic, against the mythos's two green power sources: the Guardians' scientific Central Power Battery and the Starheart, the wild magic they quarantined, which later made Alan Scott's body pure green flame. Mauss's distinctions (magic private and rule-bound, religion communal and belief-powered, science open and collaborative) show the Guardians operate like sorcerers rather than scientists, secretive and incurious, while Kyle's Rebirth speech on the emotional electromagnetic spectrum explains why only willpower, the balancing center of the spectrum, can keep order where rage, fear, or love cannot.
- ch. 34 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 35 "Green Lantern and Philosophy" *(notes)*

## Halo and Philosophy - Intellect Evolved
