Mills illustrates with the Hogwarts house-elves whose enslaved labor underwrites the students' comfort, and concludes that defending patriotism requires reconciling it with the equal moral worth of all people.
- ch. 63 "The Importance of Community" **·** Martha Nussbaum — Cosmopolitanism — Mills rehabilitates patriotism through Martha Nussbaum's cosmopolitanism, which justifies special care for one's own in universalist terms. He models this on the Hogwarts Houses: sorting students into small, loyal Houses is the most effective way to give every student an equally good education, so Hermione's preference for Gryffindors is justified not by their superiority but because everyone prospers when each member gives special care - just as parents rightly love their own children more without thinking them morally more important. Patriotism is thus a virtue when it serves the interests of all countries' citizens and a vice when it fosters injustice.
- ch. 64 "Human Flourishing and the Preservation of Dying Cultures" **·** Aristotle — Communitarianism; Man as Political Animal — This section examines communitarianism - the view, drawing on Aristotle's claim that humans are political animals, that identity, values, and flourishing require membership in a tradition-bound community - and reads the Death Eaters' blood-purity mantra as its violent extreme, noting Voldemort's murder of the Muggle Studies teacher for advocating cultural mixing. Harry's identity as 'the Boy Who Lived' and his misery living 'under the stairs' outside the wizarding community illustrate how selves are community-constructed, while debates over the treatment of women in traditional cultures expose the tension between preserving a culture and violating the liberty of its members.
- ch. 65 "NOTES" **·** Cosmopolitanism; Communitarianism — The concluding section weighs communitarian rootedness against Jeremy Waldron's cosmopolitan claim that the hybrid, culturally mobile life is the only fitting response to the modern world. It asks whether centaurs like Firenze should be kept in the Forbidden Forest or absorbed into wizarding society, and argues the books endorse both cosmopolitanisms: equal moral worth of all persons (against Voldemort's racism) and flourishing through intercultural fluency, embodied in Harry's ease among Muggles, goblins, and house-elves and in part-this part-that characters like Hagrid, Lupin, Sirius, Fleur Delacour, and Firenze.
- ch. 66 "Unknown" *(notes)*
- ch. 67 "Is Dumbledore a Libertarian" **·** The Minimal State — Beth Admiraal and Regan Lance Reitsma open by defining political libertarianism - the only just state is a minimal 'nightwatchman' state protecting citizens from force, fraud, and theft - and report that commentators Travis Prinzi and law professor Benjamin Barton read Dumbledore and the series as libertarian. They object that the books never champion free markets, antiwelfarism, or national sovereignty, and that Dumbledore, despite holding the posts of Headmaster, Supreme Mugwump, and Chief Warlock, never advocates any general political philosophy at all, so they ask on what evidence the libertarian reading actually rests.
- ch. 68 "Barton’s Libertarian Interpretation of the Potter Series" **·** The Minimal State — The authors rebut Prinzi's three arguments that Dumbledore is libertarian-minded. Distrust of political power (the Ministry's suspension of Dumbledore, Buckbeak's condemnation, Harry's underage-magic trial) is a non sequitur since non-libertarians limit power through checks and balances too; his defense of house-elves, the giants, and despised-minority teachers like Hagrid, Lupin, and Firenze shows a devotion to liberty liberals share, not the libertarian's distinctively robust right (no paternalism, no victimless-crime laws, no redistribution); and his hands-off management - letting Snape bully Neville, standing apart from Harry's fights - cannot transfer from school to state, and even a libertarian headmaster would be obligated to stop teacher abuse and require professors to teach.
- ch. 69 "NOTES" **·** The Minimal State — This section takes up Barton's claim that the Ministry's tyranny - Azkaban, trialless death sentences, surveillance, a controlled press - makes the series libertarian invective. The authors grant the Ministry is 'a lousy bureaucracy' but note its honest officials like Arthur Weasley, the Wizengamot's majority acquitting Harry, and sensible laws such as the self-defense exception to underage wizardry, and argue the inference from bad governance to a minimal nightwatchman state is a non sequitur: corrupt Supreme Court justices are replaced, not answered by a two-justice court, so the antidote is better governance. Rowling's own politics - welfare, a 1 million pound Labour donation, support for Obama - undercuts any libertarian agenda.
- ch. 70 "Unknown" *(notes)*
- ch. 71 "Plato and Dumbledore Separated at Birth" **·** Plato — The Philosopher King — David Lay Williams and Alan J. Kellner open their essay with paired epigraphs from Dumbledore ('Those who are best suited to power are those who have never sought it') and Plato ('A city in which those who are going to rule are least eager to rule is necessarily best'). Against Lord Acton's dictum that power corrupts, they present Plato's ingeniously simple solution - power must never reside with those who lust for it, since disinterest paradoxically makes the best rulers - and announce that this lesson is central to the climax of the entire Harry Potter series.
- ch. 72 "Fudge and Umbridge The Lessons of Obviously Unfit Power-Brokers" **·** Plato — The Philosopher King; Ring of Gyges (why be moral?) — The section draws a biography-by-biography parallel between Plato and Dumbledore: each lived through upheaval (the Peloponnesian War, the Thirty, and Socrates' execution; Grindelwald and Voldemort), each renounced a political career after disillusion, and each turned from politics to education, founding the Academy and leading Hogwarts. It explains the Republic's philosopher-rulers and their four cardinal virtues, argues that intelligence alone (Lex Luthor, Hannibal Lecter) plus excessive self-love disqualifies a ruler, and introduces Glaucon's Ring of Gyges as Plato's test of incorruptibility - a legend it says Rowling revives in Harry's invisibility cloak.
- ch. 73 "Voldemort and Dumbledore Two Tempted by Power" **·** Plato — The Philosopher King — The authors run Plato's criteria on the wizarding world's obviously unfit power-brokers. Umbridge fails as a ruler: her 'I must not tell lies' blood-quill detentions and multiplying Educational Decrees expose a lust for power that grows with her authority, and she lacks all the cardinal virtues, especially self-control. Fudge's insecurity proves equally corrupting - his denial of Voldemort's return, smearing Harry in the press, and tilting Harry's Wizengamot hearing (moving its time to exclude Dumbledore) all serve only to keep him in office, so both, like Plato's tyrants, lose the power they clutch at.
- ch. 74 "Harry’s Cloak, the Ring of Gyges, and the Temptations of Power" **·** Plato, Immanuel Kant, Socrates — The Philosopher King; Humanity as an End in Itself; The Unexamined Life — This section contrasts Voldemort and Dumbledore as two figures tempted by power. Tom Riddle's egocentrism - frightening and controlling other children at the orphanage, treating his 'friends' as mere followers, killing Lily, and splintering his soul for Horcruxes - violates Kant's dictum never to use persons merely as means and fits Plato's category of the least trustworthy rulers. Dumbledore, though tempted in youth by Grindelwald's 'for the greater good' schemes and later by Marvolo Gaunt's ring, is venerable for Socratic self-knowledge: knowing 'I was not to be trusted with power,' he repeatedly refused the Ministry and so promoted justice by teaching.
- ch. 75 "NOTES" **·** Plato — Ring of Gyges (why be moral?); The Philosopher King — The concluding section argues that Harry passes Plato's Ring of Gyges test, which Rowling rebuilds around the Invisibility Cloak: unlike Gyges, Harry never uses invisibility for selfish gain, refuses Scrimgeour's offer of his dream job, hunts Horcruxes rather than making them, and at eleven pulls the Sorcerer's Stone from his pocket precisely because Dumbledore's enchantment lets only someone who wants to find but not use it do so. This indifference to the lure of power, joined to courage, justice, and self-restraint, is the Platonic quality that makes Harry fit to rule.
- ch. 76 "Unknown" *(notes)*
- ch. 77 "PART FOUR - THE ROOM OF REQUIREMENT A POTTER POTPOURRI" *(front)*
- ch. 78 "Truth in Fiction" *(no index entry yet)* — Tamar Szabo Gendler uses Rowling's 2007 Carnegie Hall announcement that Dumbledore is gay to pose the philosophical problem of truth in fiction: what makes something a fact about a story's world? She catalogs three fan reactions - delight, dismay, and, most interesting philosophically, challenges to Rowling's authority to add facts after the series ended - and notes the puzzling asymmetry that nobody objected to her other revelations, like Neville marrying Hannah Abbott or Lupin's impoverished life, before asking whether canon, readers, authorial thought, or genre conventions determine fictional truth.
- ch. 79 "So, Is Dumbledore Gay" **·** David Lewis, Aristotle — Possible Worlds — Gendler tests whether fictional truth works like historical truth and finds it fails: whereas a biographer discovers facts about the one actual world, a storyteller chooses among infinitely many imaginary worlds - David Lewis's point that 'every way that a world could possibly be is a way that some world is' - so storytelling mixes creation with discovery. She distinguishes the 'primary truths' stated on the Potter novels' 4,100 pages (discounting trivial inconsistencies like Percy's badge color and Moaning Myrtle's S-bend versus U-bend) from unstated 'secondary truths' like Hermione having ten fingers, rejects a maximal inclusiveness principle that would import every actual-world fact into the story, and closes with Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics advice to seek only the exactness a subject allows.
- ch. 80 "Closing Speculations Genre" *(no index entry yet)* — This section weighs the first two criteria for whether it is true in the Potter world that Dumbledore is gay. Textually the claim appears nowhere in the canon but is arguably implied by Dumbledore's intense bond with Grindelwald ('you cannot imagine how his ideas... inflamed me') and the absence of any heterosexual interest; anti-intentionalist theorists (Wimsatt, Beardsley, Gadamer, Barthes, Foucault, Derrida) hold that meaning is public and plural, so Rowling cannot legislate new facts and there are as many Potter worlds as readers, while intentionalists like E. D. Hirsch counter that authors fix meaning - and most fans treat Rowling's announcements as an oral appendix consistent with her hundreds of interviews.
- ch. 81 "NOTES" *(no index entry yet)* — The final section asks whether genre rules out Dumbledore's homosexuality on the ground that children's stories exclude adult sexuality, but answers that Potter is young-adult fiction where adult desires matter (Hagrid and Madame Maxime's flirtation, Snape's unrequited love for Lily), and that adults' desires being invisible to youthful protagonists actually explains the text's silence. It argues Rowling's metaphorical treatment of minority identity - Tonks marrying werewolf Lupin, Hermione's elf liberation, Mudblood and pureblood rhetoric - catches readers in 'an act of unwitting toleration,' and that Dumbledore's love for Grindelwald does real plot work, perhaps reflecting that Rowling 'could not conceive of there being a woman who was his intellectual equal.'
- ch. 82 "Unknown" *(notes)*
- ch. 83 "Choices" *(no index entry yet)* — Gregory Bassham opens with Socrates' 'know thyself' and reads the series as Harry's journey from appearance to reality, centered on the end of Chamber of Secrets: alarmed that he speaks Parseltongue like Voldemort and was nearly Sorted into Slytherin, Harry is reassured by Dumbledore's claim that 'It is our choices, Harry, that show us what we truly are, far more than our abilities.' Bassham asks what choices are, how they differ from abilities, and whether Dumbledore's claim - which Tom Morris calls one of the books' most important philosophical insights - is actually true.
- ch. 84 "How Revealing Are Our Choices" **·** Aristotle, Immanuel Kant — The section builds on Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics, which treats choice (prohairesis) as 'deliberative desire' for things within our power and a better test of character than actions - possibly the source of Dumbledore's remark. It then distinguishes three senses of choice: internal-choice (Draco choosing to kill Dumbledore but being unable to do it), act-choice (Dumbledore's poor youthful choices), and motive-choice, illustrated by Kant's two storekeepers who both refuse to cheat customers, one from fear of jail and one because it is right, so that only knowing a person's motive reveals what choice was truly made.
- ch. 85 "Abilities" **·** Aristotle, Immanuel Kant — Virtue Ethics; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Bassham tests each sense of choice against the novels: internal-choices are uninformative (Dudley resolving to give up sweets), act-choices mislead since character is settled habit - Hermione's occasional rule-breaking for Dumbledore's Army doesn't make her a rebel - and overt acts cannot reveal motives, as Snape's apparent Death Eater loyalty conceals devotion to Dumbledore and Lily. Motive-choices like Harry's decision to enter the Chamber to save Ginny are most revealing, but self-deception (Vernon Dursley's rationalizations) and wizarding complications - Harry's vision of attacking Arthur Weasley, Voldemort's implanted false memory in Morfin Gaunt, the Imperius Curse, Polyjuice Potion, and a Priori Incantatem thought experiment - show that in Rowling's world choices may be unknowable even to their maker.
- ch. 86 "How Revealing Are Our Abilities" *(no index entry yet)* — This section defines abilities as powers or capacities and shows why the concept is 'fuzzy': they come in degrees and vary with circumstance (speaking seventy words of Mermish, conjuring a Patronus only when relaxed). Choices are definite acts - did Pettigrew betray Harry's parents, did Harry break up with Ginny to protect her? - whereas abilities are gradable capacities, a first respect in which the two come apart.
- ch. 87 "Beyond Choices Toward a Deeper Self-Understanding" **·** Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Bassham concedes that most abilities reveal little: trivial, widely shared, involuntary, innate, or unrecognized capacities (Harry's Parseltongue before Sorcerer's Stone) say nothing about character, and even impressive gifts are morally neutral - the resourcefulness, determination, and disregard for rules Dumbledore lists are shared by Harry and Voldemort and differ only in how they are used. But abilities won through hard work and ethical capacities like empathy, prudence, and resilience do disclose character, so Dumbledore is right that choices are generally more revealing, provided we are also honest about what our abilities say about us.
- ch. 88 "Unknown" *(no index entry yet)* — Reflecting on one's choices alone can distort self-knowledge, as Gilderoy Lockhart shows: his inflated self-image survives the Cornish pixie chaos, being blasted off his feet by Snape's Expelliarmus, and removing the bones from Harry's arm, because he looks but doesn't see - he wears what psychologists call cognitive blinders. Bassham therefore previews four strategies for deeper self-understanding - cultivate self-examination, be alert to irrational tendencies, get help from friends, and challenge yourself - each illustrated in the Potter books.
- ch. 89 "Unknown" **·** Socrates — The Unexamined Life — The section urges a Socratic habit of self-examination, with Dumbledore as its model: knowing his besetting weakness was the love of power, he repeatedly refused the post of Minister of Magic, and after his infatuation with Grindelwald made him 'lose his moral compass completely' he distrusted his own judgment in matters of the heart. The Pensieve, which lets Dumbledore siphon off excess memories and review them from a third-person perspective to 'spot patterns and links,' is presented as a literal device for living the examined life.
- ch. 90 "Unknown" **·** Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — This section catalogues the novels' full range of irrational thinking - Percy Weasley's rule fetish, Malfoy's racism and classism, Ron's jealousy, Fudge's closed-mindedness, Luna Lovegood's credulity - before concentrating on wizards' ethical blindness toward house-elf slavery. Because elves like Winky have been conditioned to regard freedom as shameful, most wizards see nothing wrong; Hermione insists that internalized oppression doesn't justify servitude, and Harry's moral growth is marked by digging Dobby's grave with his own hands and writing 'Here lies Dobby, a Free Elf.'
- ch. 91 "Unknown" **·** Aristotle on Friendship (philia) — Because we each view life from our own inside vantage point, friends provide the external perspective self-knowledge requires: Hermione makes Harry see his insensitivity to Cho Chang, the trio helps Neville overcome his shyness, and Hagrid teaches Ron not to wreck friendship over petty jealousy. By contrast, characters without close friends - the Dursleys, Lockhart, Slughorn - stagnate, since honest friends are 'a mirror in which we see ourselves as we really are.'
- ch. 92 "The Ultimate Measure of a Person" **·** Lucretius — The section argues, with Lucretius, that only out-of-the-ordinary situations of challenge and peril tear off the mask and reveal what manner of person one really is, since ordinary behavior - brushing teeth, walking to the bus stop - is stereotyped. Harry and his friends are repeatedly tested and mostly pass (Ron's temporary abandonment in Deathly Hallows the notable exception), emerging like Frodo's hobbits as humble but battle-tested heroes who grow in self-confidence and self-knowledge.
- ch. 93 "NOTES" *(no index entry yet)* — The essay's conclusion delivers the verdict that Dumbledore is right: abilities show what we can do, but choices reveal our qualities of character and what we care about most deeply - as Tobias Wolff says, we define ourselves by the choices made 'day by day, hour by hour, over a lifetime.' The ultimate measure of a person is where he stands when tested by challenge or adversity, by which standard Harry and his friends all 'earn O's, for Outstanding.'
- ch. 94 "Unknown" *(notes)*
- ch. 95 "Positioning Our Prejudgments" *(no index entry yet)* — S. Joel Garver opens with Dudley Dursley's transformation from an entitled bully who treats Harry like 'a dog that had rolled in something smelly' to the cousin who leaves him tea, defies Petunia and Vernon, and shakes Harry's hand at the end of Deathly Hallows. He frames the change as an epistemological question - how Dudley and Harry came to know themselves and each other better - and proposes that in Rowling's world knowing is a process of personal transformation in which encountering ourselves and others makes us better knowers.
- ch. 96 "The Ignorance of Ickle Dudleykins" *(no index entry yet)* — The section introduces Hans-Georg Gadamer's claim that all understanding requires 'prejudgments' - the questions, biases, and expectations we carry like tools from a well-worn toolbox, without which interpretation could not even begin. The issue is never whether we have prejudgments but whether they fit what we are interpreting and whether we hold them loosely enough to let the situation correct them - setting down the hammer and finding the screwdriver when the box is screwed shut.
- ch. 97 "Betrayed by Biases" *(no index entry yet)* — Dudley illustrates prejudgments hardened by environment: Petunia and Vernon's doting ('no finer boy anywhere'), their excuses for his marks and bullying, and 'big-boned' spin seal his self-image against revision. The dementor attack - forcing him to relive the worst moments of his life - strips these defenses away, letting Dudley see himself as 'spoiled, pampered, bullying' for the first time and enabling the gratitude, even affection, he finally shows Harry.
- ch. 98 "Dangerous Dreams" *(no index entry yet)* — This section generalizes Dudley's case: Harry's own intellectual habits - his prejudice against Snape, misplaced trust in the impostor Mad-Eye Moody and the Half-Blood Prince's potions book, confidence in his dreams - blind him to real dangers, and because readers see through his eyes we are misled too. Dumbledore's 'for the greater good' dreams with Grindelwald, Merope Gaunt's love-potion fantasy of Tom Riddle, the locket Horcrux's amplification of Ron's paranoia until he walks out, and the Malfoys' underestimation of Voldemort all show prejudgments twisting interpretation until painful collision with reality forces a rethink - which is why truth matters as a reliable map.
- ch. 99 "The Cost of Overconfidence" *(no index entry yet)* — The section examines Harry's overconfidence about his connection to Voldemort's mind: he treats his dreams as a transparent window into the Dark Lord despite Dumbledore's warning that the connection runs both ways through Legilimency. Confident in his own insight, Harry neglects Occlumency, resists Snape's lessons out of suspicion, and dismisses Hermione's urging, so that Snape's taunt - perhaps Harry enjoys these visions because they make him feel special - comes uncomfortably close to the truth.
- ch. 100 "Memories Help Make Meaning" *(no index entry yet)* — The section traces the price of Harry's prejudices: Sirius's death. Trusting his O.W.L.-time dream of Sirius being tortured and Kreacher's shady, self-satisfied testimony - which he never checks against his friends - Harry launches the Department of Mysteries 'rescue' against Hermione's reasonable objections. Garver notes Harry is only fifteen and acting from noble intentions, and that his wishful seeing mirrors the blinders through which readers too often see what they want to see.
- ch. 101 "Moving Past Misdirection" **·** Augustine of Hippo — Augustine on Time — This section grounds prejudgment in memory through Augustine's Confessions: since the past no longer exists and the future has not arrived, time is known only in human experience, where memory of the past and anticipation of the future meet in the present - and, with Gadamer, prejudgments are personal and social memory, the tradition 'handed down.' The Pensieve embodies this epistemology: it stores and organizes memories, reveals patterns from a third-person view, and shares memory socially, as Dumbledore walks Harry through Tom Riddle's orphanage years, the framed Morfin Gaunt, and Hokey's memories, giving Harry the knowledge of Voldemort's history and vulnerabilities he needs to defeat him.
- ch. 102 "NOTES" *(no index entry yet)* — The concluding section shows every character's prejudgments yielding to evidence: Dumbledore's supremacist dreams die with his sister, Merope's fantasy collapses when she stops the love potion, Ron recovers once away from the Horcrux, even the Malfoys defect for Draco's sake, and Harry matures from naive newcomer to courageous young man able to discern what must be done. Because right knowledge underwrites virtues like bravery and loyalty - and true loyalty to Dumbledore means trusting him despite his real faults - 'epistemology is inseparable from ethics'; Rowling's narrative misdirection puts readers through the same process, so that following Harry we 'don't merely become better readers, we become better people.'
- ch. 103 "Unknown" *(notes)*
- ch. 104 "Tell Me One Last Thing" *(front)*
- ch. 105 "What Is Real" **·** Plato — Allegory of the Cave — Presents the two lines Rowling said she waited seventeen years to write: dying Harry, in the King's Cross limbo, asks Dumbledore whether their conversation is real or only 'inside my head', and Dumbledore answers that of course it is in his head but that does not make it unreal. The section frames this as the philosophical question of separating appearance from reality, cites A. J. Ayer's dismissal of his own near-death experience as hallucination, and introduces Plato's cave as the classic image of mistaking shadows for reality.
- ch. 106 "Going Mental" **·** Plato, George Berkeley, René Descartes, Immanuel Kant, John Stuart Mill — Theory of Forms; Subjective Idealism (esse est percipi); Transcendental Idealism — Argues Harry's question rests on a false dilemma between 'in the head' and 'real', which Dumbledore's reply dissolves. It distinguishes merely-in-the-head items (Hermione as fictional character) from mind-independent ones (Emma Watson, King's Cross), then surveys philosophers who make the mental real: Plato's forms via broomsticks as imperfect copies of the ideal essence, Descartes's rationalism, Kant's mind-constituted objects, Berkeley's 'to be is to be perceived', Mill's phenomenalism, and Yogacara Buddhism's consciousness-fabricated emptiness.
- ch. 107 "Rowling as an Inkling" *(no index entry yet)* — Reads Rowling through C. S. Lewis and the Inklings: Lewis's 'argument from reason', born of Owen Barfield's critique of his realism and later reformulated by Alvin Plantinga (after Lewis's debate with Elizabeth Anscombe), holds that if thought were a purely subjective naturalistic event we could not trust its deliverances, so rationality must participate in a cosmic Logos. The chapter suggests this logos tradition - Greek animating principle, Stoic divine reason, and finally Christ as incarnate logos in John 1:1 - explains how Harry's King's Cross dialogue can be both mental and real, noting Rowling's remark that the location was no coincidence.
- ch. 108 "Harry’s Near-Death Experience" *(no index entry yet)* — Applies the logos framework to Harry's near-death experience: since the wizarding world already allows witches and wizards to channel themselves through portraits, imprint themselves as ghosts, possess minds, and probe thoughts by Legilimency, Dumbledore may be genuinely present to Harry's mind even while Harry lies unawakened in the forest. The chapter ends with Emerson's 'The Over-Soul', suggesting Rowling's point is not to dismiss glimpses of the divine merely because they occur 'in your head'.
- ch. 109 "NOTES" *(notes)*
- ch. 110 "Unknown" *(notes)*
- ch. 111 "“A Swirling, Silvery Mass”" **·** Personal Identity; The Extended Mind — Opens Amy Kind's Pensieve chapter: the basin lets you offload memories like data from a hard drive, gain distance from obsessions, and, as Dumbledore tells Harry, spot patterns and links when reviewing them from outside. Its real intrigue is philosophical: if thoughts can be extracted from the mind, tampered with, stored elsewhere, shared, and discarded, then the boundaries of mind, memory, and self are up for grabs - and memory-based accounts of personal identity, on which philosophers have modeled our continuing existence, come under pressure.
- ch. 112 "The Hallows of the Mind" **·** René Descartes, Thomas Hobbes — Mind–Body Dualism — Sets Hobbes-style materialism (everything, ghosts included, must be matter; Rowling's thoughts that cling to wands like hairs and leak from dying wizards support it) against Cartesian dualism, on which the immaterial mind could exist without the body - the possibility Harry entertains in King's Cross before concluding he still has a body. It reads Rowling's 'light made liquid' description of the Pensieve's silver contents as an image of the pull between the two views of the mind.
- ch. 113 "Confundus!" **·** Andy Clark, David Chalmers — The Extended Mind — Challenges the assumption that the mind is self-contained: Tom Riddle's diary, in which a memory acts and thinks for itself, and Clark and Chalmers's 'The Extended Mind' thesis show that external objects can be part of thought rather than mere aids, as with Otto's Alzheimer's notebook and a wizard's intimate bond with his wand (Moody's eye and Pettigrew's silver hand as body prostheses). Location is incidental to ownership: Snape's gossamer memories are his whether in his skull, a phial, or the Pensieve.
- ch. 114 "Mischief Managed" **·** Andy Clark, David Chalmers — The Extended Mind — Defends extension against intuition via the occurrent/nonoccurrent belief distinction: Ron's stored Quidditch trivia and Rita Skeeter's knowledge of where Bathilda Bagshot lives function exactly like Otto's notebook entries, so Otto counts as believing the address even before consulting the page. Harry's Half-Blood Prince notes, Neville's password list, and Hermione's constant Hogwarts, A History fail the test of extension because they lack the notebook's constant availability and automatic endorsement.
- ch. 115 "Lumos!" **·** John Locke, Andy Clark, David Chalmers — Personal Identity (memory theory); The Extended Mind — Pushes the theory to its disturbing limit: if the Marauder's Map is part of both Fred's and George's extended minds, minds can overlap. Since identity rests on memory continuity (Locke's theory, the adult Harry identical with the orphaned infant), memory-sharing, Obliviation, and implanted or stolen memories - Barty Crouch Jr.'s Polyjuice theft of Moody's identity, Lockhart's memory charms, Hermione's rewriting of her parents - threaten who we are, and Harry's Pensieve encounters with Snape's worst memory and his own status as a Horcrux trigger his identity crises.
- ch. 116 "NOTES" *(notes)*
- ch. 117 "Unknown" *(notes)*
- ch. 118 "The Good" **·** John Dewey, Plato, Aristotle, Immanuel Kant — Opens Gregory Bassham's evaluation of a Hogwarts education: from a child's view Hogwarts is a magic-learning paradise, but the chapter asks what the great philosophers of education - Plato, Aristotle, Kant, and John Dewey - would say about teaching and learning there, staging the question of whether Hogwarts really is the 'model school' Susan Engel claims.
- ch. 119 "Unknown" **·** John Dewey — Experience and Education — Introduces Dewey's progressive alternative to passive, rote, undemocratic schooling and argues that Hogwarts reflects all three progressive ideals: hands-on learning, building on children's natural interests, and connecting schoolwork to real life.
- ch. 120 "Unknown" **·** John Dewey — Experience and Education — Details the hands-on strength: Hogwarts teaches magic apprenticeship-style, by demonstration, practice, individualized coaching, and mastery, as in Remus Lupin teaching Harry to conjure a Patronus and Harry coaching Dumbledore's Army - the learning-by-doing that Professor Binns's droning lectures and Umbridge's theory-only classes conspicuously lack.
- ch. 121 "Unknown" **·** John Dewey — Experience and Education — Applies Dewey's point that children learn best when hooked by natural interests: Hogwarts students eagerly develop their magical abilities, and when Umbridge refuses to let her Defense class practice, the students organize Dumbledore's Army themselves, showing they grasp the practical stakes of defensive magic for exams, careers, and resisting Voldemort.
- ch. 122 "Unknown" **·** John Dewey — Experience and Education — Uses Whitehead's 'inert knowledge' and Dewey's insistence that education is part of life rather than a preamble to it: most Hogwarts learning has an obvious practical payoff (Apparition, transfiguration, defense against dark wizards), which makes students eager, while Hagrid's Blast-Ended Skrewts and Trelawney's bogus crystal-ball gazing are the inert exceptions.
- ch. 123 "Unknown" *(interlude)*
- ch. 124 "Unknown" *(no index entry yet)* — Argues Hogwarts would be recklessly dangerous as a real school, cataloguing giant spiders and hostile centaurs in the Forbidden Forest, grindylows and the giant squid, a basilisk and three-headed dog, Peeves, vanishing staircase steps, the Whomping Willow, accident-prone Potions, injury-heavy Quidditch, the Triwizard challenges, and every child carrying a wand. Magical healing has limits, since as Dumbledore says no magic can reawaken the dead.
- ch. 125 "Unknown" *(no index entry yet)* — Grades the faculty: exemplary teachers (Dumbledore, McGonagall, Flitwick, Sprout, Lupin), flawed but decent ones (Hagrid's dangerous creatures, the disguised Crouch/Moody, Snape's bullying Slytherin bias), and the truly crummy (the ghost Binns, the fraud Trelawney, the narcissist Lockhart, the supremacist Umbridge). The deeper problem is structural: the wizarding world has no teacher-training programs or certification, so anyone can teach, and quality is bound to be sketchy.
- ch. 126 "The Ugly" **·** Plato, Aristotle, John Dewey, Jean-Jacques Rousseau, John Locke, Immanuel Kant — Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Argues Hogwarts' purely vocational curriculum gives students the tools of power without the wisdom to use them: measured against Adler's three aims of schooling, it surveys the classic accounts of education's purpose - Plato's wisdom and well-ordered society, Aristotle's eudaimonia as excellent activity, Locke's 'virtue and wisdom', Rousseau's natural child-centered education, Kant's character formation, Dewey's growth - and concludes Hogwarts fails to form lifelong learners equipped for wizarding society's problems, including, as Hermione says, wizards' sense of superiority to other creatures.
- ch. 127 "Like Bertie Bott’s Every-Flavor Beans, a Mix" *(no index entry yet)* — Names wizarding racial supremacism as the truly 'ugly' element: the series is a civil war between Voldemort's Nazi-like hierarchy of purebloods and Dumbledore's commitment to the equality of all rational creatures, with totalitarianism and racial cleansing the stakes. Yet Hogwarts never teaches against the evil - slurs go unpunished, the house-elves' oppression goes unmentioned in Hogwarts, A History, Slytherin (sorted for those who will 'use any means to achieve their ends') breeds Death Eaters, and Dumbledore, perhaps a gradualist libertarian, does little beyond hiring inclusively.
- ch. 128 "NOTES" *(no index entry yet)* — Bassham's verdict on the Hogwarts education chapter: weighing the castle, grounds, camaraderie, and hands-on magic against the ineffective teachers, dangerous environment, narrow curriculum, and undercurrent of intolerance, he would still send his child to Hogwarts - provided the deeper values of the fundamental equality of persons, democratic freedoms, and a well-rounded education are taught alongside.
- ch. 129 "Unknown" *(notes)*
- ch. 130 "PART FIVE - BEYOND THE VEIL DEATH, HOPE, AND MEANING" *(front)*
- ch. 131 "Remorse and Death" **·** Forgiveness — Opens Charles Taliaferro's chapter with Fawkes, who shields Harry in the Chamber of Secrets and Dumbledore at the Ministry, as the emblem of a philosophical model of moral repair: the wrongdoer must confess, feel remorse, repudiate the pleasure gained from the wrong - a kind of death to the old self - and emerge with radically new intentions, as in Christian baptism's dying and rising to new life, so that a friendship broken by betrayal can be reborn. The chapter will read Deathly Hallows through this model, with Voldemort as its perverse inversion, spreading death by clinging to life where Harry and Dumbledore win deeper life by accepting death.
- ch. 132 "The Inversion of Voldemort" **·** Forgiveness; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); Personal Identity — Distinguishes remorse from mere regret through Voldemort's cold 'I regret it' after ordering Nagini to kill Snape: remorse requires owning one's act, and at the final duel Harry addresses him as 'Tom Riddle' and demands he 'try for some remorse' - the death-to-self Riddle cannot perform. Dumbledore's remorse over the cursed Resurrection Stone ring and his arranged death, and Harry's voluntary death that destroys the Horcrux within him and restores his whole soul, exemplify dying to the past self for renewal, while Harry's renunciation of his temper toward Ron and Hermione and his love for Ginny mark his continuous moral regeneration.
- ch. 133 "The Integrity Objection" **·** Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); Immortality and the Soul — Draws Voldemort's inversion in full: each Horcrux rips and destabilizes his soul (Hermione notes only excruciating remorse could reunite it, a pain that could destroy him), his attempt on baby Harry leaves him disembodied vapor parasitic on others' blood and limbs, in stark contrast to the feasting, affection, and romance of Harry's circle. The Godric's Hollow inscription 'the last enemy that shall be destroyed is death' and Hermione's claim that the soul survives whatever happens to the body ground the contrast between Voldemort's unmourned, mundane death and the honored, grieved dead - Dobby, Fred, Lupin, Tonks, Colin Creevey.
- ch. 134 "The Fantasy Objection" **·** Personal Identity; Forgiveness — Takes up the integrity objection from psychoanalyst Melanie Klein: renouncing one's past self ('Bad Charles' versus 'New Charlie') risks self-deception, since the reformed person remains the self-same wrongdoer, as the alcoholic who stays sober for decades still knows himself to be. Taliaferro concedes the regeneration model can be abused but insists renewal requires a death-like renunciation, illustrated negatively by Snape, who loves Lily, feels remorse for the 'Mudblood' insult and for giving Voldemort the prophecy, yet cannot confess or release his resentment, and so remains unintegrated.
- ch. 135 "Fawkes’s Secret" *(no index entry yet)* — Answers the fantasy objection - that a world of spells and talking portraits makes the death-and-rebirth model of moral reform incredible - by insisting Rowling's ethics are not fantastic: loyalty, friendship, romantic love, fairness, opposition to enslavement ('free the house-elves!'), remorse, forgiveness, and reform are all in play in the Muggle world too, so dismissing her insights as imaginary is a failure of imagination rather than realism.
- ch. 136 "NOTES" **·** Forgiveness — Closes the phoenix chapter by reading Fawkes's departure from Hogwarts after Dumbledore's death - mourning his master with a stricken lament but neither curing the ring's poison nor blocking Snape's killing curse - as the quiet handing over of the phoenix's lesson: ruptures of friendship and community caused by wrongdoing can only be healed through a kind of death and rebirth, in which remorseful confession burns up the old self and produces a person with radically new desires and intentions.
- ch. 137 "Unknown" *(notes)*
- ch. 138 "Death and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 139 "The Approaching Battle" **·** Martin Heidegger, Epicurus, Plato — Being-Toward-Death; Authenticity; Death Is Nothing to Us — Introduces the chapter's Heideggerian frame: authenticity comes from facing mortality rather than evading it, against the Epicurean advice to be unconcerned since death is merely the end of experience and so happens to no one; death's imminence forces us to define ourselves and not live half-asleep. Harry, death-marked since infancy, lives authentically where Voldemort lives inauthentically, with Plato's dictum that true philosophers make dying their profession as ancient corroboration.
- ch. 140 "King’s Cross Station" **·** Martin Heidegger — Being-Toward-Death; Authenticity — Reads Harry's march into the Forbidden Forest as paradigmatic Heideggerian authenticity: knowing he must die to destroy the soul-fragment in him, his senses sharpen, gratitude wells up for what he never fully valued, and he accepts that he is not fetching his dead loved ones but joining them. The Godric's Hollow gravestone first strikes him as a Death Eater creed of defeating death, and Colin Creevey's return to battle at fourteen shows the same clear-eyed embrace of mortality.
- ch. 141 "Reap a Destiny" **·** Martin Heidegger — Being-Toward-Death; Authenticity; Immortality and the Soul — Reads the King's Cross limbo through Heidegger's counsel to choose a hero from the past and gain insight through dialogue with them: Dumbledore appears not as a king of that place but as the humble penitent, confessing his youthful hunger for glory, and the way-station evokes purgatory. The flayed child beneath the bench shows Voldemort's soul beyond repair - his fear of death drove the very choices that now give him reason to fear it - and Tolkien's remark that The Lord of the Rings is really about 'death and the desire for deathlessness' (Sauron pouring his life-force into the One Ring) frames the motif.
- ch. 142 "NOTES" **·** Martin Heidegger, Aristotle, William James, John Locke, Immanuel Kant, Blaise Pascal — Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); Free Will vs Determinism; Personal Identity; Immortality and the Soul — Argues via William James's 'sow a thought... reap a destiny' and Aristotle's account of how repeated action incrementally shapes the soul that Voldemort's flayed remnant is a character fully forged by choices, stuck with itself forever - a postmortem fate Heidegger's atheism cannot frame but Rowling's afterlife can. The section weighs Locke's memory-and-character account of identity, Kant's postulate that harmony of virtue and happiness requires an afterlife, and Pascal on why the immortality question must govern how we live, and ends asking whether Voldemort's evil is ignorance, as Plato held, or a cultivated appetite that only vice can satisfy.
- ch. 143 "Unknown" *(notes)*
- ch. 144 "Fulfillment for Muggles and Wizards" *(front)*
- ch. 145 "Voldemort and the Sophists" **·** Socrates, Plato — Justice in the Republic — Opens Michael Austin's chapter with the common question of Harry and Socrates - why die for one's convictions, i.e., why be moral at all? - and states Plato's Republic answer that being moral is necessary and sufficient for true happiness, positioning the Harry/Voldemort conflict as Socrates against his sophisticated enemies.
- ch. 146 "The Common Good versus the Greater Good" **·** Plato, Socrates — Justice in the Republic; Man Is the Measure (sophistic relativism); Tripartite Soul — Maps Voldemort onto the Sophists - Thrasymachus's claim that the unjust always come out ahead - through his pattern of exploiting followers' trust for power and pleasure, while Plato's two-pronged reply runs: cosmic justice in the afterlife (the flayed child beneath the bench as Voldemort's just fate, sealing it by refusing remorse) and the intrinsic goodness of justice via the tripartite soul with reason ruling spirit and appetite, the four cardinal virtues, and the health analogy. Harry, seeking Dumbledore's wisdom, showing courage in facing death, and devoted to justice for wizards and Muggles, embodies the moral life as the rational choice, while Voldemort's snakelike appearance externalizes his disordered, sick soul.
- ch. 147 "Two Keys to the Good Life" **·** Consequentialism; Communitarianism — Uses young Dumbledore's confession about Grindelwald - 'it would all be for the greater good' while Muggles were forced into subservience - to distinguish the greater good, which sacrifices individuals' rights and dignity for a supposed collective benefit, from the common good, sacrifice for the good of all, as in the civil rights movement's claim that violating some Americans' rights harms the whole community. Harry's choice to return from King's Cross and finish Voldemort is a decision for the common good of wizards and Muggles alike.
- ch. 148 "The End of the Story" **·** Socrates, Aristotle, Confucius — Eudaimonia (flourishing); The Unexamined Life — Argues Socrates and Dumbledore both die for virtue and the common good: Socrates refuses exile because the examined life matters more than wealth or reputation, his martyrdom an exemplar that could benefit Athens, and Dumbledore's arranged death protects Snape's spying for the anti-Voldemort cause. Two Muggle lessons follow - live devoted to something larger than yourself (Lily's sacrifice, with King, Gandhi, Jesus, and Socrates as real-world parallels) and find mentors, as Dumbledore's humility, honesty, and praise in the Order of the Phoenix prophecy conversation enable Harry to fulfill his destiny, with dead mentors like Aristotle, Confucius, and Jesus still able to teach us through their books.
- ch. 149 "NOTES" *(no index entry yet)* — Closes the chapter with the challenge 'Who would you rather be, Harry or Voldemort?', insisting the answer must stand even if Voldemort had won, since love's value does not depend on apparent victory, and ending with Dumbledore's words that the true master of death accepts that he must die.

## Heroes and Philosophy - Buy the Book, Save the World

- ch. 1 "section-1" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Heroes and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "The Blackwell Philosophy and Pop Culture Series" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Copyright Page" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Acknowledgements" *(notes)*
- ch. 7 "NOTES" *(no index entry yet)* — The editor's introduction argues that Heroes is more reflective than average television, opening from Mohinder's 'Genesis' voice-over about life's mysteries and claiming that we are all philosophers seeking answers to the same fundamental questions. It frames narrative (from Aesop's fables to the Bible) as a powerful vehicle for moral lessons and previews the book's questions: is the Company good or evil, does Hiro really have a destiny, could mind reading be a power we already have, is time travel possible, what does Peter owe Nathan, and is it okay to lie to hide one's powers? It closes with John McCain's SNL joke defending Heroes against its competition.
- ch. 8 "PART ONE - HEROIC OBLIGATIONS" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "NOTES" **·** Thomas Hobbes, John Rawls, John Locke, Plato — The Social Contract; The State of Nature; The Veil of Ignorance; Ring of Gyges (why be moral?) — Sharp argues that superpowered individuals break the equality assumption on which social contract theory rests: since Claire cannot be killed, D.L. can walk out of any jail, and Matt can sway juries, Hobbes's fear-of-death rationale for the contract seems not to apply to them. He then answers that the heroes remain effectively equal (Claire can still be killed by a wood splinter, Adam by decapitation) and shares Locke's and Rawls's reasons for contract, yet shows Rawls's difference principle would force the powered to serve society while the liberty principle forbids it, and that Sylar could not even be morally condemned under a pure Hobbesian reading. Cases like Claude's invisible petty theft (Glaucon's Ring of Gyges made real), the 'Five Years Gone' registered-superhumans future, and Linderman's plot to reunite society through the New York explosion lead to the conclusion that superpowers really would break society.
- ch. 10 "NOTES" **·** Peter Singer, Immanuel Kant — The Drowning Child (obligation to aid); Deontology — Miles asks whether extraordinary power generates extraordinary obligations, testing Spider-Man's 'with great power comes great responsibility' against Claude the invisible thief and the heroes' reluctance. He uses Singer's principle of minimal obligation (Micah diverting the train from the infant; Claire's negligible risk in burning-building rescues; Hiro teleporting across distance) and Kant's perfect/imperfect obligation distinction (Niki choosing between saving Monica and mothering Micah; Noah Bennet lying to protect Claire) before deploying Fishkin's three 'explosion' objections: too many endangered people, too many capable heroes, and too many repeated rescues dilute obligation. He concludes the heroes went beyond the call of duty in saving New York, so saving the world could legitimately be their paid job, like lifeguards or soldiers.
- ch. 11 "NOTES" **·** Michael Sandel — Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Utilitarianism — Robichaud evaluates the morally gray Company: its goal of mastering the evolutionary forces behind powers is tested against eugenics worries, the 'playing God' objection that natural processes have intrinsic value, and Sandel's Case against Perfection (that mastery breeds inflated responsibility, eroded community, and rigidity), each of which he finds inconclusive or outweighed by lives saved from people like Ted Sprague. He then turns to blame for the bag-and-tag program, applying Dennis Thompson's criterion to hold the founders plus hands-on agents like Bennet and the Haitian responsible while excusing accountants like Malsky. Finally he sets conditions for when an insider such as Bennet or Mohinder may blow the whistle, ruling out Bennet's murderous take-down-from-within scheme.
- ch. 12 "NOTES" *(no index entry yet)* — Southworth defends Tim Kring against comic fans' plagiarism charges (Heroes versus X-Men's Rogue and Wolverine, Rising Stars' identical plot), arguing plagiarism requires intending to pass off known others' ideas as one's own, which Kring's ignorance of the genre (he asked 'Who's Magneto?') rules out, even accidentally. But he then convicts Kring of failing his epistemic responsibility, applying Montmarquet's virtues: Kring lacked epistemic responsibility (not reading the Rising Stars Lindelof gave him), impartiality (deliberately refusing to learn the genre), and sobriety (pressing on despite Loeb's warning that everything he pitched had been done). Parallel cases from the show include Mohinder's early dismissal of his father's research and Niki's obligation to learn about Jessica.
- ch. 13 "PART TWO - SUPERMEN, SAMURAI, AND INVISIBLE MEN" *(front)*
- ch. 14 "NOTES" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — Eternal Recurrence; Amor Fati (love of fate); Fate and Foreknowledge; The Meaning of Life — Shores reads Volume 1 through Nietzsche's eternal recurrence as a self-test of life-affirmation: Adam sealed in a coffin embodies recurrence in its 'most terrible form,' Future Hiro's string map dramatizes Nietzsche's claim that every action is indissolubly tied to all others, and Linderman's speech divorcing happiness from meaning is answered by Nietzsche's demand to crave nothing more fervently than the eternal return. The chapter stresses where the show diverges: Claire insists 'the future is not written in stone' and the heroes rewrite destiny, while Arthur Petrelli ironically holds a copy of Thus Spoke Zarathustra while contradicting it. Hiro's failed attempts to save Charlie and his father (Kaito's 'we cannot play God') frame the amor fati question of whether free will means changing events or willing them eternally.
- ch. 15 "NOTES" **·** Carl Jung — Archetypes; The Collective Unconscious; Zen Buddhism — Baldwin argues the popular view of Bushido as a moral code (Nitobe, Star Wars, Kurosawa, Campbell's Jungian hero archetype) is historically wrong: by the Hagakure, Bushido is a death-ready 'mode of action' so morally neutral that even villains like Takezo Kensei or Sylar can practice it. He traces Hiro's evolution from comic-book and video-game hero-worship, through the Yaeko betrayal that turned Kensei into Adam Monroe, toward the fearless Future Hiro ideal of 'no-mind' readiness for death (echoing D.T. Suzuki's Zen reading). Because the Meiji reforms abolished the samurai class, Hiro can never literally be a samurai, only follow Bushido analogically, which lets him remain both superhero and samurai.
- ch. 16 "NOTES" **·** Plato — Ring of Gyges (why be moral?); Tripartite Soul; Justice in the Republic — Adams uses Plato's Ring of Gyges to ask whether superpowered people have reason to be moral, answering through the tripartite soul: Claire declining credit for the fire rescue shows well-governed thumos, her calculated revenge on Brody shows logistics ruling spirit, while Sylar's uncontrollable hunger for powers is textbook epithumetikon and intemperance that makes his life spin 'super' out of control. Cases like Micah's ATM thefts and Hiro's Vegas cheating (punished by a desert beating) illustrate that vice harms the agent's own soul, since justice is the soul's health. Claire's hospital apology to Brody is read through the Pagan Golden Rule and Sophocles' Ajax: enemies can become friends, so virtue, not mere conformity, is 'only natural' for heroes and ordinary people alike.
- ch. 17 "PART THREE - METAPHYSICS, REGULAR PHYSICS, AND HEROIC TIME TRAVEL" *(front)*
- ch. 18 "NOTES" **·** Aristotle, Boethius — Fate and Foreknowledge; Free Will vs Determinism — Johnson asks whether Isaac's paintings and Hiro's visions make the future fated, running Aristotle's sea-battle argument: if propositions about the future are already true (by bivalence and the correspondence theory of a 'written timeline'), then Sylar's exploding New York is inevitable and no one is free. He extends the problem to infallible foreknowledge via Future Hiro as a stand-in for God, considers and rejects Boethius's timeless-knowledge solution, and uses open theism plus a probability calculation about Ando's waffles order to show probabilistic foreknowledge is useless. He concludes most of Isaac's 'prophecies' are really self-fulfilling, as when Noah's efforts to dodge the eighth painting cause his shooting, and that the Heroes universe leaves room to deny fate.
- ch. 19 "NOTES" **·** David Lewis — The Metaphysics of Time Travel; Possible Worlds — Luck argues backward time travel is nearly useless for a hero: after the grandfather paradox (Hiro hearing of Kaito's murder only because Kaito died), he shows Lewis's linear time travel makes Hiro necessarily fail to save Kaito, Charlie, or Isaac, yet cannot explain Hiro meeting Ando two days after a five-week absence or New York being spared. Branching time travel, where each trip into the past spawns an alternate universe, fits Volume 1 coherently by building a Heroes multiverse of Hiro-1 through Hiro-4 and explaining Future Hiro's subway appearance as a 'brute fact' copied into branching universes. The price of coherence is that Hiro only ever saves other universes' New Yorks, never his own.
- ch. 20 "NOTES" **·** Thomas Aquinas — The Metaphysics of Time Travel — Faraci asks why Hiro may prevent the nuclear future ('save the cheerleader') but comes to agree it would be wrong to save Kaito from the past ('we cannot play God'). Using thought experiments (a variant where Hiro learns of his father's death in the future) he isolates the past/future asymmetry as the candidate morally relevant factor, then tests justifications: Aquinas's natural law fails because observing the past would be equally 'unnatural,' and a duty to keep the timeline intact would also forbid changing the future, which is just as 'settled' to a time traveler. Since every theory of where Hiro's 'present' lies fails or collapses into subjectivity, he concludes there is no good reason to treat altering the past differently from altering the future, while demonstrating how normative ethics evaluates moral intuitions.
- ch. 21 "NOTES" *(no index entry yet)* — Tests the science behind the powers: Claire's and Adam Monroe's rapid healing is deemed barely possible (retained telomeres, error-free cell replication) but far too fast to be realistic, while Nathan's flight survives neither rocket-propellant nor magnetic-levitation explanations, leaving undirected manipulation of gravity as the only option. Hiro's teleportation and time travel are analyzed with Minkowski diagrams, worldlines, and light cones — his rescue of Claire from Sylar and Elle in 'The Eclipse: Part II' puts his broken worldline outside every light cone — and wormholes, quantum entanglement, and quantum tunneling are each rejected as workable mechanisms. The chapter then shows Darwinian evolution cannot produce Dr. Suresh's 'evolutionary jump': even Adam Monroe fathering children across the globe for four hundred years could not spread and diversify a power gene that fast, concluding that only design (God, aliens, or destiny) could explain the show's powers.
- ch. 22 "NOTES" **·** Thomas Kuhn, William of Ockham — Paradigm Shifts; Ockham's Razor; Falsificationism — Asks how to tell genuine science from pseudoscience using Chandra Suresh's rejected Activating Evolution theory, Sheldrake's morphic fields, alchemy, and Behe's intelligent design. Kuhn's picture of normal science, anomalies, and revolutions explains why Suresh would need a full-blown crisis in evolutionary theory — such as people actually flying — before the community accepts him, and why time rather than mockery settles a theory's fate. Ockham's razor condemns the unnecessary organizing principles in both Sheldrake's and Suresh's theories, and intelligent design is exposed as bad method because, like creationism, it was devised to protect a prior commitment from disconfirming fossil and cosmological evidence.
- ch. 23 "PART FOUR - THE MINDS OF HEROES" *(front)*
- ch. 24 "NOTE" **·** John Locke, Thomas Reid, Derek Parfit, Bernard Williams — Personal Identity; Personal Identity (memory theory); Ship of Theseus; Teleporter Paradox (personal fission) — Asks whether amnesia preserves numerical identity: is the Peter who wakes in the shipping crate still Volume-1 Peter, are Niki and Jessica one person, and is the Sylar whom Matt rewrites with Nathan's memories now Nathan? The chapter builds Locke's memory criterion, breaks it with Reid's transitivity objection run through Mohinder's timeline in 'Five Years Gone,' repairs it with Grice's and Perry's memory-ancestor chains, then destroys the repair with Arthur Petrelli's wipe of Hiro (Forgetful Hiro vs. Stabby Hiro), Bernard Williams's torture thought experiment, Parfit's psychological spectrum, and the graphic novel's branching self-copier Evan. It concludes that no memory criterion can define personal identity, yet memory still matters practically: Claire must act as if she persists over time to keep her promise to Zach and to bother finishing the manatee assignment.
- ch. 25 "NOTES" **·** René Descartes, David Chalmers — Philosophical Zombies; The Cogito; Methodological Doubt (evil demon) — Distinguishes Matt Parkman's supernatural telepathy from the 'mundane mind reading' everyone performs, then argues even Matt's power cannot solve the problem of other minds: the suicidal woman's thoughts he hears in the bar ('One Giant Leap') could be part of a Maury-Parkman-style illusion, and a brain-to-brain link could never rule out that others are Chalmers-style philosophical zombies. After grounding the skepticism in Descartes' cogito and method of doubt, it defends our everyday attribution of minds by analogy (Audrey Hanson reading Claire's insincerity) on pragmatic grounds, and maps Matt's progression from hearing thoughts to nightmare manipulation and compulsion onto the four adaptive functions philosophers assign to mind reading: interpretation, prediction, coordination, and influence. It closes by showing why neuroimaging-based technological mind reading remains a pipe dream, since brain recordings are quantitative syntax without semantics.
- ch. 26 "NOTES" **·** David Hume, Adam Smith — The Impartial Spectator; Reason Is the Slave of the Passions — Takes Claude's label of Peter as an 'empath' seriously and tests three accounts of empathy against his power: Hume's emotional resonance (rejected because Peter keeps powers after the donor leaves), Scheler's absorption into another's identity, and Hume's conversion of ideas into vivid impressions, settling on the mirror-neuron thought that Peter literally replicates other people's brain structures — which explains why he copies Isaac's painting skill and even Sylar's anger in 'Kindred.' It then asks whether empathy makes you moral and answers no — Elle enjoys Peter's pain and Mohinder feels his experimental subjects' terror — arguing instead that empathy needs a filter: Hume's general point of view and Adam Smith's impartial spectator, illustrated by Hiro's imagined Kensei ideal and by Nathan serving as Peter's emotional filter until his death leaves Peter without one.
- ch. 27 "PART FIVE - VILLAINS, FAMILY, AND LYING" *(front)*
- ch. 28 "NOTES" **·** Carol Gilligan, Socrates, Aristotle, Friedrich Nietzsche, Augustine of Hippo — The Ethics of Care; The Übermensch; The Golden Mean; Socratic Ignorance (knowing that you don't know) — Reads the show's eclipse icon against Plato's sun of the good — goodness has become hard to discern — and tests four theories of evil on the characters: Socrates' claim that no one errs knowingly is half-confirmed since villains like Angela and Nathan always take themselves to be doing right; Aristotle's akrasia and vice as an excess or deficiency of disposition fit Sylar's blood-scrawled 'Forgive me' apartment and his uncontrolled hunger; and Nietzsche's Übermensch and pre-Christian master morality raise the uncomfortable question of why Sylar preying on inferiors is not heroic like Achilles. The chapter's endorsed answer is Gilligan's ethics of care: the heroes are good because they care — Matt for Molly and Daphne, Bennet for Claire — while evil is the absence of care, though Bennet's authoritarian, family-only care shows that care itself must be both widened and restrained.
- ch. 29 "NOTES" **·** Confucius, Socrates — The Ethics of Care; Xiao (filial piety) — Explains the seemingly incomprehensible things Heroes characters do for family by sorting their motives into three theories of family obligation: universal justice (framed by Socrates confronting Euthyphro for prosecuting his own father), care ethics' partiality to loved ones, and Confucian role-based duty with its asymmetrical parent–child, husband–wife, and sibling obligations. Noah Bennet turns out to be Confucian in practice ('I'm your father, Claire Bear') despite his universalist Company rhetoric, Hiro defies Kaito's Confucianism for the greater good while Kaito still teaches him the sword because that is a father's role, Niki and Janice act from care while D.L. and Micah act from roles, Mohinder's duty is Confucian sonship to his dead father, and the Haitian kills his own brother Baron precisely because stopping him is his brotherly role.
- ch. 30 "NOTES" **·** Immanuel Kant, Plato, Niccolò Machiavelli — The Categorical Imperative; The Noble Lie; Utilitarianism; The Ends Justify the Means — First defines lying — distinguishing it from mere error (Angela believing Peter dead), from the Haitian's non-verbal memory-wipe deception, and from Hiro's silent staging of Ando's fake death — then tests moral theories against the show's liars. Kant's categorical imperative bans all lying but is abandoned when Sylar demands a friend's hiding place; utilitarianism vindicates Victoria Pratt's lie about the virus vial yet also licenses Linderman's .07% explosion and Plato's noble lie of the earthborn metals, which the chapter rejects. Machiavelli's advice that princes may lie to keep power is played out by 'President Sylar' in 'Five Years Gone,' and the chapter finally endorses Sissela Bok's presumption against lying — a public-reasoning procedure modeled by Bob Bishop and Mohinder before Monica's virus injection.
- ch. 31 "CONTRIBUTORS" *(notes)*
- ch. 32 "CHANDRA SURESH’S LIST" *(front)*
- ch. 33 "INDEX" *(notes)*

## Hip-Hop and Philosophy - Rhyme 2 Reason

- ch. 1 "section-1" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Title Page" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Hip-Hop and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Epigraph" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Popular Culture and Philosophy™" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Dedication" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Foreword" **·** Socrates, Plato, William James — Practical Wisdom (phronesis); The Unexamined Life — Cornel West argues that Plato's dialogues invented philosophy as a Socratic genre of cross-examination and bold frank speech (parrhesia) about ordinary life, and that hip-hop music - like the spirituals, blues, and jazz - enacts that same parrhesia against conventional morality and entrenched power. He claims reading hip hop philosophically demands metaphilosophical subtlety, showing how paideia, phronesis, and parrhesia operate in the work of cultural producers under free-market fundamentalism and authoritarianism. Because the racial dimension of hip hop is inescapable, he says philosophers must break their discipline's silence on race and music (neglected figures like Du Bois and Alain Locke alongside Aristotle, Kant, and Rorty), closing with Cicero's Socrates who turned philosophy from the heavens to earth and William James's pragmatism that took it from the academy to the streets.
- ch. 8 "Shout Outs" *(notes)*
- ch. 9 "From Rhyme to Reason This Shit Ain’t Easy" **·** Socrates, Plato — Socratic Method (elenchus); The Unexamined Life; The Philosopher King — The editors' introduction argues that hip hop and philosophy are both street-born examinations of life in pursuit of wisdom: Socrates's elenchus is likened to MC lyrical battles and call-and-response (his interrogations compared to Rakim's technique, his execution for corrupting the youth to the fate of a controversial rapper), while Plato's philosopher-king parallels Kurtis Blow and Run-D.M.C. claiming thrones. After a Bronx hip-hop history sketch (Kool Herc, BDP's "The Bridge is Over"), they frame the volume around hip hop's suppressed fifth element of knowledge and preview its pairings: Aquinas and Rakim on God, Plato and Lauryn Hill on love, Sartre and Lil' Kim's gaze, Kant and Ice Cube on punishment, Hegel and Nas, Hobbes and Dead Prez, Mill and Chappelle on epithets. Record companies don't own hip hop and universities don't own philosophy, they conclude - both belong to whoever understands, loves, and respects them.
- ch. 10 "Disk 1 - Da Mysteries God, Love, and Knowledge" *(front)*
- ch. 11 "Ain’t No Mystery" **·** Thomas Aquinas, Socrates, René Descartes — Framed by Rakim's "The Mystery" and Five-Percenter knowledge-of-self, Darby asks who God is and attacks divine omnipotence with the "paradox of the blunt" and "paradox of the glock" - can God roll a blunt so strong even he can't hit it? - then defends Aquinas's Summa Theologica restriction of God's power to the logically possible, against Descartes's claim that God can do even the impossible. He presses further with Tupac's jailbreak paradox from a Vibe interview ("If you God, then pop the fuckin' gate") and Aquinas's paradox of sin, resolving them via Samuel Clarke's natural/moral contradiction distinction and Socrates's Crito arguments for why a perfectly good person refuses escape and accepts the state's verdict.
- ch. 12 "The Mysteries of Love Revealed" **·** Plato — Platonic Love (the ladder of love); Philosophy of Love — Shelby stages Plato's Symposium as a cipher of raps on love to answer hip-hop "love haters" like Jay-Z ("I got ninety-nine problems but a bitch ain't one"): Pausanias's vulgar versus spiritual love, Aristophanes's myth of split halves read through Method Man and Mary J. Blige's "You're All I Need" and Biggie's "Me & My Bitch", and Socrates's ladder from beautiful bodies to Beauty itself, with Lauryn Hill's "Zion" as birth of beautiful offspring. He objects that Socrates makes the beloved a replaceable means to the good, and argues that Alcibiades's drunken confession supplies what is missing - love's possession, exclusivity, reciprocity, and drama (The Roots: "lies come in, that's where the drama begins") - concluding that love ain't just 'bout da booty but demands a vulnerability the love hater rightly fears.
- ch. 13 "Proper Use of the Mind" **·** René Descartes — Green reads Del tha Funkee Homosapien's line on Gorillaz's "Clint Eastwood" ("you don't see with your eye, you perceive with your mind") alongside Descartes's Meditations window passage - judging men in the square from hats and coats - to argue against the sense-data picture that perception is mind-guided, interest-driven interpretation rather than passive reception. He then deploys the "new" adaptive unconscious (the cocktail party effect, the love-on-the-bridge misattribution of arousal, unconscious racism in housing audits, lottery winners no happier - "mo money, mo problems") to show we are strangers to ourselves, so the proper use of the mind means standing back from one's patterns, as 2Pac's "Letter 2 My Unborn" and KRS-One's "The Mind" urge.
- ch. 14 "Disk 2 - What’s Beef Ruminations on Violence" *(front)*
- ch. 15 "Hip Hop and “This Makin’ Dollars Shit”" **·** G.W.F. Hegel, W.E.B. Du Bois, Karl Marx — Struggle for Recognition; Master–Slave Dialectic; Class Struggle; The Dialectic — Pittman argues the rap battle - UTFO's "Roxanne, Roxanne" answered by Roxanne Shanté's "Roxanne's Revenge", Run-D.M.C.'s "Sucker MC's", Jay-Z's "Takeover" versus Nas's "Ether" - is hip hop's enactment of Hegel's master-slave struggle for recognition, where self-consciousness proves itself only through an all-or-nothing contest ending in domination rather than destruction. Gangsta rap (N.W.A's "Gangsta Gangsta", Ice Cube's fantasy raid on the suburbs) radicalizes the battle into a collective demand that America recognize black urban conditions, and the chapter turns to Du Bois's race-centered and Marx's class-centered revisions of Hegel's history to read "this makin' dollars shit" - Wu-Tang's "C.R.E.A.M.", Talib Kweli on capitalism and crack, Mos Def's "The Rape Over" - as the dialectic's capture of the cipher by corporate beef.
- ch. 16 "(Positive) Violence of Self-Discipline" **·** Plato, Aristotle, John Dewey, William James — Pragmatism — Shusterman opens with Plato and Socrates' ancient quarrel against poetry - banning the artists from the Republic, much as rap is demonized today - and rejects Aristotle's catharsis strategy of quarantining art from life, applying pragmatist aesthetics (Dewey, James, and the Du Bois-Alain Locke lineage) to argue that rap's aesthetic violence of beat, scratch, and "Bring the Noise" is life-enhancing force, with reality rap's metaphysics of mix and flow against Plato's changeless Forms. Reading KRS-One's "Stop the Violence" movement born from Scott LaRock's killing, Guru's "Choice of Weapons" (a mic or a gun), and Jeru the Damaja's command to "kill in thyself", he concludes rap overcomes bad violence not by abolishing violence but by channeling it into lyrical battle and the positive violence of self-discipline.
- ch. 17 "Resistance Raps" **·** Thomas Hobbes — The Social Contract; The State of Nature — James reads hardcore and underground rap - N.W.A's "F*** Tha Police", Ice-T's "Cop Killer", Dead Prez's "Police State" sampling Omali Yeshitela, Tupac's "Dear Mama" with its Panther 21/Cointelpro footage, Michael Franti's "Crime to Be Broke in America" - through Hobbes's Leviathan to argue that for criminalized black communities the social contract never held, so policing is not aberrational protection but the foundation of a police state. She distinguishes rebellion from revolution, argues capital converts the underground's insurgence into marketable alienation, faults hardcore's misogyny and policing of women and queers as counter-revolutionary, and concludes that a Leviathan which is the law cannot cohabit with democracy.
- ch. 18 "Disk 3 - That’s How I’m Livin’ Authenticity, Blackness, and Sexuality" *(front)*
- ch. 19 "No Love (for Hip Hop), No problem" **·** Cornel West — Communitarianism; The Culture Industry — Taylor starts from his own lack of passion for hip hop as a black man and the "Eminem enigma" of the respected white rapper to ask whether hip hop belongs to black people by nature, using Dre's sellout dilemma in Brown Sugar to examine the communitarian language of roots, authenticity, and alienation. Following Cornel West's "racialist reasoning", he argues classical racialism is false, and that even critical racialism - races as socially constructed groups - plus the survival, duty, and cultural-preservation arguments (hip hop as "the black CNN", the ecosystem argument for reclaiming hip hop from the culture industry) fail to obligate blacks to embrace it, so no love for hip hop, no problem.
- ch. 20 "The Possibility of Authenticity The Life We Choose" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Judith Butler, bell hooks — The Look (hell is other people); Gender Performativity; Objectification; Authenticity — Gines reads hip hop's sexual imagery through Black feminist thought - Tupac's feud with C. Delores Tucker over "Wonder Why They Call U Bitch", Patricia Hill Collins's controlling images of the jezebel and welfare mother, bell hooks on the slave-rooted devaluation of Black womanhood, Nelly's "Tip Drill" video with a credit card swiped between a woman's buttocks, Jay-Z's "Big Pimpin'". Applying Sartre's keyhole gaze from Being and Nothingness and his Anti-Semite and Jew point about hierarchical societies, she argues the gaze under racism and sexism is non-reciprocal - women are seen without seeing - and that Lil' Kim's reversal of it reinforces rather than subverts the stereotypes; Butler's gender performativity then frames the closing Sartrean choice of authenticity or inauthenticity, Nas's "I Can" and "the life we choose."
- ch. 21 "The Hunger for More Than Serious Play" **·** Frantz Fanon, Friedrich Nietzsche, Jean-Jacques Rousseau, W.E.B. Du Bois — Black Skin, White Masks — Gordon argues hip hop is in practice black adolescent culture wrongly elevated to authentic blackness, and reads the problem through Fanon's Black Skin, White Masks: the white world's sociogenic construction le nègre denies black adulthood, freezing black people in perpetual childhood so there is no coherent black adult. Using Nietzsche's Birth of Tragedy account of health as creative confrontation with suffering, Rousseau's critique of modernity's underside, and Du Bois on being made "problem people", he separates hip hop's postmodern unruly form (off-key singing, beat over song) from its often conventional content (Dead Prez's nostalgic revolutionary imagery), and finds mature alternatives in the Philly sound - Me'Shell Ndegeocello, Angie Stone, the Roots - whose adult play knows death, the hunger for more than serious play.
- ch. 22 "Disk 4 - Word Up! Language, Meaning, and Ethics" *(front)*
- ch. 23 "Hip-Hop Lyrics No Black CNN and No Art of Storytelling" *(no index entry yet)* — Thompson pits Chuck D's "black CNN" communicative-message model of lyrical meaning - lyrics tell truths rooted in the lyricist's real life - against Young Buck's narrative-story model - lyrics tell as-if truths from adopted personae like Slim Shady, Snoop Dogg, and Lil' Kim. Working through Kelis's "Milkshake" via Jerrold Levinson's theory of what an artwork says, Eminem's "Stan", the Ja Rule versus 50 Cent beef, Mario Winans's "bull shoes", Lil' Flip's freestyling, and Lauryn Hill's Miseducation persona-shuttling, he argues no synthesis survives because intentions split across insider and outsider audiences: lyrical meaning teeters on personae that remain genuine extensions of lived lives, so hip hop is neither black CNN nor pure storytelling, and that undecidability is what keeps listeners involved.
- ch. 24 "Check Out Time" **·** John Stuart Mill, Immanuel Kant, David Hume — The Harm Principle; Kingdom of Ends; Utilitarianism — McGrath and Tilahun ask whether hip hop is bad for women, setting protest and sex-positive tracks (Tupac's "Brenda's Got a Baby", Missy's "One Minute Man", Queen Latifah's "Ladies First", Ms. Dynamite) against endorsements of violence (N.W.A's "One Less Bitch", Snoop's "Can You Control Yo Ho?", 50 Cent's "P.I.M.P"). They apply Mill's harm principle - offend yes, harm no - Hume's counterfactual analysis of causation (the Derek Fisher shot case) to show that whether lyrics cause violence is an unsettled empirical question, Austin and Rae Langton's speech-act theory to argue rappers' cultural authority may let pimp lyrics subordinate women in a way pornography cannot, and Kant's kingdom of ends to charge misogynist lyrics with treating women as mere means.
- ch. 25 "Final Skit Paris’s Field Nigga Boogie" **·** John Stuart Mill — The Harm Principle — Corlett evaluates the ethics of "bitch" and "nigga" in hip hop through Dave Chappelle's comedy, using the use-mention distinction and speaker intention to argue Chappelle's gender-neutral, affectionate, equivocating uses ("now that's a nigger who's one crazy nigger", the blind Black Klansman of "Frontline: Black KKK", "Reparations 2003") are racial rather than racist language. Invoking Mill's harm principle - words should be legally barred only when they harm, as with the KKK cross-burning example, not merely offend - he defends in-group reclamation, then extends the analysis to Paris's "Field Nigga Boogie" and "AWOL", whose epithets carry a progressive, Malcolm X-style challenge to unjust wars and state evil.
- ch. 26 "Disk 5 - Fight the Power Political Philosophy’n the Hood" *(front)*
- ch. 27 "Post-Civil Rights Music" **·** John Locke, Charles W. Mills, Cornel West — The Social Contract; The Racial Contract; Natural Rights — Lawson argues political rap - Grandmaster Flash's "The Message", Public Enemy's "911 Is a Joke", Tupac's "Trapped", Dead Prez's "They Schools", Naughty By Nature, Ice Cube's "I Wanna Kill Sam" - challenges liberal political philosophy by claiming the social contract between blacks and the United States is broken: using Locke's Second Treatise account of state protection of life and property, he contends the state's failure of economic and physical protection voids blacks' political obligations. Drawing on Cornel West's diagnosis of black nihilism and Charles Mills's racial contract - a contract constructed for white supremacy - he reads conscious hip hop as post-civil-rights music continuing the black freedom struggle in a new voice, with KRS-One asking "Who protects us from you?"
- ch. 28 "Reality versus Revolution" **·** Thomas Hobbes, John Rawls — The State of Nature; The Veil of Ignorance; Communitarianism; The Social Contract — McPherson argues political rap has never been politically revolutionary, sorting it into three schools: the amoral "gangsta Hobbes" ethic (Mobb Deep's "Survival of the Fittest", 50 Cent's "get rich or die tryin'") that sees the hood as Hobbes's state of nature where no social contract with blacks ever existed; Afrocentric communitarianism, split between nationalist separatists (Brand Nubian, X-Clan, Dead Prez) and the more cosmopolitan Native Tongues (De La Soul, A Tribe Called Quest); and a radical liberalism closer to Rawls than its adherents admit. Against Chuck D's "What's a smilin' face when the whole state's racist?", he argues Rawls's original position, veil of ignorance, and difference principle - applied with real-world knowledge of racism - support rather than oppose Public Enemy's demands for racial justice.
- ch. 29 "Prison Ghetto" **·** Immanuel Kant, Jeremy Bentham, Michel Foucault — Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation; Discipline and Docile Bodies — Kelly takes gangsta rap's payback attitude (50 Cent, Nas, Dr. Dre, OutKast) as the starting point for the philosophy of punishment: rappers doubt retribution because police, courts, and prisons lack legitimate authority over them - Public Enemy's "Black Steel in the Hour of Chaos" rejects the government's letter. Weighing Kantian and Nozickian retributivism against Bentham's consequentialist rationales, she argues desert, proportionality, and authority all fail in a racist system - racial profiling, the crack-versus-powder sentencing disparity, Wacquant's prison-ghetto thesis, Goodie Mob's "Cell Therapy", Dead Prez's "Behind Enemy Lines", KRS-One's officer-overseer analogy - leaving incapacitation as racialized social control rather than justice.
